More than three hundred and fifty years ago, in the soft light of spring 1492, the dawn swept across the world with a quiet, unchanging grace. As the angel of morning moved from the eastern shores to the western edges of Europe—over the Caucasus, across the snow-laden Alps, and into the shadowed isles of the Atlantic—the earth below lay much as it does today. The same mountains cast their long shadows over the valleys; the same olive groves climbed the hillsides; the pine forests stood dark and dense; and the broad plains shimmered with young wheat or grass refreshed by rain. Cities rose beside rivers, or nestled along coastlines where masts jutted like reeds from the water’s edge—just as they do now.

The first light of day crept into human homes, touching the warm cheeks of sleeping children, the weary eyes of those awakened by pain or sorrow, the swift rising of laborers whose hands were hardened by work, and the late slumber of scholars who had spent the night questioning stars, sages, or their own souls in search of truths that might lift the veil of mortality. They sought knowledge not just to understand the world, but to glimpse beyond it—to see the hidden path of life not as an end, but as part of a vast, radiant circle.

Great rivers still shaped the lives of men. And within hearts, the currents of desire, fear, love, and longing still flowed in familiar rhythms. As we follow the slow advance of dawn, we feel the enduring pattern of human existence: hunger and toil, planting and harvest, love and death—unchanged through centuries.

Even if we pause at one place in history, waiting for full daylight, we may find a city renowned throughout the world—one that has barely altered since the time of Columbus. It stands like a steadfast monument amid the shifting tides of time, reminding us that we are more alike our ancestors than different. The principles of architecture, the weight of stone and purpose, echo across ages. If the spirit of a Florentine man—whose last breath came while Columbus still pleaded for ships at Palos—were to return and stand once more on the hill of San Miniato, he would recognize the view.

He wears the clothes of his time: a black silk robe, called a *lucco*, falling straight from neck to ankle; a plain cap with a long strip of cloth, the *becchetto*, hanging down like a scarf. His face is sharp, perhaps not beautiful, but strong—his mouth firm, his chin clean-shaven. It bears the marks of a life lived deeply, among the banks of the Arno, beneath the sunlit towers of Florence.

As he looks out, the sense of familiarity overwhelms him. He sees the mountains, the river winding westward, the dark slopes of Mount Morello opposite, the valley of the Arno stretching toward the marble ridges of Carrara. He sees Fiesole crowned with monasteries and cypresses, and the green hills dotted with villas he once knew by name.

But it is not only the landscape that calls to him. Closer still, he sees the city itself. Though the seventy towers that once circled Florence like a crown are gone, his gaze skips past that absence. Instead, it draws to the single tall tower rising from the Old Palace—a slender column of stone, standing proud and unbroken after four centuries. Then there is the dome, larger than any other in the world, built from the vision of a small, keen-eyed architect—Brunelleschi—who once dreamed it into existence. It still rises above the hills, bold and unshaken.

And the bell towers—Giotto’s, with its distant glow of color; the graceful spire of the Badia—these he remembers from childhood, seen from his nurse’s shoulder. “Florence still knows how to ring her bells,” he thinks, “with that solemn sound that once stirred the hearts of her people.” And near by, Santa Croce stands in its long, dark form—where the city’s great dead were laid to rest, honored with laurels and praise. But then, there was no spire. “Why did the Franciscans build this?” he wonders. “If I had lived to see it, Brunelleschi or Michelozzo would have made something nobler—something worthy of Arnolfo’s church.”

He sighs and turns his eyes to the walls. Five of the eleven gates are sealed shut. The towers, once symbols of strength and pride, have been torn down. “Is peace so complete now?” he asks. “Do Florentines live without fear of exile, conspiracy, or armed return?” These questions linger uneasily. It is easier to know what remains than to understand what has changed.

Below, the Arno flows as ever, crossed by bridges—especially the Ponte Vecchio, unlike any other bridge in the world, lined with shops where he once paused, perhaps to watch the progress of Messer Luca Pitti’s grand palace, built from stones quarried from the Hill of Boboli. Or maybe he stopped to trade with the cloth-dressers in Oltrarno. The roofline of the Pitti is hidden from this height, but his heart does not yearn to see it. He wants to walk again among the narrow streets, the busy squares, to breathe the air of the city he loved.

Are the citizens still voting with black and white beans? Who holds power now in the Palazzo Vecchio? Do the Priori still dine with tripe and boiled partridges, laughing at the unfortunate target of their jests? Are the banners still hung from windows, displayed every two months beneath Orcagna’s Loggia?

Life once meant everything to him—not just wealth and trade, which reached from Syria to Britain, but also politics, fierce and intimate. He had known the thrill of public office: he had served on the Ten, managing war; on the Eight, overseeing internal affairs; among the Priori, leading the government. He had even risen to the highest rank—Gonfaloniere—and traveled as ambassador to the Pope and Venice. He had directed armies, though their battles were bloodless, their victories hollow—men fell not from courage, but from misstep or trampling.

Through all this, he had learned to judge men without hatred, seeing life as a game of skill, yet never forgetting honor. He believed in tradition, in duty, in the dignity of action—even when it was coarse or cruel. He loved chess under the loggia, and sharp wit, and jokes that mocked the powerful. He took pride in his learning, collecting rare manuscripts, ancient vases, and busts—some missing noses, but authentic nonetheless. In old age, he hurried to read the first pages of Homer printed in Florence, a marvel of the new press.

Yet he was not blind to faith. He kept a waxen image of himself beneath the Madonna Annunziata. He gave generously to shrines, even though he had joked about the monks. For the unseen powers were real. Who could say what names hid behind them? Was there not force in gems, even if they only touched the skin? Were not all things charged with hidden meaning?

Lucretius spoke wisely—he was ancient, a great poet. Luigi Pulci, too, seemed right when wine flowed and laughter rang, though he doubted all things above the roof. Some scholars claimed Aristotle was irreligious—yet a true thinker must consider all ideas. Still, doubt often felt false when time passed and the world turned with heavier faces.

Had not Christianity come to rule? Had he not been baptized in San Giovanni, where the dome is filled with images of judgment, and the altar holds a crucified Christ who unsettles complacency?

This spirit was not a pagan philosopher, nor a poet lost in thought. He was a man of the fifteenth century—caught between belief and disbelief, between Epicurean ease and superstitious dread, between rote ethics and raw passion, between a longing for pagan freedom and a deep, restless conscience.

He may have smiled, shaken his head, when common folk spoke of a Pope Angelico who would come to cleanse the Church of corruption and renew the clergy’s virtue. Such hopes seemed too far removed from the reality of Innocent VIII’s reign—a world too corrupt for a shrewd merchant to take seriously.

Yet he felt the sickness of his time. He cared for the city. Public spirit, though it could be flawed, was never wholly immoral—for it was rooted in care for the common good.

In that Lent of 1492, he heard a Dominican friar named Girolamo Savonarola preach in San Lorenzo. The man spoke boldly against the worldliness of the clergy, demanding that Christians stop living for comfort when injustice ruled. He urged them not to spend their wealth on lavish churches when their neighbors suffered from hunger and disease.

The preacher went too far, perhaps, for older ears. But it was remarkable to see women remove their jewelry and give it up to help the poor.

“That Prior of San Marco,” the spirit reflects, “was a man of fire—perhaps too harsh, too quick to speak of divine vengeance. Ah, *Iddio non paga il Sabatol*—God does not pay on Saturday. The sins of men often go unpunished for long. I saw evil thrive for generations. But a preacher trying to move a city—how could he be gentle? Still, he might have spoken less sharply to Lorenzo de’ Medici, whose family built San Marco. Was their quarrel ever settled? And Lorenzo himself—so wise, so subtle—did he recover from his illness at Careggi? He left the world with a face heavy with sorrow. And his son… many feared he would be another Rehoboam.

How has it all ended? Which party is exiled now? Whose houses lie empty and broken? Is there a new Lorenzo—someone whom the great Turk honors with gifts of exotic animals, relics, manuscripts, or fugitives? Someone both learned and devout, yet capable of wrath?

And who writes the Republic’s Latin letters? Who lectures on Dante in the Duomo, then returns home to write scathing attacks on critics who dared question his spelling? Are the wise leaning toward the Pope and Naples—or toward the voices of France and Milan?

There is wisdom to be found in the streets below, on the marble steps before the churches, beneath the sheltering loggias—where surely Florentines still talk, joke, argue, as they always have. The buildings are still there. The changes have not been so great.

“I will go down,” the spirit decides. “I will walk the familiar stones. I will hear the speech of my people once more.”

Go not down, good spirit. For the changes are deeper than you imagine. The words of Florentines would sound like riddles to your ears. Do not mingle with politicians on the marble. Ask no questions about trade in Calimara. Avoid inquiries into scholarship, whether in cloister or court.

Only look. Look at the sunlight and shadows playing on the great walls—walls built with strength and purpose, enduring through time. Look at the children, their laughter creating a new kind of light in the midst of age. Look into the churches, where the chants remain unchanged, where the images still speak of suffering for a greater end, of love that lifts upward, of glory that ascends.

See the faces turned upward, the lips moving in prayers for help—prayers that have not changed.

These things endure. The sun and shadow still bring beauty at dawn, noon, and dusk. The children still represent the eternal union of love and duty. Men still long for peace and justice. They still believe that the highest life is one of conscious sacrifice.

For the Pope Angelico has not yet come.

The Loggia de’ Cerchi stood at the heart of old Florence, tucked away in a maze of narrow lanes behind the Badia—streets now seldom trod by visitors unless they were hunting for a certain plain doorway bearing this inscription:

To Dante’s ears, these same streets had once echoed with the clash of swords and shouts of rival families locked in deadly feud. But in the fifteenth century, their noise was less about blood and more about woolcarders from San Martino and Garbo, trading insults as freely as they traded cloth.

On the morning of April 9th, 1492, two men stood facing each other beneath the loggia: one bent slightly forward, peering down with sharp curiosity; the other sprawled on the pavement, blinking up like a sleeper startled from dreams.

The standing man spoke first. He was broad-shouldered and gray-haired, shaped by labor and hardened by time—a man who, in Tuscan terms, had been "molded with the fist and polished with the pickaxe." Yet his face bore lines of self-importance, as though he meant to compensate for any roughness in his appearance. At his feet lay a large leather sack, bulging with goods, and beside it hung a peddler’s basket filled with small wares—thread, pins, shards of glass likely exchanged for such things.

“Young man,” he said, pointing to a ring on the reclining figure’s finger, “when your chin has grown a stiffer beard, you’ll know better than to sleep in street corners with a jewel like that on your hand. By the holy gospels! If it had been anyone but me watching over you just now—but Bratti Ferravecchi is no thief. A cat won’t eat her mouse unless she catches it alive, and Bratti won’t taste gain unless it comes with a fair bargain. Three years ago, on Saint John’s Day, the saint sent me a dead body—a blind beggar, his cap lined with coins. But I turned my stomach against the money, until I remembered that Saint John owed me those pieces for my yearly offering at the festival. Then I buried the man and paid for a mass. That made it a fair trade. But what brings a young fellow like you, with the face of Messer San Michele, sleeping on stone with only the wind for a curtain?”

The man’s voice was deep and guttural, hard for the newly awakened listener to follow. But he understood the gesture toward the ring. He glanced down, then without thought pulled it off and slipped it into his doublet, rising as he did so and stretching his arms.

“Your tunic and hose don’t match that jewel,” Bratti observed slowly. “Anyone might say the saints have sent *you* a dead body. But if you took the jewels, I hope you buried him—and you can afford a few masses in his name.”

A sudden tension passed through the younger man. His careless stretch froze. For a moment, he frowned sharply at Bratti. Then, as quickly, he regained composure. He removed the red Levantine cap hanging low over his left ear, pushed back his long dark curls, and looked down at his clothes, smiling.

“You speak truth, friend,” he said. “My garments are stained like an old sail—not because they’re ancient, but because they’ve seen both sea and rain. The fact is, I’m a stranger in Florence. Last night, footsore and weary, I chose this porch over searching for lodgings that might be worse than a den of wolves.”

“A stranger, indeed,” Bratti replied. “Your words flow sweetly, so smooth that even a Florentine Christian can’t tell a hook from a hanger. Not from Genoa? More likely Venice, by the cut of your clothes.”

“At this moment,” the stranger answered, still smiling, “it matters less where I came from than where I can go for breakfast. This city gives me a cold look. Can you show me a livelier quarter where I might find food and shelter?”

“That I can,” Bratti said. “And it’s your luck that I happened to walk in from Rovezzano this morning, stopping at Mercato Vecchio to say an Ave at the Badia. That’s your good fortune. But let’s see what profit I make from it. Nothing for nothing, young man. If I show you the way to Mercato Vecchio, you’ll swear by your patron saint to let me have the first bid on your worn suit when you buy something better—as you surely will.”

“Agreed, by San Niccolò,” the stranger laughed. “But let us go now. I feel the need of something warmer inside this doublet you seem so eager to claim.”

“Claim? Not at all,” Bratti said, slinging his bag over his shoulder and setting off. But he broke off mid-sentence, letting out a loud, grating cry like the squeal of a cartwheel:

(Who wants to exchange rags, broken glass, or old iron?)

“It’s not much,” he added, returning to normal tone. “Your clothes are worth little. Still, if you want a lute worth more than any new one, or a sword once carried by a Ridolfi, or a paternoster of the finest sort, I could give you a great deal—if you let me take your clothes as part payment. I may stand here simply, but I run the best shop in Ferravecchi, right near the Mercato. Praise be to the Virgin—I carry no pumpkin on my back. And I don’t sit cooped up all day. I’ve a wife and a raven to watch the stock. _Abbaratta, baratta, chi abbaratta?_”

“And now,” he continued, “where do you come from, and what brings you to Florence?”

“I thought you liked nothing unless it came with a price,” the stranger said. “You’ve offered me nothing yet in return for that information.”

“Well,” Bratti replied, “a Florentine knows how to pay fair value for news—even if it doesn’t fill his pockets. If I take you to the prettiest girl in the Mercato for a cup of milk—that’ll be a fair trade.”

“No,” the stranger said. “I can find her myself, if she truly is there. Pretty faces often peek from doors and windows. Besides, a sharp trader like you should know that someone bidding for nuts and news might find them hollow.”

“Ah!” Bratti said, glancing sideways with a hint of admiration. “You weren’t born on a Sunday—the salt-shops were open when you came into the world. You’re not a Hebrew, then? From Spain or Naples? Let me tell you—those Frati Minori are trying to heat Florence as hot as Spain for those dogs of hell who want to hoard all the profits of usury for themselves, leaving none for Christians. And if you walk Calimara with a yellow cloth in your cap, it will ruin your beauty more than a sword-cut across that smooth olive cheek of yours. _Abbaratta, baratta, chi abbaratta?_ I tell you, grey cloth fights yellow cloth. There’s enough grey cloth in Florence to make a gown and cowl for the Duomo. But not enough yellow to make hose for Saint Christopher—blessed be his name, and send me a sight of him today! _Abbaratta, baratta, b’ratta?_”

“All very amusing,” the stranger said, somewhat scornfully. “But it concerns me not. I am no Hebrew.”

“See?” Bratti said triumphantly. “I’ve made a bargain with mere words. I’ve made you tell me something, though you’re as slippery as a lamprey. San Giovanni be praised! A blind Florentine beats two one-eyed men. But here we are—at the Mercato.”

They stepped from the narrow alleys into the wide piazza known to older writers as Mercato Vecchio, the Old Market. Though it had served as a marketplace since ancient times—perhaps even the spot where the ancestors of the Florentines descended from their high hills to trade with valley farmers—it had never been shunned by wealth. In the early decades of the fifteenth century, the Medici and other powerful *popolani grassi*, the commercial nobility, had homes here. They didn’t mind the roar of dialects or the butcher stalls, which the poet Antonio Pucci once called a chief glory—*dignita*—of a market that, in his view, surpassed all others. But now, Lent was not yet over. The proud guild of butchers was quiet. Instead, the season belonged to market gardeners, cheesemongers, sellers of macaroni, corn, eggs, milk, and dried fruits. Women’s voices dominated the chatter. Yet every season brought its own sounds: pots clanging, money changers clicking coins, vendors shouting bargains, dicers challenging passersby, linen and wool being vaunted, kettles and pans displayed. Mules and carts choked the narrow entries, and insults flew—much like those used by modern women under similar provocation.

Ladies and gentlemen visiting the market witnessed more amateur fighting than they’d see today, and far more ragged poverty and disorder than modern households could imagine. As the day advanced, the gaming houses revealed their grim drama: trembling hopes, despairing groans, curses, blows.

Yet there were brighter sights too: brood rabbits, innocent as ever; doves and singing birds for children’s gifts; kittens for sale, even handsome *gattuccios* famed for catching mice. And above all, fresh young faces—soft cheeks and bright eyes, glowing from the early morning journey from distant castelli—not to mention older ones, warm with honest kindness, always present in scenes of human toil.

At the center of the square, atop a venerable column taken from San Giovanni’s church, stood Donatello’s statue of Plenty. Nearby, a fountain bubbled where, according to Pucci, good wives washed their utensils—and their throats—not from lack of wine, but to save money for their husbands.

But on this morning, something had changed. The stalls were set up, buyers gathered, ready for the best vegetables and butter. Yet something else held everyone’s attention. Traders turned from their wares, joining clusters of talkers scattered across the square. A secondhand cloth seller, dangling hose, accidentally looped them around his neck in haste to join a group. A cheesemonger, knife in one hand, cheese in the other, scribbled notes on his prize piece of *marzolino*. Elderly women, baskets askew, wailed in unison.

The Florentine boys, ever present and especially mischievous—like sour crabs—saw their chance. Some darted for nuts and figs; others for cooked treats. Dogs, trained to enjoy Lenten fare, sniffed and vanished beneath stalls. Mules, tangled in baskets, stretched their muzzles toward aromatic greens.

“Diavolo!” Bratti exclaimed as they entered unnoticed. “The Mercato has gone mad—as if the Holy Father had excommunicated us again. I must know what’s happening. But don’t worry: it seems a thousand years before you see Tessa and get your milk. But hold fast to me—I’ll keep my end of the bargain. Remember: I get the first bid on your clothes, especially the hose. Stained as they are, they’re the finest *panno di garbo*—ruined, yes, but still good.”

“Ola, Monna Trecca!” Bratti shouted, turning to an old woman near the nearest group. “Your mules are trampling your parsley—has the world ended?”

“Monna Trecca” (Dame Greengrocer) spun around, half-furious, half-dazed—first at Bratti, then at her ruined goods, then back at him.

“May you have a bad Easter and a bad year—and die by the sword!” she shrieked, lunging for her stall. But Bratti ignored the curse, slipping past her to hear the story gripping her. He signaled the stranger to stay close.

“I saw it myself,” said a fat man clutching leeks. “I was at Santa Maria Novella. I saw it. The woman jumped up, threw her arms out, screamed—she saw a great bull with fiery horns charging the church to crush it. I saw it!”

“Saw what, Goro?” asked a slim man with a razor at his belt, wearing a jerkin and skullcap tilted carelessly. “The bull—or just the woman?”

“The woman, of course,” Goro replied, annoyed. “But it’s all the same—*mi pare*. Doesn’t change the meaning—*va!*”

“Meaning?” several voices chimed in. Then chaos erupted: “Lights flashing over San Lorenzo for three nights!” “Thunder in clear starlight!” “Duomo’s lantern struck by Saint Michael’s sword!” “Medici *palle*—all smashed!” “Lions tearing each other apart!” “Ah! They might well!” “*Boto* fell in Santissima Nunziata!” “Died like a true Christian!” “God will forgive him!”

Only Bratti, the barber, and a thin-lipped man with spectacles and a pen-case remained silent—Bratti piecing together the fragments, the barber listening, the scholar waiting.

“*Ebbene*, Nello,” Bratti said, sidling near the barber. “So the Magnifico is dead—may his soul rest. And the price of wax will rise?”

“Exactly,” Nello replied, then added gravely, swiftly: “And his waxen image in the Nunziata fell at the same moment—though perhaps later, whenever the Frati Serviti choose. Several cows and women have borne stillborn calves this Lent. And since Carnival, no one’s counted the rotten eggs. Ah! A great man—a great politician—a greater poet than Dante. Yet the dome didn’t fall—only the lantern. *Che miracolo!*”

A sharp, prolonged “Pst!” sliced through the din. It came from the pale man in spectacles. Instantly, silence fell. All eyes turned to him.

“You Florentines are blind,” he began, voice sharp and clear. “It seems you need only hay to become cattle. Do you think Lorenzo’s death is God’s scourge for Florence? Go! You’re sparrows praising a dead hawk. What! A man who tried to slip a noose around every neck in the Republic, tightening it at his pleasure? You love that—you like magistrates chosen in secret, citizens denied rights unless they’re Medicean. That’s what *netto di specchio* means now—not paying your dues, but turning a blind eye to robbery—of public funds, of daughters’ dowries. Playing chamberlain one day, philosopher the next—listening to bawdy songs at Carnival and crying ‘Bellissimi!’—then sacred hymns and crying ‘Bellissimi!’ again. That’s what you love. You rage over your *quattrini bianchi*, but ignore the hole in the treasury draining gold into Lorenzo’s pockets. You pay for footmen to march before a citizen, so he may seem noble and kind. ‘Look,’ you say, ‘what a tall Pisan we keep, flashing his sword before him!—and yet Lorenzo smiles. How generous!’ And you think his death is God’s warning—portents everywhere. But I tell you: there’s another scourge in the air.”

“Nay, Ser Cioni,” Nello said. “Stay politics, not prophecy. Politics is the better horse.”

“But if you speak of portents,” another man said, “what greater sign than a pious notary? Balaam’s ass was nothing.”

“A notary without work, inkwell dry,” a third added. “Better wear a cowl, Ser Cioni. Everyone will believe your fasting.”

The notary turned and left with a look of contempt, revealing a short, sallow man behind him—bent shoulders suggesting a life of deskwork.

“By San Giovanni,” said Goro, suddenly uncertain, “perhaps Ser Cioni speaks truth. I’ve reason to complain about those *quattrini bianchi* myself. Grumble? Suffocation! We do grumble. And if anyone says the word, I’ll join the crowd—sooner than let our money be tampered with as if magistrates were necromancers. And Lorenzo could have stopped such things—if he’d wanted. As for the bull with flaming horns—yes, Ser Cioni may be right: it could mean many things. Perhaps more than taxes. When God sends a sign, it’s not one meaning. It’s many.”

“Spoken like an oracle, Goro!” Nello said. “When we mortals pack two meanings into one sentence, it’s blasphemous not to believe the bull means everything anyone in Florence wants it to.”

“You scoff, Nello,” said the sallow man, now visible. “But it’s true: every revelation—vision, dream, portent, scripture—has many meanings. Only the enlightened can unfold them.”

“Assuredly,” Nello replied. “I’ve heard the Frate in San Lorenzo. But I’ve also heard Fra Menico in the Duomo. According to him, your Fra Girolamo runs after the wind of Mongibello, and those who follow him are like swine rushing into the sea—or worse. With San Domenico roaring *è vero* in one ear, and San Francisco screaming *è falso* in the other—what is a poor barber to do? Unless he’s enlightened? But clearly, Goro is beginning to see—he already thinks the bull means first himself, then all aggrieved taxpayers, determined to gore the magistracy at the first chance.”

“Goro is a fool!” a deep bass voice thundered, dropping like a bell amid the chime. “Let him take his leeks home and shake his flanks over his wool-beating. He’ll mend matters more that way than by parading his barrel-shaped body in the square, as if everyone could measure his grievances by the size of his belly. The burdens that harm him most are his heavy flesh and his idleness.”

After Bratti had joined the cluster of talkers, the young stranger, weary of waiting and indifferent to the cause of the city’s stir—matters that seemed to concern only native Florentines—decided to wander through the piazza. He sought not news, but food: a simple vendor who might sell without prying into politics or gossip. Yet as he walked, his hand dipped into a worn leather pouch at his waist, rummaging with growing frustration. “Not an obolus, by Jupiter!” he muttered in a tongue foreign to Tuscany, perhaps even to Italy itself. “I thought I had one last coin left. Then I must buy my breakfast with kindness.”

He did not go far before spotting a quiet corner where such a bargain might be struck. There, apart from the throng, stood two mules adorned with red tassels and collars. One bore wooden milk jugs; the other carried panniers full of fresh herbs and greens. Leaning against the milk-laden mule was a girl no older than sixteen, her face framed by a red hood that hid her hair entirely. Her features were soft and childlike, almost delicate in their innocence. She appeared to have fallen asleep mid-task, her body half-slumped, half-supported by the beast.

But the stranger saw no reason to hesitate. With gentle intent, he touched her lightly—so softly it seemed to her, still dreaming, like a sprig of thyme brushing her lips as she bent to gather herbs. The spell broke. She blinked open blue eyes wide with surprise, startled to find a tall, dark-haired youth standing before her.

He spoke to her in a voice low and strange, yet sweet—a tone so unfamiliar that even if she’d been alert, she would have taken it for nonsense. Instinctively, she turned her head slightly and lifted a corner of her green serge mantle as a shield.

He repeated himself, this time choosing words she knew well: “Forgive me, pretty one, for waking you. I’m dying with hunger, and the scent of milk makes breakfast seem more tempting than ever.”

The phrase *muoio di fame* stirred recognition. The mantle dropped. Moments later, a large cup of fragrant milk was offered to him.

He said no more, lifting the cup to drink. As he sipped, the girl dared to look up. His clothes were torn and travel-worn, yet there was something noble in his bearing—unlike any beggar she had known. And though his voice had first sounded eerie, almost unnatural, now it softened, becoming strangely clear.

While he drank, her hand slipped into a bag beside the mule. When he set down the cup, she held out a thick piece of bread, her eyes meeting his—blue, shy, yet encouraging.

“But perhaps that is your own meal,” he said gently. “No, I’ve eaten enough already. A thousand thanks, my gentle one.”

She did not answer. But the bread moved closer, as if urging him to take it. He looked into her face, then smiled.

“Ah, then, if I must take the bread,” he said, laying his hand upon it, “I shall grow bolder still—and ask for another kiss to make it sweeter.”

His words grew clearer despite the odd cadence of his speech. The strange tone, once alarming, now seemed playful. She blushed deeply, raising her mantle again to cover her mouth.

Just as he leaned forward, fingers brushing the arm that held the veil, a harsh voice cut through the air.

“Who are you? With a murrain on you! No honest buyer, I’ll wager—you’re some follower of dice-men, or worse. Go on, dance off, or I’ll give you a tune faster than you’ll care for!”

The stranger stepped back, unafraid, his gaze steady. A smile flickered across his lips—bright, teasing—as he took in the speaker.

It was a stout woman, dressed in a man’s jerkin over a rough green gown, her face wrapped in a faded hood. Though coarse and weathered, her features bore a startling resemblance to the girl’s—same shape of brow, same curve of lip—a maternal likeness both tender and grim, like a shadow foretelling the future.

Her voice cracked with anger, but the stranger’s smile remained. It was oddly soothing, yet Monna Ghita showed no mercy.

“Yes, yes,” she snapped, “you can grin like any monkey in cap and jerkin. You’re a minstrel, or a mountebank—I know it. You look as foolish as a tumbler just turned upside down. And what tricks have you been up to, Tessa?” she demanded, turning on her daughter, whose frightened face made her rage sharper. “Giving away milk and bread? Ay, ay, you’d carry water in your ears for any idle vagabond who didn’t want to stoop! Turn your back and lift the herbs—unless you want to say ten Aves without counting!”

“Nay, Madonna,” the stranger replied, his voice softening with a pleading smile, “do not punish your lovely Tessa for showing pity to a hungry traveler who found himself without a quattrino. Your face looks so fine when it frowns—but I long to see it lit by a smile.”

“_Va via_!” she spat. “I know your kind. You may tickle me with that straw until doomsday, but I won’t laugh. Get gone, with a bad Easter! Or I’ll leave a few beauty spots on that face that’ll ruin your kissing until Advent.”

As she raised her hands, sharp and fierce, Bratti arrived—having followed the stranger earlier. “What think you of this parrot, Nello?” he asked his companion. “Doesn’t his tongue smell of Venice?”

“Nay, Bratti,” the barber whispered, “your wisdom has much of the ass in it—especially about ears. This man is Greek, else I’m not the barber who shaved Demetrio and pulled teeth from his learned jaw. This youth could pass for a godling straight from Olympus—if he’d had a touch of my razor.”

“_Orsù_, Monna Ghita!” Nello chimed in, amused. “What storm has come upon you? Has this young man misbehaved?”

“By San Giovanni!” Bratti interjected, still wary of the stranger’s shabby clothes and hidden wealth. “He did right to run from _me_, if he meant trouble. I found him under the Loggia de’ Cerchi, wearing a ring like Bernardo Rucellai’s own. Not a penny’s worth more do I know about him.”

“The truth is,” Nello said, grinning, “this _bello giovane_ has been too bold with your charms, Monna Ghita. He tried to kiss you while Tessa turned her back. I see she’s busy—no time to look this way. Was it not so, Messer?”

“You have guessed the offense like a prophet,” the stranger laughed. “Only I did not find Monna Ghita here at first. I begged milk from her daughter, accepted this bread as a gift, and was offering thanks when I had the honor of seeing these more mature beauties—though perhaps I was too bold in admiring them.”

“_Va, va_! All of you, off to purgatory! Stay there till I pay to bring you out!” Monna Ghita roared, shoving Nello aside and leading her mule forward, forcing the stranger to step aside. “Tessa, you fool, move your mule—here comes the cart!”

As Tessa reached for the bridle, she glanced back—just long enough to catch the stranger’s hand beckoning her farewell. He was walking away with Nello, heading toward the narrow street that led to the Piazza del Duomo.

“_Ebbene_,” Bratti called out, loud enough to carry over the cart, “I leave you with Nello, young man. I cannot carry my basket farther. I have business at home. But remember our bargain—because if you found Tessa without me, it was not my fault. Nello will show you my shop in the Ferravecchi, and I’ll not turn my back on you.”

“A thousand thanks, friend!” the stranger replied, laughing, and vanished into the alley with Nello, leaving behind the scent of milk and the echo of laughter.

CHAPTER III. The Barber’s Shop.

“To be honest,” said the young stranger to Nello, as they stepped away from the jumbled carts and mules, “I’m not sorry to leave behind that patron of mine and fall into the hands of someone with a more civil tongue and a less mysterious trade. Is it common in Florence for a wandering peddler of broken glass and rags to speak of running a shop where he sells lutes and swords?”

“Common? Not at all,” replied Nello, whose talk flowed like water from an overfilled vessel—no small measure could contain it. “Bratti is no ordinary man. He has a philosophy, and lives by it—more than I can say for any philosopher I’ve had the honour to shave. Bratti believes in squeezing every drop of pleasure out of life—that is, the pleasure of hard bargaining—and plans to conclude his earthly dealings with a final bargain: a swift passage through purgatory by handing over his winnings to the Church when his time comes. He even made his will on the cheapest terms a notary would accept. But I’ve often told him, ‘Bratti, your deal is lame, and you’re on the weak side of it. Doesn’t it sadden you to gaze upon the paintings of Paradise? You’ll never haggle there over rags or rusty nails—the saints and angels need neither pins nor tinder. And unless you strike a deal with Saint Bartholomew, who carries his skin around rather inconveniently, I see little chance of trading second-hand clothes.’ But God forgive me,” added Nello, crossing himself, “such jesting is ill-suited to a morning when Lorenzo lies dead and the Muses are tearing their hair—always a sorrowful sight for a barber. And you yourself, sir, must be under some shadow; for when a man of your bearing and speech takes shelter in such a poor lodging, it suggests misfortune has overtaken him.”

“What Lorenzo do you mean?” asked the stranger, so absorbed in the mention of death that he seemed to overlook the subtle question that followed.

“What Lorenzo? There’s only one Lorenzo whose passing could send the Mercato into chaos, make the Duomo’s lantern leap in despair, and stir the lions of the Republic to devour each other. I mean Lorenzo de’ Medici, the Pericles of our Athens—if I may make such a comparison before a Greek.”

“Why not?” laughed the other. “I doubt Athens, even in Pericles’ time, could have produced so learned a barber.”

“Yes, yes,” said Nello swiftly, “I knew I wouldn’t be mistaken—else my shaving of the venerable Demetrio Calcondila would have been in vain. But forgive me—I’m astonished: your Italian surpasses even his, though he’s lived here forty years. It’s better even than Marullo’s, who might be said to have wedded the Italic Muse in more ways than one, since he married our learned and lovely Alessandra Scala.”

“You’ll find it easier to believe if I tell you I come from a Greek lineage planted in Italian soil longer than the mulberry trees that thrive so well here. I was born in Bari, and raised by an Italian—so, in truth, I am a Greek, much as your peaches are Persian. The Greek blood was subdued in me, I suppose, until long travel and residence in the land of gods and heroes re-dyed me. And now, to confess something personal: this Greek heritage, along with a few ancient gems I carry, is all the fortune shipwreck left me. But—when towers fall, it’s bad news for those who build small nests—the death of your Pericles makes me wish I’d turned toward Rome instead. I would have, but for a misleading Minerva in the form of an Augustinian monk. ‘At Rome,’ he said, ‘you’ll vanish among hungry scholars; but in Florence, every corner is touched by Lorenzo’s patronage. Florence is the finest market in Italy for goods like yours.’”

“_Gnaffè_, and so it shall remain,” said Nello. “Lorenzo was not the only patron of learning in our city—heaven forbid! Just because he was a large melon, does that mean every Florentine is a pumpkin? We have Bernardo Rucellai, Alamanno Rinuccini, and many others. And if you seek knowledge, I, Nello, am your man. It feels like a thousand years since I last served a scholar of your calibre. First, let us attend to your hair. That beard, young man, must go—were it as dear to you as your dream nymph. In Florence, we don’t care for men whose noses hang over cascades of hair. But remember: once shaved, you’ll have crossed the Rubicon. If you regret it later and let it grow back after resisting the razor, your mouth will soon resemble not Messer Angelo’s divine lips, but a dark cavern fringed with bristling thorns.”

“A dire prophecy,” said the Greek, “especially if Florentine maidens are as fair as the little Tessa I stole a kiss from this morning.”

“Tessa? She’s a rough-handed country girl. You’ll win favour with ladies who carry no scent of mule-stables. But to do so, you must not look like a _sgherro_, a man of ill repute. You must appear a courtier, a polished scholar—like our Pietro Crinito—someone who sins among well-bred, well-fed folk, not one who swallows cheap wine in a roadside tavern.”

“With all my heart,” said the stranger. “If the Florentine Graces demand it, I’ll sacrifice my beard—but—”

“Yes, yes,” interrupted Nello. “I know what you’d say. It’s the _bella zazzera_—the hyacinthine locks you won’t part with. But there’s no need. A little trimming—ecco!—and you’ll look not unlike the illustrious Prince Pico di Mirandola in his prime. And here we are, just in time, at the Piazza San Giovanni and the door of my shop. But you hesitate—I see: naturally, you want to admire our wonder of the world, our Duomo, Santa Maria del Fiore. Very well, a quick glance—but I beg you wait till you’re shaved. My art stirs me to the very edge of the razor. Ah, then, come this way.”

The lively barber took the stranger’s arm and led him to a point on the south side of the piazza, where he could see the vast dark shell of the dome, the slender grace of Giotto’s campanile, and the quaint octagon of San Giovanni before them, its unique bronze gates still gleaming faintly with original gilding. The inlaid marbles were fresher then—pink, white, and purple—than they are now, weathered by four centuries into the rich ochre of aged meerschaum. The cathedral’s façade stood proudly, not yet shrouded in faded stucco, but adorned with the grand promise of half-finished marble inlays and sculpted niches Giotto had designed a hundred and fifty years earlier. As the campanile rose in harmonious colour and form into the clear April air, it seemed a living symbol: human life, too, must someday shape itself into harmony with such pure, soaring beauty.

But the Greek did not seem moved. His eyes lifted upward, yet there was a hint of scorn on his lip. When they fell again, they swept the scene with a cool, assessing gaze that nettled Nello’s Florentine pride.

“Well, my fine young man,” he said, impatiently, “you treat our Cathedral as if you were the Angel Gabriel fresh from Paradise. Tell me—have you seen finer work than Giotto’s tower? Or a dome that wouldn’t look like a mushroom beside Brunelleschi’s? Or marbles more beautiful or skillfully wrought than these our Signoria brought from distant quarries—at a price that could buy a dukedom? Come now—have you seen anything to match them?”

“If you asked me with a scimitar at my throat, Turkish style—or even your own razor,” replied the young Greek, smiling, and moving toward the Baptistery gates, “I might confess. But with my neck free of danger, I must say your buildings smack too much of Christian barbarism for my taste. I feel a shudder at what lies within—smoked Madonnas, fleshless saints in mosaic staring down idiotic rebuke from the apse, skeletons draped in skins, pierced with arrows, stretched on gridirons; monks and women with heads twisted in eternal lamentation. I’ve seen enough of such wry-necked favourites of heaven in Constantinople. But what is this bronze door, rough with imagery? These women seem shaped by a different spirit from those starved saints I mentioned—these high-relief faces suggest a mind within them, not just an index to perpetual spasms and colic.”

“Yes, yes,” said Nello, with quiet triumph. “We’ll show you soon enough that Florentine art is far from barbarous. These gates, my fine young man, were cast half a century ago by our Lorenzo Ghiberti, who was barely as old as you are.”

“Ah, I recall,” said the stranger, turning away as if his curiosity were satisfied. “I’ve heard your Tuscan sculptors and painters have studied the antique. But with monks for models and tales of mad hermits and martyrs for subjects, even Olympus itself would be of little help.”

“I understand,” said Nello, shrugging meaningfully as they walked. “You think as Michele Marullo does—yes, even as Angelo Poliziano, despite his canonry, when he relaxes in my shop after lectures and speaks of the gods waking from long sleep, making woods and streams alive again. But he mocks Roman scholars who wish to make us all speak Latin: ‘My ears,’ he says, ‘are already raw from the barbarisms of the learned, and if the common people begin to speak Latin, I’d rather have been in Florence the day they beat every kettle in the city because the bells weren’t enough to appease the saints.’ Ah, Messer Greco, if you wish to taste Florentine scholarship, visit my shop—it is the focus of our intellect, the navel of the earth—as my great predecessor Burchiello claimed of his shop, though on the lighter ground that his street, Calimara, was the city’s centre. And here we are at the sign of ‘Apollo and the Razor.’ Apollo, you see, bestows the razor upon Triptolemus, the first reaper of beards, the sublime _Anonimo_, whose hidden identity is marked by a shadowy hand.”

“I see you’ve already had customers, Sandro,” Nello continued, addressing a solemn, dark-eyed youth who stepped aside for them. “Now clear the way for this gentleman. Prepare the finest-scented lather—he has a learned and handsome chin.”

“You have a pleasant little adytum here,” said the stranger, glancing through a latticed screen that separated the shop from a room of equal size, which opened onto a smaller walled courtyard where a few bay trees and laurels surrounded a stone Hermes. “I suppose your circle of _eruditi_ meets there?”

“There, and no less in my shop,” said Nello, leading the way into the inner room, furnished with benches, a table holding an open manuscript and a printed book in capitals, a lute, a few oil sketches, and a model or two of hands and ancient masks. “For my shop is no less a haunt of the Muses—you’ll see when you feel the sudden clarity of thought and serene inspiration that follows a clean chin. Ah! You can play that lute, I see. I have some skill myself, though serenatas are useless when daylight reveals a face like mine—no fresher than an apple that’s endured winter. But look at this sketch—it’s Piero di Cosimo’s fancy, a strange painter who claims he saw it by staring at a mouldy wall.”

The sketch showed three masks: a drunken laughing Satyr, a sorrowing Magdalen, and between them, the rigid, cold face of a Stoic. They rested obliquely on a child’s lap, whose cherubic features looked up with a divine promise, the kind painters had begun to give to the Christ Child.

“A symbolic image,” said the young Greek, touching the lute to draw a soft murmur. “Perhaps the child is the Golden Age—needing neither worship nor philosophy. And the Golden Age returns as long as men are born infants, not arriving in cassocks or fur-lined robes. Or perhaps the child stands for Epicurus’ wise philosophy—free from the gross, the sad, and the severe.”

“Ah! Everyone interprets that picture differently,” said Nello. “Ask Piero himself, and he’ll say his works are an appendix Messer Domeneddio has appended to the universe. If anyone doubts their meaning, they should ask Holy Church. He was asked to paint a full version, but he covers his ears and shakes his head—the idea is gone. A peculiar creature, our Piero. But now—everything is ready for your initiation into the mysteries of the razor.”

“Mysteries indeed,” continued the barber, spirits rising at the prospect of a long discourse, as he wrapped the young Greek in the shroud-like shaving cloth. “Mysteries of Minerva and the Graces. I gather the finest thoughts from men, because I catch them in the moment after shaving. (Ah! You flinch slightly—yes, the lather tickles the edges of the nose.) That’s why a barber’s shop is fit for wit and learning. Consider a druggist’s shop: there’s a dull gathering at ‘The Moor,’ which pretends to rival mine. What inspiration can one draw from the smell of foul vegetable decoctions?—not to mention the doctor of physic waiting at the door like a giant spider in fur and scarlet, or even seated on a bony horse, inspecting saliva. (Raise your chin slightly—look at the angel blowing the trumpet above you. I painted it expressly to guide my clients’ chins.) Besides, your druggist, who gathers herbs and brews potions, is biased—he’s poisoned people by system and must defend it. But a barber can remain impartial. The only thing he defends is the razor—unless he’s an author. That was Burchiello’s flaw—he was a poet, and thus had a bias toward his own verses. I avoided that. I saw early that authorship narrows the mind, while the art of the razor demands affection for all chins. Ecco, Messer! Your chin and lip are as clear as a maiden’s. Now fix your mind on a knotty question—ask whether Virgil should be spelled with an _i_ or an _e_, and see if you don’t feel a new clarity. Only—keep your choice to yourself until you’re wealthy, for the _e_ has the stronger following in Florence. Ah! I see a spark of sharper wit in your eye. Niccolò Macchiavelli himself, keen enough to discern _il pelo nell’ uovo_, and fond of fine shaving—though his beard is barely two years old—says that as soon as hairs begin to grow, he feels a certain coarseness creeping over his mind.”

“Let me look at myself,” said the stranger, laughing. “This intellectual clarity may be blocked by uncertainty about my appearance.”

“Behold yourself in this mirror,” said Nello. “It’s a Venetian mirror from Murano—the true _nosce teipsum_, as I call it. Compared to steel or silver mirrors, it is pure darkness. See how careful shaving preserves the human outline of your lower face, instead of blurring it into the visage of a bearded owl or a Barbary ape. I’ve seen men whose beards invaded their cheeks so thoroughly that one might pity them as victims of a brutal punishment worthy of Dante’s Inferno—had they not strutted with strange pride in their wild hairiness.”

“It seems,” said the Greek, still gazing into the mirror, “that you’ve taken some of my capital with your razor—I mean, a year or two of age, which might have earned me greater credit for my learning. Under a patron whose eyesight has dimmed, I’ll look dangerously like an eighteen-year-old girl in boy’s clothing.”

“Not at all,” said Nello, clipping the excessive curls. “Your proportions are not those of a maiden. And your age? I remember seeing Angelo Poliziano begin his Latin lectures with a younger beard than yours. Between us, his youthful ugliness matched his precocious learning. But you—no, no, your age is not a problem. Yet, between us, let me whisper: being Greek—even Apulian Greek—is not in your favour. Some of our scholars claim Greek learning is a degenerate plant until transplanted into Italian minds. With so many superior minds now flourishing, native teachers are seen as mere propagators of decay. Ecco! Your curls are now properly proportioned to neck and shoulders. Rise, Messer, and let me free you from this cloth. _Gnaffè_! I almost advise you to keep your faded jerkin and hose a while longer—they give you the air of a fallen prince.”

“But the real question,” said the young Greek, leaning against the high back of a chair, meeting Nello’s admiring gaze with one of quiet inquiry, “is where I should carry this princely air to rise from my fallen state. If Florentine patrons share this scholarly disdain for Greeks, I see no hope for refuge here, as you suggested earlier.”

“_Pian piano_—not so fast,” said Nello, tucking his thumbs into his belt and nodding to Sandro to restore order. “I won’t hide that there’s prejudice against Greeks among us. Though, as a barber unbound by authorship, I hold no prejudices myself, I must admit Greeks aren’t always as refined as you. Their learning often appears unruly, their Italian thick with barbaric accents—making conversation no more pleasant than a German in a drunken state. And then—we Florentines value speech freely. We believe the tongue, so skilled in flattery and promise, was made partly for such purposes; truth is a riddle for eyes and wit to solve, not for the tongue to betray. Still, we draw limits—beyond which we call deceit treachery. But it’s said Greeks begin their honesty where we end ours. Since the old Furies slept, your Christian Greek has such a light conscience he’d use his father’s corpse as a stepping-stone.”

A flush crept across the stranger’s face—a natural reaction of resentment. Nello, ever good-natured, hastened to apologize.

“Do not take offence, _bel giovane_. I only repeat what I hear in my shop—as you see, my eloquence is merely the cream skimmed from my clients’ talk. Heaven forbid I should lose my impartiality by holding opinions. And regarding this scholarly hostility,” added Nello, with a mocking grimace, “the truth is, you are heretics, Messer. Jealousy has nothing to do with it. If you simply changed your view on leaven and adjusted your Doxology, our Italian scholars would wait a thousand years to yield their chairs to you. Yes, yes—it’s mostly religious scruple, and partly the authority of a great classic—Juvenal, isn’t it? He, I gather, felt as strongly about the swarm of Greeks as our Messer Angelo, who loves quoting a passage about their incorrigible impudence—_audacia perdita_.”

“Pooh!” said the Greek, recovering his composure. “That passage is a compliment.”

“‘Ingenium velox, audacia perdita, sermo Promptus, et Isaeo torrentior.’”

“A rapid mind and fluent speech may excuse a bit of boldness.”

“Indeed,” said Nello. “And since you know Latin literature as well as Greek, you won’t fall into Giovanni Argiropulo’s mistake—running headlong into Cicero and calling him nearly a pumpkin-head. Let me give you one piece of advice, young man—trust a barber who has shaved the finest chins and kept his eyes and ears open for twenty years: oil your tongue well when speaking of ancient Latin writers, and give it an extra dip for modern ones. A wise Greek may win favour here—see our excellent Demetrio, loved by many, not hated beyond reason even by the most renowned scholars.”

“I see the wisdom of your counsel clearly,” said the Greek, his bright smile lighting up his fine face, “so I’ll ask for a little more. Who, for instance, would be the most likely patron for me? Is there a son of Lorenzo who inherits his tastes? Or another wealthy Florentine especially fond of ancient gems? I possess a fine Cleopatra carved in sardonyx, and several other intaglios and cameos—curious and beautiful—worthy of a prince’s cabinet. Happily, I secured them inside my doublet before setting sail. Moreover, I’d like to raise a small sum from this ring,” he said, drawing it out and replacing it, “if you could recommend me to an honest trader.”

“Good day, Messer Domenico,” Nello greeted the first of the two men who stepped into his shop, nodding silently to the second. “You arrive at just the right moment—like cheese on macaroni. Ah! You’re in a hurry? Need a shave without delay? _Ecco_! And this is a morning when every soul carries weighty thoughts. Florence stands orphaned—the very heart of Italy torn away—Heaven itself seems uncertain what to do next. _Oimè_! Yet still the sun marches steadily toward noon, and as I said, you come like cheese already grated. For this young stranger here was seeking an honest merchant to lend him a sum against a ring of great worth, and if I had counted every goldsmith and money-lender in Florence upon my fingers, I could not have named one more reputable than Menico Cennini. Besides, he has other wares that suit your trade—Greek learning, and eyes still bright with youth—a double treasure that printers always crave.”

The solemn elder man, son of Bernardo Cennini—who twenty years earlier had cast the first types in Florence after hearing of German printing—stood motionless, lathered and passive, while Nello poured forth his words. But he turned his gaze slowly toward the stranger, studying him with quiet scrutiny.

“This young gentleman,” Nello continued, fond of elaboration, “is fluent in Greek, Latin, or Italian—whichever you desire. He is a marvel of youthful scholarship, equal to Francesco Filelfo or our own unmatched Poliziano. A second Guarino, too, though he has suffered misfortune: shipwrecked, no doubt losing precious manuscripts that might have brought even greater accuracy to your fine editions, Messer Domenico. Fortunately, he has saved a few rare gems. His name is—what did you say, sir?”

“Tito Melema,” the stranger replied, slipping off his ring and offering it to Cennini, whom Nello had now freed from the shaving cloth with swift precision.

Meanwhile, the second man who had entered—the tall figure of about fifty, with a neatly trimmed beard, an old felt hat, and a worn mantle—had fixed his eyes on the young Greek. Now he spoke abruptly:

“Young man, I am painting a scene of Sinon deceiving old Priam. I would be glad to use your face for Sinon—if you’d grant me a sitting.”

Tito Melema started, his pale face flashing with surprise, as if accused of some hidden crime. But Nello left no room for hesitation.

“Piero,” the barber declared, “you are the most astonishing mixture of whims and fancies ever packed into a human frame. What trick will you play with this scholar’s noble countenance to make it fit a traitor? Why not ask him to lift his eyes upward? Then you could paint him as Saint Sebastian—sure to draw crowds of devout women. Or, if you prefer classical themes, crown his curls with myrtle and make him a youthful Bacchus—or better yet, Phoebus Apollo, for his face glows like a summer dawn. It made me his friend in the space of a single ‘credo.’”

“Ay, Nello,” the painter replied, speaking in short, deliberate bursts, “and if your tongue could ever cease its endless chattering long enough for your mind to reflect, you’d see that you’ve just given me the reason why this young man’s face suits my traitor perfectly. A true traitor must wear a face untouched by vice—lips that can lie with a smile, eyes so clear and deep as agate that no shame can dim them, cheeks that rise fresh from murder without looking haggard. I do not say this young man is a traitor—I only mean he has a face that could become the perfect mask for one, should he possess the heart. Which is to say nothing more than that he has a beautiful face, alive with warm youth, nourished by good blood and food, able to keep its color without much help from virtue. He may have the courage of a hero along with it—I claim no such thing. Ask Domenico there: can lapidaries always judge a gem by sight alone? Now I’m going to plug my ears with tow—for your talk and the bells together are more than I can bear. Say no more. Just trim my beard.”

With these words, Piero—known as “di Cosimo” after his master, Cosimo Rosselli—pulled out two bits of tow, stuffed them into his ears, and settled into Nello’s chair. The barber shrugged, then gave the Greek a sly, knowing glance, as if to say, *See? A peculiar fellow. Everyone treats his words as jests.*

Tito, who had stood frozen, his dark eyes locked on the painter, seemed to regain himself as Piero shifted position. Satisfied with the explanation, he returned his attention to Cennini, who now said:

“This is a curious and valuable ring, young man. The intaglio—a fish beneath a crested serpent—set in the black layer of onyx, or rather nicolo, is beautifully framed by the blue upper stratum. The ring must have a story?”

“Yes, indeed,” Tito answered, meeting the man’s gaze frankly. “It was found in Sicily. Those who study gems and sigils tell me both stone and carving carry power: to bring fortune, especially at sea, and to restore lost things to their owner. But,” he added, smiling, “though I’ve worn it constantly since leaving Greece, it hasn’t made me entirely fortunate at sea—unless escaping drowning counts as proof of its virtue. Whether my lost chests resurface remains to be seen. But to leave no chance untried, Messer, I beg you only to hold the ring as pledge for a small sum—far less than its worth—and I shall redeem it as soon as I can sell certain other gems I carry within my doublet, or earn money through scholarly work, if fortune grants me such an opportunity.”

“That may be arranged,” Cennini said. “Come with me. My brother Pietro, who judges scholarship better than I, may offer you a task to test your abilities. In the meantime, take back your ring until I can give you the florins. If you wish, come along with me.”

“Yes, yes,” Nello chimed in. “Go with Messer Domenico—you could not choose better company. He was born under the constellation that grants skill, wealth, and honesty—whatever that sign may be, which matters little, since babies cannot choose their horoscopes. Indeed, if they could, we’d have chaos at certain times of year. Besides, our Phoenix, the incomparable Pico, has shown that all astrology is mere nonsense—which is a relief. _Addio_, fine young man! Don’t forget to return to me.”

“No fear of that,” Tito replied, waving farewell as he turned his bright face at the door. “You’re doing me a great service—that’s the surest guarantee I’ll come back.”

“Say what you will, Piero,” Nello called after the departing figure, “but I’ll never look at a face like that without sensing a kind heart. Soon you’ll tell me Leonardo, whom you praise endlessly, should have painted Judas as beautiful as Saint John! But you’re as deaf as the peak of Mount Morello with that cursed tow in your ears. Well, well—I’ll learn more of this young man’s tale before I send him to Bardo Bardi.”

The Via de’ Bardi, a street etched into the memory of Florence, lies in Oltrarno—the southern bank of the Arno River. It stretches from the Ponte Vecchio to the Piazza de’ Mozzi near the Ponte alle Grazie. On its right, it is backed by the steep slope once known as the hill of Bogoli, famed for its stone quarries that supplied much of the city’s pavement—though unstable when rain soaked through. To the left, the buildings line the riverbank, their irregular façades forming a picturesque, uneven front mirrored softly in the water as the sun begins its descent toward the western hills. Though these structures are charming, some seem too modern to the historian’s eye—replacements for the grand homes of the Bardi family, razed by popular fury in the mid-fourteenth century.

The Bardi were proud, fierce, and driven. They stood at the forefront of Florence’s earliest civil conflicts, when noble towers loomed over narrow streets and blood stained the gutters. Mars, the old god of war, might have smiled, seeing how little reverence Florentines truly held for him, given their endless devotion to the Baptist. Yet the Bardi were not merely warriors; they had hands that grasped swords with strength, but also fingers that loved the feel of minted coin. Their eyes, sharp and true, saw power not only in conquest, but in commerce and influence. By the mid-1300s, they had risen from humble *popolani* to landowners and feudal Counts of Vernio—a rank that stirred envy among their republican neighbors.

Their wealth came from trade, and soon they stood at the peak of European finance—much like the Rothschilds of later centuries. They funded Edward III’s wars, receiving revenues in kind, especially wool, the most valuable cargo for Florentine galleys. But when the English king defaulted and Sicilian creditors demanded repayment, their credit collapsed. The shock rippled across the Mediterranean coast, devastating their business empire. Yet unlike many ruined men, the Bardi did not crumble. Instead, they stood taller, more defiant than ever—so arrogant that in 1343, during a time of civic unrest chronicled by Giovanni Villani, they provoked the wrath of the people. Fortifying themselves between the two bridges, they resisted the advancing banners of the mob until attacked from behind on the hill. Then, their twenty-two riverfront houses—*palagi e case grandi*—were sacked and burned. Many Bardi fled the city. But roots ran deep in Florence. Over time, the name reappeared in public life, sometimes in dignity, sometimes in disgrace—proof of a lineage marked by cycles of fortune and fall.

By 1492, the Bardi no longer owned the old street along the river. It had passed to other families, notably the Neri, who held a long stretch of homes on the hillside.

Yet within one of those Neri houses lived a descendant of the Bardi—of the very branch that had become Counts of Vernio. He carried the family pride, the hunger for distinction, the desire to leave an indelible mark upon the world. But his world was different. He was not a warrior, nor a lord of castles, nor a merchant bold enough to risk empires on trade routes. He was a scholar—blind, poor, and alone. His hands, once strong and sure, were now cramped from years of copying manuscripts. He ate simple meals, wore worn clothes—first by choice, then by necessity. He sat surrounded by books and ancient fragments, living in the light of memories from youth, which still burned brightly in his mind. This was Bardo de’ Bardi—the man whom Nello, the barber, had promised to introduce to the young Greek scholar, Tito Melema.

Bardo’s house stood on the hillside side of the street, one of those massive, somber stone buildings with small windows and a roofed terrace or loggia, common in old Florence. A heavy door with iron hinges opened onto a vaulted courtyard, bare except for a large iron lamp hanging from the ceiling. A smaller door led to the stone staircase and ground-floor rooms, used as warehouses. Both floors stored goods destined for distant shores—the Scheldt, Africa, the Aegean, the Black Sea. Maso, the elderly servant, returned from the market with vegetables and climbed slowly to the second floor, where he entered the master’s chamber. We follow him through the antechamber to the left-hand door, which he opens.

He glances inside and nods. From the far end of the room comes a clear, young voice: “Ah, you’re back, Maso. Good. We’ve needed nothing.”

The speaker stands before a carved reading-desk—such as one finds in Italian church choirs—her long hair catching the dim light. She is seventeen or eighteen, tall and graceful, her golden-red hair, wavy and unbroken like autumn clouds at sunset, bound loosely by a black ribbon above her ears. It falls forward, framing her neck beneath a square-cut black gown of coarse serge. Her eyes are fixed on a large book open before her. One hand rests lightly on the desk; the other grips the back of her father’s chair.

Her father sits with head raised, turned slightly toward her—as if he sees her. His face, pale and lined, is crowned with white hair and a black velvet cap. The resemblance between them is striking: both share a refined brow, delicate nostrils, and a firm mouth beneath a powerful chin—an expression of quiet strength and stubborn resolve. The girl mirrors this in the set of her head, the curve of her neck and shoulders. Her face holds a tension—between nobility and fire—that makes one hesitate to say whether she inspires love or awe tinged with fear. Only the eyes can reveal the truth—and hers are bent on the Latin pages of Politian’s *Miscellanea*, reading aloud at Chapter Eighty:

“There was a nymph of Thebes named Chariclo, beloved by Pallas. She was the mother of Teiresias. Once, in summer heat, Pallas, bathing with Chariclo in the Heliconian Hippocrene, was seen unveiled by Teiresias, who came to drink. The sight caused him to lose his sight, for the Saturnian laws decree that anyone who beholds the gods without their consent must pay a heavy price... When Teiresias fell into this misfortune, Pallas, moved by Chariclo’s tears, granted him prophecy and long life. Even after death, his wisdom endured, and an oracle spoke from his tomb. She gave him a staff, so he could walk without stumbling... Thus, Nonnus, in Book Five of the *Dionysiaca*, has Acteon exclaim that Teiresias is blessed—for though blind, he has seen Minerva unveiled, and thus carries her image forever in his soul.”

At this point, Romola’s hand slips from the chair and touches her father’s, which he has just raised. She does not turn, but continues reading, her voice slightly altered—something held back. The old man speaks:

“Stay, Romola. Bring me my copy of Nonnus. It is better than any Poliziano possesses. I made corrections no one else has seen. I finished it in 1477, when my sight began to fail.”

Romola walks to the far end of the room, moving with the natural grace of a queen—unselfconscious, yet regal. She steps with ease, her frame tall and finely shaped.

“Is it in the right place, Romola?” Bardo asks, always needing confirmation that reality matches the precise image in his mind.

“Yes, father,” she replies. “On the west end, third shelf from the bottom, behind the bust of Hadrian, above Apollonius Rhodius and Callimachus, below Lucan and Silius Italicus.”

As she speaks, a trained ear might detect the faintest strain in her voice—a weariness beneath patience. But when she sees her father’s arms outstretched, trembling with anticipation, her hazel eyes fill with compassion. She hurries to lay the book in his lap and kneels beside him, looking up as if love alone could pierce the darkness that veils his world. In that moment, the sharpness of her features softens—pride and passion give way to tenderness. The deepest part of her, usually hidden, flows through her gaze. She is no longer merely beautiful—she is profoundly human.

But Bardo does not see it. He is flushed, agitated, his fingers tracing the edges of the book.

“The vellum has yellowed in thirteen years,” he says.

“Yes, father,” Romola answers gently. “But your letters at the back are still dark and clear—fine Roman script. And the Greek…” She opens the book on his knee. “It is more beautiful than any manuscript you bought.”

“Indeed,” Bardo murmurs, running his finger over the page as if seeking form. “What hired scribe can match one who loves the words? To such a man, an error is worse than blindness itself. Even these new printers—those who threaten to make learning vulgar—must rely on our manuscripts, where scholars have read deeply, almost divinely, into the poet’s soul. Without us, they would flood the world with nonsense, turning Parnassus into a poisoned stream. Now find the passage in Book Five—Poliziano refers to it. I know it well.”

Romola takes the book, seating herself on a low stool near his knee. She reads the four lines where Acteon declares Teiresias fortunate.

“It is true,” Bardo says when she finishes. “A true vision. For what is the narrow light men use to see only their own petty world, compared to the broad, enduring light that reveals centuries of thought, nations’ lives, and the minds of immortals? I lived with the dead even when I could see. The living seemed mere shadows—lifeless, empty. Unlike the Lamiae Poliziano mocks—Florentines who put on their eyes when they go out and take them off when they return—I come from the streets as from a dream, and return to my books, saying with Petrarch, ‘Books delight me, speak with me, counsel me, and bind me in a living, wise companionship.’”

“And in one thing you are happier than Petrarch,” Romola says, smiling softly. “He looked at his Homer and mourned that the Greek was a dead letter to him. He suffered inner blindness—worse than yours.”

“True,” Bardo replies. “I carry within me the fruit of my fervent study under Crisolora, Filelfo, Argiropulo. I wished to weave all my discoveries into a single work—the harvest of my life. But blindness stole it. And I lacked a fitting assistant. The endurance needed for such paths is less compatible with feminine nature than with frail bodies.”

“Father,” Romola says sharply, flushing. “I will read anything you wish. I will find passages, make notes—whatever you need.”

Bardo shakes his head, smiling with a bitter pity. “Like a pentathlete trying to win five contests with the limbs of a nymph. Have I forgotten your fainting while searching for references to explain a single line of Callimachus?”

“But father, it was the weight of the books. Maso can help. It was not lack of attention.”

He shakes his head again. “I do not need limbs. I need a sharp mind—to cut through the fog of my failing senses. Blindness acts like a dam, forcing thought backward, blocking progress. If my son had stayed—if he had not abandoned me, seduced by fanatical dreams fit only for madmen who dwell among tombs—I might have continued. He was gifted. But now… now the path is closed. All but the narrow track left to me—alone in my blindness.”

Romola rises abruptly, carrying the book back to the shelf. The pain of his words stings her. She returns, standing apart, arms lowered, fingers tightly clasped. Her face is sorrowful as she looks at the silent objects around her—the parchment spines, the broken marble, the rusted bronze and clay.

Bardo, lost in memory, does not notice her retreat.

“Yes,” he continues. “With my son, I might have shared in the triumphs of this age. The names of Bardi—father and son—might have been honored by future scholars, not for idle poetry or pretentious philosophy, but because we offered a lamp to illuminate the greatest works of the past. Why should Poliziano, born after I debated Thomas of Sarzana, be remembered as a great commentator? Why should Ficino, whose Latin offends me and who wanders blindly through superstition, be called the high priest of Platonism—while I, who surpass them, have done nothing but scattered work, claimed by others? Because my son—whom I raised to renew my learning with youth—left me to wander with fanatics, to scream at midnight, to preach on pilgrimages fit only for men who know no past beyond the missal and crucifix—left me when the night had already begun to fall.”

His voice trembles, falling into a tone of sorrow so raw it moves Romola. She turns, her heart swelling with pity. She sits beside him, placing her hand on his knee—not to speak, but to be near, to comfort without words.

“Yes, Romola,” Bardo says, letting his heavy hand, ringed with protective bands, rest too firmly on her blue-veined wrist. She bites her lip to keep still. “If Florence remembers me, it will be as Niccolò Niccoli did—because I chose knowledge over wealth, collected ancient wisdom, and left it, as the Romans did, for my city’s eternal good. But why limit myself to Florence? If Florence remembers me, will not the world?”

He pauses, voice softening. “Yet Lorenzo’s early death has brought new trouble. He promised—would have sworn—that my collection would bear my name, never be sold. But there is enough for the harpies to seize—enough for you too, Romola. Besides, you will marry. Bernardo scolds me for not seeking a proper *parentado* for you. We will delay no longer. We shall consider it.”

“No, father,” Romola says quickly. “What could you do? And it’s useless. Wait until someone seeks me.”

“Nay, child,” Bardo replies. “That is not a father’s duty. The ancients did not think so. And Florentines have not fallen from their ancestors’ ways.”

“But I will study,” Romola insists, her eyes wide with anxiety. “I will become as learned as Cassandra Fedele. I will serve you as if I were a son. Perhaps some great scholar will want to marry me—someone who won’t care about dowry. He’ll want to live here, to be your son in place of mine… and you’ll not regret that I was a daughter.”

A sob rises in her voice. The last words touch something deep in Bardo. He reaches upward, searching for her hair. She leans her head into his hand, and he strokes it gently, leaning close as if he sees a faint glow in her presence.

When Maso opened the door once more and ushered in the two visitors, Nello stepped forward first, bowing deeply to Romola before gently guiding Tito ahead of him toward her father.

“Messer Bardo,” he said, his voice measured and respectful—unlike his usual manner—“I have the honour of presenting to you a Greek scholar who has long desired an audience with you. Not only because I have spoken of your learning and your rare collections, but also because your patronage might offer him support during this difficult time, brought on by shipwreck. His name is Tito Melema—at your service.”

Romola’s astonishment was as great as if the stranger had entered wearing a panther-skin and carrying a thyrsus. The barber had told her nothing of the man’s age or appearance; among her father’s scholarly guests, she had seen only middle-aged or grey-haired men. There was just one face she remembered vividly—one young, beautiful man: her brother, who years ago had lifted her onto his knee, kissed her forehead, and vanished forever. A fair face, golden hair like her own. But the pride instilled in her by her father’s complaints about life’s injustices—a proud independence that refused even a smile to beg for anything—was like a snowbank holding back a sudden flood of admiration.

Tito’s face, bright and radiant, showed its rich beauty beneath his simple black *sajo* and tunic that reached to his knees. It seemed like a spring garland dropped into Romola’s young, wintry world—world built on memories: of a dead mother, a lost brother, a blind father’s past joys, and far-off light, love, and beauty buried deep within books, waiting to be lit again by some known joy. Yet she returned Tito’s bow with her usual pale composure. But as he approached, the ice melted. When he glanced at her again while Nello spoke, a soft flush spread across her cheeks—only to vanish instantly, as though her will had retracted it.

Tito’s gaze, however, held a quiet, pleading admiration—the kind most likely to soften a proud, shy woman, perhaps the only compensation a man can offer when he is too handsome. His charm came not from boldness, but from absence of demand. He seemed like a swift, dark-eyed animal with soft fur—delighting you not by fleeing, but by unexpectedly resting its chin in your palm and looking up, yearning to be touched—as if it already loved you.

“Messere, welcome,” Bardo said, with gentle condescension. “Misfortune joined to learning—especially Greek learning—is a letter of credit worthy of every educated Florentine. As you know, since your countryman Manuelo Crisolora brought the light of Greek scholarship to Italy nearly a century ago, no true scholar is thought worthy unless he knows more than the Latin tradition alone. Those who study only the transplanted literature are called *opici*, or barbarians—just as the Romans themselves admitted they drew their wisdom from the source. I am, as you see, and as Nello has surely warned you, completely blind—an affliction common among us Florentines, whether due to the cold winds sweeping down from the Apennines in spring, or the harsh contrast between our dim homes and the blinding summer sun, which once made so many *lippi* among the Romans. Or perhaps it is some hidden cause beyond our understanding. But please, take seats—Nello, my friend, sit as well.”

Bardo paused, listening carefully until he sensed the others had settled—and that Romola had taken her usual place beside him. Then he asked:

“From what part of Greece do you come, Messere? I had thought your homeland had given up most of its scholars, those who still carry the memory of ancient glory. Though, truth be told, the Ottoman Sultans seem lately willing to graft precious vines onto their wild stock—though they themselves have cut them down and trampled them.”

“I sailed last from Nauplia,” Tito replied. “But I’ve lived in Constantinople and Thessalonica, and traveled through regions rarely visited by Western Christians since the Turks rose to power. I should add, Messere, that I was born not in Greece, but in Bari. I spent my first sixteen years in southern Italy and Sicily.”

As Tito spoke, a subtle tremor passed over Bardo’s delicate features. He leaned forward, reaching out his hand toward Romola, turning his head as if to speak to her—but then corrected himself, turning away and speaking softly.

“Forgive me—may I ask… are you young?”

“I am twenty-three,” Tito answered.

“Oh,” Bardo murmured, his voice low with emotion. “And you must have had a father who cared for your early education—one perhaps a scholar himself?”

There was a pause before Tito replied—long enough for Romola and Nello to notice a visible shudder pass through him, a quiver at the corner of his lip. Clearly, something painful stirred in his memory.

“Yes,” he said. “At least, a father by adoption. He was Neapolitan, highly learned in both Latin and Greek. But…” He hesitated again. “…he is gone—lost at sea, on a reckless journey to Delos.”
