Bardo sank back, too sensitive to press further. He knew the sorrow was fresh. Romola, watching closely, understood how deeply Tito’s words had touched her father—how swiftly this stranger had breached the wall between them and the outside world.

Nello, sensing the lull in conversation, saw his chance to excuse himself.

“In truth, it’s clear as Venetian glass that this young man has had excellent training. The Cennini brothers already have him working on their Greek manuscripts. And I say, if there are two things that cannot be hidden—love and a cough—then there is a third: ignorance. Once a man must do more than nod, he cannot hide it. The clumsy barber always reveals himself when he picks up the shears. Is it not so, Messer Bardo? I speak like a barber, but as Luigi Pulci says—

‘Perdonimi s’io fallo: chi m’ascolta
Intenda il mio volgar col suo latino.’”

“Nay, my good Nello,” Bardo replied, with mild severity, “you are not entirely illiterate. You might have advanced farther had you avoided street gossip, so dear to our Florentines, and especially had you not cluttered your mind with frivolous works—of which Luigi Pulci offers the worst example. A jumble of absurdities and incongruities, far removed from classical models. They resemble the *grylli*—those fanciful conceits of a time when mystic meaning justified monstrous form—except here, the monstrosity remains, but the meaning is gone. A poor contrast to Virgil, whose great poem, as I once held with Filelfo—before Landino claimed the same view—wove profound philosophy into graceful, coherent fable. And I cannot help seeing the rise of such babbling, lawless writings—even supported by Lorenzo himself, otherwise a patron of real learning—as a sign that the hopes of our age are fading into darkness. Worse than iron, perhaps: an age of tinsel and gossamer, where thought lacks substance to take lasting shape.”

“Once more, forgive me,” Nello said, palms open, shoulders shrugging. “I find myself speaking Tuscan before I can recall the Latin. And Messer Luigi’s rhymes slip from my customers’ lips—they corrupt me. Besides, I’ve left my shop and reputation too long in the hands of slow Sandro, who deserves not even the title *tonsor inequalis*, but simply a bungler in the vulgar tongue. So, with your permission, Messer Bardo, I shall take my leave—understanding I remain at your service whenever Maso calls. It feels like a thousand years until I dress and perfume the damigella’s hair, which deserves to shine in the heavens like a constellation—though truly, it would be a pity if it ever went so far beyond reach.”

Three voices chimed together in farewell as Nello bowed to Romola and nodded to Tito. The sharp-minded barber saw that the young man, who had somehow charmed him, had already won Bardo’s favor. Satisfied his introduction had succeeded, he withdrew.

The brief burst of irritation sparked by Nello’s quote had shifted Bardo’s mood. Now, he addressed Tito calmly.

“Ah, young man, you are fortunate to have combined travel with study. You will be welcome among us—bearer of fresh news from a land now alien to us, save through limited trade or fleeting pilgrims. For the days I once knew—when men like Aurispa and Guarino journeyed to Greece as to a treasure house, returning laden with manuscripts every scholar coveted—seem distant. Even the time when learned Greeks fled to our shores for refuge now feels remote—farther than the onset of my blindness. But I suppose research into antiquity was not foreign to your travels?”

“Certainly not,” Tito replied. “On the contrary, my companion—my adopted father—was eager to risk his life in seeking inscriptions and traces of ancient civilization.”

“And I trust there is a record of his work?” Bardo asked eagerly. “For such records must be more valuable than those of Ciriaco, whom I have studied diligently—though his insights are not always matched by depth of learning.”

“There *was* a record,” Tito said, “but it perished in the shipwreck near Ancona. Everything was lost. The only record now is what remains in my memory.”

“You must write it down without delay,” Bardo urged, growing more animated. “You doubtless remember much—if you helped with transcription. At your age, I absorbed words as if carved in stone. That is why I often marvel at my daughter’s memory: it clings to certain things fiercely, yet lets slip the very details that matter for accuracy—the soul of scholarship. But I suspect no such flaw in you, if your will matches your training.”

When Bardo mentioned his daughter, Tito turned his eyes toward her. At the remark about her memory, his face broke into a warm, bright smile—reflected instantly in Romola’s. How soothing that smile was to her! She had never imagined a scholar could smile at a weakness she was constantly blamed for. It felt like the awakening of a new sense—the sense of kinship. They did not look away immediately, as if the moment were stolen; they held each other’s gaze, smiling freely.

“She is not really so cold and proud,” Tito thought.

“Does *he* forget too?” Romola wondered. “Yet I hope not—else he’ll displease my father.”

But Tito had to turn away and answer Bardo.

“I have practiced transcription,” he said. “But in places marked by danger and emotion—where fear of the Turk loomed like a vulture—my memory may have weakened, even if my hand wrote faithfully. On the plain of Eurotas, or amid the colossal stones of Mycenae and Tiryns, the mind wanders, though the eye dictates. Still, I believe I retain something—something that might serve, if enriched by deeper learning than mine.”

“Well said, young man,” Bardo replied, delighted. “And I will gladly lend you my aid, if you wish to share your recollections. I foresee a work that could complement Christoforo Buondelmonte’s *Isolario*, rivaling Ciriaco’s *Itineraria* and Ambrogio Traversari’s admirable studies. But beware—such a book will draw criticism. If it contains novelties, you’ll be accused of forgery. If my interpretations clash with another’s, we’ll be attacked personally—our characters slandered, our lives impugned. You may hear that your mother was a fishwife, your father a renegade priest or hanged criminal. I myself, for correcting a single preposition, was vilified with charges of treason, fraud, indecency, and worse. Such, my young friend, are the flowers that line the path of scholarship! But tell me—what of Byzantium and Thessalonica? I learned much from Demetrio Calcondila, who recently left Florence. But you, it seems, have seen less familiar lands?”

“Yes,” Tito said. “We undertook what I call a dangerous pilgrimage—to visit places almost forgotten by the West. Places beyond the paths of pilgrims. My father believed scholars barely imagine them exist outside books. He dreamed of a new era in learning, when men would seek commentaries not in texts, but in ruins, rivers, valleys, mountains.”

“Ah!” Bardo exclaimed, fervently. “Your father was no ordinary man. Was he fortunate? Did he have friends?”

“No,” Tito said quietly. “He made enemies—mostly, I think, through blunt honesty. They blocked his advancement, so he lived in obscurity. He refused to compromise—he never forgot an insult.”

“Ah,” Bardo murmured, deeply moved.

“One memory stands out,” Tito continued, wanting to shift the subject. “A hurried visit to Athens. Our haste, the danger of capture by Turks, and the threat that our galley might sail without us made it feel like a fever-dream. The wide plain, the surrounding mountains, ruined columns and porticos—some standing far apart, as if retreating from us, others jammed among Christian homes, or Turkish forts and towers. The Acropolis itself is now a stronghold of the Moslems.”

“You surprise me,” Bardo said. “Then Athens is not utterly destroyed, as I feared?”

“Few even among Greeks beyond Attica know its condition today. As we rounded Sunium, our Greek pilot pointed to the great columns atop the rock—the remains of Athena’s temple. ‘That,’ he said, ‘was the school of Aristotle.’ In Athens itself, our monk-guide insisted on showing us where Saint Philip baptized the Ethiopian eunuch—or some such tale.”

“Do not speak of monks and their legends,” Bardo interrupted sharply. “To think that ground once trodden by philosophers and poets is now crawled over by fanatic insects or howling hypocrites—this alone freezes human hope.”

“_Perdio_, I have no love for them,” Tito said, shrugging. “Servitude suits a religion that rejects all that makes life meaningful. Their chants are drowned by the muezzin’s call from the tower on the Acropolis—built upon the Parthenon itself. Every time we looked toward it, our guide cried out in grief that a temple once freed from pagan uses to honor the Virgin Mother of God was now twisted to the cursed ends of Islam. It was seeing those walls of the Acropolis, revealed from afar as we leaned over the galley in Piraeus, that fired my father’s resolve to see Athens at any cost—despite warnings that if we waited for wind to change, they’d leave without us. But we could not approach the Acropolis. The sight of men examining ‘old stones’ raised suspicion—we were feared as Venetian spies, and we fled back to the harbor.”

“We shall speak more of these things,” Bardo said, eager. “Recall everything—every trace. Your memory will earn gratitude from future generations. You will record Greece as it was before the barbarians erased every trace described by Pausanias and Pliny. Take them as your guides. And whatever critical insight my older mind can offer, I will give freely. You have traveled in the Peloponnesus?”

“Yes—and in Boeotia. I rested in the groves of Helicon, drank from Hippocrene. But every sacred site bears the mark of conquest—ruins layered with conflicting claims, only careful study can untangle. From Lacedaemon to Thermopylae, towering above every hillside, stands some massive Frankish fortress—once home to French or Italian lords, now abandoned or held by Turkish bands.”

“Stay!” Bardo exclaimed, his thoughts now fixed on the book they might write. “Will you write in Latin or Greek? Greek is natural to your thoughts, noble in its form. But Latin reaches more readers, more rivals. And if you’re less fluent, I will assist you—yes, I will pour into this work the knowledge I saved for another project, one in which I hoped to have a partner.”

He paused, then added softly:

“But who knows? That work may yet be done. For you too, young man, were shaped by a father who poured his life’s learning into your mind. Our help might be mutual.”

Romola, watching her father’s excitement, felt a strange anxiety. She kept glancing at Tito, fearing he might dismiss her father’s dreams. But no—Tito looked calm, gentle. He must feel, as she did, that the old man’s passion deserved patience. How much more so if he knew about her brother! A girl of eighteen imagines the feelings behind a face that moves her—just as ancient peoples read the gods’ moods in the sky. What else could she believe in, if not this vision spun from within?

And Tito was indeed far from impatient. He enjoyed sitting there, knowing Romola watched him, knowing he could glance at her. He pleased in Bardo’s interest. He did not take the prospect of their collaboration seriously enough to feel burdened—only amused, agreeable.

“I shall be proud and happy,” he said, answering Bardo, “if my efforts can serve your mature scholarship. But surely”—he looked toward Romola—“your lovely daughter makes all other help unnecessary. I heard from Nello she has been trained in the highest studies from childhood.”

“You are mistaken,” Romola said, her voice steady, proud. “I am no match for my father. I lack the gifts needed for true scholarship.”

Bartolommeo Scala, secretary to the Florentine Republic—a man of considerable influence and ambition—lived in a grand palace near the Porta Pinti, now known as the Casa Gherardesca. His coat of arms, bearing a blue ladder across a golden field with the motto _Gradatim_ above the entrance, declared plainly that he had risen from humble origins by his own merit. Once the son of a miller, he had climbed steadily upward, not through favor or fortune alone, but through perseverance and pride. And though he was vain and prone to pomp, he was also honest in his belief that he deserved every honor he had received.

For twenty years, he had held the office of secretary—an office long associated with dignity and public trust. He had delivered orations on the platform of the Old Palace before visiting princes, while Marzocco, the republican lion, wore its golden crown in celebration, and the crowd roared, “Viva Messer Bartolommeo!” He had served on an embassy to Rome, where he had been named titular Senator, Apostolic Secretary, and Knight of the Golden Spur. Eight years prior, he had even become Gonfaloniere—the highest office a Florentine citizen could attain. With each step upward, however, came greater wealth—and greater gout. Now, on this May afternoon, he sat beneath the loggia of his palace, one foot propped up on a cushion, unable to move freely despite the elegance of his surroundings.

The sun bathed the garden beyond in golden light. The heavy silk lucco had been discarded, and a loose mantle draped over his tunic. Beside him sat his daughter Alessandra and her husband, the Greek poet-soldier Marullo. On the other side, two less illustrious friends—men of modest fame, therefore better listeners—occupied the conversation. Yet even in this moment of comfort, Scala’s contentment was shadowed. Before him lay a bundle of letters, the source of a quiet but bitter dispute: a correspondence between himself and Politian, the most renowned scholar of the age.

It was not uncommon in those times for men of learning to relish intellectual quarrels, especially when they could be shared with friends. Scala had done so before, seeking their judgment on whether he or Politian had the stronger claim to reason. This latest exchange began harmlessly enough—over a few playful Latin epigrams. But what started as jesting soon became a clash of egos, pride, and scholarly pretension.

The root of the matter lay in Politian’s unrequited affection for Alessandra, Scala’s daughter. Though rejected, he harbored a lingering resentment toward the secretary—one who had chosen a soldier-poet over the greatest mind of the era. Scala, meanwhile, was a man of achievement, both in public service and in self-congratulation. He was lucky, successful, and increasingly arrogant—qualities that grated on a scholar who valued intellect above status. Yet there was irony in his situation: though he wielded power, he craved recognition as a writer. And so, like many men of rank who dabbled in literature, he composed verses—not for publication, but for private amusement, sending them to friends in letters that served as informal literary journals.

His friends were generous with praise. Ficino and Landino admired his Latin prose as elegant and vigorous. Even Joseph Scaliger, though far removed in time, would later dismiss him as ignorant of classical grammar. But such admiration was never enough. The need to impress someone more critical—someone who might truly judge his work—was irresistible. That someone was Politian.

And thus, the conflict ignited.

Scala sent Politian a Latin epigram, written in a light tone, featuring a mosquito—_culex_, a creature familiar along the banks of the Arno. But in his verse, he assigned it the feminine gender. Politian, ever attentive to detail, corrected him in a polite epigram of his own, noting the error with scholarly precision. Scala responded with a witty retort, subtly mocking Politian’s failed courtship. Politian found the joke charming—yet deeply flawed in Latin form. He decided, out of friendship, to send Scala a Greek epigram of his own, on the same subject. It was a clever piece, weaving myth into zoology: Venus, born from the sea, and the mosquito, likewise emerging from water—both creatures of watery origin.

Scala, in reply, claimed his verses were never meant for a scholar so refined as Politian, whose taste was so delicate that even sturgeon must seem bland. He defended his work, admitting it was hastily written, meant only as a summer pastime for those satisfied with mediocrity—unlike others who sought fame through booksellers. As for the Greek epigram, he confessed he barely understood it, let alone appreciated its subtleties. Still, he ventured a critique: comparing the mosquito to Venus might offend the goddess herself. Moreover, wasn’t Thales’ doctrine—that all things originated from water—more applicable to fish than insects? Perhaps a bat or owl, given their nocturnal habits, would be more fitting metaphors.

Politian seized the opportunity. He dismissed Scala’s defense as trivial. Virgil himself, he reminded him, called the mosquito “the nursling of waters”—an ancient authority that settled the matter. As for the comparisons to birds and beasts, they were “nihil ad rem” (nothing to the point). The eagle flew higher, but that did not mean the mosquito could not fly at all. He added, with measured scorn, that he had no deceit in his nature and would not be fooled by flattery in his absence—quoting Homer to underscore his integrity.

The quarrel escalated. Scala grew more defensive, insisting on his reverence for antiquity and lamenting the decline of his age. He accused Politian of arrogance, of pretending to be a Hercules purging literary monsters, yet failing to recognize his own excesses. In return, Politian returned fire with biting wit. He called Scala a “branny monster,” born in the refuse of a mill, fit only to turn the family grindstones—“in pistrini sordibus natus et quidem pistrino dignissimus.” The insult was both personal and profound.

It was against this backdrop that Tito Melema arrived. The papers detailing this feud had been brought forth specifically to test the young Greek scholar’s judgment. Scala wanted an impartial opinion—especially on Politian’s Greek epigram. After exchanging pleasantries about Tito’s travels and examining a collection of ancient gems—among them an agate showing Cupid riding a lion, and a “Jew’s stone” with a serpent crowned by a lion’s head—conversation turned naturally to classical studies and the state of learning in Florence.

Politian, they discussed, was brilliant but overbearing. He styled himself a guardian of purity in literature, writing letters without signing them as if they were divine revelations. Yet his own critiques were often questionable. He mocked those who imitated Cicero too closely, ending every sentence with “esse videtur,” yet didn’t he himself fall into another trap—favoring rare words and affected phrases?

Tito, having just read Politian’s ‘Miscellanea,’ offered thoughtful observations. He praised some passages, critiqued others—always with care and balance. His insights pleased Scala immensely. Here was a man who could see both strength and flaw in scholarship, who could appreciate satire even in vulgar speech.

Encouraged, Scala produced a few of his own sonnets—light-hearted verses poking fun at Politian’s quirks—though he hesitated to share them fully. But Tito listened with interest, laughing gently at the barbs.

As the interview concluded, Tito was warmly invited to return. His gems were intriguing, yes—but he himself was more so. He had come recommended by Bardo de’ Bardi, a respected scholar and old friend of Scala’s. But now, he had earned his place through his own merits.

Tito left the palace under a bright sky, walking through the Porta Pinti with a smile. The day had gone well. He had passed the test, won the approval of a powerful man, and seen the absurdity of a learned quarrel over a mosquito. Fortune, it seemed, had taken his hand once more. And as he walked, he allowed himself a quiet laugh—knowing full well that the real battle had only just begun.

To northern folk, the dawn of Midsummer brings no special meaning—only the soft light on dew-kissed grass, the fresh green shoots among ancient oaks, and the quiet meadow beyond the gate. But for the Florentine, especially one born into the turbulent soul of the fifteenth century, this morning was different. The sun rising over the Arno carried more than warmth—it bore the weight of tradition. The bells rang not merely with sound, but with purpose: they announced the great festival of San Giovanni, patron saint of Florence since long before memory, a day when the city’s pride and piety intertwined like threads in a rich tapestry.

San Giovanni had watched over Florence for centuries—since Queen Theodolinda of Lombardy first decreed his honor, and perhaps even earlier, as chronicler Villani claimed, back to the days of Constantine and Pope Sylvester. Then, the Florentines had cast aside their old god Mars, not without care. Though they dedicated their noble temple to God and the “Beato Messere Santo Giovanni,” they placed Mars respectfully atop a tower near the river, honoring him as a guardian whose power could not be lightly broken—lest the city suffer ruin. But by the 1400s, that reverence had faded. Mars had vanished from the banks of the Arno; the Republic now trusted not in spear and shield, but in wit, wealth, and coinage. And upon every gold florin, gleaming under the Tuscan sun, stood the image of San Giovanni.

The city had prospered under his protection. Yes, there had been bloodshed—the Guelfs and Ghibellines, the Blacks and Whites, the faithful and the heretical Paterini—but also triumphs. Florence had conquered cities once stronger than itself, most notably Pisa, whose marble towers were too proud, whose ships too honored along Mediterranean coasts. Now, Florence’s name echoed across Europe, even into Africa, celebrated for its pure gold, its dyes, its learning, its poets, and its bankers. A pope once quipped that Florentines were “the fifth element”—so essential they seemed to transcend nature. And though fortune favored stars and saints alike, much of Florence’s greatness was owed to San Giovanni, whose likeness graced the very currency of the land.

So it was fitting that his feast day should be marked with grandeur. The night before, the city had kept vigil in true Florentine fashion—dancing in the streets, jesters mocking, windows shattered, stones thrown—but above all, with marvels only Florence could devise. Thanks to Cecca, the brilliant engineer and architect, the heavens themselves seemed to descend. Saints floated on clouds, surrounded by cherubs, their halos glowing as in Perugino’s paintings. Giants, taller than any man, moved slowly through the narrow lanes—men balanced on stilts, masked and padded like Greek actors, carrying divine images with solemn grace. Clouds were woven cloth; saints, mere mortals supported by iron bars. Yet few thought of such mechanics. To the Florentine mind, these visions carried a sacred echo—was not holiness reflected in the act of creation?

And then came the demons—black, clawed, armed with thongs and whips—performing farces of mock punishment, tearing clothes, dancing wildly. It was their way of keeping vigil too, their own kind of devotion. The Tuscan spirit slipped easily from reverence to satire, as water flows around a stone. The saints had passed, paid homage at the gates of San Giovanni, and now the city waited for the next day’s glory.

On the morning of June 24, 1492, the sky was clear and bright, the air crisp with anticipation. In the Piazza della Signoria, the heart of Florence’s civic life, the procession began to gather. The Palazzo Vecchio loomed beside the Loggia di Orcagna, both silent witnesses to history. Above, the blue sky stretched like a canopy, pierced by the clangor of bells so fierce that even spirits would flee. Terraces and windows overflowed with faces; draperies hung from balconies, turning stone houses into festive theaters. The towers of the Bargello and the Badia stood watch, while below, banners, horses, and towering wax tapers—*ceri*—awaited their moment.

These *ceri*, some as tall as buildings, were hollow structures of wood and pasteboard, gilded and painted with scenes of warriors, maidens, beasts, and fruits. Inside, men spun them endlessly, creating a dizzying spectacle of motion and color. They were not just candles—they were symbols of faith, art, and civic pride, descended from the torches used in early Christian catacombs. And now, beneath a sky of blue, the people gathered in the Piazza del Duomo, where a vast canopy draped in blue cloth adorned with golden lilies and coats of arms formed a second heaven. Banners drooped like rainbows beneath it, offering shelter and splendor to those who leaned from windows or lined the pavement.

Among them sat Tito Melema, radiant in the morning light. He rested his elbow on the red-draped sill of the window above Nello’s shop, his head tilted back, curls brushed against his ear. His face held that calm charm—neither excitable nor dull, neither bitter nor boastful—a man beloved by many, yet never intrusive. Since his arrival two months prior, he had grown brighter, as if sunlight had finally found its way into his soul.

Behind him stood Nello, dressed in holiday finery, and beside him, Pietro Cennini—the scholar, not the practical Domenico. Around them, others chattered: Piero di Cosimo, the painter, grimacing at the bells’ relentless toll; Francesco Cei, the poet, making jests; and Cronaca, the architect, offering dry wisdom. Tito glanced down at the crowd, then up at the faces near him, when suddenly—just at the edge of the marble steps before the Duomo—he saw a face turned toward him.

It was a friar—tonsured, robed in black and white, common enough in Florence. But the look in his eyes… it held something deeper. Not anger, not curiosity, but recognition—fleeting, yes, but undeniable. Like a spark in the dark.

“Nello!” Tito called, quickly. “Look—there! That tall friar going up the steps. Do you know him?”

“One of the Frati Predicatori,” Nello replied, half-joking. “Why should I know the private lives of crows?”

“I’ve seen that face before,” Tito said, frowning slightly. “It’s unusual.”

“Fra Girolamo?” asked Pietro. “Too tall. And he doesn’t appear in public like this.”

“Besides,” added Francesco Cei, “that fire-breathing ‘hound of the Lord’ is not in Florence now. He’s taken Piero de’ Medici’s advice and gone on pilgrimage with his prophecies.”

“The Frate doesn’t rail at men,” said Cronaca. “Only at vice. If you think that attacks your poetry, Francesco, it’s not his fault.”

“He’s gone into the Duomo,” Tito murmured, watching the figure vanish. “No, not Fra Girolamo. He has a high nose, thick lip. This man… he’s different.”

“Enough with your descriptions!” Cennini interrupted. “Look—here come the horsemen!”

And indeed, the procession advanced. First, the banner of the Guelf party—a red eagle clutching a green dragon, crowned with a red lily—held aloft by a rider in white trappings. Around it rode the chief officers of the Guelf faction. “Our proudest banner,” Cennini declared. “It means Florentine will, the triumph of the popolani.”

“And which means,” Cronaca retorted, “the fat popolani triumphing over the lean—and the fattest over all.”

“Sententiousness,” Cennini warned. “Fra Girolamo’s sermons are spoiling you. Soon you’ll carve morality into your cornices.”

Tito smiled. “They’re fine riders,” he said. “But aren’t there foreigners among them? I see strange costumes.”

“Certainly,” Cennini replied. “There are Orators from France, Milan, Venice—behind them, English and German nobles. It’s custom for distinguished visitors to pay tribute to San Giovanni in the train of the gonfalon.”

“Florentine cavaliers ride well,” said Cennini proudly. “Better than those northern cut-and-thrust types whose minds are in their heels. And that Venetian—wouldn’t he be happier riding a dolphin?”

Piero di Cosimo, captivated by color, leaned forward. “The banners—ah! Velvet, satin, brocade—light dancing on fabric. Human beings are dull compared to form and hue. _Va_! Your talk is tame. Only passion lives in color.”

“Ay,” Nello laughed, “if Satanasso could paint, you’d sell your soul to learn his secrets.”

“But angels are poor at chiaroscuro,” Piero added. “See the Madonna Nunziata—how flat she looks!”

Now came the banners of Pisa and Arezzo. “Messire Pisano,” Cennini said, “don’t sulk. Carry your banner with grace. ‘Pisans false, Florentines blind’—but the second part no longer holds.”

Then followed the standards of Florence’s subject towns—Arezzo, Prato, Pistoia—each to be hung in the Baptistery until next year’s festival.

“They’re beautiful,” Tito said. “San Giovanni must be pleased. As Minerva was with her peplos. Though he wears little himself.”

“But those spinning towers!” he added, squinting. “I’d fall out the window from vertigo.”

The *ceri*—those colossal tapers—were indeed a marvel. Hollow, painted, carved, they rotated with inner figures, creating a hypnotic swirl. Some were so large they rolled on wheels. They were the people’s favorite, though critics mocked them as “tower-puppets” when poorly made.

“_Pestilenza_!” Piero muttered, pulling away. “Those circles drive me mad. Warn me when the last one passes.”

“You’ll want to see the contadini,” Nello said. “The peasants of Mugello and Casentino—Leonardo would sketch them a hundred times.”

“No,” Piero insisted. “I’ll wait for the car of the Zecca. I’ve seen enough clowns with tapers.”

Then it came—the car of the Zecca, the Mint. Once a wooden *cero*, it had evolved into a pyramidal triumphal car, drawn by two oxen in rich harness. Atop, thirty feet high, stood a living Saint John the Baptist—bare limbs, tiger-skin robe, golden halo, iron cross fixed in hand. He was not the gentle boy of later art, but the austere prophet of cloister walls.

This role was not prestigious. The actor received ten shillings, a fourteen-pound cake, two bottles of wine, and sweets—paid by the Zecca. From a basket lowered from a nearby window, he ate his share, tossed the rest to the crowd, and clutched the giant cake like a shield as the car lurched forward.

“There go the Masters of the Zecca!” Cennini shouted, pride swelling. “And there’s my brother—you see him, Melema?”

Behind came the Calimara—foreign cloth dealers, dignified elders. Then the Art of Money-changers, the backbone of Florence’s commerce.

And still, Tito’s gaze lingered on the space where the friar had stood. The moment had passed, but the glance remained—like a shadow in the sun.

CHAPTER IX. A Man’s Ransom.

Tito moved quickly through the crowd, his steps light despite the weight of thoughts beneath his surface ease. Though he had brushed off Nello’s casual inquiry about the girl he’d seen earlier—her bright blue eyes peering from beneath the white linen hood typical of a contadina at festival time—he could not entirely dismiss the fleeting wish that he might meet her again. He knew full well it was Tessa, yet he had chosen silence. What concern was it of Nello’s? Tito possessed an almost instinctive gift for concealment, a talent that operated without conscious intent, like breath or heartbeat. It was as natural to him to withhold a truth as it was to others to speak plainly. And so, even when the matter bore no real secrecy—like the sight of a flock of crows passing overhead—he would sometimes hide it, simply because the act felt right.

But this thought of Tessa soon faded, overtaken by the persistent image of the friar whose face had stirred something deep and inexplicable within him. Why should a frail, fasting man with sunken eyes have fixed his gaze on Tito in particular? Where had he seen that face before? The memory clung like a cobweb, irritating and vague. Foolishness, surely—such impressions were best swept aside. Tito had more pressing matters to occupy his mind.

By the time he turned into a narrow side street from the Corso degli Adimari, his thoughts had settled upon the sun high in the sky and the delay the procession had caused. He was late for his visit to the room in the Via de’ Bardi, where he knew Romola and her father waited anxiously for him. He could already picture the moment: the blind man’s face lighting up with joy, like a lamp glowing softly behind translucent glass; Romola’s cheeks flushing pink, a brief warmth that only enhanced her quiet dignity, her expression both tender and startlingly open, like that of a child who has just discovered a secret but feels no need to hide it.

They were companions in quiet harmony during those hours spent around the blind man’s chair. Romola often turned to Tito for answers, and he gladly shared what he knew—but still, he found himself strangely subject to her presence, drawn by a simplicity that seemed to elevate him without pride. For the first time, he felt a kind of reverence—not worship, perhaps, but something akin to it—before a woman whose nature was noble, serene, and deeply rooted in truth. She was not one to be approached lightly, nor could he imagine them alone together, sharing words that might cross into love. He could only dream wildly—knowing it was impossible—that she might one day say to him, “I love you.”

Once, in Greece, while leaning over a sunlit wall, a small dark-eyed peasant girl had crept near, resting her water jug beside him. With shy innocence, she had asked him to kiss her, lifting her smooth olive cheek. Tito had been accustomed to such unbidden affections. But Romola’s love would never come so easily. Would it come at all?

He was young, not inflamed with passion but deeply impressionable. His feelings for Romola were as inevitable as sunlight reflected in clear water—natural, pure, undeniable. Yet he carried no vanity. He understood, with a quiet certainty, that Romola stood far above him. Many men have felt such humility before a woman whose soul seems larger than her years. Still, Tito had enjoyed swift success—success that might have made another man arrogant. But not him. He had earned favor from Bartolommeo Scala, the secretary to the Signory, and their growing friendship had opened doors. It had led to a decision that, even apart from other reasons, would have drawn him permanently to Florence.

Politian, the renowned scholar, taught both Greek and Latin in the city, though the university itself had long since moved to Pisa. For years, Demetrio Calcondila, a distinguished Greek exile, had held a parallel chair in Greek, balancing Politian’s influence. But Calcondila had gone to Milan, leaving Politian without a rival. And now, thanks to Scala and others who valued balance in learning, it was decided that Tito should take up the Greek chair—supported, as Calcondila once had been, by the patronage of Lorenzo himself. This was no mere honor; it was a mark of recognition, a promise of place and purpose.

Tito sailed under favorable winds. He had proven his worth to discerning minds, and he wore his fortune with modest grace. No one resented his success. He was likely to enter the highest circles of Florentine society—where wealth gleamed brighter than silver on brass dishes, and where the Signory did not forbid the wearing of the richest brocades. Who could refuse a handsome young scholar who played the lute and sang with charm? His face, his smile, his voice—each seemed to bring a holiday mood to life, like music and colors raised in defiance of sorrow. Here was a professor who might make the ancient Greeks beloved among noble sons.

And yet, Tito’s good fortune ran deeper. He had sold all his jewels except one ring he refused to part with, and now he held five hundred gold florins—more than most men ever saw in a lifetime.

But the moment he received that sum, it became the turning point in his life—the first time his easy nature faced a true test.

A thought, long shadowed, now seized him with sudden force. He could no longer ignore it. He must choose: surrender to duty, or reject it—and bear the consequences forever.

It happened in the room above Nello’s shop, now Tito’s own lodging. There, the elder Cennini handed him the final installment from Bernardo Rucellai, the buyer of the two most valuable gems. “_Ecco, giovane mio_,” said the printer and goldsmith, “you have now a fine fortune. If you’ll take my advice, let me place your florins safely, where they may grow instead of slipping away in banquets and follies common among our youth. Scholars, especially those like Pietro Crinito, who think their learning needs perfume and embroidery, often squander one hand only to beg with the other. I’ve brought you the money. You’re free to choose wisely or not. I shall see which way the scale tips. We Florentines do not admit a man to any art until he has shown his skill and been enrolled. And no man is truly enrolled in the art of life until he has been well tempted. If you decide to lend your florins to usury, tell me tomorrow. A scholar may marry, and should have something ready for the _morgen-cap_. Addio.”

As Cennini closed the door, Tito turned, his smile fading. He stared at the coins lying on the table, motionless, thumbs tucked into his belt, transfixed by the weight of what lay before him.

“A man’s ransom!”—who had said that five hundred florins was more than enough to buy a man’s freedom?

What if, somewhere under this same midday sun, on some distant coast, a man—older now, worn but still noble-hearted—was toiling as a slave? A man who once rescued a starving boy from filth and cruelty, raised him with care, treated him as a son? That man—Baldassarre Calvo—might now be hewing wood, drawing water, beaten for being slow. And what if he whispered to himself: *Tito will find me. He had only to carry the manuscripts and gems to Venice. He will raise money. He will not rest until he finds me.*

If that were certain, could Tito stand there, look at the gold, and say: *I will stay in Florence, where soft airs whisper of love and prosperity, and risk nothing for him?* No—surely not, if it were certain.

But it was not certain.

The galley had been captured by a Turkish ship en route to Delos—this much was known, from the survivor’s report. But there had been fighting, bloodshed. A man had fallen overboard. Who lived? Who died? Had Tito not himself nearly drowned in a shipwreck? He knew how fragile plans were, how easily fate could unravel everything. Rumors spoke of pirates settling in Delos—but such tales were unreliable. And what if he left Florence, went to Venice, obtained official letters, and set out across the Archipelago? The likely result? He might be seized himself, spend all his money on useless preliminaries, and end once more as a penniless wanderer—with no gems left to sell.

Tito saw that outcome more clearly than the possibility of finding his father alive. It would be unfair, wouldn’t it? To turn his back now, in the bloom of youth, on love and promise, only to lose them forever?

“And yet,” he murmured aloud, “if I were certain Baldassarre was alive—and that I could free him, no matter the danger—I would go now. Now I have the money. Before, it was useless to debate. I would go to Bardo, to Scala, and tell the whole truth.”

He did not say to himself—though he knew it—that if Scala and Bartolommeo had known the truth, there would have been no choice: he would have had to search. He did not say aloud what he knew well—that many Greeks of distinction had crossed seas again and again, begged help from kings and bishops, to free family from Ottoman slavery. Such deeds were not seen as extraordinary, but as duties.

This was his first true conversation with himself. Until now, he had followed impulses, often concealing half-truths. He had never paused long enough to confront why he hid. What use was telling all? True, the thought had crossed his mind since leaving Nauplia: *After all, it’s a relief to be rid of Baldassarre.* He had even wondered, with a pang of curiosity, *Who fell overboard?*

Such thoughts arise naturally from burdensome relationships. Baldassarre had been demanding, growing more so with age. He scrutinized Tito constantly, measuring his thoughts against his own grand expectations. Age had hardened him—a thickset man beyond sixty, heavy-browed, bald, whose intellectual fervor had become monotony. Among strangers, such a man would not command admiration. Youth, beauty, rank—they would not kneel at his feet. Novelty does not warm hearts for the dimmed and weary. Only love rooted in memory, in fidelity, in enduring tenderness, can keep such souls from loneliness.

But surely such memories were not absent in Tito?

Far back in time, when Tito was seven, Baldassarre had pulled him from abuse, taken him to a home that felt like paradise—food, comfort, gentle hands. From then until their parting, Tito had been the center of his benefactor’s world. He had been obedient, quick, eager to learn—bright, lovely, graceful. A youth without vice, as if his very form embodied perfect balance. When silent, he did not seem sullen; when refusing labor, he would fall to the ground with a half-smile, half-plea, so charming that the pleasure of seeing him outweighed the disappointment. His mouth curved with effortless humor. And his mind—how swiftly it grasped philosophy, poetry, even street songs caught in passing!

Would anyone have said Tito had failed his benefactor? That gratitude had waned?

He did not believe it. But the truth remained: it was not certain Baldassarre was alive. Not certain he was enslaved.

“Do I not owe something to myself?” Tito asked, shifting slightly—the first movement he’d made since looking at the gold. “Before I abandon everything, before I face again the risks I am already weary of, I must have a reasonable hope. Am I to spend my life searching? _I believe he is dead._” Cennini was right—his florins deserved protection. “I will give them to him tomorrow.”

And so, the next morning, when Tito acted on his decision, he had chosen his path. He had sealed his future. He had made it impossible to desire otherwise—to want, deep down, that his father was dead. Impossible not to be tempted toward falsehood rather than expose the truth of his actions.

Under every hidden secret lies a nest of guilty wishes, nurtured in darkness. The harm of sin often lies less in the deed than in the way it distorts desire—how self-interest aligns itself with deceit. Conversely, confession purifies, not by removing guilt, but by destroying the illusion that lies can save us. The soul regains its honesty.

Now, in this first clear reckoning with himself, scattered thoughts had coalesced. The small streams of selfishness had merged into a single current, strong enough to overcome resistance. Before, Tito had merely hesitated—uncertain whether he would return to seek his father’s fate. Now he had made a firm excuse. He had admitted to himself a choice he would never confess to others, a choice that would shame him if Baldassarre ever returned.

Yet the inner shame, the echo of a moral law written deep in human conscience—even without sympathy, without impulse—was showing its blushes in Tito’s quiet assertion: *He is dead. Or, at least, hope is gone.*

It had been three weeks since Tito handed over his florins to Cennini, and on this day of San Giovanni, he walked toward the Via de’ Bardi with a light step and a clear mind. He carried no burden, neither in thought nor in spirit. The world around him flowed easily into his nature—there was no friction between what he saw and what he felt. His joyousness was untroubled by shadows; the distant future held no fears for him, and the hidden corners of life never stirred unease. As he stepped from the bright sun into the cool shade of a narrow street, he removed his black cloth berretta—the simple cap with its upturned brim—and pushed back his brown curls, letting his head toss freely in the breeze. There was nothing deceitful in his face, nor any unmistakable mark of innocence either. It was simply a young man’s brow—strongly formed, smooth, and square—with no trace of anxiety or pretense. His eyes, wide and dark-brown, scanned the upper windows of the houses with a gaze that belonged to youth: open, undimmed, and full of quiet curiosity. They were not cunning, nor were they naive—they were simply alive.

Was it possible that Tito’s face meant different things to different people? That one could read more than one truth in its lines? His presence spoke only of ease—a calm absence of vanity, ambition, or restlessness. And so, as he passed among the holiday crowds, admiration followed him not out of obligation, but willingly, like sunlight falling where there is no shadow.

The Festa had already spread beyond the main thoroughfares. The throng that had once gathered along the procession route now fanned out in every direction, chasing new diversions. Such moments—when the crowd grows restless, when energy seeks an outlet—are often the most volatile. Where amusement is expected and denied, tempers flare, and a single person may become a target for the collective mood.

Near the church of San Martino, the crowd thickened. At the hostelry known as the *Bertucce*, or “Baboons,” a knot had formed. A commotion drew Tito’s attention. Amidst laughter, he heard a small voice cry out, trembling: “Let me go! Holy Virgin, help me!” Without hesitation, he pushed through the circle of onlookers.

He saw at once—a young peasant girl, her white hood fallen off during a struggle, her hands caught in the grip of a man dressed in the motley colors of a *cerretano*, a conjuror. The man laughed, trying to soothe her, clearly enjoying the scene. The spectators chattered, calling her names—*tonta*, *bambina*, *povera sciocca*—as if her resistance were foolishness to be corrected.

At first, the girl’s face was turned away. Tito saw only her light-brown hair, braided and pinned with a long silver clasp. Then, in the next breath, she turned—and he recognized her. It was Tessa. Her babyish features were streaked with tears, her lower lip quivering. And then, through her misted eyes, she saw *him*. A sudden spark of hope lit her face, like a child finding shelter in the arms of someone familiar.

In a flash, Tito broke through the crowd, grasped Tessa’s waist, and said firmly, “Release this child. What right do you have to hold her against her will?”

The conjuror—a man whose sharp angles of eye, nose, and jaw all seemed to point upward—grinned, showing neat teeth. He released her hands, shrugged, and backed away slightly, half-apologetic, half-defiant.

“I mean her no harm, Messere,” he said. “Ask these good folk. I only meant to show my tricks—she’d make a fine assistant with that sweet face, which would assure everyone of honesty. I promised her confetti as a reward. But perhaps you’ve better treats in your pocket? She knows it.”

A ripple of laughter went through the crowd. Tessa clung tightly to Tito’s arm, her relief plain. To her, the laughter was just noise—like the roar of beasts behind her. But Tito knew better. He sensed the crowd’s irritation at being deprived of their entertainment. He moved quickly, pulling her away before their mockery could turn sharper.

“Here’s your hood,” the conjuror called after them, tossing the white cloth over her head. “_Orsù_, don’t bear me ill will. Come back when you can spare her.”

“Oh, Maestro Vaiano,” one man shouted, watching Tessa flinch, “she’ll come back soon enough—just like the toad said to the harrow.”

Tito hurried down a side street, annoyed at the delay. He wanted only to reach the Via de’ Bardi. But Tessa, clinging to him, looked frightened again.

“No!” she whispered. “I’ve lost my mother and my father-in-law. They’ll be angry—he’ll beat me. It happened near San Pulinari—someone pushed me, and I couldn’t stop. I don’t know where they are. Please, don’t leave me!”

Her voice cracked. Tears welled again, and she sobbed quietly.

Tito walked faster. The Church of the Badia was close. He could take her inside—perhaps leave her there. But the church was closed. Instead, they paused under the cloister arches, where the air was still and shaded.

“Do you have a cousin or friend in Florence?” he asked gently. “Someone you could find? Or are you afraid to walk alone now?”

“Oh, I’m afraid,” she said, her voice trembling. “He was the devil—I know he was. And I don’t know where to go. No one—no one at all. My mother meant to eat somewhere, but I don’t know where. Holy Madonna! I’ll be beaten.”

Her little chest rose and fell, beads glinting above her green serge dress. A sob escaped, then another. Tito felt helpless. Leaving her now would be cruel. His nature could not endure such sorrow.

As she lifted her apron to catch her tears, he touched it too, rubbed her cheek, and kissed her soft round face.

“My poor little Tessa,” he murmured. “Don’t cry. We’ll find a way. Where do you live?”

She didn’t answer at first, but the sobs slowed.

“Come,” he said, “if you tell me where you’re going, I’ll take you partway.”

The apron dropped. Her face softened, glowing with trust. The conjuror, the fear, the beating—all faded into distance.

“I think I’ll go home,” she whispered, looking up at him with wide blue eyes, something sweeter than a smile on her lips. “If you’ll take me.”

“Then let’s go,” Tito said, slipping her arm through his. “Which way?”

“Beyond Peretola—where the big pear tree stands.”

“Peretola? Out which gate, pazzarella? I’m a stranger here.”

“The Por del Prato,” she said, holding his arm tightly.

He didn’t know the roads well enough to avoid crowds, but he thought: maybe among more people, he’d run into Monna Ghita. So he headed straight for Porta Rossa, then Ognissanti. Along the way, people joked about the handsome young nobleman walking with his barefooted country girl—apprentices laughed, women watched, beggars held out caps mockingly, gamblers jeered. The streets of Florence in those days were rarely orderly, and Tessa’s fear was not misplaced.

When they reached the Piazza d’Ognissanti, Tito slowed. They were both warm from walking. He led her to a stone bench against the wall.

“Holy Virgin,” Tessa breathed, “I’m glad we’re away from those boys and women. But I wasn’t really scared—you’d protect me.”

“Pretty little Tessa,” Tito smiled. “Why do you feel so safe with me?”

“Because you’re beautiful—like the saints going into Paradise. They’re all good.”

“You haven’t eaten since breakfast,” he said, seeing nearby stalls selling fruit and sweets. “Are you hungry?”

“Yes,” she said softly. “If you want some too.”

He bought apricots, cakes, and comfits, and placed them in her apron.

“Let’s walk to the Prato,” he said. “Then you might not be afraid to go alone.”

“But you’ll eat some,” she insisted, gathering her apron like a bag.

“We’ll see,” he replied aloud, but to himself: *This little contadina could inspire a better idyll than Lorenzo’s ‘Nencia da Barberino.’ If only I were Theocritus—or had time to write poetry instead of walking in the sun!* Still, it was done. He was late already. Another half-hour wouldn’t matter. *Pretty little pigeon.*

“We have a garden,” Tessa said, “and plenty of pears. Two cows, and mules. I love them all. But my father-in-law is harsh. I wish my mother hadn’t married him. I think he’s wicked—he’s very ugly.”

“And does your mother let him beat you, poverina?” Tito asked. “You said you were afraid.”

“Oh, my mother scolds me too. She loves my younger sister more. She thinks I don’t work enough. No one speaks kindly to me—except the Pievano, when I go to confession. The men in the Mercato laugh at me. Nobody ever kissed me… not like you do. You talk to me like I talk to my little black-faced kid—because I love it so much.”

It seemed Tessa had not noticed how different Tito looked now—how polished, how noble. He had not changed in her eyes. He was simply a voice and a face from another world—something kind, something pure, that had come to her from heaven. She spoke without restraint, as if he were a dream born of her own heart and the sunlight.

They reached the Prato—a large open field within the city walls, where Florentine youths played *Calcio*, a rough game of football. At midday, it was empty except near the gates, where a tent stood ready for the race.

Tito stopped at the edge of the meadow.

“Now, Tessa,” he said, “you won’t be afraid if I leave you. Addio. Shall I buy milk from you tomorrow morning, to see you’re safe?”

He said it gently, seeing her eyes widen with sorrow, her mouth droop.

She didn’t speak at first. Only opened her apron and stared at the apricots. Then she looked up.

“I thought you’d eat with me,” she said, her voice small. “We could sit under a tree outside the gate and share them.”

“Tessa, Tessa,” Tito laughed, kissing her cheeks. “You’re a little siren—you’d ruin me. I should have been in the Via de’ Bardi hours ago. No—I must go. You’re safe. Here—take an apricot. Addio!”

He took two steps away. But Tessa stood frozen, pale, a great sob rising. She turned slowly, as if accepting defeat, and began walking forward—her apron forgotten, apricots rolling onto the grass.

Tito couldn’t help it. He ran after her, picked up the fruit, and returned.

“See, silly one,” he said, handing them back. “Come, stop crying. I’ll walk with you. We’ll sit under the tree. But I must go eventually.”

So they found a great plane-tree just beyond the gate. They sat beneath it—Tessa leaning against the trunk, Tito stretched opposite, elbows resting on the mossy ground. Sunlight filtered through the leaves, dancing around them like living sparks. Tessa’s face was peaceful again. The taste of the apricots and sweets seemed delicious.

“You pretty bird,” Tito said, watching her examine the remains, debating whether to save them. “To think anyone would scold you! What sins do you confess, Tessa?”

“Oh, many,” she said. “I’m often naughty. I hate work, though I know I’ll be beaten. I give the mules the best fodder when no one sees. Then when my mother is angry, I say I didn’t do it—that makes me afraid of the devil. I think the conjuror was the devil. I’m less afraid after confession. Look—I have a _Breve_.” She pulled from her bosom a tiny bag, carefully tied. “A holy father blessed it at Easter. And I think the Madonna will protect me—she looks like she would. Maybe if I weren’t idle, she wouldn’t let me be beaten.”

“If they treat you so cruelly,” Tito said, “wouldn’t you like to leave? To live with a kind lady, if she’d take you as a servant?”

Tessa held her breath. Then whispered, “I don’t know.”

“Then would you like to be my little servant?” Tito asked, smiling. “Live with me?”

A flush of joy lit her face. “Will you take me now? Ah, I wouldn’t go home and be beaten.” She hesitated. “But I’d like to fetch my black-faced kid.”

“You must go back for your kid,” Tito said, standing. “And I must go the other way.”

“By Jupiter!” he muttered, stepping from the shade. “It’s not a pleasant walk to the Via de’ Bardi. I’d rather sleep here.”

And so he did. Tito had an instinctive aversion to discomfort—even when duty waited. He had risen early, walked in the sun, seen sights. He was tired. And Tessa made the air softer. He lay down again, placing his cap under his head. It wasn’t comfortable. So he shifted, and asked gently, “May I rest my head on your lap?”

Tessa sat still as a dove on its nest. When he slept, she dared to touch his dark curls—soft, falling backward from his ear. She was too happy to sleep, too happy to imagine he would wake and leave her. For her, this moment was everything—this shade, this silence, this breathing warmth beside her. A tiny fish needs only a small pool to feel infinite peace.

Time passed unnoticed. Then, slowly, Tito stirred. His eyes opened—first with surprise, then a smile, which faded as thought returned. He had dreamed he was in the Via de’ Bardi, explaining his lateness. The dream woke him sharply. He sat up, shook his cap, and said:

“Tessa, little one, you let me sleep too long. The sun has traveled far. I must go. Addio.”

He patted her cheek, adjusted his cap.

She said nothing. But her face told him everything. He spoke again—more seriously, almost sternly.

“Now, Tessa, don’t cry. I’ll be angry. I won’t love you if you cry. Go home to your kid. Or watch the horses start. But I can’t stay longer. And if you cry, I’ll think you’re troublesome.”

Her tears froze. She turned pale, silent, her blue eyes wide with fear.

“Look,” Tito said, soothingly, opening his wallet. “Here’s a charm I’ve kept since Sicily—a long way off.”

He emptied the contents onto her lap—coins, trinkets, scraps. Among them was his onyx ring.

“Ah, my ring,” he said, slipping it on. “I forgot it this morning. Strange—I never noticed it was missing.”

Then he found what he sought—a small piece of red coral, pointed like a horn.

“See,” he said, holding it up. “Like your goat’s horn, isn’t it? There’s a hole—so you can tie it to your cord, beside your _Breve_. It’s a ‘buona fortuna.’ If evil spirits come—dark shapes in the corner—point this coral horn at them. They’ll run away. It will keep you safe when I’m not near. Now, undo your cord.”

Tessa obeyed, her hands steady. The coral hung beside the tiny bag with the sacred parchment. She felt stronger.

“And now,” Tito said, closing his wallet, “you’ll give me a kiss. And look happy—like a good girl—and then—”

But she had already leaned forward, pressing her lips to his cheek.

“Oh, you pretty pigeon!” he laughed, cradling her face in his hands, crushing her cheeks together in a broad, gentle kiss.

Then he stood and walked away, not looking back until ten yards off—then gave a final wave.

Tessa watched him go. He could see she wasn’t crying. That was enough. He needed no sorrow in his sight.

“I wonder,” he thought, walking on, “when Romola will kiss me like that?”

The journey seemed longer now. He almost wished he hadn’t lingered. But no excuse was needed for Bardo or Romola. He could simply say he’d been delayed. Their pride would accept it. He reached Ognissanti, ate quickly, crossed the Arno by Ponte alla Carraja, and hastened toward the Via de’ Bardi—his thoughts already turning ahead, yet still carrying the echo of a child’s kiss beneath a plane-tree.

When Fra Luca fell silent, Tito remained rooted to the spot, caught in a quiet turmoil. Only when the crowd dispersed and the friar slowly made his way into the church of Santa Felicita did Tito finally move, turning toward the Via de’ Bardi with measured steps. His mind raced ahead of him.

*If this monk is truly from Florence—if he stays here—then all will be revealed.* The thought struck him like a sudden storm. A new crisis had arrived, but it did not shake him visibly. He still had business to attend to: a visit to Bardo, and an apology for his absence. Tito had always been skilled at concealment, but lately that talent was evolving into something more deliberate, almost refined. He could still choose to come clean—to admit Baldassarre’s existence—but doing so would cast a shadow over his earlier silence. It might seem less like modesty and more like calculated evasion, designed to avoid fulfilling a claim he knew was real. And what about the florins? Hadn’t he settled comfortably in Florence, invested wisely, while the fate of his benefactor remained uncertain? That, too, would now appear suspect.

For now, discretion was safer. He would wait. There was still the night to think, and no one need know precisely when the letter had reached him.

So he climbed the stairs to Romola’s home—the room on the second floor where her father sat among scrolls and marble, untouched by the bustle of the city, even on feast days. Tito entered with only a slight dimming of his usual cheer, as if regretting his lateness. But there was no need to dramatize it—he had missed the Corso deliberately, just to show he cared. Now, he set about livening the evening with animated talk, though inside, his thoughts churned like a hidden machine, forging ideas apart from the words he spoke. By the time he descended the stone steps and stepped out into the starlit street, his mind had already begun to shape a plan.

The next day, after his teaching duties ended, he walked up the Via del Cocomero toward the convent of San Marco. His purpose was clear now. He would go to Fra Luca—not to confess, but to probe. How much did the friar suspect? On what grounds? And how long would he remain at San Marco? With answers to those questions, Tito believed he could craft a response that would allow him to stay in Florence, unshaken by duty or guilt.

He had never before felt the need to invent a clever lie. But now the moment had come—the kind of moment that arises naturally from silence, from half-truths left unchallenged. And his mind, sharp and self-assured, was ready. He had convinced himself he was under no obligation to seek out Baldassarre. Once, he had said he would go if he had proof of his father’s survival. But now, he questioned that vow. *Why* should he go? What was life, ultimately, if not the pursuit of pleasure? And wasn’t his own young, vibrant life far more capable of joy than the worn-out years of a man whose passions had hardened into rigidity?

Baldassarre had lived his life, drawn his share of meaning from it. Now, Tito reasoned, it was his turn. The future stretched open before him—“I think they are going to take me to Antioch,” he’d heard whispered. Imagine it: a long journey, months or years spent searching for someone whose whereabouts were uncertain, perhaps even nonexistent. And all the while, he would leave behind a life of promise, love, and distinction. Even if he found his father, it would mean returning to the same old demands, the same familiar constraints—known, predictable, stifling.

Yes, the gems—and thus the florins—belonged to Baldassarre, in the narrow sense of property law. But in a deeper, truer sense—the sense of nature and vitality—they belonged to whoever could enjoy them most. And that was Tito. He acknowledged this wasn’t what society expected. Men around him would demand that he use the money to rescue his father. But what was society’s morality, really? A tangled web of outdated customs, shaped not by reason but by tradition. No wise man should follow it blindly—except when it suited his comfort.

And yet, he admitted, he didn’t care much for the florins themselves—save perhaps for Romola’s sake. He could give them up without hesitation. What mattered was the joy he stood to lose—the joy within reach, already warming his soul. To abandon that, to walk away thirsty and empty-handed, seemed unjust. Any rule that demanded men sacrifice their own happiness for others was merely selfishness disguised as virtue—a doctrine created by those who wanted others to suffer for their benefit.

He didn’t wish Baldassarre harm. He disliked suffering in anyone. But could any philosophy prove that one person’s pain should outweigh another’s joy? To do so, he would have to love his father deeply—and he did not. Was that his fault? Gratitude? Upon closer inspection, it held no true claim. Baldassarre had lived a full life; hadn’t he found satisfaction in it? Should Tito feel indebted simply because his father had enjoyed his own existence?

Once started, Tito’s reasoning became relentless—an acid eating through moral sentiment. His mind lacked the fear many called superstition: not the fear of physical danger, but the deeper dread of divine justice, the awe that once gripped religious pagans and still echoes in the hearts of believers. That fear, he knew, was not mere cowardice—it was the first instinct of conscience, the check upon unchecked desire. As the ancient Eumenides sang in Aeschylus: *“It is good that fear should sit as guardian of the soul, forcing it into wisdom—good that men should carry a threatening shadow in their hearts under the full sunshine; else, how should they learn to revere the right?”* That guardianship might someday become unnecessary—but only when duty and love flowed together as one.

As Tito approached the outer cloister of San Marco and asked for Fra Luca, no such shadow haunted him. He was too well-read, too skeptical, to believe in omens. He had grown up mocking priests whose lives contradicted their sermons, and debating the “Chief Good” with scholars who never reached agreement. For him, ethics were a matter of taste, not law.

Yet fear was not absent. It was a powerful force in Tito—fear of anything that might steal his pleasure. And he feared Fra Luca might be the instrument of his exile from Florence.

“Fra Luca?” replied a lay brother, a _fra converso_, with a thick country accent and dull eyes. “Ah, he has gone to Fiesole—to the Dominican monastery there. He was carried away on a litter this morning. The poor Brother is very ill. Can you leave a message?”

Tito thanked him and turned away, relief washing over him. *This friar is not likely to live,* he thought. *I saw how thin he was, how fragile. And at Fiesole, no one will remember me. If he returns later, my explanation will serve just as well.* Still, a small question lingered: *What was it about his face that stirred something in me?*

Tito walked lightly, the immediate dread passed, his usual cheerfulness returning as he made his way to see Romola. Yet her life—so full of quiet devotion, patient endurance, and gentle self-sacrifice—was a mirror of everything he had once avoided. He had not turned from goodness entirely; nor was he yet ready to embrace vice. Still young, with a heart open to beauty and joy, he remained untouched by corruption—for now. He had sold himself to evil, but it had not yet changed the shape of his days. Life still felt familiar, unaltered in its rhythm. He meant for things to continue as before: within and without. He would earn praise through diligence, learning, and charm. He would not do anything to disturb the harmony he cherished. And above all, he cared deeply for Romola. He longed to make her his wife—beautiful, loving, irreplaceable. Though wealthier matches might one day be possible, no woman in Florence could compare to her. When she looked at him with those honest hazel eyes, he felt a force like music—a deep, rhythmic sway that pulled him inward, making him want only to return to that moment again and again.

Even before reaching the door, standing on the stone steps with only Maso near, he felt that pull already stirring in his chest.

“Welcome, Tito mio,” came the old man’s voice, warm and alert, before Tito had spoken. There was new strength in Bardo’s tone, new brightness in his blind face since their first meeting over two months past. “You’ve brought fresh manuscript, I suppose? But since we spoke last night, I’ve had new thoughts—we must go back, widen our scope.”

Tito bowed to Romola as he stepped forward, then moved to Bardo’s chair as custom dictated, placing his hand into the one offered, settling onto the leather seat beside the old scholar.

“Yes,” he said softly, “I’ve brought the manuscript—but it can wait your pleasure. My legs are young; I’ll climb the hill again without trouble.”

He didn’t glance at Romola as he spoke, but he knew her eyes were upon him, filled with quiet delight.

“That is well said, my son,” Bardo replied, using the term more often lately. “And I am glad to see you do not shrink from labor, without which, as the poet wisely said, life has given nothing to mortals. Too often, ambition seeks the *palma sine pulvere*—glory without the dust of struggle. But what says the Greek? ‘In the morning of life, work; in the mid-day, give counsel; in the evening, pray.’ Though I may seem to have reached that twilight hour, I am not helpless so long as I have words left unspoken. My mind, as I’ve told you, was once dammed up—waters dark and still. But you, my Tito, have opened a channel, and now they surge forward with a force even I cannot fully command. Now, I wish us to revisit our earlier work, broaden our commentary and illustration. Otherwise, I may lose chances I now see clearly—chances that may never come again. You understand me, Tito?”

He bent slightly to retrieve his manuscript, which had slipped down, and Bardo’s sharp hearing caught the faint rustle.

Tito might have winced at the thought of more labor, but he was not naturally impatient. He had been trained in the rigorous scholarship of the age—minute, thorough, vast—and with Romola near, every effort felt lightened by the warmth of her presence.

“Certainly,” he answered. “You wish to expand our notes on certain passages we’ve cited.”

“Not only that. I wish to insert occasional *excursus*, where we’ve mentioned authors I’ve studied closely. For I may die too soon to complete any separate work. And now is no time to let learning lie idle—especially when we face not just ignorance, but men like Calderino, who, as Poliziano showed, resort to false citations to feed vanity and glorify their own errors. Therefore, Tito, I believe we should not miss this opportunity. Let us return to the point where we quoted Thucydides. First, I want you to go through all my notes on Lorenzo Valla’s Latin translation—the one Pope Nicholas the Fifth paid five hundred gold scudi for, a sum not unjustly granted. Though Valla’s reputation is otherwise questionable, he is praised for his strict scholarship, and the epigrammatist joked that since he descended to the shades, Pluto himself dares not speak in ancient tongues. Thus, his name must not become a seal for falsehood. So I will include an *excursus* on Thucydides, where my corrections of Valla’s text may properly belong. My Romola, bring the necessary volumes—you know them—they are on the fifth shelf of the cabinet.”

Tito rose at once, saying, “Let me fetch them if you’ll show me,” and followed Romola into the small adjoining room, whose walls were lined with books arranged in perfect order.

“There,” she said, pointing upward. “Every book is exactly where Father left it.”

Tito stood beside her, not hurrying to reach the volumes. They had never been alone in this room before.

“I hope,” she said, turning her gaze fully upon him, her expression calm and sincere, “he won’t weary you. This work brings him such joy.”

“And me too,” Tito murmured, “if you’ll allow me to say—I love you—if you’ll think me worth loving even a little.”

His voice was barely a whisper, and his face—dark, beautiful, closer to hers than ever before—looked at her with tender pleading.

“I do love you,” Romola whispered back. Her eyes held his with the same quiet dignity as always, but her voice had never dropped so low. It seemed to both of them that they stared at each other for a long time before her lips moved again—though it was only a breath before she said, “I know _now_ what it means to be happy.”

Their faces nearly touched; dark curls mingled with golden waves for a fleeting instant. Then, swift as lightning, Tito stepped onto a ledge of the shelves and retrieved the volumes. They returned to silence, content to be apart—because that first shared moment, that fragile awareness of one another, had been all the more powerful for being free of immediate sensation.

It had passed as quickly as water merging into water—so fast that even the watchful Bardo did not grow restless.

“You have the books, Romola?” the old man asked as they returned. “Now, prepare your pen. As Tito marks the scholia we decide to extract, you shall copy them immediately—number them carefully, so they match the numbers in the text he writes.”

Romola always had a role in their joint work. Tito stood at the leggio, writing and reading, while she sat at a table directly before him, ready to hand Bardo anything he needed or take a volume from his lap. They had occupied these positions since the beginning, but today it felt different—new. It was different to sit opposite one another, different for Tito to take a book from her hands as she lifted it from her father’s knee. Yet there was no calculated gesture, no attempt to prolong a glance or touch. Each person shapes the tokens of love in their own image, and Romola’s deep, tranquil joy wrapped Tito like the soft, rich light of dusk—calming, steady, dissolving all unease.

They had worked for two hours when the fading light prompted them to pause. Then the door opened, and a figure entered—strangely out of place amid the quiet study and solemn thoughts. It was Monna Brigida, a short, stout woman of fifty, with black eyes and a black velvet berretta embroidered with pearls. Beneath it, thick braids of hair framed a broad forehead, falling in heavy curves over her ears. A startling crimson blush colored her fat cheeks, contrasting with her pale skin. Three rows of pearls and a gold necklace rested on her thick neck. Her trailing black velvet gown and rose-colored damask sleeves, though slightly faded, bore the mark of skilled craftsmanship—six months’ labor, perhaps. The rose petticoat beneath, edged with dimmed white fringe and seed-pearl patterns, was displayed deliberately to signal its value. A coral rosary hung from one side of her belt, secured by a silver clasp engraved in niello; on the other side, a crimson velvet purse stitched with pearls dangled from her waist. In her plump right hand—pale, swollen as if baked—she carried a devotional book, also adorned with velvet, pearls, and silver.

She was familiar enough to Tito not to startle him. Monna Brigida often visited Bardo, spared from the banishment of trivial women due to her kinship with his late wife and her care for Romola in childhood. Now Romola smiled warmly and rose to move the leather seat away from her father’s chair, so the coming flood of talk wouldn’t disturb his ear.

“La cugina?” Bardo asked, sensing her steps and drapery.

“Yes, it’s your cousin,” Monna Brigida replied sharply, raising her fingers toward Tito in greeting, then lifting her face for Romola to kiss. “Always the troublesome cousin interrupting your wisdom,” she continued, seating herself and fanning herself with a white veil. “Well, well—if I didn’t bring you news now and then, I’d swear you’d forget there was anything alive but these dusty ancients, who need holy water to cleanse them, if all I hear is true. Not that the world isn’t rotten enough now—scandals popping up everywhere. I don’t want to see or hear such things, but you can’t walk with your head in a bag. Just yesterday—well, well, don’t burst out at me, Bardo, I’m not going to tell anything. I may not be as wise as the three kings, but I know how many legs go into one boot. Still, Florence is a wicked city—isn’t it, Messer Tito? You go out into the world. Not that we shouldn’t sin a little—Messer Domeneddio expects it, else what are the sacraments for? What I say is, we must honor the saints, not pretend we’re like them, or life becomes unbearable. Especially if things keep going like this. For what do you think? I went to a wedding today—Dianora Acciajoli’s with the young Albizzi everyone’s been talking about—and everyone wondered why it was today instead of yesterday. But, _cieli_! Such a wedding it was—it might have waited until next Lent for a penance. The bride looked like a white nun—not a pearl on her—and the groom as solemn as Saint Joseph. True! Half the guests were _Piagnoni_—that’s what they call them now, funeral mourners, these new saints of Fra Girolamo’s making. To think two families like the Albizzi and Acciajoli would adopt such ideas when they could afford the finest clothes! Well, they invited me—had to, since my husband was Luca Antonio’s uncle by the mother’s side—and I had a dreadful time waiting under the canopy before they let us in. I couldn’t stand in my clothes without offending someone. Monna Berta, who’s had worse secrets than I ever could, glared at me under her hood like a _pinzochera_, and told me to read the Frate’s book on widows—she found great comfort in it. Holy Madonna! It seems widows are supposed to buy their coffins now and count the years till they enter them, instead of enjoying themselves when they finally have freedom. And what music did we have to liven dinner? A long sermon from Fra Domenico of San Marco on the doctrines of their blessed Fra Girolamo—the three doctrines we’re all to memorize. He marked them off on his fingers until I shivered: first, Florence—or the Church, I’m not sure which—shall be scourged. If he means plague, the Signory should stop such preaching—it’s enough to make the lymph swell in fear. Then, Florence will be regenerated—but what good will that do if we’re all dead? And third, and most often repeated: it will happen in our lifetime. He marked that on his thumb until I trembled like the jelly before me. They served jellies, indeed—with the arms of the Albizzi and Acciajoli raised in color—so the world hasn’t turned completely upside down yet. But all they talk about is returning to old ways. Francesco Valori, whom I danced with in Via Larga when he was a bachelor and as loyal to the Medici as anyone, gave a speech about the old times—before Florentines stopped shouting ‘Popolo’ and started shouting ‘Palle’—as if that had anything to do with a wedding! He said we should follow the rules the Signory set centuries ago—what we could and couldn’t wear, eat—how our manners were corrupted, and we read bad books. Though he can’t accuse _me_—”

“Stop, cousin!” Bardo interrupted sharply, needing to speak. Monna Brigida jumped, pursed her lips, and stared at him wide-eyed.

“Francesco Valori is not entirely wrong,” Bardo continued. “Bernardo thinks poorly of him, believing he calls private grudges public zeal. Though I admit Bernardo is too slow to believe in pure patriotism, so clear among the ancients. Still, Tito, our manners have declined somewhat from the noble frugality that, as seen in our citizens’ public deeds, gives birth to true magnificence. Men now spend on a Giostra sums that could fund a library, a lasting gift to mankind. Yet I still believe Florentines hold more of that generous sobriety—hating petty extravagance so they may be grandly generous on grand occasions—than any other Italian city. I’ve heard Neapolitan and Milanese courtiers laugh at our lack of plate, scorn our noble families for borrowing tableware from one another. But in their foolish laughter, wisdom hears half its applause.”

“Laughter, indeed!” Monna Brigida exploded the moment Bardo paused. “If anyone wanted laughter at the wedding, they were disappointed. When young Niccolò Macchiavelli tried a joke, telling stories from Franco Sacchetti’s book—how the Signoria’s laws mean nothing to us women, because we’re cleverer than all painters, architects, and logicians, since we can make black look white, yellow pink, crooked straight, and rename anything forbidden—Holy Virgin! The Piagnoni grew even gloomier, and someone called Sacchetti’s book wicked. Well, I don’t read it—they can’t accuse _me_. Save me from weddings if that’s the fashion! All of us who weren’t Piagnoni felt like wet chickens. I’ve never been trapped worse than once—when I went to hear their precious Frate last Lent in San Lorenzo. Perhaps I never told you, Messer Tito? It nearly freezes my blood to recall it. How he railed at poor women—and men, too, though I didn’t mind that. He called us cows, flesh, wantons, mischief-makers—I could endure that, for others were fleshy and spiteful. But then he came to false hair—O misericordia! He painted a picture—I see it now—of a young woman lying pale and dead, and us light-hearted widows—of course he meant me—rushing to the corpse like bare-headed vultures, cutting off her hair to adorn our aging heads. Oh, the dreams I had after that! Then he cried, wrung his hands, and I wept too. On the way home, I took off my jewels—this very clasp—and packed them away.
