I almost sent them to the Good Men of Saint Martin for the poor, but by heaven’s mercy, I remembered my confessor, Fra Cristoforo, at Santa Croce. He told me it was all the devil’s work—Fra Girolamo’s preaching and prophecy, the Dominicans trying to turn the world upside down. I was never to listen again, or I’d have to do penance. The great preachers Fra Mariano and Fra Menico had shown Fra Girolamo preached lies—and I’d heard them both in the Duomo. And how the Pope’s dream of Saint Francis propping up the Church was still being fulfilled, while the Dominicans began to tear it down. Well and good—I left _con Dio_, and found peace. I won’t be frightened by a Frate Predicatore again. And all I say is, I wish it hadn’t been the Dominicans that poor Dino joined years ago. Then I’d have rejoiced when I heard he’d returned—”

“Silenzio!” Bardo shouted, his voice agitated. Romola half-started from her chair, clasping her hands and turning to Tito as if seeking help. Monna Brigida gasped, biting her lip.

“Donna!” Bardo said again, sternly. “Hear my will. Bring no talk of that name here. And thou, Romola, I forbid thee to ask. My son is dead.”

The old man trembled, his whole body vibrating with emotion. No one dared speak. Monna Brigida shrugged silently, rose quietly, nodded repeatedly to Tito and Romola—warning them not to move—and crept from the room like a guilty spaniel who had barked at the wrong time.

But Tito’s mind raced. Bardo’s son was not truly dead—he was a monk. He had “come back.” And Fra Luca—yes! That face, so like Bardo and Romola, had seemed vaguely familiar. If only he were dead now, at Fiesole! That selfish, desperate wish surged forward. True, Bardo’s rigid will would protect Romola from any contact with her brother—but not from the danger of Tito’s knowledge leaking, especially when Romola’s name was linked with that of Tito Melema, whose description he wore around his neck. Only Fra Luca’s death could end the threat. And death was likely. After the shock, Tito calmed, letting himself rest in the certainty of that hope.

They sat in silence, the room growing darker by the minute, until Romola ventured:

“Shall I light the lamp, father? Shall we continue?”

“No, my Romola,” Bardo said. “We will work no more tonight. Tito, come sit by me.”

Tito moved from the desk and sat beside Bardo, close to his left elbow.

“Come nearer, figliuola mia,” Bardo said after a pause. Romola sat on a low stool, resting her arm on his knee so he could stroke her hair, as he often did.

“Tito,” Bardo began, forgetting his earlier restraint, “I never told you I once had a son. But he left me—he is dead to me. I have disowned him forever. He was a gifted scholar, like you, but fiercer, quicker to rage, yet sometimes lost in himself, like a flame fed by uncertain winds. From the start, he turned from reason and philosophy, bowing to a dim mysticism that defied duty and argument. And so it ended. We will speak no more of him. He is dead to me. I wish his face could vanish from the world of memory, where the distant grows clearer and the near fades.”

Bardo paused. Neither Romola nor Tito dared speak—his voice shook, his composure uncertain. But then he placed his hand gently on Tito’s shoulder and continued, striving for calm.

It was a languid afternoon on the seventh of September, more than two months since Romola and Tito had confessed their love to one another. Tito stepped into Nello’s shop, finding the barber sprawled across his bench, cap pulled low over his eyes, one leg drawn up, the other dangling toward the floor—dragging with it a manuscript volume of verse, its pages crushed beneath the weight of slumber. In the corner, Sandro sat alone, absorbed in a game of *mora*, his fingers moving slowly, deliberately, as if counting time itself.

With quiet steps, Tito picked up the lute and bent over the sleeping man, plucking the strings gently as he sang:

“Quant’ è bella giovinezza,
Che si fugge tuttavia!
Chi vuol esser lieto sia,
Di doman non c’è certezza.”

[“How fair is youth in bloom,
Yet it slips away!
Who would be joyful, let him be,
For tomorrow brings no surety.”]
—A Carnival song by Lorenzo de’ Medici.

Nello stirred instantly, like a bird roused from dreams. His cap flew off, and he sprang upright. “Ah, my Apollino! I’ve overslept my siesta on this sweltering day. That’s what happens when you don’t sleep naturally, but take a draught of potent poetry instead. Hear me—how I begin to echo words with the same first letter, like a Troubadour? It’s one of my ill signs: I fear my fine wine of reason may run out through the spigot of authorship, leaving me an empty cask with only the scent of dregs—like so many brilliant minds I know. What is it, my Orpheus?” He stretched his arms wide, then swept them around Tito, playfully grasping his curls. “What do you want from your well-tamed Nello? I can hear the coaxing tone in your melody. Let me see the smallest thread of your desire, as the great poet says, so I may thread it.”

“That’s just a tailor’s image,” Tito replied, letting his fingers dance lightly across the strings. “But you’ve guessed why I’m so eager to wake you. I need a little extra care—not on my chin, no—but on my hair, which is as tangled as Florence’s politics. You have a way with your comb that pleases the skin and stirs the spirits nicely. And perhaps a touch of your finest orange-scented oil wouldn’t hurt—I’m bound for the Scala palace, where I’ll meet radiant company. Young Cardinal Giovanni de’ Medici will be there, along with Bernardo Dovizi of Bibbiena, whose wit moves so swiftly I can think of no defense except the fragrance of orange blossoms.”

Nello had already seized his comb, guiding Tito backward into the chair and wrapping the cloth about him. “No talk of rivalry, my dear young man. Bernardo Dovizi is sharp enough—like a weasel, though not quite as sly as Ser Piero, whom Piero de’ Medici keeps handy to slip through narrow cracks. But you—you surpass all others. Soon you’ll reach the stars with a single finger. They say you’ve even won over the sour Messer Angelo, who now admits you’re less of an ass than expected—especially given your close friendship with the Secretary.”

“And between us, Nello mio,” Tito said, smiling faintly, “Messer Angelo has more genius and learning than all the other Florentine scholars combined. It’s true they praise me now, while Poliziano is down—grief-stricken, ill, or something. I could spar with Pietro Crinito, a mere sparrow-hawk, but Poliziano—he’s an eagle with a broad beak who’d swallow me whole, feathers and all, and never notice.”

“I won’t argue with your modesty,” Nello said, “but we Florentines don’t live by truth alone. We cry down Dante, praise Francesco Cei—just for variety. And when we call you the new Poliziano, heaven sees to it that it’s not quite the lie it might be. And aren’t you a model of virtue in this wicked city? Your ears are double-waxed against every siren song that might lure you from the Via de’ Bardi and the great work meant to astonish future generations?”

“Future generations, indeed,” Tito answered. “They’ll likely be astonished—as the universe is—by the impossibility of seeing how it was ever planned.”

“Exactly,” Nello said. “That was the very prophecy here the other day. Cristoforo Landino claimed the excellent Bardo is like a knight in heavy armor, toppled over, then angry because he’s been trampled—though I suspect that quote wasn’t his own. For all men who feed you with empty promises and gag you with endless talk, Messer Cristoforo is the pearl. Ecco! You’re perfect now.” He removed the cloth. “Nothing could improve upon this. But love isn’t always fed on learning, is it? I’ll have to style your hair again soon—for the betrothal, perhaps?”

“Perhaps,” Tito replied, smiling. “Unless Messer Bernardo suggests Bardo wants me to yoke a lion and a wild boar to the Zecca’s chariot before I win my Alcestis. Still, he’s right to say I’m unworthy of Romola. She is a Pleiad—she might dim if she marries any mortal.”

*Gnaffè*, Nello said, “your modesty is perfectly placed. Yet fate seems to have carved you for the niche left empty by the old man’s son—who, by the way, Cronaca told me, is now back at San Marco. Did you know?”

A sudden jolt ran through Tito, though he showed nothing. He stooped to pick up the fallen book, smoothing its crumpled pages. “No. I thought he was still at Fiesole. Are you certain he’s returned to San Marco?”

“Cronaca is my source,” Nello said, shrugging. “I don’t frequent that place, but he does. Ah,” he added, taking the book from Tito’s hands, “poor Nencia da Barberino! It pains your scholarly soul to see her pages dog-eared. I fell asleep under the charm of her rustic verses—‘prettier than the turnip-flower,’ ‘with cheeks sweeter than cheese.’ But to truly smell such a contadina, one must lie on velvet cushions in the Via Larga—not go to watch the Fierucoloni trudge into the Piazza della Nunziata after sunset.”

“And who are the Fierucoloni?” Tito asked, adjusting his cap.

“The peasant women from the mountains of Pistoia, Casentino, God knows where—they come to keep vigil at the church of the Nunziata and sell yarn and dried mushrooms at the little Fair, as we call it. A strange sight, with paper lanterns, chanting hymns to the Virgin on this eve of her nativity—if you had the time to see. No? Then I’ve seen enough. There’s chaos in the square. One might get hit by stones without asking for it, and I’ve never liked that kind of attention. Addio.”

Tito left with a quiet uneasiness. His visit ended sooner than expected—the young cardinal Giovanni de’ Medici, later Pope Leo X, had been detained by hunting and failed to appear. Sunset was still half an hour away when Tito stepped from the Scala palace, intending to go straight to the Via de’ Bardi. But scarcely had he walked far when he saw Romola approaching from the Borgo Pinti.

She wore a thick black veil and mantle, yet there was no mistaking her figure or stride. Beside her walked Monna Brigida, her usual finery replaced by plain, unassuming clothes.

Romola had never been raised in piety, and her outings beyond the roof loggia were rare, always carefully arranged—because Bardo disliked being left alone. Tito knew something urgent must have caused this unexpected departure.

She saw him through her veil and quickened her pace.

“Romola,” he said, turning to walk beside her, “has anything happened?”

She did not answer at once. Monna Brigida spoke instead. “Ah, Messer Tito, you do well to turn around—we’re in haste. And isn’t it unfortunate? We must go round by the walls and up the Via del Maglio because of the Fair. The peasants block the road near the Nunziata, which would have taken us to San Marco in half the time.”

Tito’s heart leapt, pounding hard. “Romola,” he whispered, “are you going to San Marco?”

They had passed the Borgo Pinti and were now beneath the city walls, gardens stretching to their left, the air calm and still. Romola lifted her veil to breathe freely, and Tito saw the quiet turmoil in her face.

“Yes, Tito mio,” she said, looking directly at him with sorrowful eyes. “For the first time, I am doing something unknown to my father. I’m glad I met you—now I can tell you. But if you’re going to see him, please don’t say you saw me. He thinks I’m visiting my cousin, who sent for me. I left my godfather with him—he knows where I’m going and why. Do you remember the evening when my brother’s name came up, and my father spoke of him to you?”

“Yes,” Tito murmured.

A storm of emotions surged within him. His hopes sank at the thought of a great change, yet his mind raced through what he might do when it came. And still, he looked at her with a desperate hunger—this might be the last time she gazed at him with full trust.

The cousin had heard he had returned. The night before—San Giovanni’s Eve—he had been seen by Maso near their house. When Maso checked at San Marco, Dino—her brother, baptized Bernardino after their godfather, now known as Fra Luca—had been moved to Fiesole due to illness. But this morning, a message arrived: he was back at San Marco. Maso went to him.

He is gravely ill, and he begged me to come. I cannot refuse, though I hold him guilty. I still remember loving him as a child, before I knew he would abandon my father. Perhaps he has words of repentance for me to carry. It cost me much to act against my father’s wishes—which I’ve always believed just. I’m almost certain you’ll think I’ve done right, Tito, because I’ve noticed your nature is softer than mine. You don’t grow angry easily. Forgiveness comes easier to you. Though—if you had seen your father abandoned by one to whom he gave his deepest love, one in whom he planted all his labor and hope—abandoned when he needed him most—even you, Tito, would find forgiveness difficult.”

What could he say? He couldn’t pretend such betrayals should be forgiven. Nor could he speak against her choice.

“You are right, my Romola. You are always right—except in thinking too well of me.”

There was truth in those words. Tito looked pale, his lip trembling slightly as he spoke. Romola, seeing everything magnified by her high ideals, felt a tearful brightness in her eyes—pleased that he felt so deeply as she did.

Yet she did not pause. “Now, Tito, I ask you to leave us. My cousin and I will be less noticed if we enter the piazza alone.”

“Yes,” Monna Brigida said, “it would be better. To tell the truth, Messer Tito, everyone watches you. No matter how Romola hides herself, people want to see what lies beneath her veil—she walks like a procession. Not that I blame her—only, it doesn’t suit my pace. And honestly, I’d rather not be seen going to San Marco. That’s why I’m dressed like one of the Piagnoni themselves, as old as Sant’ Anna. For if it weren’t poor Dino—someone who ought to be forgiven if dying—what good is holding a grudge against the dead? Make them feel it while they live, I say.”

No one interrupted Monna Brigida. Tito had just kissed Romola’s hand and whispered, “I understand. I obey you.” Then he turned away, lifting his cap—a gesture of reverence rarely made by Florentines, which Bernardo del Nero would scorn as Greek fawning, but which Romola, who cherished honor, found deeply noble.

He was half glad to be dismissed, half wishing to cling to her until the last moment she loved him without suspicion. For he knew—this brother would surely want to know everything. Romola would tell him of her father’s condition, of her own life, of the years that had changed both. She would speak of her coming betrothal to a young scholar meant to fill the role long left vacant by a wandering son.

And when she named the husband… Fra Luca would reveal all he knew, all he suspected. Tito saw no way to lie now—no falsehood could save him.

His future in Florence was over. Messer Bernardo del Nero would rejoice, confirming his advice to delay the betrothal until Tito’s character and standing were proven. And the tale of the young Greek professor whose patron was enslaved would spread beneath every loggia.

For the first time, Tito felt too agitated to control himself. He abandoned his visit to Bardo and wandered beneath the city walls, pacing until the western light faded entirely. Then, without purpose, he turned down the first street—the Via San Sebastiano—leading directly toward the Piazza dell’Annunziata.

He stood at one of those reckless moments when desire rules, and the path ahead suddenly closes. He was ready to follow any impulse that offered him an immediate purpose.

CHAPTER XIV. The Peasants’ Fair.

The moment Tito stepped into the piazza, the crush of bodies and the jumble of sounds—shouts, laughter, music, and sudden crashes—flooded his senses. It reminded him of Nello’s talk about the Fierucoloni, those wild country fairs that drew crowds from the hills and valleys. He pushed through the throng with a strange kind of pleasure in the chaos: the shoves, the cries, the confusion. For a while, it dulled the heavy weight of thought that had settled on him—the dread of wandering alone, searching for some uncertain fortune, perhaps even going to seek Baldassarre again. But here, in this uproar, he could forget, just for a time.

At each entrance, people surged into the square, their movements matched by paper lanterns swaying above them like ghosts on poles. A slow, rhythmic chant rose above the din, a steady thread beneath the storm of noise—children’s jeers, drumbeats, bells ringing sharply, and every now and then, a lantern shattered by a stone thrown in reckless play. A scream followed, then more shouting. Yet at the edges of the crowd, others kept the night’s observance with more dignity. Ragged men with sharp eyes called out, inviting villagers to gamble fairly; two figures in masks, balanced on stilts, danced with stolen lanterns, flickering like fireflies across the dark. Near the loggia of the Foundling Hospital (Spedale degl’ Innocenti), a vendor sold hot *berlingozzi*, a favorite flour-based treat, while another man stood atop a barrel, advertising pills from Salerno—guaranteed to stop toothache and drownings alike. Not far away, a tumbler performed tricks on a small platform, and a group of apprentices, bored with throwing stones, played a quiet game of *bocce* near the narrow gate of Via de’ Febbrai.

Tito, forced to weave through gaps in the press, found himself suddenly beside a procession of barefoot peasant women. Their sun-bronzed faces were worn, their clothes ragged and faded, stitched from old fabrics, so odd and dusty they seemed like relics from a forgotten age. To city folk, they looked like ancestors returning from a pilgrimage begun long before memory. These were the hardy women of Pistoia’s mountains, carrying the year’s labor in bundles of yarn on their backs, hearts full of faint hope and deeper fear—hope for mercy, fear of harm—both laid at the feet of the Blessed Virgin, whose miraculous image, said to have been painted by angels, would be unveiled tonight, on the Eve of her Nativity, to let its power flow unobstructed.

Then, swept sideways by the current of the crowd, Tito reached the quieter edge where vendors had set up shop. Among them was Bratti, whom Tito recognized at once. He leaned against a pillar, mouth pursed in disdain, eyes scanning the passersby with cold superiority. His hand rested firmly on a serge cloth covering a basket slung before him. Tito was surprised—Bratti never acted so aloof. As two women approached curiously, Bratti pulled the cloth tighter and turned away.

“Before I tell you what’s inside,” he said, “I must know if you’re willing to buy. I can’t show such goods to every fly that lands and expects nothing. My wares are too fine for that. Besides, I’ve only two left—and I won me not to soil them. With the pestilence whispered about, these may soon be worth their weight in gold. No, no—*andate con Dio*.”

The women exchanged glances.

“And how much?” asked one.

“Not within your purse, buona donna,” Bratti replied, voice laced with pitying scorn. “I suggest you trust Messer Domeneddio and the saints. Poor folks do no better.”

“Not so poor!” snapped the second woman, pulling out her purse. “Come now—what do you say to a grosso?”

“Twenty-one quattrini,” Bratti said coolly. “But not from me—I haven’t that change.”

“Two, then?” she insisted, growing impatient, while her companion watched with envy.

After more haggling, Bratti finally relented.

“Since you’ve set your mind on it,” he said slowly, lifting the cover, “I’d hate to bring you misfortune. Maestro Gabbadeo used to say, when a woman wants something and doesn’t get it, she’s more at risk of the plague than ever. Ecco! I have only two left—and let me tell you, the other is on Maestro Gabbadeo’s finger. He’s gone to Bologna—a wise doctor, the best at any door.”

The items were two crude iron rings, shaped like ancient Roman ones, often dug from the earth. Rust clung to them, and their mystery seemed real enough that both women paid without complaint. One, lacking coins, bartered a bundle of yarn and walked off with her prize, triumphant. Bratti covered his basket—now filled with odds and ends, likely traded under similar conditions—and moved from his post, colliding with Tito, who wished he’d had time to slip away unnoticed.

“By San Giovanni’s head!” Bratti exclaimed, pulling Tito back to the pillar. “This is luck! I was talking of you this morning, Messer Greco. Said, ‘He’s risen among the signori now.’ And I’m glad—though I helped lift him there. But I rarely see him anymore. He’s not lying on the stones, as he once did. But it’s your luck, not mine—except for a small token, to repay me for my trouble.”

“You speak in riddles, Bratti,” Tito said. “I don me sharpen my mind like you do over iron rings. Be plain.”

“By the Holy Gospels! That bargain was easy. If a Hebrew gets thirty-two percent, I expect a Christian may take a little more. I could’ve gotten twice the money and twice the yarn—if I hadn’t borne conscience. But speaking of rings—your ring, the one on your finger—that I can get you a buyer for. A good one. A buyer with deep pockets.”

“Truly?” Tito asked, staring at his ring.

“A Genoese heading straight to Hungary, as I understand. Came into my shop, looking for old things—things I didn’t know the value of. He thought I had a pumpkin on my shoulders. He’d been hunting every shop in Florence. He wore a ring—not like yours, but similar. When we talked of rings, I said, ‘I know a young man—my close friend—who has one just like it.’ He said, ‘Who is he? Tell him I’ll give him fair price.’ I meant to go to Nello’s tomorrow to find you—but now, since I’ve met you, it’s fate. You’re not the sort to watch the Arno turn to broth and stand by.”

Tito had listened closely, though his thoughts raced. Why keep the ring? It had been sentiment, mere fancy, that stopped him from selling it with the others. Had he done so, he might have avoided Fra Luca’s recognition. Yes, it had once belonged to Baldassarre, placed on his own finger when his hand grew large enough. But what real good did it do anyone to cling to such superstitions? The ring had led to recognition. And Tito began to resent recognition—it was the past claiming him. This foreigner’s offer, if genuine, was a chance to rid himself of the ring without chasing a buyer.

“You speak wisely, Bratti,” Tito said. “I’ll hear what the Genoese offers. When and where shall I meet him?”

“Tomorrow, three hours after sunrise—at my shop. And if your wit is as sharp as I’ve always thought, Messer Greco, ask a high price. He cares not for money. I believe he buys for someone else—perhaps a great lord.”

“It is well,” Tito said. “I’ll come, if nothing prevents.”

“And you’ll treat me fairly, for old friendship’s sake,” Bratti added. “No need to fix the sum now. Seems like a thousand years till I leave this piazza. A fair is dull, even wicked, when you’ve no goods to sell.”

Tito nodded quickly and moved away. The crowd pushed him forward, then back, helpless to control his path. In this way, he drifted to the far end of the piazza, opposite the church, where a spectacle drew the crowd’s attention. Laughter burst out, interrupting a speaker’s rambling monologue—sometimes slow and solemn, sometimes frantic and silly. A girl was shoved into the center, unwillingly; another, bold and giggling, fought her way in with elbows. The light was dim—stars above, lanterns below, casting shadows and blurring shapes. But in this alcove, a stronger glow lit the scene. Spectators’ heads appeared in silhouette as Tito was pressed closer. Above them loomed a man wearing a white mitre adorned with yellow cabalistic symbols.

“Behold, my children!” he declared. “Behold your chance! Do not neglect the holy sacrament of matrimony—for only a white quattrino! The cheapest marriage ever offered, and dissolvable by special bull at any man’s will. Behold the bull!” He held up a parchment with seals attached. “Behold the indulgence granted by His Holiness Alexander the Sixth, newly elected for his piety, who intends to purify the Church—beginning by ending priestly monopolies on marriage. Spit once, my sons, and pay a white quattrino! That is all. The quattrino is the only difference the Holy Father allows between us and the clergy—who spit and pay nothing!”

Tito thought he knew the voice—sharp, nasal, familiar. But the face was hidden in shadow. Stepping nearer, he saw an altar-like table draped in red, stitched with golden symbols. Half-a-dozen candles burned behind it, and scattered across it lay conjuring tools, a large open book, and at one corner, a monkey tied to a ring, holding a taper that wobbled constantly. An impish boy in a surplice cuffed the monkey and adjusted the candle. The man wore a surplice and a chasuble marked with the zodiac. Now Tito was certain: it was Maestro Vaiano, the mountebank from whom he had rescued Tessa. Little Tessa—had she come with the peasants?

“Come, my maidens!” the conjuror shouted. “Now is the time for the pretty, who may have many chances, and for the plain, who have few. Marriage—hot, eaten, and done with as easily as *berlingozzi*! And see!” He raised a cluster of tiny bags. “To every bride, a *Breve*—a secret inside. The secret alone is worth the price. But I won’t tell you what it is—that makes it double-secret. Hang it round your neck, never look at it. Perhaps that’s best—you’ll see things you don’t expect. Though if you open it, you may break your leg, *è vero*, but you’ll know a secret! Something no one else knows! And mark—I give you the *Breve*, I don’t sell it—as many holy men do. The quattrino is for the marriage. The *Breve* is free. *Orsù, giovanetti*, come like dutiful sons of the Church and buy the Indulgence of His Holiness Alexander the Sixth!”

The crowd loved it. The *fierucola* was small, so the townspeople accepted jokes less indecent than those at carnival. The women, especially, were eager for the *Brevi*. Several couples had already undergone the ceremony—mock vows, grimaces, the monkey’s antics, even spitting—each drawing laughter. Now a young man in a red cap and loose tunic pushed forward, holding a plump brunette in ragged dress, her arms and legs exposed in a vivid contrast.

“Fetter us quickly, Maestro!” he said. “I must take my bride home and paint her by lantern-light.”

“Ha! Mariotto, my son,” the conjuror began, “I commend your pious haste…”

But a sudden screech from behind warned of trouble. The monkey, annoyed by the boy’s cuffing, leapt violently when the taper tilted. The knot loosened, and he jumped to the other end of the table, growling fiercely. The boy grabbed a wand. The monkey sprang onto a tall woman’s head, dropping the taper, and chattered wildly. Panic broke out—many feared the monkey carried hidden mischief. The conjuror himself flinched, worried for his pet. In the struggle to catch the string, Tito slipped out of the circle. Not wanting to fight his way back, he let the crowd push him toward the church of the Nunziata, slipping in among the worshippers.

Inside, the light dazzled—golden, thick, almost tangible after the pale glow outside. For a moment, Tito saw nothing clearly. The incense mingled with breath, creating a thin yellow haze. To the peasant women, unused to such brilliance—whose skies were often blocked by mountains—this was divine. The choir’s chant was a soothing balm after the pandemonium outside. Slowly, the scene resolved. On the left, in a chapel wreathed with silver lamps, the miraculous fresco of the Annunciation was unveiled. From Tito’s angle, it seemed darkened by the surrounding radiance. The church was packed with women—some kneeling, some standing. Mountain dwellers, coarse-skinned and dressed in grime, stood beside valley folk, softer-faced, draped in white or red. Above them, the walls and ceiling teemed with votive wax figures—effigies of popes, emperors, kings, condottieri, Florentine lords in black silk, even Muslims, all in their living attire. Some were cracked and blackened by time, others fresh and bright—exact duplicates of the living. Arms, legs, and body parts were wedged in among them, except where images had been removed in disgrace or fallen—like Lorenzo’s six months earlier. It was a resurrection of the dead, mirrored in the crowd below—darkness and light, decay and renewal.

Tito scanned the crowd, searching. He had thought of Tessa. The white hoods made him wonder if he might find her beneath one. Better than seeing Romola’s changed eyes. But he found no trace. Disheartened, he turned to leave—when, just by the doorway, he saw her. Only two yards away. She knelt, back against the wall, behind a group of women waiting to approach the sacred image. Her head hung slightly, eyes half-closed, lost in weariness. Her blue eyes drifted toward an altar-piece showing Archangel Michael—young, armored, surrounded by saints. In her right hand, she held a bunch of cocoons, dangling listlessly. Her cheek was pale, lips pressed together in a pout, eyelids fluttering. She looked like a large, sweet child asleep.

Tito felt an overwhelming urge to go to her—to feel her innocent trust, her gentle chatter. This child, without judgment, without suspicion, offered a refuge from the isolation he feared. He glanced around—Monna Ghita was nowhere near. Quietly, he knelt beside her and whispered, “Tessa!”

She barely stirred, as if a soft breeze touched her. She turned, saw his face—closer than the Archangel’s, more beautiful, more real. She smiled, silent and happy. That closeness filled her world.

“My little Tessa,” he said. “You look very tired. How long have you been here?”

She paused, gathering her thoughts.

“I’m very hungry.”

“Come then. Come with me.”

He lifted her gently and led her out under the cloisters—open now, untouched by Andrea del Sarto’s frescoes.

“How are you alone, and so hungry, Tessa?”

“The Madre is ill. Bad pains in her legs. She sent me with these cocoons to the Santissima Nunziata—they’re wonderful, see?” She held up the bundle, arranged neatly on a stem. “She saved them to bring herself, but couldn’t. So she sent me—she thinks the Holy Madonna will take away her pain. And someone took my bag with bread and chestnuts. The crowd pushed me back. I was scared. I couldn’t get near the Madonna to give the cocoons to the Padre—but I must. Oh, I must.”

“Yes, my little Tessa,” Tito said softly. “You shall. But first, let me give you some *berlingozzi*. There’s a stall nearby.”

“Where did you come from?” she asked, confused. “I thought you’d never come back. You never came to the Mercato for milk. I prayed Aves, hoping they’d bring you back—but I stopped. They didn’t.”

“You see,” Tito said, “I come when you need someone to care for you. Perhaps the Aves brought me—it just took a long time. But what will you do if you’re here alone? Where will you go?”

“Oh,” she said, “I’ll stay and sleep in the church. Many do. I did once, with my mother. The *patrigno* is coming with the mules in the morning.”

When Romola reached the entrance of San Marco, a friar stood waiting, as if expecting her. Monna Brigida withdrew into the adjacent church, and Romola was led to the door of the chapter-house in the outer cloister—where the sick brother had been brought. Women were not permitted beyond this threshold. As the door opened, the dim light from outside mingled with the soft glow of two candles placed behind a low bed. There, propped up nearly upright, lay Fra Luca. His face was thin and drawn, his golden hair shaved in the monk’s tonsure, and his deep-set hazel eyes fixed upon a small crucifix held before him. In the quiet stillness, Romola felt a sudden awareness: another monk stood beside the bed, hooded and silent, moving toward the door as she entered. Behind them, on the frescoed wall, loomed the pale figure of Christ on the cross, flanked by sorrowful faces that seemed to watch in silence.

Then, their eyes met—Fra Luca’s rising from the crucifix to meet hers. And in that instant, recognition struck Romola like a wound. This frail, robed figure was Dino—her brother, once so fair and young. “Dino!” she whispered, the word escaping her lips like a cry of pain. Yet she did not move forward. She remained standing, rigid, at arm’s length. A deep repulsion rose within her—a revulsion not just for the monkish garb, but for what it symbolized: the abandonment of duty, the surrender to a faith that called betrayal piety. Her father, a man of proud honesty and simple virtue, had raised her apart from the Church, treating its claims as irrelevant to reason and conscience. To her, religion belonged to the noisy, mixed world they had always avoided. And so, her brother’s choice—this flight into monastic life—had long been a source of cold contempt.

Yet now, though she stood aloof, her gaze searched his face with a hidden longing. Was there any trace of the boy she once knew? Any echo of warmth or regret?

But there was none. He looked at her—not with affection, but with the distant gaze of one who sees only shadows from beyond the veil. “My sister,” he murmured, voice weak yet clear, “it is well you have come. I have a message for you—and time is short.”

Romola took a step closer. Perhaps, she thought, he would speak of their father. Perhaps he would express remorse, beg forgiveness. She had no hope of erasing the years of desertion—but if he acknowledged the wrong, if he spoke of their father’s suffering, then pity might soften her heart. She imagined him asking about his father’s blindness, how he endured the darkness, what life was like without him. She waited for words of tenderness.

Instead, Fra Luca said, “Romola, I have seen a vision. Three times in the past two months—each clearer than the last. I came from Fiesole because I believed it was a message from heaven. And now I know my breath is spared only to deliver it—to you.”

His breathing faltered, cutting off the sentence. Romola’s heart chilled. A *vision*—the same kind her father had mocked so bitterly. Anger surged within her. “Dino,” she said sharply, “I thought you had something to say to my father. You left him when he lost his sight—you made his life lonely and empty. Have you never felt guilt? Never regretted it? What kind of religion makes visions more important than human duty?”

The monk turned his eyes slowly toward her. For a moment, he seemed to weigh whether to answer. Then, softly, almost without emotion, as if speaking through a dying shell, he said: “No. I have never repented fleeing from the poison of sin that choked me. It was thick around me—hot, suffocating. My father could not hear the voice that called me day and night. He did not see the demons trying to pull me back. He lived among sin and suffering, yet denied their reality—as if picking gems in a mine while a plague raged above. I warned him, but he scorned me. I told him the studies he wanted me to pursue—history, poetry—were either childish trifles or else poisoned by worldly ambition. They fed on lust, on pride. All the beauty he admired was shaped by desires that corrupted the soul.”

Romola could not remain silent. “But has not my father lived a pure and noble life? He sought no honors, he lied to no one, he denied himself every luxury. He lived like an ancient sage. He never asked you to chase power or pleasure—he asked you to live as he did, guided by reason and philosophy!”

She spoke with conviction, her cheeks flushed, her body trembling. But Fra Luca remained unmoved. His eyes, calm and distant, studied her passion. “What good were your father’s maxims to me?” he replied. “They told me to be strong when I felt broken. When I longed, like Saint Benedict, to roll in thorns, to welcome pain as freedom. Because the Divine love had found me—had filled me with a hunger no world could satisfy. Like a seed needing space to grow. I had been raised without true faith. I hadn’t studied our doctrine—yet it seized me like a flood. I saw a life of perfect love, where no desire tormented, no fear haunted, no question divided the soul. Before I knew the saints, I sensed their joy. Even pagan philosophy hinted at it: that man can die to earthly needs and live in God’s eternal peace.

“But to reach that, I must leave the world. I must renounce all ties—love, hope, attachment. I must live among men only as souls bound to the unseen. That need consumed me. It came in visions, when my mind grew weary of reading dead men’s passions. It came after I fell into sin and recoiled from the taste of emptiness. And in those visions, I saw the meaning of the Cross.”

He paused, gasping. Romola did not speak. She could not bridge the chasm between their worlds—between her rational soul and his spiritual fire.

When he resumed, his chest was calmer. “I knew whom I must follow. But I saw that even among the servants of Christ, I would be suffocated by hypocrisy, pride, lust. God did not call me to fight evil within the Church, as He called Dominic or Francis. He called me to flee. So I took vows and fled—to lands where danger, scorn, and poverty drove me ever closer to God. I lived as a hermit, served pilgrims. But my task was brief. The veil between me and eternity grows thin. I returned to Florence to—”

“Dino,” Romola interrupted, her voice softer now, touched by the image of her father alone in darkness, “you *did* want to know if he was alive, didn’t you?”

“—To urge my brothers to study the Eastern tongues, to go farther than I did. And I find they are already doing so. But since I came, Romola, I have felt I was sent to you—not to renew earthly bonds, but to deliver a heavenly warning. I have seen the vision three times. And for years, since the first call, I have been guided by visions. For in waking thought, we mix our own errors with truth. But in dreams and visions, we are passive—our souls become instruments in God’s hand. Listen now. Speak no more. Time is short.”

Romola resisted. She did not believe in such things. But the weight of his words, the certainty in his voice, pressed upon her. Still, she refused to listen.

Then, from the shadowed doorway, another monk stepped in. He stood on the other side of the bed, hood drawn over his head. “Kneel, my daughter,” he said, his voice rich and commanding, yet gentle. “The Angel of Death is present. He waits while heaven’s message is delivered. Bend your pride—before it is broken by iron.”

Romola turned. The speaker’s face was hidden beneath the cowl. But her eyes fell on his hands—folded across his chest, delicate, almost translucent, like carved ivory. They carried a strange authority. She wanted to resist—monks had always been figures of mockery in her world. But the hands seemed to plead, not command.

Then, the right hand gently took the crucifix from Fra Luca’s grasp. The left dipped a wet sponge and touched the dying man’s lips. As he bent, the cowl slipped back.

And Romola saw his face.

It was the face of Fra Girolamo Savonarola, Prior of San Marco—the man she had heard of, the one she had judged harshly for his loud sermons, his fanatical zeal. But now, in the candlelight, his features were striking: high nose, firm jaw, dark brows, blue-grey eyes under auburn lashes—eyes that held both fire and gentleness. And again, the voice spoke: “Kneel, my daughter. The pride of the body blocks the gifts that purify the soul.”

Romola hesitated. Then, slowly, she sank to her knees. As she did, a tremor ran through her. Something shifted—not just posture, but spirit. She felt herself yield, not to force, but to a deeper presence.

Fra Luca began again, his voice fading with each breath. “Romola… I saw your father’s room—the library—books, marbles, the leggio where I read. And I saw you—your hair long and fair—sitting before his chair. At the legpto stood a man whose face I could not see. I looked… and it was blank, like a painted face erased. Then he took your hand. And you took your father’s hand. Together, you descended the stone steps into the streets—this man leading. You stood at the altar in Santa Croce. The priest who married you had the face of death. Graves opened. Dead men in shrouds followed you like a bridal train.

“You walked through the city, through the gates, into the valley. The man hurried you faster than you could bear. The dead grew tired and turned back. At last, you reached a barren place—no water, no trees, no grass. Instead of water, parchment unrolled everywhere. Instead of plants, bronze and marble figures rose around you. Your father fell, fainting from thirst. The man released your hand and left. As he walked away, I saw his face—and it was the Great Tempter.

“You wrung your hands, searching for water. None. The statues offered cups—but when you grasped them, they turned to parchment. Then they became demons, snatching your father’s body. The parchments shriveled. Blood flowed instead. Fire burned the blood. Everything vanished. The plain was bare and stony. You were alone.

“And then—night fell. I saw no more.”

He paused. Longer now. Then, barely audible: “Thrice I have seen this, Romola. I believe it is meant for you. A warning against marriage—against the enemy’s temptation. It calls you to dedicate yourself—”

His breath failed. He gasped, eyes fixed on the crucifix as if it were the last light. After a moment, he managed a whisper: “Renounce the vain philosophy of the heathens. Their pride will turn to mockery in sorrow. Their gods—”

The words died.

Romola sat in silence. She had dismissed visions as mere dreams—products of memory and imagination. But this one, though grotesque, stirred her deeply. Its images were cruel, unbearable. And she had never faced death before—not truly. This brother, returning in agony, was like a ghost from another world. The mournful faces on the fresco seemed to lean closer, sharing his fate.

“Frate,” the dying voice whispered.

Fra Girolamo leaned down. No reply came.

“Romola,” he breathed.

She leaned forward. Silence.

“Fra Girolamo… give her—”

“The crucifix,” said Fra Girolamo.

No sound followed.

“Dino!” Romola cried, the name torn from her throat. She knew now—there would be no understanding, no reconciliation.

“Take the crucifix, my daughter,” Fra Girolamo said after a pause. “His eyes see it no longer.”

Romola reached out. Her fingers closed around the cold wood. And in that touch, a great sob burst from her. She bowed her head beside her brother’s body and wept openly. It felt as though the world had changed forever. The light itself seemed altered.

Fra Girolamo moved to the door and summoned a lay brother waiting outside. Then he approached Romola. “Rise, my daughter. Be comforted. Our brother is with the blessed. He leaves you the crucifix—as a reminder of the warning. May it be a beacon in the darkness.”

She rose, trembling, wrapped her veil tightly, and hid the crucifix beneath her cloak. Fra Girolamo led her out into the cloistered court, now lit only by stars and a lantern held near the gate. Several figures in fine robes stood nearby—visitors to the Prior, who had waited upon hearing he was with the dying man.

Romola was half-aware of footsteps, rustling garments. She heard Fra Girolamo murmur, “Our brother is departed.” A hand touched her arm. Then the door opened, and she stepped into the wide piazza of San Marco—alone with Monna Brigida and the servant carrying the lantern.

The open air revived her. The terrible scene receded, though it remained vivid. She quickened her pace, eager to return to her father—and to Tito. Were they together? Had anything changed? The images of the vision clung to her like a nightmare, yet they only deepened her longing for the living voices that would confirm her reality.

Tito, we know, was not with Bardo. His future was being shaped by guilt—the crushing belief that Romola now knew the truth, the truth that would end their bond. And now, the lips that could have spoken it were sealed.

The revelation Fra Luca had offered came not from love or family, but from a realm beyond human feeling. The wisdom that might have come from simple questions—about father, brother, home—was buried in silence. And Romola, standing in the moonlit square, carried with her not just grief, but a haunting uncertainty—whether the world she knew was still safe, or whether the future was already written in blood and parchment.

Early the next morning, Tito returned from Bratti’s shop in the narrow lane of the Ferravecchi. The Genoese stranger had taken the onyx ring, and Tito carried away fifty florins. For a fleeting moment, he wondered whether Fortune—by one of her cunning turns—might spare him the need to leave Florence. If so, it would have been better not to have parted with the ring, since it was known he wore it for reasons of deep personal significance. But such thoughts were slight, scarcely worth dwelling on; and at that moment, he no longer trusted Fortune. The sharp anxiety of the first alarm had faded into a dull, heavy regret. He valued the approval of others so deeply that now he wished he had never shied away from what they called duty—never risked shame by refusing their expectations.

Yet our actions are like children born to us: once they come into being, they live apart from our will. Children may be silenced or even killed, but deeds cannot be undone. They possess an unyielding life, both within and beyond our awareness—and this relentless vitality of past choices was pressing upon Tito for the first time.

He walked back toward his lodgings in the Piazza di San Giovanni, avoiding the Mercato Vecchio, though it was the shortest route. He did not want to see Tessa. He wasn’t in the mood to seek anything; he could only wait for the first sign of change in his fate.

The piazza shimmered under the warm autumn sunlight, the kind that lingers after dew has kissed the stones and invites quiet contemplation without fatigue. It was a festival day—the Nativity of the Virgin—and the air felt thick with soft warmth, as if urging people to linger and look. Signs of the fair dotted the square: stalls spread with fruit and flowers near the octagonal baptistery, mules laden with goods quietly chewing from their nose-bags while their drivers slipped inside the sacred doors to kneel before the Virgin. On the broad marble steps of the Duomo, groups gathered—beggars, talkers, and idle watchers. An old woman with sunburnt skin and white hair coaxed a barefoot child to walk on the warmed stone, while a dog nearby sniffed warily at the spectacle. Two rough-haired boys leaned over to watch a pale, crippled boy carve a face into a cherry pit. Above them, men stood on the platform, making knots in ropes or arguing fiercely in pairs.

But the most crowded spot was where Tito needed to go—the barber’s shop of Nello. It was peak hour, and during the warm season, many preferred to be shaved under the red-and-white awning outside rather than in the cramped interior. There is little dignity in sitting with a lathered chin tilted back, your nose used as a handle—but shaving was a mark of Florentine respectability, and men took themselves seriously when they all followed the same custom. This was also the time when yesterday’s gossip was freshest, and Nello’s tongue ran as smoothly as his razor. The two instruments seemed to move in tandem, driven by the same spring. Tito knew he couldn’t escape the circle—he would have to smile, respond, appear perfectly at ease. Well, it was only another trial, like swallowing bread and cheese pills. Anyone who let fear of discovery paralyze him was simply weak-nerved.

Just then, a hand landed on his shoulder. No amount of resolve could prevent the jolt of unease that shot through him. His face betrayed the shock as he turned—until the man who had touched him laughed lightly. He was a young man about Tito’s age, sharp-featured, with closely cropped hair and a clean-shaven face, giving the impression of a mind stripped bare of unnecessary matter. His eyes sparkled with hope and friendliness—like so many others who, years later, would close in bitterness. At the time, however, Niccolò Macchiavelli was seen as a promising youth, destined to restore his family’s fallen fortune.

“Why, Melema,” said the young man, “what terrible dream did you have last night that made my light touch feel like that of a _sbirro_ or worse?”

“Ah, Messer Niccolò,” replied Tito, regaining composure quickly, “it must have been extra sluggishness in my veins this morning that made me flinch at your wit. But truly, I’ve had a bad night.”

“That’s unfortunate,” said Macchiavelli. “You’ll be expected to shine today at the Rucellai Gardens. I assume you’re going.”

“Messer Bernardo honored me with an invitation,” Tito answered, “but I shall be elsewhere.”

“Oh? I remember—you’re in love,” said Macchiavelli, shrugging. “Otherwise, you’d never have such inconvenient engagements. We’re to eat peacock and ortolans under the loggia among Bernardo Rucellai’s rare trees. The finest minds in Florence, the best wines. Only, since Piero de’ Medici is coming, we may find ourselves drowned in impromptu poetry contests. I hate that game—it’s a trick for small minds, who always get inspired by the smallest occasions.”

“What do you mean about Piero de’ Medici and small wits, Messer Niccolò?” asked Nello, whose slender figure now dominated the space beside the bulky Niccolò Caparra.

The famous ironworker—last seen in the Mercato Vecchio with bared arms and leather apron—was dressed in festive clothes today. As he sat submissively while Nello skipped around him, lathering his face, gripping his nose, and shaving him with swift precision, he looked much like a lion who had donned linen and a tunic to enter society.

“A private secretary won’t rise far if he mixes great and small so freely,” Nello continued. “When great men can’t marry their children as they wish, small men shouldn’t expect to marry their words as they please. Have you heard the news Domenico Cennini has been telling us—that Pagolantonio Soderini slapped Ser Piero da Bibbiena for encouraging Piero de’ Medici to interfere with the marriage between young Tommaso Soderini and Fiammetta Strozzi? And now he’s to be sent as ambassador to Venice as punishment?”

“I don’t know which I envy more,” said Macchiavelli, “the offense or the punishment. The offense will make him the most popular man in Florence. The punishment will send him to the only people in Italy who’ve ever managed their own affairs.”

“If Soderini stays long enough in Venice,” added Cennini, “he might learn their ways and bring them home. The Soderini have long been allies of the Medici, but this event may open Pagolantonio’s eyes to the old Florentine habit of changing harness when the old one chafes. We haven’t quite lost that knack in fifty years.”

“No, we haven’t,” said Caparra, now free to speak again. “Eat eggs in Lent, and the snow will melt. That’s what I tell our people when they grow loud at San Gallo and talk of starting a _romor_. Never plan a revolt—it’s like trying to fill the Arno with buckets. When there’s enough water, the river fills itself. And that won’t happen until the torrent is ready.”

“Caparra,” said Nello, “your oracle-like wisdom comes from my excellent shave. You couldn’t have spoken so wisely with that dark stubble on your chin. Ecco, Messer Domenico—I’m ready for you now. By the way, my learned friend,” he added, seeing Tito move toward the door, “old Maso came looking for you, but your room was empty. He’ll return soon. He looked troubled, and hurried. I hope nothing’s wrong in the Via de’ Bardi.”

“Doubtless Messer Tito knows that Bardo’s son is dead,” said Cronaca, who had just arrived.

Tito’s heart leapt—had the death occurred before Romola saw him?

“No, I hadn’t heard,” he said, calm as the situation allowed, turning and leaning against the doorpost as if he’d abandoned his intention to leave. “I knew his sister had gone to visit him. Did he die before she arrived?”

“No,” said Cronaca. “I was at San Marco at the time and saw her emerge from the chapter-house with Fra Girolamo, who told us the dying man’s breath had been miraculously preserved so he could make a confession to his sister.”

Tito felt his fate sealed. His mind raced through every detail of his departure from Florence, and a plan formed: recover his money from Cennini before the revelation became public. With the money, he wouldn’t need to endure scathing glances or cruel words for long. He would wait, then leave with Cennini and reclaim his funds immediately. With that thought, he stood still—hands tucked in his belt, eyes fixed absentmindedly on the ground.

Nello glanced at him and sensed he was consumed by worry over Romola. He found Tito’s expression so touching—a picture of self-forgetful sorrow—that he subtly pointed his razor at him and gave a challenging look to Piero di Cosimo, whom he had never forgiven for dismissing any character reading in his favorite’s handsome face. Piero, leaning near Tito, shrugged. Nello’s repeated challenges had transformed the painter’s neutrality into a quiet skepticism about the praised Greek.

“So you’ve got your Fra Girolamo back, Cronaca?” said Nello. “I suppose we’ll have him preaching again this Advent?”

“And not before it’s needed,” said Cronaca gravely. “We’ve had proof of his words since the last Quaresima. Even wickedness has become a plague. Vice is rotting in the nostrils of the very wicked. Nothing has changed in Rome or Florence since then that doesn’t confirm the Frate’s warning: the harvest of sin is ripe, and God will reap it with a sword.”

“I hope he’s had a new vision,” said Francesco Cei, sneering. “The old ones are stale. Can’t your monk get a poet to help him imagine?”

“He has poets around him,” replied Cronaca coldly, “but they’re great poets, not petty ones. They’re content to learn from him, and don’t think truth stale because God gave it to him—just as they don’t think moonlight stale. But perhaps certain high prelates and princes who dislike the Frate’s warnings would be pleased to hear that while Giovanni Pico, Poliziano, Marsilio Ficino, and most notable men in Florence revere Fra Girolamo, Messer Francesco Cei despises him.”

“Poliziano?” Cei scoffed. “Of course he believes in your new Jonah—look at the fine orations he wrote for Sienna’s envoys to tell Alexander VI that the world and Church had never been better off since he became Pope.”

“Francesco,” said Macchiavelli, smiling, “a scholar of many parts must hold many opinions. As for the Frate, whatever we think of his holiness, you judge his preaching too narrowly. The secret of oratory isn’t in saying new things, but in saying things with such power that they move the listener. Without that, as Filelfo said, your speaker deserves to be called ‘not an orator, but a ploughman.’ By that measure, Fra Girolamo is a great orator.”

“That’s true,” said Cennini, speaking from the chair. “But part of the secret lies in prophetic visions. Our people—no offense, Cronaca—will follow anyone who looks like a prophet, especially if he predicts disasters.”

“Rather say,” replied Cronaca, “that the real secret lies in the Frate’s pure life and strong faith, which mark him as a messenger of God.”

“I admit it,” said Cennini, raising his palms as he rose. “His life is blameless—no one has accused it.”

“He’s satisfied with the pleasure of arrogance,” burst out Cei bitterly. “I see it in his proud lip and smug eye. He hears the air filled with his name—Fra Girolamo Savonarola of Ferrara, the prophet, the saint, the mighty preacher who frightens Florence’s babies into dropping their toys.”

“Come, come, Francesco,” said Nello soothingly. “Let me stop your mouth with a bit of lather. I won’t let my friend Cronaca grow angry—I care for his chin, and it hasn’t changed since he became a Piagnone. As for me, I confess: when the Frate preached in the Duomo last Advent, I fell into the habit of slipping in to listen. I might have become a Piagnone myself—if not for the generosity of my trade, and the length of the sermons, which sometimes take ages to reach the emotional point. But as Messer Niccolò says, the Frate holds the people by some power beyond prophecy. Monks who prophesy aren’t rare. As Luigi Pulci said: ‘Dombruno’s scimitar was said to be enchanted—but I suspect it cut well because it was forged from hard steel.’ Yes, yes—paternosters may shave cleanly, but only if said over a good razor.”

“See, Nello!” said Macchiavelli. “What doctor approaches on his Bucephalus? I thought your piazza was free of those furred, scarlet-robed servants of death. This man looks as if he’d had a night like Boccaccio’s Maestro Simone—his hat and cloak soaked in gutter water—though he himself is sleek as a miller’s rat.”

“A–ah!” said Nello, drawing out the syllable as he looked up. A round-headed, round-bodied man rode a young, stubborn horse that kept its head high and resisted commands, constantly trying to veer sideways. Its attitude suggested views on authority far ahead of its time.

“And I’ve prepared a few surprises for him,” Nello whispered. “I hope they’ll drive his curious nose elsewhere. He’s a doctor from Padua—they say he stayed three months in Prato, now he’s come to Florence to see what he can catch. His specialty is visiting peasants. Note those large saddlebags—they’re meant to carry the fat capons, eggs, and flour he collects from poor farmers who lack coin. He sells his own remedies, so he avoids apothecaries. For the past week, he’s sat in my piazza daily, attracting asthmatics and crying infants. It infuriates me to see the toad-faced quack handling greasy coins or accepting a pigeon in exchange for his pills. But I’ll put thorns in his saddle—else I’m no Florentine. Laudamus! He’s coming to be shaved—that’s what I’ve waited for. Messer Domenico, stay—wait. You’ll witness a rare jest I devised two days ago. Here, Sandro!”

Nello whispered to Sandro, who rolled his eyes solemnly, nodded, smiled slowly, and darted away with surprising speed.

“How are you, Maestro Tacco?” said Nello as the doctor struggled to turn his horse toward the shop. “Fine young horse, though a bit raw in the mouth, eh?”

“It’s a cursed beast—may the _vermocane_ take it!” the doctor exclaimed, dismounting and fastening the rope-repaired bridle to an iron staple. “Still,” he added, recovering, “a sound animal, valuable if one wants to buy and train it for profit. I got it cheap.”

“Too much riding for someone carrying your weight of learning, eh, Maestro?” said Nello. “You seem hot.”

“Indeed, I am likely to be,” said the doctor, removing his hat and revealing a bald, low forehead, flat broad face, high ears, wide lipless mouth, round eyes, and deep arches above prominent brows—making Nello’s “toad-faced” description oddly apt. “Riding from Peretola under the sun is no match for kicking heels on a shaded bench like your Florence doctors. Besides, I’ve had trouble navigating through carts and mules into the Mercato to find the husband of Monna Ghita, who suffered a fatal seizure before I arrived. And had I not demanded payment—”

“Monna Ghita!” said Nello, as the sweating doctor paused to wipe his face. “Peace to her angry soul! The Mercato will need one fewer whip if her tongue is silenced.”

Tito, pulled from his reverie, felt a sudden surge of half-formed thoughts about Tessa—those that had crossed his mind the night before. If Monna Ghita were truly gone, it might be easier to see Tessa again—whenever he chose.

“_Gnaffè_, Maestro,” Nello continued sympathetically, “you’re the servant of rude mortals who, without you, would die like beasts. It’s sad to see your learned fluid oozing like mere sweat. You think my shaving will cool and clear you? One moment—I finish Messer Francesco here. It feels like a thousand years until I serve a man who carries all the science of Arabia in his head and saddlebags. Ecco!”

Nello held up the shaving cloth invitingly. Maestro Tacco stepped forward, absorbed in his heat and importance, deaf to the irony in Nello’s polished politeness.

“It’s fitting,” said Nello, adjusting the cloth, “that a great medicus like you should be shaved by the same razor that served the illustrious Antonio Benevieni, master of surgery.”

“The surgical art!” interrupted the doctor, offended. “Is it your Florentine custom to place medicine’s noble science—Hippocrates, Galen, Avicenna—on equal footing with barbers who mend broken limbs like carpenters, stitch wounds like tailors, or carve flesh like butchers? _Via_! A manual craft, fit for any artisan, practiced by simpletons like yourself—how can it compare to the divine knowledge of stars, plants, and gems?”

“No, Maestro,” said Nello, applying lather slowly, as if determined to prolong the process. “I never claimed equality. I know your science rivals the miracles of Holy Church. But here’s the pity—your lofty knowledge remains hidden from the vulgar, so you become envied and slandered. I grieve to say it, but there are low fellows in this city—mere _sgherri_, wearing nightcaps and long beards—who delight in poisoning every man’s broth who thrives. Let me tell you, as a stranger—you must carry a large nail ready to pin down Fortune’s wheel when your side rises. Already, stories—fables, doubtless—are spreading about you, and I wish I could hear you heading for Arezzo. I wouldn’t want a man of your caliber stoned. Though San Stefano was stoned, he wasn’t a great physician like San Cosmo and San Damiano…”

“What stories? What fables?” stammered Maestro Tacco.

“_Lasso_! I fear you’ve walked into the trap for your cheese, Maestro. The fact is, there’s a gang of mischievous youths who prowl houses with sharp tools in their pockets—no door, window, or shutter safe from them. They’re possessed with diabolical patience, watching those who believe themselves private. It must be them who’ve done this—it must be them who’ve spread tales about you and your medicines. Have you noticed any cracks in your door or shutters? No? Then I advise you to check. It’s widely rumored that you’ve been seen at your house in the Canto di Paglia, preparing your secret remedies at night: grinding dried toads in a mortar, mixing salve from crushed worms, making pills from dried rat livers mixed with saliva spat during blasphemous incantations—which these witnesses claim to repeat verbatim.”

“It’s a lie!” shouted the doctor, struggling to speak, then silencing himself as the razor neared.

“You needn’t say that to us, doctor,” said Nello calmly. “We’re not the sort to plant such carrots. But what of it? What are a few reasonable men against a crowd with stones? Some of us believe Cecco d’Ascoli was an innocent sage—and we all know how he burned for being wiser than his peers. Ah, doctor, you won’t learn Florentines by studying in Padua. My belief is, they’d stone the Holy Father himself if they could find a reason. They’re convinced you’re a necromancer trying to raise the plague with secret medicines—and I’m told your remedies truly smell foul.”

“It’s false!” cried the doctor as Nello moved the razor aside. “It’s false! I’ll show these honorable gentlemen the pills, powders—this salve—it has a wonderful scent—an odor of… of salve.” He rose, lather still on his chin, cloth around his neck, searching his saddlebag for the maligned medicines. In an instant, Nello shifted the chair near the horse’s head, and Sandro, returning at his master’s signal, stepped beside the bridle.

“Behold, Messeri!” declared the doctor, holding up a small box of medicines and opening it before them.

The loggia atop Bardo’s house rose above the surrounding buildings, forming a gallery around a square courtyard. On the street-facing side, columns supported the roof; on the other three, a wall pierced with arched openings allowed for sheltered views beyond. From this rear side, Romola could gaze over a jumble of uneven, poorly constructed homes toward the distant hill of Bogoli—unseen by any passerby below. Near one of these arches, close to the door through which she would enter, Tito waited, his heart heavy with the weight of sunlight that fell across the stone floor and mingled with the wreckage of his hopes.

He had never truly believed that Romola’s love for him could overcome the shock of learning his secret. He lacked the arrogance that might have blinded him to the truth: her affection was rooted in her belief in who he was, not in what he might be. Yet as he imagined her approaching—her radiant beauty softened by the gentle warmth of her hazel eyes—he found himself wishing she were less noble, less pure, so that he might hold her tightly, kiss her once more, before they parted forever. He had never known true intimacy with her—only fleeting glances, brief kisses, the pressure of hands. So often, he had longed for solitude with her. Now, they would be alone. But as he saw her silhouette appear at the doorway, he felt her presence not as closeness, but as distance—cold, unyielding, inevitable.

Then the door opened.

Romola stood there.

It happened like lightning—fleeting, overwhelming. He did not see her clearly, but *felt* the light around her head, the sorrow in her eyes, the cry of love that escaped her lips: “Tito!” And in an instant, she was in his arms, weeping into his chest, her face buried against his shoulder.

How deeply Romola had longed to see that face again! The vision of death, the bewildering tale of a life she could not comprehend, the haunting echo of her brother’s last breath—these had made her realize more than ever that it was Tito who first brought warmth and hope into her world, who had eased the pain of remembering Dino. She must tell him everything. There was no one else. All morning, she had held herself together in front of her father. Now, the dam broke.

Proud and composed before all others, Romola was, in Tito’s presence, as open and tender as a child. She had been content with silence, with mere glances. But now, needing only to be near him, she clung without hesitation.

“My Romola! My goddess!” Tito whispered, cradling her gently, pressing his lips to the golden waves of hair at her neck. In that moment, he was in paradise. Shame, disgrace, separation—none of it mattered anymore. This joy was too strong to be tainted by the knowledge that she still loved him, unaware of the truth. Indeed, he rejoiced in her ignorance. Concealment had been wisdom. Only the fear he had carried—unnecessary, perhaps—remained a shadow, though even that seemed worth enduring for the rapture now flooding his soul.

The sob passed. Romola lifted her head. Neither spoke. They simply looked at each other, caught in the quiet wonder of young love—she with her pale hands resting on his dark curls, he with his fingers tracing the gold of her hair. Each was beautiful to the other. Each experienced, for the first time, that perfect mutual recognition, untouched by doubt or distance.

A new sadness settled on Romola’s heart, making her linger longer in the comfort of their silence. Tito dared not move closer, afraid that even a kiss would break the spell.

“Tito,” she said at last, “it has been terrible. But I must tell you everything. Your strength will help me resist the thoughts that reason cannot shake.”

“I know,” Tito replied softly. “I know he is dead.” His voice was calm, but his eyes betrayed the storm within—the longing that had shaped so many silent prayers, the wish that had burned in his heart long before the deed was done. Yet Romola saw only her own purity reflected in those deep, lustrous eyes, as if reading a letter written in dreams.

“So changed,” she murmured. “To think it was Dino. And so strange—no word to my father. Only a vision, as if he wanted to speak to me. And yet… it was heartbreaking. The labored breath, the eyes fixed on the crucifix, yet seeing nothing. I shall never forget it. It feels as though it will stand between me and every sight I meet.”

Her voice faltered. Her heart swelled, and she paused. But the need to share with Tito pressed upon her, urging her to push past the grief.

“It was strange, Tito,” she continued, her thoughts drifting. “The vision was about our marriage. Yet he knew nothing of you.”

“Tell me, my Romola,” Tito said, leading her to the bench nearby. A sudden fear stirred in him—what if the vision somehow concerned Baldassarre? The shift in emotion prompted him to change their position, to create space, however slight.

Romola recounted every detail from her entrance into San Marco, sparing none of her brother’s words—each spoken sentence seared into her memory. When she reached the end of the vision, she stopped. The rest remained too vivid to speak aloud. She sat staring into the distance, half-unaware of Tito beside her.

His mind, meanwhile, was at peace. The vision had passed over him like mist, leaving no trace. He waited silently, expecting her to continue.

“I took it,” she said, as if he had read her thoughts. “I took the crucifix. It’s downstairs in my room.”

“And now, my Romola,” Tito said, pleadingly, “banish these terrible thoughts. It was merely a monkish vision—born of fasting and fanaticism. It means nothing to you.”

“No, Tito,” she answered quietly. “But poor Dino—he believed it was divine. It’s strange, how men live in such fervent beliefs that seem madness to others. Dino wasn’t a vulgar zealot. And Fra Girolamo—his voice… it seemed to pierce me, as if there were truth in what moves them—truth I do not understand.”

“It was only your emotions, Romola,” Tito insisted. “Your brother’s state was no different from the theosophy that has plagued dreamers since time began—the same ideas Marsilio Ficino studies in the New Platonists. But Dino’s passion drove him to act, while others only write. And Fra Girolamo? He’s just a narrow-minded monk with a gift for preaching and terrifying crowds. Any voice would have shaken you then. Once your mind rests, you’ll judge these things as you always have.”

“Not about Dino,” Romola said firmly. “I was angry with him. My heart closed against him while he spoke. But since then… I’ve thought less of myself, more of him. Oh, Tito—how pitiful it was, watching his young life fade like that. That look at the crucifix, gasping for air—I can never forget it. Last night, I stared at the crucifix for hours, trying to believe it helped him. Until the lamplight made it seem… as if the suffering face looked down with pity.”

“Promise me,” Tito urged, “you won’t dwell on such thoughts. They belong to weak nuns, not to my golden-haired Aurora—made to scatter shadows, not gather them. Don’t think of them now. We won’t be alone much longer.”

His tone was tender, beseeching. With a gentle touch, he turned her face toward him.

Romola had been gazing absently through the archway, not seeing the hill beyond. She had been back in the chapter house, among the pale images of sorrow and death. Tito’s touch, his voice, pulled her back. Now, in the warm sun, she saw the world anew—rich, dark, alive. Purple vines wound between elms, corn ripened under the heat, birds darted among flowers, dancers beat cymbals in rhythm, melodies danced on strings—nature reveling in its strength.

What a transition—from the pallid visions of suffering to this vibrant youthfulness, as if a sun-god walked without knowing night. How could the anguish on Dino’s face, that desperate yearning for something unseen, coexist with this fullness of life? Was there no reconciliation? Only blind worship of conflicting gods—first in ecstasy, then in mourning?

For the first time, Romola felt this question rise like a dizzying ache—a need to grasp something, anything. It lasted only a breath, yet in that moment, centuries of human inquiry condensed into her single, trembling mind. But there was no answer. The feeling vanished, swept away by the return of youthful joy, the radiant beauty of Tito, like dawn after a glance at the grey west.

“Your mind wanders from us, my Romola,” Tito murmured, softly reproachful. “It seems forgotten to you.”

She looked into his eyes, silent. Then the sadness melted, replaced by warmth.

“My joy!” she said, her voice clear and bright.

“You care enough to banish those cold fancies?” Tito asked, smiling. “Or will you always suspect me as the Great Tempter?”

“How could I not care for you more than anything?” she replied. “Everything I’ve ever felt—about my father, about loneliness—was preparation for loving you. You’d laugh if you knew what kind of man I imagined marrying—some scholar with deep lines, like Alamanno Rinuccini, grey hair, agreeing with my father on Aristotelian matters, willing to live with him. I read about love in poems, but never dreamed it could happen here, in Florence, in our old library. Then you came, Tito—so dear to my father—and I began to believe life could be happy for me too.”

“My goddess,” Tito breathed, awed by the majesty and simplicity in her. “But if you truly cared about our marriage, you’d persuade your father and Messer Bernardo to stop delaying.”

“I do care,” Romola said. “But I know my godfather will insist on more delay because of Dino’s death. He’s never agreed with my father about disowning Dino. And you know he’s always said we should wait until you’ve been in Florence a year. Don’t blame him. He’s prejudiced, yes—but noble. He says it’s folly for my father to keep the library separate, so it bears his name—yet he’d still try to fulfill his wish. That, to me, is great nobility—the ability to respect a feeling he doesn’t share.”

“I have no resentment toward Messer Bernardo,” Tito said. “For thinking you too precious for me.”

“But does your father know?” Romola asked.

“Yes. I told him. I couldn’t help it. I told him where I’d been, that I saw Dino die—but nothing else. He’s forbidden me to speak of it again. But he’s been silent today, restless—those movements that wound me so. It’s as if he’s trying to escape the prison of his blindness. Let us go to him now. I persuaded him to sleep—he didn’t rest well last night. Your voice calms him, Tito. Always.”

“And not one kiss?” Tito asked gently, with a tone that made him seem both dependent and charming—rare gifts that excused his boldness.

A blush, swift as light, spread across Romola’s cheeks and neck as she leaned toward him. It seemed impossible their kisses could ever grow common.

“Let us walk once round the loggia,” she said, “before we go down.”

“There’s something grim about Florence,” Tito remarked, pausing at the front of the house, where they could see across rooftops to the river. “Even in its merriment, it’s shrill, hard—biting rather than joyful. I wish we lived in Southern Italy, where thought is broken not by weariness, but by sweet languor—something the ‘ingenia acerrima Florentina’ never seem to know. I’d love to see you under that southern sun, lying among flowers, lulled into pure delight, while I sang to you on the lute—something unconscious, born of light and warmth. You’ve never known that joy, my Romola.”

“No,” she said, “but I’ve dreamed of it since you came. I thirst for a deep draught of joy—for a life as bright as you. But let’s not think of it now, Tito. It seems to me there will always be pale faces among the flowers, eyes looking in vain. Let us go.”

When Tito left the Via de’ Bardi that afternoon, he felt a deep relief, almost a triumph, at having escaped the danger that had once seemed so imminent. Freed from the shadow of fear, his mind turned back to those anxious hours when he had been haunted by regret—not only for what he had felt, but for what he had done. He now saw clearly that he should never have parted with his ring. To Romola, and others who knew it well, such an act would seem petty, even shameful, unless he could invent some plausible reason—like using the money for a special gift. But Tito was weary of pretense. The lie had passed; fortune had spared him, and he thought it just that she should. Who had truly suffered? Baldassarre’s fate remained uncertain, beyond reckoning. But Tito wanted no lingering chains—no invisible ties that might pinch him later, however faintly, beneath the surface of his life with Romola.

Yet he did not realize that the very joy he now felt—the satisfaction of escape—was dulling the conscience that might have warned him against future entanglements. Still, the sale of the ring was a small matter. Was it not also trivial that little Tessa still clung to a dream that would surely shatter her innocent heart? Should he seek her out alone and gently break the illusion, or let time and chance unravel it?

Dreams were sweet, and fragile. They faded easily with morning light and the bustle of the world. And yet, how could he forget her—small, round-faced, pouting with innocence? She stirred something in him, though he tried to dismiss it. Perhaps he would bring her a gift someday, just to see if her stepfather treated her more harshly now that her mother was gone. Or should he reveal the truth at once, then tell Romola so they could find her a better life?

No. That affair—the _cerretano_, the marriage, his letting her go believing he loved her—must never reach Romola’s ears. And since Tessa could not be awakened from her delusion, there would always be a risk of exposure. Besides, the child might one day feel bitterness when her hopes crumbled. Yes, she must be in love with him, however foolishly. It would be kinder not to see her again.

Still, it was only a silly country girl’s fancy—one that might fade when a sturdy peasant boy courted her properly, and she confessed everything, finally understanding she was free. _Dunque_—farewell, Tessa. Best wishes. Tito resolved that this childish episode would end here. And men often believe their own resolutions will hold firm.

As for the fifty-five florins—his ring’s price—he already knew what to do with part of it. A clever idea had formed: one that would help explain the missing ring to Romola, and perhaps protect her from slipping into the monkish fantasies that repelled him so deeply. With this plan in mind, he walked to the Via Gualfonda, seeking Piero di Cosimo, the painter whose skill in mythological whimsy matched exactly what Tito needed.

The courtyard of Piero’s house was quiet. On the heavy iron knocker hung thick woolen wrappings, tied tightly with cords—a familiar defense, Tito recalled, against the painter’s habit of stuffing his ears to block out noise. He tapped lightly first, then shook the door more insistently. No answer. Just as he turned away, chiding himself for wasting time, a small girl entered, carrying a basket of eggs. She stood on tiptoe, pushed up a small iron plate set into the door, and called through the opening in a thin voice: “Messer Piero!”

In moments, bolts scraped, the door creaked open, and Piero appeared—wearing a red nightcap and a loose brown tunic, sleeves rolled to the shoulder. He glanced sharply at Tito, but without greeting, reached out for the basket. After taking it inside, he returned with the empty container and asked, “How much?”

“Two grossoni, Messer Piero,” the girl replied. “They’re all boiled, my mother says.”

Piero pulled coins from a leather pouch at his belt, and the child skipped away, casting several glances upward at the strange young nobleman.

Piero’s gaze, however, was far less admiring. “What do you want at my door, Messer Greco?” he said. “I saw you this morning at Nello’s. If you’d asked then, I could’ve told you—I don’t receive anyone without knowing their purpose in advance.”

“Pardon me, Messer Piero,” Tito answered, unruffled. “I acted without thinking. All I remembered was your talent for beautiful inventions—so when a sudden desire struck me, I came straight to you.”
