Piero made his usual grimace—an exaggerated grin, brows pressed down, eyes narrowed—as if resisting flattery. After a pause, he asked, “And what is it you need?”

In truth, the idea excited him. “I want a delicate miniature design, drawn from the poets’ fables,” Tito began. “You’ll know how to combine them. It must be painted on a wooden triptych—here’s the size.” He held up his hands. “The inside can be plain gold leaf. But the outside—I want a new version of a favorite Florentine theme: Bacchus and Ariadne’s triumph. But not as Ovid tells it. Young Bacchus seated in a ship, his head crowned with grape clusters, holding a spear wrapped in vine leaves. Dark-berried ivy winding around the masts and sails, oars shaped like thyrsi, flowers curling about the stern. Leopards and tigers crouched before him, dolphins leaping around. And beside him—Ariadne, fair-haired, crowned with gold—immortalized. That part isn’t in Ovid, but you’ll imagine it. Above them, young Loves, like the ones you paint so well, shooting arrows tipped with roses—”

“Enough!” Piero interrupted. “I have Ovid in the vulgar tongue. Find the passage. I hate being choked with other men’s thoughts. Come in.”

He led Tito through the first room, where the basket of eggs sat near the hearth, beside a pile of broken shells and ashes. Amidst the mess stood a low ebony bedstead, draped carelessly with a rich Eastern carpet—once perhaps used to cover the steps of a Madonna’s altar—and a carved chest with painted scenes on its sides and lid. Elsewhere, casts, wooden steps, easels, and rough boxes hung with cobwebs filled the space.

The next room was larger, more crowded. A double door beneath the high window opened onto a wild garden—more thicket than plot—where fig trees and vines tangled among nettles and hemlocks, and a tall cypress rose above a choking mass of yellow mulberry leaves. Even indoors, nature crept in: long grass and feathery fennel grew in a corner among marble fragments and rusted armor. A tilted stone vase poured out ivy like water.

On the walls hung sketches—sea monsters, dancing satyrs and maenads, Saint Margaret rising from the dragon’s mouth, Madonnas glowing with divine light, plant studies, grotesque faces. Shelves bore books mixed with dried corn, ox horns, honeycombs, lichen-covered stones, skulls, peacock feathers, and bird wings. From the floor rose mannequins—one dressed as a Vallombrosan monk, topped with a barred helmet; another buried under brocade and furs. Among them, pigeons perched calmly, toads crawled near the threshold, and a white rabbit twitched its nose contentedly atop a box of bran.

“Now, Messer Greco,” Piero said, gesturing to a low stool near the door, “don’t try to take in everything at once, like Messer Domeneddio. Tell me—how large shall this triptych be?”

Tito indicated the dimensions. Piero marked them on paper. “Now, the book,” he said, pulling down a manuscript.

“There’s nothing about Ariadne here,” Tito noted, handing over the passage. “But I want her beside Bacchus—crowned, golden hair.”

“Ha,” Piero muttered, lips pursed. “And you want likenesses, eh?” He looked closely at Tito’s face.

Tito laughed, blushing slightly. “I know you’re good at portraits, Messer Piero—but I couldn’t ask Ariadne to sit. The painting must remain secret.”

“Ah,” Piero said, sharp-eyed. “Then I want her to sit. Giovanni Vespucci wants me to paint Oedipus and Antigone at Colonos, as he explains it. I’m fond of the subject. I want Bardo and his daughter to model for it. You—ask them. Then I’ll put their likeness into Ariadne.”

“Agreed—if I can persuade them. And your price for Bacchus and Ariadne?”

“_Baie_! If you get them to sit, that’s payment enough. I’d rather not take your money. You may pay for the case.”

“And when shall I sit?” Tito asked. “If we have one likeness, we need two.”

“I don’t need yours,” Piero said. “I already have it. Only I made you look frightened. I must remove that fear for Bacchus.”

He laid down the book and searched among paintings propped against the wall. Returning with an oil sketch, he said, “This is as fine a portrait as I’ve ever done.”

He turned it toward Tito. There was Tito—right hand raised, wine cup in hand, posture triumphant—but his face twisted in terror, eyes wide, lips pale, as if seeing a horror beyond words. A chill ran through Tito’s body, as though he were reliving the moment.

“You’re beginning to look like it already,” Piero said, laughing shortly, and moved the picture away. “He sees a ghost—that fine young man. One day I’ll finish it—when I decide what kind of ghost is most terrible: solid, like a risen corpse, or half-transparent, like mist.”

Tito, embarrassed by his sudden sensitivity—so unlike his usual calm—said lightly, “That’s a fitting subject for you, Messer Piero—a revel interrupted by a ghost. You seem to love mixing the terrifying with the joyful. That’s why your shelves are full of skulls while you paint those mischievous Loves stealing Mars’s armor. I begin to think you’re a Cynic philosopher, disguised as a master painter.”

“Not I,” Piero replied. “A philosopher is the last thing I’d wish to be. I find it enough to live, without spinning lies to explain it. Fowls cackle, asses bray, women chatter, philosophers spin false reasons—that’s what the world brings out in them. I am an animal that paints instead. And now—our business is done. Will you keep your side of the bargain about Oedipus and Antigone?”

“I’ll do my best,” Tito said, already moving toward the door. “I’ll let you know at Nello’s. No need to come here again.”

“I understand,” Tito added, smiling, lifting his hand in farewell.

CHAPTER XIX. The Old Man’s Hope

Messer Bernardo del Nero proved as unyielding as Romola had anticipated when it came to delaying the marriage until Easter—a decision Bardo accepted without question, for he was entirely guided by his shrewd and practical friend. Yet even Bernardo, though utterly immune to the charm that others found in Tito, could not ignore the power of success, especially among men who navigated the world with their eyes fixed on fortune and influence. Tito was rising swiftly in the favor of high society. He had particularly won the regard of the young Cardinal Giovanni de’ Medici, who had already spoken of inviting him to join his scholarly entourage on a journey to Rome. With his quick wit, gentle demeanor, and ability to engage without ever provoking offense, Tito became a welcome guest at elegant dinners along the Via Larga and at social gatherings where he might not excel in skill but always admired the grace of Piero de’ Medici with such sincerity that no one felt slighted.

And so, the reputation of being a delightful companion in refined company only enhanced the perception of Tito’s learning and talent—though in truth, he possessed enough genuine ability to make such admiration both plausible and harmless. Messer Bernardo, though burdened with long-held opinions and loyalties, began to reassess his earlier reservations about the young Greek, whose polished ways had once seemed too supple for comfort. To an old Florentine like Bernardo, there was no dignity in ignoring the approval of the city’s most respected families. And now that Tito had fully entered that circle—whose judgment defined social worth—it seemed absurd to continue doubting the value of such a son-in-law for Bardo or such a husband for Romola.

It was undeniable: Tito’s arrival had brought new life to both father and daughter. Not only had hope returned, but it had deepened beyond expectation. Once, Bardo—the blind scholar whose unwavering honesty refused the flattery and vituperation common among academics—had fallen into obscurity, even among his peers. When mentioned at all, he was often spoken of with pity for his blindness and the loss of his son, yet also with mild impatience over his persistent defense of his own work. His discontent, some said, was ill-placed in a man who had once turned down clerical appointments, despite offers from powerful patrons, simply because they required ordination—an obstacle that made patronage difficult to secure.

But since Tito came into their lives, the tone around Bardo had changed. No longer did his name evoke only sorrow and silence. It was whispered that he had ceased his lamentations; that his daughter, once confined by pride and poverty, would no longer wait in vain for a suitable match. Tito’s presence—his charm, his growing status—had transformed their home into a place of quiet warmth and renewed interest. Visitors now found their way to the library, drawn either by nostalgia or curiosity. Older men returned, stirred by the memory of past acquaintance now rekindled by talk of the family’s revival. Young men whom Tito had invited once soon found themselves returning, catching up with him near the Via de’ Bardi, their hands resting lightly on his shoulder as they fell into easy conversation.

For what could be more pleasing than seeing Romola? Her beauty, dignified and serene, reminded one of Firenzuola’s ideal of womanly greatness: “When a woman is noble in form, carries herself well, sits with grandeur, speaks with gravity, smiles with modesty, and seems to emit a fragrance of queenship—then we say she has majesty.”[1] And under the gentle breath of affection and respect, Romola bloomed like a white lily, strong and pure. To her, this new life—fueled by Tito’s love—was everything.

Tito had done more than bring joy—he had kindled a renewed hope in Bardo’s heart: that before he died, he might see his lifelong dream fulfilled—the preservation of his library. That it would remain separate, untouched by other collections, never absorbed into a monastery, nor renamed after religious orders. That it would stay forever known as the Bardi Library, open to Florentines for generations. For though Bardo’s trust in the Medici had waned over time, it had not vanished. Their influence still held sway over the republic, and if Tito could gain the ear of Cardinal Giovanni, perhaps he could do more than Bernardo ever could—to convince the learned and powerful of the unique value of Bardo’s collection.

Tito himself spoke with confidence of this possibility, eager to lift the old man’s spirits. And Romola, listening, responded with a devotion that surpassed mere gratitude—her love for her father was mirrored in her tenderness toward Tito, a love that honored not just his kindness, but his intellect and spirit.

This matter of the library became the subject of many private discussions between Bernardo and Romola after Christmas, as the wedding drew nearer—but always out of Bardo’s hearing. For Bardo clung to a fragile belief: that his remaining property, apart from the library, was sufficient to meet any financial demands. He refused, except in moments of bitter despair, to admit that the will which disinherited Dino left Romola nothing but debt. Nor did he acknowledge that he needed anything beyond the assurance that the library would be granted a permanent space within the Republic—secured through a deed of gift, in exchange for its protection.

“I believe,” Bernardo told Romola one afternoon beneath the loggia, “that since you are to marry, and Messer Tito will have a steady income, we should begin settling affairs. Let us determine exactly how much is needed to protect the library, rather than allow debts to grow. Your father needs little—only his daily mutton and macaroni—and I think Messer Tito can provide that for the years ahead. We might call it his *morgen-cap*.”

“Tito has always known my life is bound to my father’s,” Romola replied gently. “And he treats my father better than I ever could. He delights in making him happy.”

“Ah,” Bernardo smiled, “he’s not cut from the same cloth as most men, is he?”

“You taught me to read Greek and Latin,” Romola teased, “to shut out the folly of women. But I’ve fallen just as easily for the first pair of bright eyes and soft words that came my way—just as if I’d only memorized Paternosters like every other girl.”

“Now, godfather,” she continued, shaking her head with a light laugh, “as if it were only bright eyes and sweet words that made me love Tito! You know better. I love you and my father because you are good. And I love Tito because he is good—so deeply good that I see it in everything he does. And if he is handsome too—why shouldn’t I love him the more for it? Beauty, to me, is part of the language of goodness. And you—you must have been very handsome in your youth,” she added, lifting her eyes to him with a radiant smile. “You’re nearly as tall as Tito, and your eyes are fine—though yours look sterner, prouder.”

“And Romola likes to keep all the pride for herself?” Bernardo asked, amused but touched.

“Well,” she said softly, “it is true that Tito asks nothing of me—not even a dowry. In that way, his demands are simpler than those of any Florentine nobleman we might have found for you.”

So it was settled—between Bernardo, Romola, and Tito. Bardo gave his assent with a wave of his hand when Bernardo suggested beginning to sell property and clear debts. He saw such matters not as numbers or obligations, but as dense woods, impenetrable to his mind, which he never attempted to traverse.

And Tito, keen to earn Bernardo’s respect, immersed himself in the intricacies of Florentine finance—learning the workings of the *Monti*, the value of land, the profits of banking. He studied the city’s economy with the same diligence he applied to philosophy and poetry.

“You’ll forget he’s not Florentine soon enough, godfather,” Romola said, watching him. “See how quickly he learns everything about our city.”

“He seems more like one of the *demoni*,” Bernardo replied, smiling, “those beings without a homeland. His mind moves too swiftly to carry the weight of all the baggage we men drag around in our hearts.”

Romola smiled back—content, hopeful, full of quiet joy.

The final days of Carnival in Florence were alive with noise and color, as if the city had shed its usual gravity to embrace a feverish celebration. The streets teemed with masked processions, their voices rising in rhythmic chants—customs first introduced by Lorenzo the Magnificent and now inseparable from the season’s spirit. In the piazza, beneath a sky sharp with frost and blue as lapis lazuli, young men danced the rigoletto, a lively round dance that swirled with energy and laughter. Jokes ran rampant—from tossing sweets to hurling stones—and the latter, though dangerous, was a cherished tradition. Boys and young men, ever restless and bold, blocked passersby with poles, demanding tribute for suppers and bonfires. Then came the stone-throwing, a ritual so familiar it carried no shame, though it sometimes ended in injury or worse. A painter who sought to capture this scene truthfully would have to depict both its joy and its brutality—laughter beside wounds, festivity alongside violence. Such a work might be turned away from view, except when history demanded it be displayed to justify reforms born not of cruelty, but of ignorance.

Yet amid the chaos, there remained a kind of innocent gaiety, born of a people quick to feel and quick to act. There was no sluggishness in their merriment, nor the sly violence that sometimes flares in southern lands where quarrels end in knives. This morning, though still high in the day, the crowd lingered, reliving last night’s antics, when Tito Melema walked briskly toward the Via de’ Bardi. With him was Young Bernardo Dovizi, later known to posterity through Raphael’s portrait as the sharp-eyed Cardinal da Bibbiena. As they moved, they spoke animatedly about matters far removed from the revelry around them. Yet both, from time to time, glanced sharply about—aware, perhaps, of eyes watching them.

At the turn toward the Lung’ Arno, leading to the Ponte Rubaconte, Tito caught sight of someone he wished above all things to avoid: Tessa. She stood not far off, her face pale and uncertain, moving slowly toward the very path he was taking. His thoughts were already full—his mind fixed on an event unlike any other in his life. That evening, he would formally betrothed to Romola. Though preparations had been underway for some time—Tito’s money spent on furnishing rooms in Bardo’s house, once bare save for the library—the wedding had originally been planned after Easter, when his year of probation under Bernardo del Nero would be complete. But then came an invitation: to accompany Cardinal Giovanni to Rome, to assist Bernardo Dovizi with Greek texts in organizing a library—an opportunity too promising to refuse. So Tito had pressed urgently for the betrothal to happen now. It would bind him to Romola before his departure, making their separation less painful, and giving him a claim no one could undo. For in Florence, the exchange of rings and signing of contracts made the betrothal nearly as binding as marriage itself, the nuptial blessing to follow later.

Romola had welcomed the idea, and the elders consented. Now, with his departure set for the next day, Tito hurried to see her before the ceremony, to say whatever words needed saying before they met again at the church.

He did not wish to be seen by Tessa—not now, not here. And yet, there she was, unmistakable in her white hood, walking toward the bridge. He knew she had seen him; he felt her presence behind him, even without turning. There was no escape along this route—no way past the Ponte Rubaconte without her catching up. But she wouldn’t dare speak while he was with another man. He would keep Bernardo close until they reached Bardo’s door.

He quickened his pace, shifting the conversation, but the awareness of Tessa growing closer gnawed at him. It was irritating—yet tinged with pity. She was so small, so fragile, so helpless. To ignore her completely felt cruel, even as it seemed necessary. He pushed on, arriving at the doorway, exchanging a swift farewell with Dovizi without glancing back. Up the stone steps he went, telling himself she might not have seen him after all.

But when he stepped out again, he saw her—small and still, near the entrance, her figure outlined in white against the gray stone. Before he could react, a hand touched his arm. Not a timid touch, but firm, desperate—Tessa, having seen his haste, had rushed forward.

He stopped. Turned.

Her face was filled with fear.

“Tessa!” he said, sharper than he meant. “Why are you here? You must not follow me. You must not wait for me outside doors.”

Her blue eyes brimmed with tears. She said nothing.

Tito feared more than mockery—he feared being seen with her by Bernardo del Nero or anyone else who might see it as a stain on his reputation. The Via de’ Bardi was quiet, lined with noble homes, but danger could come at any moment. Even without the betrothal, this encounter was awkward. Yet he could not harshly dismiss her. That foolish affair with the _cerretano_—a secret buried for months—had risen again, unbidden, like a weed in spring.

He couldn’t be cruel. But he couldn’t linger either.

“Tessa,” he said quickly, “I cannot talk here. I will wait for you on the bridge. Follow me slowly.”

He turned and walked swiftly to the Ponte Rubaconte, leaning against the wall of one of the quaint houses built into the bridge. He waited, watching the path she would take.

When she came, her face was paler, her round cheeks drawn with sorrow. The bridge was nearly empty—lucky, for such moments needed privacy.

As soon as she drew near, he spoke.

“Now, Tessa. I have little time. Do not cry. Why did you follow me?”

“I thought…” she whispered, fighting a sob that rose despite her efforts. “I thought you’d come back soon. The _patrigno_ beats me. I can’t bear it anymore. I always walk when I’m free, looking for you—but I never find you. Please don’t send me away again! It’s been so long… I cry every day because you don’t come. I can’t help it. The days are so long…”

She broke down, tears falling fast.

Tito could not leave her like this. He must send her away—yes, that he must do. But he could not break her heart entirely. He saw trouble ahead, but the moment demanded mercy.

“Tessa, my little one,” he said, softening his voice, “you must not cry. Bear with your stepfather a little longer. I will come back. But I am going to Rome—a long way. I’ll return in weeks, and then I promise I’ll visit you. Promise me you’ll be good and wait.”

It was the old voice—the one she trusted. Just hearing it calmed her. She looked up, her eyes still wet, but willing.

“Promise me, Tessa.”

“Yes,” she whispered. “But won’t you be long?”

“No, not long. But I must go now. Remember what I told you—no one must know you see me. If they do, you lose me forever. When I leave, go straight home. Never follow me again. Wait for me. Goodbye, my little Tessa—I *will* come.”

He turned without looking back. He had no time to spare. When he glanced again, he was already in the Via de’ Benci, unable to see the bridge. But Tessa obeyed—she stayed, silent, waiting.

The incident was over—for today. Yet the memory of her plea, of her pain, would linger. But Tito’s mind was filled with brighter thoughts. Love, ambition, and hope intertwined in his heart. Romola awaited him, and Rome called. The suffering of others—especially a child like Tessa—was distant, like a faint cry from a forest while he rode through sunlit fields.

Later that day, crossing the Ponte Rubaconte again, Tito felt no shadow of guilt. He was wrapped in his mantle, not just for warmth, but to hide his fine clothes from curious eyes. He climbed the steps two at a time and found Maso waiting.

“Where is the damigella?”

“In the library. She is ready. Monna Brigida, Messer Bernardo, and Ser Braccio are there, but no one else.”

“Tell her I need a few minutes alone. I’ll wait in the _salotto_.”

He entered a room painted in vivid hues—nymphs and lovers dancing among flowers and birds, the walls glowing with light. The furniture was simple: red leather seats, a central table, two tall vases, and a small statue of a faun playing the flute—crafted by a young Michelangelo Buonarotti. The air felt open, almost outdoor, warm and joyful.

Tito kept his mantle on, standing near the door. Soon Romola entered—white and gold, like a lily grown tall in sunlight. Her silk dress was bound with a golden belt, tassels hanging low. Her hair, a cascade of gold, crowned her head, covered by a long veil of white mist, fastened with pearls gifted by Bernardo del Nero. The veil fell back, framing her face.

“Regina mia,” Tito said, taking her hand and kissing it, still wrapped in his cloak.

He stepped back, unable to stop admiring her. She stood calmly, radiant under his gaze, aware of her beauty, yet humble in love.

“Romola,” he said, remembering the time, “will you show me the next room? You said I’d see it when everything was arranged.”

Without a word, she led him to a narrow chamber, bright with paintings of birds and flowers. Old furnishings lined the walls—faded trinkets, mirrors, a cabinet holding relics of her mother’s life. Above it hung the portrait of her mother. Below, on the cabinet, sat the crucifix she had brought from San Marco.

“I have something under my mantle,” Tito said, smiling.

He removed the cloak, revealing a small tabernacle painted by Piero di Cosimo. The artist had captured Tito’s vision perfectly—after months of delay.

“Do you know what this is for?” Tito asked, taking her hand and guiding her to the cabinet.

“It’s a shrine—to hide away your sadness. You’re done with sorrow now. We’ll bury all memories of it—entomb them in joy. Look!”

A tremor passed over Romola’s face as he lifted the crucifix. But she didn’t resist. She, too, wanted to silence the doubts that still flickered in her mind.

He opened the triptych, placed the crucifix inside, closed it, took the key, and set the tabernacle in the spot where the cross had stood.

“Now, Romola—look. Are you pleased with how Piero painted us?”

She stared, eyes shining with tears. “Ah—it’s you. It’s perfect! I am Ariadne, and you crown me! Yes, Tito—you’ve crowned my life.”

They held hands, gazing at the image: Bacchus with purple grapes, Ariadne crowned in gold.

“And our dear Piero painted it?” she asked.

“He insisted I allow him to paint you and your father—so he could use your likeness for this.”

“Oh! Now I understand why you gave up your ring. You had a plan to please me.”

Tito smiled. Romola’s gentle illusions no longer troubled him—they delighted him.

“I could have spared the ring. Piero takes no payment. He says painting you is reward enough.”

Then, gently, he continued: “While I’m away, you’ll look at these symbols every day—the ship on calm seas, the ivy that never fades, the Loves who no longer wound us, but shower petals like our kisses. The leopards and tigers—your troubles—now subdued. And the sea-monsters, with their merry eyes—those are the dull passages in heavy books, which now seem amusing because we’ve read them together.”

“Tito mio,” she said, laughing softly, “but will you give me the key?”

“No,” he replied, playfully dropping the key into his scarsella. “I’ll drown it in the Arno.”

“But if I want to see the crucifix again?”

“That’s why it’s hidden—hidden by images of youth and joy.”

He kissed her brow lightly. She said nothing. Strong souls submit when resistance feels unjust.

Then, together, they joined the others—twenty in all, modest in number, chosen for personal bonds rather than family pride. Bernardo del Nero walked ahead, Bardo followed by Tito on one side and Romola on the other. Bardo had married at Santa Croce and wished the same for Romola, hoping one day to rest there among Florentines who had served the city well.

The procession moved slowly, fitting the solemnity of the blind father. They passed through the quiet streets, avoiding the loudest parts of Carnival. The west burned red as they crossed the bridge, casting a golden glow over the group.

When the ceremony ended, and Tito and Romola stepped out onto the church steps, the evening deepened into starlight. Torches lit the way.

As they emerged, a strange chant echoed—like a _Miserere_. From the far end of the piazza, a procession approached from the Borgo de’ Greci.

“It must be one of their masked parades,” Tito said, now alone with Romola.

Then, slowly, a monstrous figure appeared—Winged Time, scythe in hand, hourglass at his side, surrounded by winged children, the Hours. He stood atop a black-draped chariot pulled by oxen shrouded in darkness, only their horns gleaming white. From afar, it seemed as if Time and his children floated in the air.

Behind them came figures draped in white, like ghosts gliding above shadows, chanting in mournful tones.

A cold dread seized Romola. For a moment, it felt as if her brother’s vision—the one she could never forget—was unfolding before her.

She clung to Tito.

He understood.

“What grim jests your Florentines enjoy!” he said. “This is likely Piero di Cosimo’s invention—his taste for dark humor.”

“Tito, I wish it hadn’t happened. It makes the vision harder to forget.”

“Nay, Romola. You will only see our happiness now. I’ve locked all sadness away from you.”

“But it’s still there—only hidden,” she murmured.

“See? They’re gone.” Tito pointed. “You’ll forget this horror when we’re in the light, when we can see each other’s eyes. My Ariadne must never look backward—only forward, to Easter, when she will triumph with her Care-dispeller.”

It was November 17, 1494—more than eighteen months since Tito and Romola had stood together beneath the joyous light of Easter, united in marriage, as comfits rained down upon them in ancient Greek fashion, symbolizing a life blessed by heaven’s own sweetness. Since that springtime union, Florence had changed beyond recognition. Like a tree whose blossoms depend on the flow of sap from its roots, so too were the fates of Tito and Romola bound to the shifting currents of politics and power sweeping across Italy.

Just over a week earlier, the spirit of old Florence had stirred anew. The great bell in the palace tower had tolled with fierce urgency, summoning the people to arms. Men gathered with rusted weapons, farming tools, and makeshift clubs, driven by rage and pride, to expel the Medici from their city. The gates of San Gallo closed behind Piero de’ Medici, fleeing toward Bologna with his hired horsemen in disarray; his younger brother, the cardinal, slipped away disguised as a Franciscan monk. A price was set on both their heads. In the aftermath, homes were looted in accordance with tradition, the dishonorable portraits of past traitors were erased from public walls, and exiled foes of the Medici were welcomed back. The tyrants were gone, and the Republic had reclaimed its will.

Now, seven days later, the grand palace on Via Larga was being readied for a new guest—one whose arrival would stir both hope and unease. Draperies of rich color stretched across the streets, banners fluttered from windows, carpets and tapestries covered every step and stone where noble feet might tread. This was no ordinary welcome—it was a spectacle of celebration, though not without tension beneath the surface. From the palace, the river of color wound through the Cathedral, past the Piazza della Signoria, across the Ponte Vecchio, and ended at Porta San Frediano, the gate facing Pisa. There, a platform and canopy had been erected for the Signoria. Messer Luca Corsini, doctor of law, felt his heart quicken—he was to deliver a Latin oration. Every elder of note prepared himself: clean-shaven, silk robes neatly pressed. Young noblemen admired their newly tailored French-style tunics, meant to impress the visitor with Florentine elegance. And the clergy—archbishop in radiant vestments, monks in black, white, and grey—gathered early to plan their procession, bearing relics, sacred banners, and consecrated jewels, all timed precisely for the king’s expected arrival at three o’clock.

This was no ordinary guest. He had crossed the Alps with an army unlike any seen before: thousands of Swiss mercenaries, hardened warriors who fought for honor as much as coin; noble knights proud of their lineage; infantry lines where one in every hundred bore an arquebus; and cannons of bronze, firing iron balls instead of stones, drawn by horses, not oxen, capable of firing again before a wall could be repaired. Some compared him to Charlemagne, the legendary restorer of Florence, conqueror of degenerate kings, guardian of Christendom. Others saw him as Cyrus, liberator of Israel, redeemer of temples. For he came with grand designs: to march through Italy amid jubilant crowds, settle disputes in Rome, claim Naples by right and force, and then launch a crusade against the Turks—some to be slain, others converted to Christianity.

Such ambitions seemed fit for the Most Christian King, ruler of France, now the strongest monarchy in Christendom, thanks to the cunning of Louis XI, who had died ten years prior, haunted by fears of death and damnation. This heir to that shrewd king was Charles VIII—youthful, ambitious, and now advancing into Italy with divine purpose in his eyes.

To many, this invasion was not merely political—it was providential. It was believed that marble statues wept, phantasmal warriors clashed in the skies, and beasts gave birth to strange creatures—signs that this was no ordinary event, but a divine intervention, a turning point in history. And such belief did not stem only from fear of war, but from deeper moral tremors in the soul of the age—a sense that justice was about to descend.

At the heart of this conviction stood Fra Girolamo Savonarola, Prior of the Dominican convent of San Marco in Florence. On a September morning, as news spread of the French army entering Italy, he preached in the Cathedral from the words: “Behold, I, even I, do bring a flood of waters upon the earth.” He claimed it was no accident that he had reached this passage during Lent—this “flood” was the French army, a divine judgment, both punishing and purifying. His audience—among them some of the most learned minds in Florence—listened in stunned silence, moved by awe and dread.

Savonarola possessed a rare power: to move men’s hearts, to make them believe what he believed. Four years earlier, he had warned from the same pulpit that a scourge was coming to cleanse Italy and the Church. Many had scoffed—but now they listened. He saw himself as a prophet, and many believed he was one. While visions and omens were common enough in those times, Savonarola stood apart. Where others saw fleeting glimpses, he saw a beacon—illuminating the path of God’s justice.

In 1492, when Lorenzo de’ Medici died and Tito Melema arrived in Florence as a wanderer, Italy appeared peaceful. Crops were bountiful, palaces rose in cities, villas dotted hillsides. The wealthy feared nothing, for armies were hired like goods, and violence was kept at bay. Even the threat of the Turks had faded—the Pope preferred bribes to battle. The world seemed content under a false peace, a realm of luxury built on hypocrisy, greed, and sin. Art flourished, yes, but also served vanity—painters depicted saints and sinners alike with equal skill, while scholars composed flattery in elegant Latin. The Church, though corrupt, prospered. Heaven smiled; earth showed no cracks.

Yet Savonarola had spoken for two years, warning of doom. He had seen a sword hanging in the heavens—the sword of divine justice. As a young man in Ferrara, he had been struck by the contradiction between faith and life, and at twenty-three, he entered the cloister. He believed God had entrusted the Church with the lamp of truth—but now it was buried beneath corruption. Scandals multiplied; lies became bold. Was there no justice? No God?

No, he insisted. Scripture revealed the pattern: when the chosen people sinned, God punished. Reason itself demanded retribution. Unless the Church repented, how could the world be saved? He had seen these truths in visions, but his strength lay in passion—his fury at injustice, his unwavering trust in a just and pure God. To him, divine intervention was not a distant hope, but an imminent reality.

Beneath the glittering façade of Italian courts, tensions simmered. Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan, patron of Leonardo da Vinci, desired the crown for himself and feared the Neapolitan royal family—King Ferdinand and Prince Alfonso—who despised treachery and opposed poisoning a relative for personal gain. The Neapolitans, in turn, feared Pope Alexander Borgia. All watched Florence, wary of its strategic position. Venice, cautious and powerful, sought to expand across the Adriatic and along the western coast. Lorenzo de’ Medici had balanced these forces, maintaining alliances while keeping peace. But young Piero, rash and vain, shattered that balance. Ludovico, sensing danger, devised a plan: invite the French king to invade Italy and claim Naples as heir to the House of Anjou.

Ambassadors—called “orators”—moved between courts. A recusant cardinal, refusing to recognize a pope elected through bribery, added fervent rhetoric to the plea. Charles VIII listened—and agreed.

By 1493, rumors grew louder: the French king would cross the Alps with a vast army. Italians, long accustomed to foreign powers arbitrating their fate, began to see him as a judge, a deliverer. And Savonarola heard it as confirmation: the prophecy was unfolding. What filled the prophets of old but the sound of marching armies sent by God? He no longer looked to the horizon—he pointed to the storm approaching.

The French army was the new flood. Charles VIII was the instrument of God, like Cyrus of old. Let Italy repent—especially Florence, beloved by God, for it had received the warning first. Turn from sin, as Nineveh once did, and the storm would pass, leaving only cleansing rain.

But now, after three months in Italy and nearing Florence’s gates, the mood was not one of pure joy. Charles had not yet redressed wrongs. He held Florence’s frontier fortresses—given up by Piero without ransom or honor. He had done nothing to quell Pisa’s revolt, which had grown bolder because of his presence. Diplomats, even with a prophet among them, could extract no promise—only that everything would be settled once he entered the city.

Still, there was satisfaction: Piero had been cast out, humiliated. The Republic had regained its fire.

And so, beneath the festive drapes and banners, Florence made other preparations. Hidden within walls were soldiers hired by the Republic, summoned hastily from nearby lands. Old weapons were polished, tools sharpened, cudgels ready. Boards and stakes lay in wait to form barricades. Stones were stockpiled, to be hurled from upper windows if needed. Above all, the people were eager to fight—having recently tasted resistance with fierce delight.

Their mood darkened further with each sight of Frenchmen arriving ahead of the king—some with hawks on their wrists, others with chalk in hand, marking doors for lodging. Historians suggest that many Frenchmen carried themselves with a swagger, a arrogance that only inflamed Florentine pride.

Thus, on the morning of November 17, 1494, Florence stood poised—between celebration and defiance, hope and fear—waiting for the king who might be both savior and conqueror.

The sky was overcast, but in the Piazza del Duomo, the atmosphere was anything but somber. A festive canopy of blue drapery stretched overhead, adorned with golden lilies and noble coats of arms, transforming the square into a stage for celebration. Banners fluttered from the corners of the Baptistery, though no carpet yet lay upon the steps of the cathedral—its marble worn smooth by countless feet, none of them particularly distinguished. It was the hour of the Advent sermons, and just as the city’s streets had been painted with holiday colors, so too did the spiritual needs of the people demand the presence of preaching in the Duomo. Yet not all who moved through the piazza were bound for worship. Men of every rank hurried about their business, their strides brisk and purposeful. Clusters of talkers stood scattered across the open space—some indifferent to the sermon, others deliberately late, or simply uninterested. Their faces bore no trace of idle pleasure; instead, they carried expressions of tension, concentration, or quiet vigilance. Many turned their heads sharply at the sound of approaching footsteps, scanning strangers with wary eyes.

At the edge of the piazza, where the bright artificial light gave way to the dull grey of the Via de’ Cerretani, a group of working men gathered—men whose clothes bore the marks of their daily toil. Most carried tools or weapons: hammers, axes, knives, or staffs that could double as cudgels. Standing in the dimness, their bare arms and grimy garments formed a stark contrast to the splendor of the square. They listened intently to Ser Cioni, a thin notary on his way to the Duomo. Once scorned in the Mercato, he now spoke with the confidence of one who knew his party held power—and who sensed influence among the common folk.

“Never speak to me,” he declared in his sharp, cutting voice, “of bloodthirsty Swiss or fierce French infantry. Let them march through mountain passes if they like—but not our streets. Peasants have shattered the finest armies of our condottieri when trapped between cliffs. I say this plainly: Florentines need fear no army within their own walls.”

“That’s true, Ser Cioni,” replied Oddo, a dyer whose hands and arms were stained crimson—like blood, some might think. A small hatchet hung at his belt. “And those French cavaliers, strutting in their fine doublets the other day, got a taste of what we could serve them. I was carrying cloth near Ognissanti when I saw them pass—looking around as if the houses of the Vespucci and the Agli were beneath them, eyeing us like proud roosters pitying the poor. ‘Yes, my fine *Galli*,’ I said, ‘puff out your chests—I’ve got a meat-axe here that’ll slip right inside you.’ Then came the lowing of the cow—the great bell of Palazzo Vecchio—so I dropped my cloth, grabbed my axe, and ran after them toward the Vigna Nuova. ‘What is it, Guccio?’ I asked when he caught up. ‘I think it’s the Medici returning,’ he said. *Bembè!* I expected nothing less! We raised a barricade. The Frenchmen looked back and saw they were trapped. Our *Ciompi* surged forward—one man swung a scythe and sliced off a knight’s plume. True as truth! And the women threw stones to frighten them. But Piero de’ Medici didn’t come after all. A shame—because we would have left him no legs or wings to flee with.”

“Well said, Oddo,” chimed in a young butcher, knife at his side. “And I believe Piero won’t be back soon—he looked like a hunted chicken when we chased and stoned him in the piazza. Cowardly, or he’d have fought harder with his horsemen. But no more Medici, whatever the French king wants to force down our throats.”

“But I don’t like those French cannons they’re talking about,” said Goro, still stout despite two years of hardship. “San Giovanni protect us! If Messer Domeneddio truly cares for us, as your friar claims, Ser Cioni, why didn’t he send the French another way—to Naples?”

“Ay, Goro,” said the dyer. “That’s a fair question. You’re not such a fool as I thought. Why couldn’t they go through Bologna? Or Arezzo? We wouldn’t mind them going to Arezzo.”

“Fools!” Ser Cioni snapped. “It will be for Florence’s good and glory!”

But before he could continue, a cry rose from several voices: “Look there!”

All heads turned toward the Via de’ Cerretani, where a procession was emerging. “Lorenzo Tornabuoni,” Ser Cioni muttered, “and one of the French noblemen staying in his house.” He added with disdain, “He pretends contentment—that deep Tornabuoni—but he’s a Medicean at heart. Remember that.”

The group was indeed impressive: Lorenzo Tornabuoni, dignified and composed; a French nobleman in dazzling white linen and rich embroidery; two Florentine gentlemen in fine attire prepared for the coming procession; and beside the Frenchman, a figure whose presence commanded attention without needing adornment. He wore only black—mourning dress—but would soon don a red mantle as Latin Secretary to the Ten. This was Tito Melema.

Tito had become indispensable in dealings with the French, thanks to his knowledge of Southern Italy and fluency in French, learned in youth. He had visited the French camp at Signa multiple times. Now, fortune shone upon him. He smiled, listened, interpreted—his usual grace and ease undimmed. Yet even the keenest observer might have noticed a subtle shift in him—not due to time alone, but to a deeper transformation: the final shedding of moral youth, the conscious embrace of a role in life. His face remained soft, his eyes clear, but something vital had gone—like the fleeting glow of dawn before full day. It was the quiet certainty of self, now fixed in place.

The Frenchman sought last-minute guidance on ceremony before returning to Signa. He wished to survey the piazza, where the royal procession would pause for religious rites. As they entered, all eyes followed them—but not with uniform admiration. Murmurs arose about the Frenchman’s hoof-shaped shoes—mocking references to royal excesses. Slight sneers slipped out, barely disguised: “Mediceans,” they whispered, with venomous undertones.

Yet Lorenzo Tornabuoni mastered the art of masking annoyance—a necessity for one seeking favor. Tito, meanwhile, felt no such passion. As an outsider, he viewed local feuds with detachment, though he still responded with charm. When they reached a vantage point near the Duomo, they paused. The decorations above the central door stirred debate. Tornabuoni summoned Piero di Cosimo, who, as usual at this hour, lounged outside Nello’s shop. Soon, a lively exchange erupted—amusing to the Frenchman, who found Piero’s blunt wit astonishing. Tito translated faithfully, and laughter spread, even among those who had earlier glowered. Snarling onlookers softened into half-smiles, humbled by missing the joke. For Tito, it was a moment of pure joy—not triumph, but simple delight in being liked. He basked in the approval, glowing under the rainbow light that now seemed to crown the laughing group. Inside, the churchgoers had vanished. The piazza shimmered with the illusion of a real Florentine festival.

But beyond the decorated square, in the shadowed alleys, another procession approached—without finery, without cheer. Here, too, French dress and strange footwear stood out—but now they were surrounded by an ever-growing crowd of angry Florentines. Leading the group were three men, stripped nearly bare, hands bound together with rope, another looped around neck and body, allowing the captors to choke them if needed. The guards were French soldiers, using broken Italian phrases and blows from knotted ropes to force the prisoners to beg.

Two complied. To every passerby, they stretched their bound hands and pleaded in trembling voices:
“For the love of God and the Holy Madonna, give us something for our ransom! We are Tuscans—we were taken in Lunigiana.”

But the third man refused to speak. He was unlike the others—older, perhaps sixty-five or more. His beard and hair, long and unkempt, were almost white. Though gaunt, his frame remained strong, upright—an echo of strength still clinging to age. His dark eyes and thick brows stood out vividly against a face pale, wrinkled, and yellow, marked by exhaustion and neglect. Yet his gaze flickered—fierce one moment, lost the next. But his lips stayed sealed. His hands, clenched at his sides, refused to lift.

The sight enraged the Florentines. Some at doors or windows tossed coins—out of habit, or fear of the French soldiery, whose cruelty had bred a silent dread. But as the group advanced deeper into the city, resistance grew. The soldiers found themselves flanked by a swelling mob of men and boys, shouting insults loud enough to reach foreign ears.

“French dogs!”
“Bullock-feet!”
“Snatch their pikes!”
“Cut the cords and let them run! They’ll flee faster than geese—look at their webbed feet!”

The soldiers understood these as jeers—and possibly threats. They wanted to draw swords, but dared not, lest they lose control of their prisoners. So they hurried, hoping to find shelter in an inn.

“Santiddio! Look!” the dyer exclaimed, recognizing the scene. “And the fools just hoot. Come on!” He yanked his axe from his belt and charged, followed by the butcher and others—except Goro, who fled up a narrow lane.

The sight of the dyer rushing forward, axe raised, sent a spark through the crowd. He didn’t fight—just pushed ahead, joining the throng and waving his weapon like a banner of rebellion. And then, like a sudden flame, Lollo, the impish apprentice of the conjuror, darted forward. He had no pity for the prisoners, but he possessed a sharp knife—and a sense of mischief. From the start, he’d imagined leaping forward, slicing the rope, and retreating before the soldier could react. Now, as the crowd surged, he saw his chance. He was close to the oldest prisoner. In a flash, he cut the cord.

“Run, old man!” he cried into the prisoner’s ear, then bolted as if winged.

The prisoner reacted instantly. Escape had been his constant dream. The crowd’s fury had given him new hope. He ran—though not fast enough, had it not been for the Florentines who surged between him and his captor. He raced into the piazza, but behind him came the tramp of boots—the other two prisoners freed, the soldiers struggling forward, hindered by their clumsy shoes and the mob.

One younger prisoner veered into Borgo di San Lorenzo, easing the chaos. But the main clash headed toward the Duomo, drawing all eyes with alarmed curiosity. The cause was hidden—only glimpses of French uniforms visible through the press.

“An escape!” Lorenzo Tornabuoni said, turning as a prisoner sprinted past. “The people aren’t satisfied with emptying the Bargello yesterday. If no authority appears, they’ll attack the sbirri and free thieves. Ah—there’s a French soldier. That’s more serious.”

The soldier struggled along the north side of the piazza, but his quarry had taken another path. The eldest prisoner had circled the Baptistery and now raced toward the Duomo, determined to seek refuge within its sacred walls.

As he climbed the steps, his foot caught. He stumbled forward, crashing into a group of signori whose backs faced him. He grasped one man’s arm to steady himself.

It was Tito Melema.

Tito turned. And there, inches from his face, was the visage of Baldassarre Calvo—his adoptive father.

They stared at each other. No words. Only silence—deep, suffocating. Baldassarre’s eyes burned with dark intensity, his grip tightening on Tito’s velvet-clad arm. Tito’s face drained of color, frozen in horror. Time stretched—yet it lasted only a heartbeat.

Then, a short laugh broke the stillness. Piero di Cosimo, standing nearby, was the only one who saw Tito’s face.
“Ha, ha! Now I know what a ghost looks like.”

“This is another escaped prisoner,” said Tornabuoni. “Who is he?”

“_Some madman, surely_,” Tito murmured.

He didn’t know how the words had escaped. In moments like this, passion speaks before reason. And sometimes, such words carry poison—poison that acts like lightning, fulfilling what years of thought could never achieve.

Their eyes locked. And when Tito spoke, it felt as though a venomous glance had pierced him, racing through his veins.

Then—relaxation. The grip loosened. Baldassarre vanished into the church, leaving only silence and shock behind.

“You’re easily frightened, though,” Piero said, chuckling with contempt. “My portrait doesn’t do justice to the original. But that old man *did* have a tiger’s look—let’s go into the Duomo and see him again.”

“It’s no pleasure to be seized by a madman—if he truly is one,” Lorenzo Tornabuoni replied, speaking gently in defense of Tito. “But perhaps he’s merely a brute. We’ll hear more soon enough. Still, I think we should check whether we have the authority to stop this quarrel between our men and yours,” he added, turning to the Frenchman.

With swords drawn, they moved forward through the crowd, the quiet onlookers parting like an escort before them. Tito followed—though not from choice. He had to know what others knew about Baldassarre. The initial surge of terror was fading, replaced by frantic calculations born of mortal danger: the timid mind, when pressed, invents swiftly.

The rabble—boys and rough men alike—were more inclined to jeer at the soldier than harm him. They scattered as the noblemen approached, their weapons gleaming in the sun. The French soldier was questioned.

He and his comrades had brought their prisoners into Florence only to solicit ransom money. Two were Tuscan soldiers captured in Lunigiana; the third—an elderly man—had been traveling with a group of Genoese when the French foragers clashed near Fivizzano. The prisoner might be mad, but he posed no real threat. The soldier admitted he couldn’t understand a word the old man spoke.

Tito heard this much—but nothing beyond it until directly addressed. It was Tornabuoni who spoke.

“Will you return with us, Melema? Or, since Messere is departing for Signa, will you follow the times’ fashion and go hear the Frate? He’ll be at full flood this morning—like a torrent unleashed. We all must, if we wish to preserve our Medicean skins. I would go myself, if I had the time.”

Tito’s color had returned. He managed a smile, forcing lightness into his voice. “Of course, I admire the inspired orator,” he said. “But alas, I shall be detained by the Segretario until the procession begins.”

“I’m going back into the Duomo to see that savage old man again,” Piero declared.

“Then show mercy and take him to one of the hospitals for travelers,” Tornabuoni replied. “The monks may determine whether he needs caging.”

They parted ways. Tito walked toward the Palazzo Vecchio, where Bartolommeo Scala awaited him. The journey was short, yet for him it stretched into eternity—each step across street and piazza a moment thick with memory, dread, and foreboding. It felt as though a serpent had begun coiling around his limbs.

Baldassarre lived. In Florence. And that was a living vengeance, restless as a serpent, never still until its prey was crushed.

That man did not let wrongs pass. His love and hatred burned with such fierce intensity that they consumed everything else within him—driving him to sacrifice himself, even, for the sake of passion, as if it were a god demanding worship through self-destruction.

Once, Baldassarre had vanished. Tito understood the silence well: it meant the vengeance was being prepared—studied, shaped, made certain.

If only he had spoken differently. If only he could have summoned the courage, the composure, to admit recognition. Would the danger then have been less? He might have claimed he possessed proof of Baldassarrne’s death. Only two people could ever contradict such a claim: Fra Luca, who was dead, and the crew of the galley that had brought news of the pirate encounter. The odds were vast against Baldassarre ever meeting any of them again. And now, bitterly, Tito thought: a well-timed lie, carefully constructed, might have spared him all this.

But to lie required control—a mastery of self in the midst of shock. He had spoken without thought. The words burst forth like a child born in darkness, nurtured in secrecy.

Tito was learning a cruel truth of the human soul: we shape ourselves through repeated choices—good or evil—until character becomes fixed, and sudden acts are inevitable.

Now, there was only one hope: that Baldassarre failed. That his pursuit ended in futility.

And then—Tito grasped a darker idea, sharper than any he’d ever allowed himself: perhaps his own unthinking words held some truth. Enough truth, at least, to defend himself against any accusation Baldassarre might make.

The old man looked wild, deranged. With a heart so eager and a mind so tormented, madness was possible. Suffering could twist reason. If so, then the vengeance aimed at disgrace might collapse under its own absurdity.

Yet another kind of vengeance remained—one that no lie could escape.

Baldassarre came from a breed for whom the dagger feels as natural as the tiger’s claw. Tito recoiled—not just from shame, but from physical fear. A soft nature, indulgent and pleasure-seeking, fears death not as release, but as a violent end. He longed for armor—some hidden shield—to protect him from a fate no deceit could deflect.

He marveled at the power of his own terror. It was as though a blight had struck him, turning the warmth of youth into cold pain.

Still, one path remained open: he could turn back, seek out Baldassarre, confess everything—to him, to Romola, to the world. But he never considered it.

True repentance—what breaks ties with evil—requires more than fear. It demands moral strength. Tito lacked that sense. He saw no safety in truth. His only trust lay in cleverness, in concealment.

Now that the first wave of panic had passed, he believed he could face anything—with calm lies and defensive plans.

It was telling, in this crisis, that Tito never conceived of any direct way to remove Baldassarre. All other options crossed his mind—even flight from Florence—but not a single plan to destroy or neutralize his enemy.

His fear bred no hatred. He still wished no one pain. He had simply chosen to live comfortably, to carry life lightly, avoiding every pinch. And this choice, over time, had led him into traps he never foresaw.

The question was no longer whether he would share in the common burden of fate with others. It was whether lies—every trick, every falsehood—could save him from being crushed beneath the weight of his own choices.

When Baldassarre, hands bound and a rope tight about his neck and body, forced his way behind the heavy curtain and stepped into the cathedral, he froze in astonishment. He had imagined the great church as a hollow shell—empty of life, filled only with cold relics: dim altars, unlit candles, faded paintings, stiff statues. Perhaps a few distant worshippers murmuring prayers in the choir. That was how churches appeared to one who entered them not for faith, but by accident or necessity.

But what he saw instead was alive—overwhelmingly so. The vast nave teemed with people, faces turned upward, breath held, eyes fixed on the pulpit where a Dominican friar stood, crucifix in hand, preaching with fierce intensity. The crowd was a cross-section of Florence—nobles in fine robes, merchants in sober dress, artisans in rough wool, peasants from the hills—all united in silence, drawn into the power of the man’s voice.

For a moment, Baldassarre saw nothing of the sermon. His entrance, though quiet, had drawn glances. Some near the door turned toward him, surprised, wary. The rope spoke plainly: prisoner, fugitive. Yet this was a sanctuary. He could claim it. His age, his gaunt face, the wild despair in his eyes—all suggested sorrow rather than threat. And when he stood still, staring blankly at the stone floor, those who had noticed him soon looked away, pulled back into the spell of the preacher’s words.

Among them was Romola. She had come late through a side door and stood where she could see the main entrance clearly. Her gaze fell upon Baldassarre at once. Grey hair always stirred her compassion; and the deep lines of suffering on his face, confirmed by the cord around his neck, touched something deep within her—a tenderness shaped by years of witnessing human pain. She thought their eyes met briefly, but in truth, Baldassarre saw nothing. His mind was a storm—Tito’s betrayal, his own rage, the crushing weight of past failures—swirling like fire in his veins. Images flashed: memories of youth, love, loss, all blurred by thirst and anguish. No future formed in his thoughts. Only a sense of weakness, and a resolve to distrust everything, everyone.

Then—suddenly—he felt a vibration in the air, a thunderous voice that seemed to echo his own inner fury.

“The day of vengeance is at hand!”

The words struck him like lightning. He jerked upright, searching for the source. He could not see the preacher clearly, only a dark figure with outstretched arm, holding aloft the cross. But the voice—deep, commanding, ringing with certainty—pulled him forward. It wasn’t just sound; it was a promise. A relief. A kind of ecstasy.

There was a pause. Then the friar lowered his arm, placed the crucifix on the pulpit edge, and folded his arms across his chest, gazing steadily at the crowd as if seeing each soul.

“All ye in Florence are my witnesses,” he began, “for I spoke not in a corner. Four years ago, before war came or tribulation showed its face, I warned you: the cup of your iniquity is full. I lifted my voice like a trumpet to princes, prelates, and people of Italy. Behold, the thunder of the Lord gathers—and it shall fall, shattering the cup. Your sins, which you call sweet wine, will be poured out like molten lead upon you.

“And you, O priests, who mock and say, ‘Ha, ha! There is no presence in the sanctuary—the Shechinah is gone!’ You who sin behind the veil—know this: God will reveal Himself in His temple as a consuming fire. Your sacred garments shall become shrouds of flame. Sweet music shall turn to shrieks. Soft couches to thorns. The breath of wantonness shall bring pestilence.

“Do not trust in gold or silver. Do not trust in high walls or iron fortresses. Though they were of adamant, the Most High will strike terror into your hearts, weaken your councils, and you shall flee—like women.

“He will break mighty men without number, and raise others in their place. For God cannot endure the pollution of His sanctuary. He will purge His Church.”

He paused, then continued, voice rising like a storm.

“And since Scripture says God reveals His purpose to His prophets, He has chosen me—His unworthy servant—and shown me His will in the Word, in history, in visions sent by angels. His word possesses me. I am like a branch shaken by the wind—I cannot remain silent, even if mocked.

“For four years I have preached obedience to the Divine will. In the face of scorn, I have spoken three truths: _God will regenerate His Church_. _Before regeneration comes the scourge over all Italy_. _And these things will come quickly_.

“But hypocrites—those who pretend piety while hating the truth—have said, ‘Frate, cease your prophecies. Teach virtue instead.’

“To them I answer: You say in your hearts, ‘God is far off. His word is dead parchment.’ But I cry again: God is near. His judgments do not change. He is the God of armies. Strong men go forth to battle—His ministers, like storm and fire and plague. He drives them by the breath of His angels, and they descend upon the land that has broken the covenant.

“And thou, O Italy, art that land. Has not God placed His sanctuary within thee? And hast thou not polluted it?

“Behold—His ministers of wrath are upon thee. They stand at thy very doors!”

The voice dropped, yet carried deeper. The silence that followed was heavier than any sound.

“Yet there is a pause—even as in the days when Jerusalem fell, there was time for the children of God to flee. There is stillness before the storm. Blackness above—but not a leaf trembles. The winds are stayed, that God’s warning may be heard.

“Hear now, O Florence, chosen city in the chosen land! Repent. Forsake evil. Do justice. Love mercy. Purify yourselves, that the spirit of truth and holiness may fill your souls and breathe through every street and home. Then the pestilence shall not enter, and the sword shall pass over you unharmed.

“For the sword hangs from the sky. It quivers. It is about to fall! _The sword of God upon the earth—swift and sudden!_

“Did I not tell you years ago I had seen the vision and heard the voice? Behold—it is fulfilled! Is there not a king with an army at your gates? Does not the earth shake beneath horses and swift cannon? Is there not a furious host that can strip the land bare as with a razor?

“I tell you: the French king and his army are God’s instrument. He shall guide him like a hand guides a sickle. The joints of the wicked shall melt before him. They shall fall like stubble. None shall escape. None shall be delivered.

“And the tyrants—those who built thrones on the vices of the people—the unbelieving priests who trade in souls and defile the sanctuary with lust—shall be cast from their soft beds into burning hell. The pagans and those who sinned under the old covenant shall stand aside and say: ‘Lo, these men have brought a new stench into the everlasting fire.’”

Then the tone shifted. The preacher’s voice softened, pleading.

“Listen, O people—over whom my heart yearns as a mother for her children! God is my witness: if it were not for you, I would live in solitude, singing low to my Beloved, who is mine and I am His. For you I labor. For you I suffer. My nights are spent in prayer. My soul melts with heaviness.

“O Lord, I am ready. Take me. Stretch me on Thy cross. Let the wicked laugh, wag their heads, spit at me. Let thorns press upon my brow. Let my sweat be anguish. I desire to be made like Thee in Thy great love.

“But let me see the fruit of my travail. Let this people be saved. Let them be clothed in purity. Let their voices rise in harmony, like angels’ songs. Let them seek wisdom only in Thy eternal law, beauty only in holiness.

“Then they shall lead the nations. Peoples from the four winds shall follow them, gathered into the fold of the blessed. For it is Thy will, O God, that the earth be converted to Thy law. That wickedness cease and love reign.

“Come, O blessed promise! Behold, I am willing—lay me on the altar. Let my blood flow. Let the fire consume me. But let my witness endure among men—that iniquity shall not prosper forever.”

As he finished, Savonarola raised his arms, eyes lifted to heaven. His voice trembled, then rose again, but finally broke. He fell to his knees, burying his face in his mantle.

A sob tore through the congregation—deep, collective, overwhelming. Even those who doubted the friar’s visions, who scoffed at his claims, were swept up by the emotion. It was not reason, but feeling—the shared pulse of a people trembling between fear and hope.

And among them, Baldassarre sobbed too.

No one in that cathedral felt the sermon more deeply than he. Yet his response was not like theirs. His soul was stripped bare. Every string of his being had snapped except one—the thread of vengeance. The preacher’s fire, the promise of divine retribution, struck him like water to a dying man. The teachings of philosophers, the old rationalism, the scorn for priestly superstition—gone. Like ash. What could they offer him now? Nothing. Not when he needed a reckoning.

The thunder of condemnation resonated in his bones—not as doctrine, but as truth. He did not question. He did not reflect. He *was* the anger. The voice spoke of judgment, and he drank it in. He had no room for mercy. No nerve to feel pity. Only the hunger for punishment.

And then—when the friar offered himself as sacrifice, when he cried, “Let my blood flow!”—Baldassarre found release. That self-giving, that willingness to die for a cause, echoed his own secret vow. He too was offering himself—not to God, but to hatred. To an endless fire that would cool his burning soul.

“I rescued him—I cherished him—if I might clutch his heart-strings for ever! Come, O blessed promise! Let my blood flow; let the fire consume me!”

The single string of his soul vibrated to its breaking point. He clenched his fists, driving his nails into his palms, and joined the cry of the multitude—a sob torn from the depths of a broken man.

While Baldassarre stood transfixed by Savonarola’s words, lost in the storm of emotion they stirred within him, he did not notice that another man had slipped through the doorway behind him. This newcomer paused a few paces away, watching silently. It was Piero di Cosimo, who had come not to hear the sermon, but to see the prisoner who had escaped from custody. He paid no attention to the preacher’s voice, his gaze fixed solely on the man before him.

As the crowd fell into a hush—caught in a wave of unspoken feeling—Piero stepped forward and gently touched Baldassarre’s arm. The prisoner turned, tears still tracing slow paths down his cheeks, but with a deep breath, as if releasing something long held within. His expression was weary yet resolute.

“Shall I cut your cords?” Piero whispered. “I’ve heard how you were taken.”

Baldassarre hesitated, his eyes narrowing with suspicion. Then, after a moment, he nodded once. “If you will.”

“Better go outside,” Piero said, already turning toward the door.

Again, Baldassarre studied him warily. Piero, sensing the doubt, smiled faintly, pulled out a knife, and severed the bonds. As he did so, he thought: *He might be mad—there’s something wild in his face. But if he causes trouble, they’ll bind him again. Let him have this chance, at least.*

“You’re afraid of me,” Piero murmured. “You won’t tell me anything about yourself.”

Baldassarre rubbed his arms, savoring the return of sensation after long confinement. His tone softened slightly, though caution remained. “No,” he said quietly. “I have nothing to say.”

“As you wish,” Piero replied. “But perhaps you need shelter. You don’t know how Florence treats strangers—especially those with torn clothes and empty stomachs. There’s an hospital for travelers just beyond the gate. And if you like, I can show you where it is. Your French soldier has been sent away.”

Baldassarre gave a single nod and followed without a word. Together, they left the church.

“Would you mind sitting for a portrait?” Piero asked as they walked along the Via dell’Oriuolo, heading toward Santa Croce. “I’m a painter. I’d pay you well.”

A flicker of distrust returned to Baldassarre’s eyes. “No,” he said firmly.

“Aha,” Piero muttered, short and sharp. “Then go straight ahead—you’ll find the Porta Santa Croce. Outside, there’s the hospital. So you won’t accept any help from me?”

“I thank you for what you’ve done,” Baldassarre answered. “I need nothing more.”

“Very well,” Piero said, shrugging. They parted ways.

*What a curious creature,* Piero thought. *Like an old tiger—ugly, deeply lined, as if life had plowed its fields across his heart. A striking contrast to my smooth Messer Greco—my Bacco trionfante, who married Antigone against all reason and history. Ah, yes—the scholar’s blood must have curdled when that man gripped him.*

When Piero returned to the Piazza del Duomo, the crowd was streaming out from every entrance. The urgency with which people dispersed showed how deeply the sermon had gripped them—how essential it had felt to pause their daily lives for these words.

Leaning against the corner of the Baptistery, Piero watched the throng pass by, delighting in the diversity of faces and garments—faces much like those Masaccio had painted half a century earlier, or those Ghirlandajo still captured today. This morning was exceptional. Savonarola’s audience had drawn from every stratum of Florentine society.

There were noble men—some newly prominent in opposing the Medici and championing popular rule—men now fervent followers of the friar, calling San Marco the new seat of divine leadership. Among them were figures like Francesco Valori, bold and proud, thrilled by the idea that God Himself sanctioned their rebellion; others, like Soderini, more cautious, blending piety with political wisdom. Then there were men of lineage like Piero Capponi—no theologians, but patriots who valued liberty above doctrine, ready to fight rather than debate.

Beside them stood scholars and lawyers whose studies had not dulled their passion—men like Luca Corsini, whose scholarly hands would one day hurl stones in defense of faith and freedom. And among the dignitaries in black robes or fur-lined cloaks, there were quieter souls: scholars bearing noble names like Strozzi and Acciajoli, already considering the monk’s habit; artists inspired by Savonarola’s message, such as the young painter who had recently adorned the friar’s cell with a fresco of the Christ Child—unaware that one day he too would take vows and become Fra Bartolommeo.

There was Girolamo Benevieni, the mystic poet, rushing perhaps to bring news of Savonarola’s coming to his friend Pico della Mirandola—who would never live to see dawn again. Women of high birth moved among them, dressed with austere elegance, their grace marked not by finery but by restraint.

Yet most numerous were the *popolani*—the middle class, members of both Major and Minor Arts, burdened by war taxes and fearful for their livelihoods. And then came the poorer artisans and tradesmen—men whose faith in Savonarola ranged from simple trust in a champion of the poor to intense devotion to biblical mysteries, finding meaning in scripture during long hours of labor. For them, the friar’s words lit a flame in the dimness of their workdays—a light they believed divine.

But not all present were true disciples. Some were Mediceans pretending reverence out of fear or calculation. Others were republicans who saw Savonarola not as a saint, but as a powerful tool—ambitious, half-crazed, yet effective. Still others were enemies: aristocrats determined to reclaim power, or reckless youths who hated him for spoiling their pleasures.

The sermons had become events—political spectacles as much as spiritual ones. Men like Niccolò Macchiavelli came to observe, to report. Men like Dolfo Spini came to hate, to gather evidence against a figure they deemed a nuisance.

Savonarola’s influence was immense—but his audience was a mosaic of motives. His preaching reached far beyond theology. It spoke to the noblest ideals, yes—but also to greed, curiosity, and superstition. His visions, allegories, and claims of divine knowledge captivated minds of every kind. Even as he preached with fire and conviction, his need for dominance shaped his message. Yet within his soul, that ambition was tempered by genuine piety, a deep sense of justice, and a fierce love for humanity.

For the masses, however, it was not the depth of his faith that mattered—it was the certainty of his revelations, the drama of his warnings, the way his words echoed like political pronouncements. Once he held them, he could not let go. To keep them, he had to lower his standards—to meet their fears, their desires, their ignorance.

And so, inevitably, the man changed. No leader retains power over such a mixed multitude without being altered by it. His vision narrowed. His truth bent to fit their needs.

Yet in Savonarola, we see not a villain, nor a saint—but a complex human being. His life is a tragedy of conflicting forces: zeal and pride, holiness and hunger for control. Up to this point, his desire for power may have burned hidden beneath the greater flame of his mission.

It was once common, when preparing an ox for sacrifice to Jupiter, to paint over its dark spots, making it seem pure white. Let us discard that false whitewash. Yes, the victim is flawed. But does that make his sacrifice meaningless? No. For even with blemishes, his mighty heart rests upon the altar of mankind’s highest hopes—and that, alone, gives his life weight.

By six o’clock that evening, the people of Florence breathed a collective sigh of relief. The grand entrance of the new Charlemagne—King Charles VIII of France—had at last come to an end. Earlier, when the thunder of drums, the blare of trumpets, and the steady tramp of horses along the Pisan road began to blend with the frantic ringing of church bells, it had been a moment of awe for those perched atop turreted roofs. From there, they watched the long, imposing procession unfold against the backdrop of green hills and valleys. Though the sky was overcast and no sunlight illuminated the banners, spears, plumes, or silken surcoats, the absence of dust allowed every detail to be seen clearly. The picked troops advanced in disciplined order: tall, wiry Scottish archers; broad-shouldered Swiss halberdiers; agile Gascons ready to shift formation or scale walls; and cavalrymen who looked like knights from legend, each mounted on a powerful steed and armed with a spear that seemed capable of piercing any foe. To the citizens, this display of martial strength was reassuring—it meant these men would harm only the enemies of God.

With that certainty, the spectacle became more than mere pageantry. It was a triumph of power and piety alike. Nobles and knights rode in full regalia, their swords encrusted with jewels, their scarves embroidered with sacred symbols or gallant emblems, their chains and aigrettes gleaming under the dim light. Horses were draped in rich brocade, and above the king himself floated a magnificent canopy carried by chosen youths. As one old chronicler noted, though his language lagged behind the splendour he witnessed—“_fù gran magnificenza_.”

Yet for the Signoria, gathered on their platform near the city gates, the occasion had been less glorious. They had waited anxiously, knowing they must march out at precisely the right moment, led by their orator, to greet the royal guest. But when rain began to fall, it disrupted the ceremony. Messer Luca Corsini, tasked with delivering a formal Latin welcome, found his words obscured by the downpour. The sudden discomfort among men and horses caused the speech to falter, and the dignitaries, unprepared for such chaos, were forced into improvisation. In desperation, Soderini called out, “Someone step forward and say a few words in French!”

No high-ranking official stepped forward, fearing another failure. Then Gaddi, hesitating, pushed Tito Melema forward. “You, Melema—you can speak.”

Tito moved without hesitation. With the natural grace and deep deference that came as easily to him as breathing, he delivered a brief, polished greeting in French on behalf of the Signoria. He then stepped aside, allowing the king to pass. His composure—so absent earlier that morning during the crisis—now served him well. It was a rare quality: the kind of presence of mind that appears only when danger is not immediate, but real.

When praised later, Tito dismissed the moment as trivial, even jokingly attributing the success to Gaddi. That humility won him favor. People often judge others by how well they serve their own pride—and Tito knew how to make them feel generous.

Still, other aspects of the day had gone poorly. The Florentine procession of clergy and laymen had been blocked, forcing them to take a detour through narrow back streets just to reach the Cathedral. And while the young king sat beneath his golden canopy, lance resting on his thigh, he did not look like the noble Charlemagne of legend. Instead, he resembled a hastily carved puppet—his leg thin and deformed, his mouth too wide, his nose and head exaggerated in outline. Yet none of this mattered much to the crowd. The leg rested on cloth of gold and pearls; the face was merely a small flaw amid the blaze of rubies, velvet, and embroidery. The canopy itself was a masterpiece of artistry, and when the people tore it apart—as custom demanded—they roared _Francia, Francia!_ with fervor. The king kissed the altar, and despite the missteps, he and his retinue were lodged in the Palace of the Via Larga. The nobles dispersed among the great houses of Florence, and the army took up positions in the Prato and other open spaces.

The day’s work was done.

But Florence remained alive under the November stars. Where once darkness had settled, broken only by faint lamplight or the glow from open doors, now every window was lit. The streets shimmered with illumination, so bright that walking at night felt as safe and easy as daylight. Again, the chronicler wrote—“_fù gran magnificenza_.”

At about eight o’clock, Tito Melema walked home along the Via dei Benci, passing from the Piazza di Santa Croce toward the Borgo Santa Croce. He had spent the entire day managing his duties while wrestling with private fears. Now, finally free from the noise and obligation of supper parties, he walked in silence, trying to gather his thoughts. He hoped, if he could fully confront his situation, that he might rid himself of the childish dread that still clung to him.

He regretted not recognizing Baldassarre earlier—under that shock of surprise. Had he done so, things might have turned out differently. Still, he felt a pang of guilt. He wished his father could live prosperously, happily. But now there was no turning back. No conflict remained. Only survival. He must protect himself.

As he approached the corner of the Borgo, a sound struck his ear—not music of revelry, but the rhythmic clang of metal on metal. The music of the anvil.

Tito quickened his pace. The sound belonged to Niccolò Caparra, the renowned blacksmith whose workshop was famed throughout Florence for its exquisite ironwork. Why was the giant working so late? Tito wondered. But it was good fortune. He could attend to his business tonight instead of waiting until tomorrow.

Though preoccupied, he paused before the shop, drawn by its grandeur. The wide doorway stood at the truncated angle of a large block of buildings, topped by a loggia with fluted tiles and stone columns bearing roughly carved capitals. Against the red glow within, framed by the architectural lines, stood Niccolò—a towering figure, arms rising and falling in perfect rhythm, hammering the steel. His face, firm-jawed and strong-browed, appeared and disappeared in shadow.
