Two smaller figures worked beside him—one at the anvil, the other pumping the bellows—highlighting Niccolò’s massive presence.

Tito stood in the doorway, silent. There was no point speaking until the smith noticed him.

It wasn’t until Niccolò finished sharpening the head of an axe and set it aside that he turned, hammer resting on his hip, and gave a gruff but friendly nod. “What is it, Messer Tito? Business?”

“Assuredly,” Tito replied. “Else I wouldn’t dare interrupt you when you’re working beyond hours. I take it as a sign your work is urgent.”

“I’ve been at the same task all day—making axes and spearheads,” Niccolò said. “And every fool who passed by stuck his head in and asked, ‘Niccolò, will you come see the King of France?’ I told them all, ‘No. I don’t want to see their faces—I want to see their backs.’”

“Are you forging arms for the citizens?” Tito asked. “So they may have better weapons than rusty scythes and kitchen spits if trouble breaks out?”

“We shall see,” Niccolò answered. “Arms are good. Florence may need them. The Frate says we’ll reclaim Pisa. I believe him. But how will it happen if we don’t have proper weapons? The Frate sees far ahead—that I grant. But he doesn’t think birds can be caught by blinking at them, as some of our people pretend. He sees reason, not nonsense.”

“You’re a bit of a Medicean yourself, Messer Tito Melema,” Niccolò added. “Ebbene! So was I, once—before the cask began to sour. What do you want?”

“Only to know the price of the fine coat of mail I saw here the other day,” Tito said. “I wish to buy it for someone who needs protection beneath his doublet.”

“Let him come and buy it himself,” Niccolò replied bluntly. “I’m careful whom I sell to. I like to know my customer.”

“I understand your scruples,” Tito said. “But this is defensive armour. It cannot hurt anyone.”

“True,” Niccolò said. “But it may make the man who wears it feel safer—safer to hurt someone else. It’s not my own work—but it’s fine craftsmanship by Maso of Brescia. I wouldn’t want it covering the heart of a villain. I must know who will wear it.”

“Well, then,” Tito said, “I want it myself.” He knew persuasion would fail. “To be honest, I am planning a journey—and you know what journeys mean these days. You don’t suspect me of treason against the Republic?”

“No,” Niccolò said, straightforward as ever. “I know no harm in you. But do you have the money? You’ve passed my shop often enough to see my sign—the burning account-books. I trust no one. Twenty florins. It’s second-hand, but still worth that. You’re unlikely to carry so much coin tonight. Let it wait till tomorrow.”

“I happen to have it,” Tito said. He had won at cards the day before and hadn’t emptied his purse. “I’ll take it now.”

Niccolò reached down and lifted the finely forged coat of mail. It folded into little more than two handfuls. After Tito counted the florins into his palm, Niccolò handed it over.

“There,” he said. “It will guard you from sword, poniard, or arrow. But for my part, I’d never wear such a thing. It’s like carrying fear with you.”

Tito smiled, though the words struck him deeply. “Ah, Niccolò,” he said, “we scholars are all cowards. A pen does not strengthen the arm as your hammer does.”

“Addio,” Niccolò said.

Tito folded the armour under his mantle and hurried across the Ponte Rubaconte, the weight of it pressing against his chest—not just metal, but a garment of fear.

Romola paced the length of the old library, her steps quiet on the worn stone floor, her eyes fixed on the door as if she could will Tito’s return through sheer longing. Outside, Florence buzzed with the arrival of the French king and his court—windows flung open, crowds gathered, faces bright with anticipation. But Romola had not looked out. Not since her father’s death three months earlier had she taken part in any festivity. That day, when he had fallen suddenly in his chair, still waiting for Tito to come write with him, the silence that followed was more final than any word.

He had asked, just once, “Is not Tito coming to write?”—a question born of hope, not fear. She had answered gently, knowing the truth: Tito had been summoned to a supper at the cardinal’s, one of many such invitations that now crowded his evenings. Bardo had sighed, then rallied with a spark of urgency: “Ah! Then perhaps he will bring news about the library? The cardinal promised last week.” He paused, then flushed, and said, “I must go on without him, Romola. Get the pen. I have no new text—but I must say what I want to say about the New Platonists. I shall die and nothing will be done.”

She brought the pen. He spoke no more.

She remembered that moment later with a pang of shame—not because she mourned too little, but because even in grief, a whisper had stirred within her: *Now my life with Tito may truly begin.*

It was not that she blamed him. Tito had always been tender, attentive, respectful toward her father. But she saw now, with clarity, that no one else could bear the weight of those endless hours—of reading aloud, of copying manuscripts, of enduring the slow erosion of a man’s mind by sorrow and unfulfilled dreams. Even she, who had loved her father with all the passion of youth, had sometimes felt rebellion stir beneath her patience. Before marriage, Tito had seemed stronger in endurance; but then, novelty made effort light. Now, it wore thin.

She told herself she had been foolish once—a girl dreaming of a perfect life where love, duty, and joy would merge into one radiant whole. But reality was less luminous. The sweetness of marriage had been tempered by the quiet ache of absence. Tito was never truly absent from her heart, yet he was often gone—drawn into circles she could not enter, into conversations that left her feeling like a shadow beside him.

She did not resent him. How could she? His caresses were as warm as ever. When she hesitated, timidly asking him to stay, he smiled and lingered, brushing her hair, murmuring words that melted her fears. But she knew—deep down—that this was not enough. It was not his fault. It was simply that their natures were different. Hers was rooted in devotion, in loyalty to memory, in the sacredness of obligation. His was shaped by charm, by ease, by the thrill of the world beyond the walls of their home.

And so she struggled—to understand, to forgive, to hold back the thoughts that might wound him. She refused to let resentment grow. To admit that Tito acted unworthily would be to betray the love she cherished. Instead, she turned inward, seeking strength in endurance. If he could not give her the time she needed, she would make do with the moments he gave. And now, with her father gone, there was no longer any claim dividing them. Their lives could flow together at last.

Yet even this hope carried a weight. The thought of joy in his absence felt like a betrayal. So she clung harder to her purpose—to fulfill her father’s dream. The library, his life’s work, was not merely a collection of texts and relics. It was a testament. A promise kept. And though Bernardo del Nero had saved it from creditors, offering a thousand florins against a lien, the future remained uncertain. The Medici were gone. Their collections, once safe in the Via Larga, now faced dispersal. The French agents already eyed Lorenzo’s gems as rightful spoils. Florence, burdened by war and debt, might sell its treasures for gold.

To Romola, these political shifts mattered only insofar as they threatened her father’s legacy. She had lived apart from the world, raised in books and silence. Heroism, revolution, statecraft—these were distant ideas, like myths from ancient Greece. But now, the fate of the library pulled her into the currents of history. She began to care. She wanted to know what Fra Girolamo preached, what power he wielded over the people, what role he might play in the city’s fate.

That morning, for the first time, she had gone to hear one of his Advent sermons in the Duomo. After visiting her father’s grave in Santa Croce, she walked on, drawn by curiosity and a sense of duty. The memory of Dino still stirred in her—the boy whose voice had once pierced her soul with questions about justice and faith. But that moment had faded under the weight of married life, subsumed by love and routine.

Still, she had expected something from Fra Girolamo. His voice, his presence, had struck her before. She hoped he might move her—not as a fanatic, but as a man of depth. And he did. Not with terror or guilt, but with a strange resonance. When he spoke of martyrdom, she wept. Not from fear, but from a sudden, inexplicable connection—a feeling as if she stood on the edge of something vast, something beyond her own small world.

It was like music remembered, then heard again in full force. But it did not settle in her heart. It passed, leaving only a trace.

Now, she waited. Listened. For three months, each day had felt like a new beginning. She had tried to be gentle, to suppress the criticism that rose when Tito spoke of banquets and politics. She had held back suggestions about frugality, about paying off Bernardo del Nero. She knew Tito valued comfort. He worked hard, yes, but for pleasures that were not hers. And so she resolved to change—no more sharp edges, no more silent judgments. She would become softer, more yielding, so that their union might be whole.

At last, she heard the great door creak open. She hurried to the top of the stairs, heart beating.

Tito ascended slowly, his face tired, his cloak thrown back. The lamp above cast shadows across his features. In eighteen months, Romola had changed more than he. Her expression was softer now, her gaze beseeching. A flush warmed her cheeks as she saw him.

“Tito,” she said, “you are weary. Was it a long day?”

Maso stood nearby, but no further words were spoken until they entered the library. The fire burned brightly in the grate, casting flickering light across the room. Tito kissed her briefly, then sank into his high-backed chair near the flames, tossing aside his cap.

“I wish you would stop sitting here,” he said, his voice heavy with fatigue. “Our rooms are warmer. This weather is cruel.”

Romola blinked. This was not the Tito she knew—the man who always noticed her cold, who brought her wine, who laughed at her smallest worries. Something was wrong.

“You forget,” she said softly, “I am finishing the catalogue—my father’s plan. You cannot help me, so I must work alone.”

Tito closed his eyes, rubbing his temples. He felt alienated—unlike himself. He could not explain why. Some deep fear, some secret dread, had begun to gnaw at him, making him feel distant from her, even afraid of her mind. He had never meant to be cold. But the effort to smile, to speak kindly, felt impossible.

“I am not well,” he said. “Do not be surprised if I seem irritable.”

Romola knelt beside him, brushing his hair from his forehead. Then, suddenly, she froze. “What is that under your tunic, Tito? Something hard… like iron.”

“It is iron,” he said quietly. “Chain-armour.”

She stared. “You bought it? For yourself?”

“For protection,” he replied. “There was danger today. I shall wear it often.”

Her breath caught. “Danger? What threatens you?”

“Everyone who is not a rabid enemy of the Medici,” he said, forcing a smile. “But don’t worry. This armour will keep me safe from ambush.”

She clung to him, trembling. “Then my godfather—what of him? He takes no precautions. Should he not?”

“Precisely because I am less important,” Tito said, “I am more vulnerable. They suspect me of being an envoy. Men like Bernardo are protected by family and influence. I am a Greek—no one will avenge me.”

“But Tito,” she whispered, “is it a specific threat? Or just fear?”

He hesitated. “I have received threats. But I beg you—do not speak of this. Not even to your godfather.”

“I will not,” she said, blushing. “But… this will make you unhappy.”

“What will make me wretched?” he asked, a flicker of pain crossing his face.

“This fear… this heavy metal. It feels like a curse. As if some fiend had turned your skin to steel. It is so unlike you—so unlike my bright, joyful husband.”

Tito smiled faintly. “Then you’d rather have me stabbed or shot? I’ll give up the armour—shall I?”

“No,” she said quickly. “It is fanciful. Do not mind me. But are such crimes common in Florence now?”

“They may become so,” he said. “With hatreds growing daily.”

They sat in silence for a while. Then Romola lifted her head. “Tell me about today. Did everything go well?”

“Excellently,” he said. “First, the rain ended Luca Corsini’s speech—no one wanted to hear it. Then someone—Gaddi, or Melema, or perhaps it was me—gave the Cristianissimo a brief, clumsy welcome in broken French.”

Romola laughed. “It was you, wasn’t it? You never take credit for anything.”

He grinned. “After that, there was a parade of arms and jewels—more than at any Florentine joust. Strutting, prancing, chaos. The crowd roared. The king smiled. Then dinner, flattery, games. I was at Tornabuoni’s. I’ll tell you tomorrow.”

“Not tonight,” she said, leaning closer. “But—do you think peace is possible for Florence? Will the Republic avoid fresh troubles?”

Tito shrugged. “Florence will have no peace unless it pays dearly for it.”

Romola’s smile faltered. But she forced cheerfulness. “You wouldn’t guess where I went today. I went to the Duomo. To hear Fra Girolamo.”

Tito stiffened. He had thought of Baldassarre—had feared he might have entered the church. But Romola mistook his look.

“You’re surprised?” she said, smiling. “I wanted to understand the times. I wanted to hear what the Frate says about the French invasion.”

“And what did you think of the prophet?”

“He has a mysterious power,” she said. “Much of it I expected. But once—I was deeply moved. I wept with the others.”

“Take care,” Tito said lightly, relieved she hadn’t mentioned Baldassarre. “You have a touch of fanaticism. I’ll have you seeing visions like your brother.”

“No,” she said. “It was the same for everyone. Except perhaps Dolfo Spini—he was making faces. There was a man, too—wild-eyed, with a rope around his neck. An escaped prisoner, I think. He wept openly, listening.”

Tito was silent a moment. Then he said, “I saw him. I was outside with Lorenzo Tornabuoni when he ran in. He fled from a French soldier. Did you see him leave?”

“No. Piero di Cosimo took him out. I saw him cut the rope. But you’re ill, Tito? You need rest?”

“Yes,” he said, rising. “I’m tired.”

And as he walked away, the weight of secrecy pressed upon him—the fear that Baldassarre had said something terrible, done something worse—and that every step he took now might lead him deeper into darkness.

Four days had passed since Romola last set foot in the bustling streets of Florence, and now she walked toward Piero di Cosimo’s house on the Via Gualfonda, her steps measured and purposeful beneath the weight of a quiet anxiety. The city was no longer the same place it had been when she first came to know it—its air thick with tension, its silence broken by whispers of foreign power. French soldiers lined certain thoroughfares, their eyes scanning the ancient stone buildings as if claiming them, while Florentines returned the gaze with narrowed brows and guarded glances. There was little warmth in either direction. The French, accustomed to being at the center of Europe’s admiration, carried themselves with an air of superiority that grated upon those who had long prided themselves on intellect, art, and independence. And Florence, though outwardly compliant, simmered with quiet defiance.

The royal guest—this new Charlemagne, they called him—had come not as a friend but as a conqueror, his entrance marked by the clang of steel and the pomp of empire. He spoke of leaving a viceroy behind, of restoring the Medici—those exiled lords whose very name stirred bitter memories among the people. It was a strange logic: that Florence, which had resisted tyranny under Piero de’ Medici, should now be punished for its own liberty. The citizens met in secret councils within the Old Palace, their voices low but resolute. And already, the defiant spirit showed itself in bold emblems painted across the walls—symbols meant to mock the arrogance of the Frenchman.

Yet Romola moved through this charged atmosphere without fear. Cloaked in black, her face veiled, she was unseen by most, her presence softened by the old servant Maso walking beside her like a shadow. Her thoughts were not on politics or war, but on something far more personal—a portrait.

She had come to see Piero di Cosimo. Not for idle conversation, nor merely out of affection, but because he had promised her a copy of her father’s likeness as Oedipus—the only image of him from the time he was blind. That portrait, painted years ago, was precious beyond words. It held the memory of a man whose strength had never faltered, even when sight failed him. Romola clung to such relics not just with love, but with a deep sense of duty—her devotion demanded that nothing be lost, not even the faintest echo of his face.

Piero was unpredictable. He would abandon a canvas for months, burying it in a chest or corner where it might vanish entirely. He worked only when inspired, and inspiration came irregularly. But Romola had earned his favor; he often bent to her wishes. Still, trust was fragile with him—his whims could override any promise.

He had said the week before that the painting might be done by now. And so, with a nervousness that stirred her breath, Romola hurried to his door.

She lifted the iron slide and called in a soft, clear voice—like a child summoning Tito once had—“Piero!”

He answered quickly, but when he opened the door, he gave a half-apologetic shrug. “Ah, Madonna Romola? I thought my eggs had arrived—I needed them.”

“I’ve brought you something better than hard eggs,” Romola said, smiling gently as she drew back her veil. “Maso has a basket full of cakes and *confetti*.”

She took the basket from Maso and stepped inside. “I know you enjoy these things when they come easily,” she added, teasingly. “Confess you do.”

“Yes,” Piero replied, folding his arms and watching the sweets as Romola uncovered them. “When they arrive as naturally as light.” Then, lifting his eyes to her face, he smiled—truly smiled—for a moment. “And today, they’ve come with the light.”

Her hood slipped back, revealing her hair, and he looked at her with the painter’s eye, lingering on the curve of her brow, the glow of her skin. But then he turned away, catching himself. “But I know what these treats mean,” he said. “They’re bribes to keep me quiet while you scold me.”

“You may go ahead,” Romola laughed. “But first, let me see the picture.”

He gestured toward the next room. “Come in. I’ve worked on it since you were last here—though it’s not finished. But I didn’t promise, you understand. I never promise.”

He quoted aloud, with a touch of irony:

> *“Chi promette e non mantiene
> L’anima sua non va mai bene.”*
> *(Who promises and does not keep,
> His soul will never be at ease.)*

The door to the garden stood shut now, and the room was dim, lit only by the afternoon sun slanting through the window. Piero was at work—but not on her father’s portrait. That stood propped against the wall, untouched. As he reached to move it into better light, he revealed another painting hidden behind it—a small oil sketch, newly completed.

Romola froze.

“That is Tito!” she breathed, her face flushing with surprise.

Piero turned slowly, his expression unreadable. He gave a silent shrug—regretful, perhaps, for having forgotten where he’d placed it. She stared at the sketch, her mind racing. The figure was unmistakable: Tito, tall and noble, yet caught in a moment of stillness, almost vulnerability. Beside him stood a gaunt, chain-armored man, his hands bound, his face twisted in terror. A rope encircled his neck, dragging him forward.

“What a strange picture,” Romola whispered. “When did you paint this? What does it mean?”

“A mere fancy,” Piero said, pulling off his skullcap and scratching his head with the habitual grimace that masked his emotions. “I wanted a handsome young face—your husband’s was perfect.”

He stepped forward, picked up the sketch, and turned it away from her, pretending to examine it critically. “Not worth showing,” he muttered. “Just a reckless dabbling.”

But Romola wouldn’t let it go. “Don’t put it away,” she insisted, stepping close. “Let me look again. That man—he wore armor. I saw him. I saw you come to him in the Duomo. What made you paint him with Tito?”

Piero hesitated. Then, seeing no way around it, he offered part of the truth. “It was chance. The man was fleeing—up the steps, stumbling. He grabbed your husband’s sleeve. I happened to be there. I thought the fellow looked fierce enough for a subject. But it’s nothing—just a rough sketch. Worthless.”

He moved it swiftly to a high shelf, out of sight, and turned back to her. “Now come and see the Oedipus.”

But he had done too much. His haste to hide the sketch had only deepened her unease. Something about it troubled her—not just the image, but the way he had reacted. And yet, she could not press further. To question Tito now, after everything, would feel like betrayal. Her pride, her love, refused to allow suspicion—even in thought.

She said only, quietly, “He looked pitiful—strange, that prisoner. Do you know anything more of him?”

“No,” Piero replied. “I showed him to the hospital. That’s all.”

Then he turned to the Oedipus, holding it up for her inspection.

Romola stepped forward, her attention drawn fully to the portrait. She studied it in silence, her breath slow, her eyes tracing every line—the furrowed brow, the solemn mouth, the distant gaze that seemed to listen beyond the world. At last, she spoke.

“Ah,” she said softly. “You have given it what I asked for. More of the listening look. My good Piero…” Her voice trembled slightly. “I am truly grateful.”

Piero turned sharply. “Now, that’s what I can’t stand about women—you always pour out gratitude where none is due. Why should you thank me for a painting I’m paid to make? Especially when I make you wait? If I paint well, it’s for my own satisfaction, not yours. Should I be thanked for not being a fool or a thief? Let the man thank God for giving him a decent mind. But women think walls are built with honey.”

“You crusty Piero,” Romola said, laughing through her tears. “I forgot how sharp you can be.” She reached into the basket and pulled out a crisp, sweet treat. “Here—put this in your mouth.”

He accepted it with the same awkward grace as a bear might accept a pear in a dream—pleased, but hiding it beneath a gruff exterior. “It’s good,” he said, reaching again for another. “Madonna Antigone—good.”

Romola watched him, her heart softening. For a moment, the troubling vision of the sketch faded. She stood opposite him, content in the simple warmth of the moment.

“Good-bye, Piero,” she said finally, setting down the basket. “And I promise—I won’t thank you if you finish the portrait soon and well. You were bound to do it for your own credit.”

“Good,” he replied curtly, helping her fold her mantle and veil with practiced care.

Once the door closed behind her, Piero leaned against it, exhaling. “I’m glad she asked no more,” he thought. “I’d hate for her to guess I thought her fine husband made a good model for a coward. But I played it cool—she’ll forget.”

But Piero was mistaken. As Romola walked home, the image of the sketch burned in her mind—the armored man, the rope, Tito’s stillness. And the memory of the morning when the chain-armour was adopted—when Tito had first worn it—came flooding back. Was it coincidence? Or had something darker happened?

“It means nothing,” she told herself. “Only chance.”

Yet her heart beat faster. “Shall I ask Tito?” she wondered. Her mind answered firmly: *No. I will not question him unless he speaks first. It would be a betrayal of trust.* But her heart whispered: *I dare not ask.*

There was a crack in her certainty—a flaw in the faith she had placed in him. And like one who holds a fragile treasure, she feared to move too quickly, afraid that the slightest pressure might shatter what she believed unbreakable.

“The old fellow vanished—left for Arezzo the next morning, I reckon, not liking the scent of the French after his time in their hands. I went to the hospital to see if those monkish cooks had figured out whether he was still right in the head. They said he showed no signs of madness—just stared blankly at questions, as if planting a vine twenty miles away. A curious old cat, that one. I’d have liked to know more about him.”

It was in Nello’s shop, on the twenty-fourth of November—just one week after the French entered Florence—that Piero di Cosimo spoke these words. A small gathering of six or seven men had gathered there, unusually late in the afternoon, drawn by the electric tension that gripped the city. The air buzzed with anticipation: Florence stood poised on the edge of a political reckoning. Every public square teemed with people; every shopkeeper without a wife or assistant stood at his door, thumbs tucked into his belt, eyes scanning the streets. Artisans drifted through like idle splinters, ready to surge forward at any spark of news.

Nello had been plucking at his lute, half-sitting on the wooden board against the shop window, keeping watch over the piazza. Suddenly, he set the instrument aside.

“Ah!” he declared, with a note of satisfaction. “I wouldn’t have missed seeing those French soldiers waddling after their escaped prisoners! That’s what happens when I leave my shop to shave noble chins. Always—always something erupts in my piazza just when I’m gone.”

“Yes,” replied Piero, sharp as ever, “you should’ve been there—just to see your beloved Greek look as terrified as if Satanasso himself had seized him. I love watching your polished Messeri caught off guard, forced to show their true colours. What colour do you suppose a man’s liver is when he turns pale as bleached deer the moment some stranger grabs him?”

“Piero,” Nello retorted, “keep your sour sauce for your own eggs. What’s wrong with Tito Melema looking startled when a madman lunges at him? Your scholar isn’t like those brutish Swiss and Germans—whose heads are fit only for battering-rams, whose appetites are so vast they’d swallow a cannonball before breakfast. We Florentines value more than mere brute courage—something you can buy by the dozen from fools. When men realized they were smarter than oxen, they made oxen pull their carts. And when we Florentines saw we were smarter than others, we made them fight our wars.”

“Treason, Nello!” came a voice from the inner room. “That’s not the doctrine of the State. Florence is forging her weapons. And the Frate’s last vision showed Mars standing atop the Palazzo Vecchio, arm around San Giovanni Battista, who offered him a honeycomb.”

“Good enough, Francesco,” Nello replied calmly. “Florence has skulls thick enough to bombard Pisa with. The rest—the finer minds—can stay home to think and shave. As for our Piero here, if bravery means so much to him, let him take his biggest brush as a sword and his palette as a shield, then challenge the loudest-mouthed Swiss in the Prato to single combat.”

“_Va_, Nello,” growled Piero, “your tongue runs like a mill when the Arno floods—whether there’s grain or not.”

“Then let it be excellent grain,” Nello shot back. “For it would be as absurd to expect a grizzled painter like you to relish being pierced by a javelin as it would be to expect a man sharpened on the classics to enjoy having his face clawed by a wild beast.”

“There you go again,” Piero snapped, “trying to trap people into wearing sacks because you call them hose.”

“And who said anything about beasts or unarmed men charging into battle?” Piero countered. “Fighting is a trade—and not mine. I wouldn’t chase danger, but I could meet it if it came.”

“How come you’re so scared of thunder, then?” Nello pressed, determined to corner him. “You ought to understand why one man trembles at what another ignores—you who hide with the rats at the first sign of a storm.”

“It’s not courage or conscience,” Piero insisted. “It’s a sensitivity to loud noises. Nothing more.”

“Well,” Nello replied, “Tito Melema may have a peculiar sensitivity to being grabbed by an escaped prisoner. People are born with aversions—I can’t stand the smell of mint. Tito was born with a distaste for old men stumbling and clutching. Ecco!”

A ripple of laughter ran through the group. It was clear Piero’s dislike of Tito wasn’t shared by the others. The painter scowled, pulled tow from his pouch, and stuffed his ears in silent indignation. Nello continued, triumphant:

“No, my Piero, I won’t allow my _bel erudito_ to be slandered. Nor should Florence—especially now, when scholars are dropping like leaves at forty. Our Phoenix Pico has just flown straight to Paradise, as the Frate told us. And Poliziano, not two months ago, vanished—let’s hope he didn’t end up among the learned souls in Malebolge.”

“By the way,” said Francesco Cei, “have you heard Camilla Rucellari has bested the Frate in prophecy? Two years ago, she said Pico would die during the season of lilies. He died in November. ‘Not lily-time,’ scoffers said. But Camilla replied: ‘I meant the lilies of France—and look—they’re already under your nose.’ I say, _Euge, Camilla_! If the Frate can prove any of his visions matched this well, I’ll become a Piagnone tomorrow.”

“You’re too flippant about the Frate, Francesco,” said Pietro Cennini, the scholarly one. “We owe him much these past weeks—for preaching peace, quiet, and unity. Men of shallow sight would welcome the people breaking free from the Frate’s guidance now. And if the Most Christian King refuses to sign a fair treaty today, Fra Girolamo is the one we must trust to bring him to reason.”

“You speak truth, Messer Pietro,” Nello agreed. “The Frate is one of Florence’s firmest nails—so say the most respectable chins I’ve the honour to shave. But young Messer Niccolò mentioned earlier—no doubt Francesco feels the same—that visions stretch like Dido’s bull’s hide. To me, a dream means whatever comes after. As Franco Sacchetti says: a woman dreams of a serpent biting her, breaks a cup the next day, and cries, ‘See! I knew something would happen—now I know what the serpent meant.’”

“But the Frate’s visions aren’t like that,” interjected Cronaca. “He doesn’t just predict change—he says it will come swiftly. The Church will be scourged and renewed, the heathens converted. He’s not a slippery charlatan with loopholes. He’s—”

“What is this? What is this?” Nello cried, leaping from the board and peering out the door. “People are flooding into the piazza, shouting! Something’s happened in the Via Larga. Aha!” He burst out laughing, waving his cap. The others rushed to the door.

News from the Via Larga—exactly what they’d been waiting for. But the manner of its arrival surprised them all.

Carried aloft on a bench snatched from the street, sat Tito Melema, smiling with amused resignation. His cap had slipped off, dangling by the becchetto tied loosely around his neck. Spotting the group at Nello’s door, he raised a hand in recognition.

The next moment, he sprang from the bench onto a cart piled high with bales, positioned between the Baptistery and the Duomo steps. The crowd swarmed around him, clamorous as hungry fowl. But silence fell when he began to speak—his voice clear, warm, commanding:

“Citizens of Florence! I have no authority to speak except your will. But the news is good—and harmless to tell. The Most Christian King has signed a treaty honourable to Florence. Yet you owe this victory to one of your own—a citizen who spoke as the ancient Romans might have spoken. You owe it to Piero Capponi!”

A roar erupted. “Capponi! Capponi! What did he say?”

“Ah! He wouldn’t be sent from Herod to Pilate!”

“We knew Piero!”

“_Orsù_! Tell us—what did he say?”

As the clamour subsided slightly, Tito resumed:

“The King demanded too much—refused to yield—then declared, ‘I shall order my trumpets to sound.’ Then, Florentine citizens, your Piero Capponi—speaking with the voice of a free city—answered, ‘If you sound your trumpets, we will ring our bells!’ He tore the dishonourable terms from the secretary’s hands, ripped them apart, and turned to leave the royal presence.”

Another wave of shouts surged forth—demands for more.

“Then,” Tito continued, “the great majesty of France felt, perhaps for the first time, the true majesty of a free city. The King himself hurried from his seat to recall Piero Capponi. The spirit of Florence worked—not through force, but through a single, mighty word. And the King consented. The treaty is signed—preserving both Florence’s safety and her honour. The banner of France will fly over every Florentine galley as a sign of friendship and shared privilege—but above it, written boldly, will be the word: _Liberty_!”

“That is all I have to say—is it not enough? For it is glory to each of you, citizens of Florence, that you have among you a man who knows how to speak your will.”

Shouts rose once more. Tito surveyed the crowd with quiet amusement—each man believing Capponi had spoken for him, that he was the mind behind the mouthpiece. He enjoyed the irony: suddenly, an alien, a friend of the Medici, had become a herald of liberty, speaking to a people eager for meaning in a word they couldn’t define. He was glad he’d been seized and carried along—unplanned, yet perfect. Speaking to such crowds was easy, pleasant even. A man who could persuade needed fear nothing—could convince every faction he was deceiving the others.

From above, the faces of weavers and dyers were comical. Tito was growing comfortable in his role—so much so that he failed to notice the shift within himself. He stood with one hand holding his recovered cap, the other at his belt, a gentle smile lighting his long, bright eyes. He bowed respectfully before stepping down—when suddenly, his gaze met another.

This man was not part of the jubilant throng. Clean-shaven, hair closely cropped, wearing a plain felt hat—ordinary, yet unmistakable. One glance would not have confirmed it to anyone else—but to Tito, it was instant recognition. Not merely the escaped prisoner from the palace steps—but a face he had known long ago. In that split second, memory flashed: Baldassarre staring at him, the shock like a firebrand through his veins, the leap from the cart.

He would have jumped down anyway—he was in haste to reach the Palazzo Vecchio. This time, no hesitation, no betrayal. He vowed never to be caught off guard again. He would expect this face—like a recurring shadow in diseased vision.

But now, seeing Baldassarre shaven, clothed like a decent though poor citizen, the dread deepened. Madness seemed less likely. If he were sane—if he retained all his intellect and learning—why linger? Why not reveal himself?

Only one answer: vengeance. He was delaying to make it complete.

And yet, he delayed. That gave Tito a chance.

As Tito turned his back on the piazza, leaving behind the role he’d played, the crowd shifted focus. With his departure, their attention turned toward the entrances of the piazza. Then—mazzieri, mace-bearers, appeared from the Via de’ Martelli. Officials were coming. The syndics—those entrusted with the treaty—must be arriving. The treaty was signed. They were returning from the royal presence.

And Piero Capponi was among them.

The crowd parted silently. Voices softened. A profound hush settled—so deep that the tread of the syndics on the stone and the rustle of their black silk robes echoed like rain at night.

Four men approached. But the crowd waited not for the learned doctors Vespucci and Bonsi, nor for the popular Valori. The moment belonged to one man—strong, grave, unyielding, neither flattering the people nor bowing to power. A man shaped by fortune into a merchant, by nature into a soldier.

Only when he stepped into the piazza did silence break. A single, thunderous cry rang out:
“Capponi! Capponi! Well done, Capponi!”

The man turned, his face calm, his eyes filled with quiet pride. Two years later, Florence would give him a grand funeral—torches carried by all magistrates, banners trailing behind. But no one knows if he took pleasure in the praise delivered over his bier. Let us be content that he received thanks while he lived.

CHAPTER XXX. The Avenger’s Secret.

It was the first time since his escape that Baldassarre had set foot in the Piazza del Duomo. He longed to hear the remarkable monk preach again—his voice, once so powerful and resonant, now seemed like a distant echo of what it once was. But the memory of how he had been seen there before—the half-naked, disheveled man with rope around his neck, hair unkempt, face gaunt—made him hesitate. That same square where he had been called a madman by Tito’s lips still burned in his mind. Not merely because of the cruelty of those words, but because they had struck true: *“Some madman, surely.”* And at that moment, the truth had pierced him—not just the insult, but the fear that he might not be able to prove otherwise.

He was not mad. Yet within him lay the bitter knowledge of a mind broken, faculties frayed, thoughts slipping away like water through fingers. He felt the weight of his own weakness as keenly as the fire of vengeance that consumed him. The desire for retribution raged inside him, but alongside it came a trembling caution—a hesitation born not of cowardice, but of dread. It was as if Tito had been guided by some unseen force, whispering secrets only the deepest wounds could reveal. And the worst part? Baldassarre knew he could not defend himself against such accusations. His reason was intact enough to know its own ruin.

This self-awareness had shaped his every move since his return. When Piero di Cosimo offered kindness, he turned away. At the hospital, he gave only sparse answers: he had been taken prisoner by the French on his way from Genoa. His age, the dignity in his bearing, the quiet precision of his speech—these marked him apart from the common beggars and travelers who passed through. The monks, recognizing this, gave him more than mere alms: a coarse woolen tunic, peasant shoes, and a few *danari*, the smallest coins in Florence. He left early the next morning, heading toward Arezzo. But before reaching the city, he stopped at a small village and spent two of his coins to be shaved and have his hair cut short—back to the style he once wore.

The barber held a small hand-mirror made of polished steel. Baldassarre hesitated. It had been years since he had looked upon his own face. As he stared into the mirror, a sudden thought gripped him: *Could Tito no longer recognize me?* The idea struck like a blow. His hand trembled. He asked the barber to hold the mirror steady.

No. He was not changed beyond recognition. The wrinkles were deeper, yes—but they were the same ones he remembered from three years ago. The skin remained rough, pitted like ancient stone, the yellow pallor of illness now fixed beneath the surface. The beard—shaggy, white—would not fool anyone who had known him for sixteen years. Anyone who had searched for him would expect change, as one searches for a lost loved one among the wreckage of a flood. But something else had altered: his eyes. They held a new intensity, a sharpness that might make recognition all the more jarring—like hearing a familiar voice cry out in pain.

Yet the doubt was fleeting. He knew, deep down, that Tito had recognized him. He pushed the mirror aside. The currents of rage surged forward again, stronger than ever.

He retraced his steps toward Florence, but not yet ready to enter the city. He veered off the main road and sat beside a quiet pool shaded by alder bushes still clinging to their yellow leaves. It was a calm November day. The water was still, like glass. He saw in it an image—his own—and bent closer, drawn by a need to examine himself slowly, without interruption.

Was there madness in his face? Was there emptiness behind the eyes?

Not now. Not when he looked with purpose. Now, his gaze was focused, determined. But what of other times? When the past slipped away like smoke, when whispers faded before he could grasp them, when shapes approached and dissolved under his outstretched hand? Then, surely, confusion must show. A blankness, like a man suddenly blind.

And worse—when the fog lifted for a moment, and hope flared, only to vanish again, leaving him helpless? Then, perhaps, his face reflected the horror of sudden loss.

Could he prove anything? Could he speak coherently, trace a thought from beginning to end? Would anyone believe he had once possessed a mind rich with learning, fluent in tongues, alive with ideas? All that was gone—or was it?

He clung to a fragile hope: that the knowledge was not truly lost, but imprisoned. Like water trapped in a cracked vessel, waiting for the breach to widen. Since the day he first walked weakly from his straw bed, his mind had shifted—some changes slow, others sudden. His body had regained strength; his will had returned. Memories tied to emotion had come back vividly. But the intellectual world—those intricate patterns of thought—remained fragmented.

Yet once or twice, during moments of intense feeling, he had glimpsed his former self. For a flash, he saw Greek script and understood it. He felt his mind move freely, unburdened. Then darkness fell again—deeper, darker. But could that moment return? Last longer?

If it did—if it lasted long enough—he could devise a revenge worthy of his suffering. Not a quick death, but a torment—deliberate, cruel, lasting—such as only a mind could inflict.

He straightened up, arms crossed, forcing his thoughts into order. He needed patience. Knowledge. Opportunity. And food. He could not live on anger alone. He dreaded being seen as a helpless old man, a fool. No one must know half his memory was gone. The strength might return. If only for a little while—that might be enough.

He already knew how to begin gathering information about Tito. The name “Bratti Ferravecchi” echoed in his mind constantly. He had learned from a man in Genoa—whose finger bore Tito’s onyx ring—that the ring had been bought in Florence from a young Greek man, well-dressed, dark-haired, at the shop of a rag-merchant named Bratti Ferravecchi, in the street of the same name.

That revelation had shaken him. Until then, he had clung to the belief that Tito was searching for him. That he had not abandoned him willingly. He had told himself: *My letter never reached him. That is why I am still in Antioch.* He believed Tito was looking, that he would find him. When he was taken to Corinth, he convinced his captors that ransom would come, ensuring inquiries would be made. He hoped: *Here, finally, he will find me.*

But then came the sickness. When he recovered, he was broken—body and mind shattered. Useless to his owners. They let a Genoese merchant take him aboard a galley, moved by pity. During months at sea, he regained strength, but upon landing in Genoa, he felt only desolation. He almost wished he had died at Corinth.

Yet one thought held him back: Tito might still be alive—imprisoned, poor, forgotten. If so, there was still a chance. A faint, distant hope that he might see his son again—his child, his beloved boy—once more.

Then he met the stranger with the ring. Though he could not describe it beforehand, the sight of it stirred something deep within. He recognized it. And the realization crushed him: Tito, nearly a year after their separation, lived in prosperity in Florence—selling the very gem meant to be kept until the last resort.

Baldassarre refused to understand it. He preferred to be stunned, confused, rather than confront the truth. He allowed himself only joy: *I shall see Tito again.* Perhaps he thought his father dead. The mystery would be cleared.

Now, standing by the pool, he whispered: *I am not alone. I shall never be alone. My revenge is with me.*

He bent again to look at his reflection—not out of vanity, but as an instrument of vengeance. The face in the water was not his own, but the vessel of a wrath that had become his life. He saw in it not a man, but a weapon.

“I was a fool,” he said aloud, though no one heard. “I loved a woman once. I believed she could care for me. Then I took a helpless child and raised him. I watched him grow—wondering if he would care for me, even a little. If he would feel sorrow when I suffered. I would have torn open my chest to warm him with my blood, just to see him grieve.”

He clenched his fists. “Curses on him! Let me see him lie with those red lips turned white as ash. Let him look to me for mercy—and see only triumph in my eyes. This world is a lie. There is no goodness but in hate. Fool! You strained for love and found none. But hatred—hatred has depth. Hatred has power. I have memory for it. I have strength in my arm. In my will. And if I can do nothing but kill him—”

But the thought of mere killing repelled him. He wanted more. An eternal torment. To stand forever beside a soul that screamed in agony, knowing it was his doing. But the real desire was earthly—slow, visible, shared under the same sky where he had once fainted with despair.

Yet as soon as he tried to plan, the sense of weakness returned—like frost creeping into his bones. This body, meant to carry out a sublime revenge, must be fed, clothed, preserved. If he waited, he must work. Humble work. He had no skill. Could he try copying manuscripts? Might seeing letters stir his mind? Perhaps. But he dared not risk his hands or brain.

So he chose labor—simple, beast-like work. Porters were needed in Florence. He could carry burdens. His limbs had regained some of their old strength.

But then a new idea came. Around his neck hung a tiny parchment bag, blackened with age, tied by a cord. He had worn it since childhood—a charm given by his mother. He had never opened it. Piety forbade it. Once, he might have thought it contained only a scroll. But now, driven by a thirst fiercer than any hunger, he pulled it free.

With trembling fingers, he tore it open.

There, nestled in the brittle parchment, was a sapphire—small, but brilliant as the Mediterranean sea. Engraved. Worth gold.

He gasped. The ducats flashed before him—not just gold, but a dagger. Not to use yet—but to possess. To hold in his hand, to test its edge. A weapon that did not require strength or skill—only will. A tool for justice too feeble to fight alone.

A spark lit behind his dark brows. He carefully wrapped the sapphire back in the parchment and tied it tight.

Dusk fell. He rose and walked toward Florence. With his *danari*, he could buy bread. He could sleep outdoors—many did in Florence’s corners and alleys.

In the days that followed, he sold the sapphire. He added to his clothes. He bought a bright dagger—from Bratti’s shop. That evening, he visited the rag-merchant, pretending to bargain for the blade. He casually mentioned he came from Genoa, where an acquaintance had bought a valuable ring from Bratti. Did he have more?

Bratti, eager to impress, boasted of his connections, his wisdom, his rare dealings. He spoke of a young signor—handsome, once poor, now prosperous—who had first appeared sleeping under the Loggia de’ Cerchi. Through a few questions, Baldassarre learned Tito’s story: his sale of jewels, his quiet settlement in Florence, his marriage, his wealth.

But one thing remained unknown: what tale Tito had told about his father?

Baldassarre could not yet uncover that. But he began to learn Florence. Yet each new detail slipped from his grasp unless repeated endlessly. He feared listening to anything new—afraid it would erase what he had already fought to remember.

On the day he was seen by Tito in the Piazza del Duomo, that anguish gnawed at him. Tito’s smooth words sounded like mockery, like the taunts of a demon.

Walking home through the Via del Garbo, he paused before a bookseller’s stall. The open volumes beckoned. Could he stare long enough to grasp a thread of memory? Could he anchor himself in the written word?

He bought the cheapest Greek book he could find.

Back in his outhouse—straw piled high, light dim—he sat and studied the pages. But the letters remained meaningless. The darkness deepened. Still, his eyes lingered on the white sheets, as if hoping to see meaning where none existed.

“My Romola,” Tito said on the second morning after his speech in the Piazza del Duomo, “I am expecting important visitors today—the Milanese Count and the Seneschal de Beaucaire, a close favorite of the Cristianissimo. I know you have no desire to endure the hollow courtesies of these grand figures, who may never return to Florence. Since they’ll want to inspect the antiquities and the library, perhaps you’d prefer to rest from your work and visit your cousin Brigida.”

Romola sensed the unspoken wish beneath his words and readily agreed. But soon she returned, wrapped in her hood and mantle, and said, “Oh, what a relief it will be when the gates of Florence swing open and the last Frenchman steps beyond them! Even you, with all your patience, are beginning to tire, my Tito—admit it. Your head feels hot.”

He was bent over his desk, writing, and she had placed her hand gently upon his head, as if to offer a farewell caress. This gesture had been familiar between them; usually, he would lift his face, lean back slightly, and meet her gaze. But now, he felt unable to raise his head—as though her touch were a weight of lead. Instead, he spoke lightly, his pen still gliding across the page: “The French are as eager to leave Florence as wasps are to abandon a ripe pear once they’ve feasted upon it.”

Romola, acutely aware of the absence of his usual response, withdrew her hand. “I’m going, Tito.”

“Farewell, my sweet one,” he replied. “I must stay here. Take Maso with you.”

Still, he did not look up. Romola left without another word.

Small things often mark turning points in marriage—and this morning, for the first time, Romola acknowledged to herself not only that Tito had changed, but that the change was directed toward her. Was it her fault? She might have thought so, had it not been for the lingering mysteries—the armor, the painting—clues to an event she could not understand.

No sooner had she stepped out of the house than Tito laid down his pen and looked up, relieved to see only parchment and broken marble before him. He felt a quiet shame at his inability to meet her glance, to act as he always had. He would have chosen to be more tender than ever—but he could not master the sudden recoil from her presence, a reaction tied deeply to those very qualities that made him long to remain faithful to her.

He was about to take a step that would provoke her deepest anger. He knew he would face discomfort, reproach, even sorrow before winning her forgiveness. And Tito had never found it easy to endure displeasure or rage. His nature recoiled from defiance, from boldness. All his instincts leaned toward preserving Romola’s affection, her tenderness.

He was not tormented by sentimental scruples—those, he had swiftly dismissed as irrelevant to practical life. Yet freedom from such scruples did not shield him from fear of pain, of wounded pride, of loneliness. Unscrupulousness may free one from guilt, but not from toothache, nor from the ache of isolation. And Tito now felt that no trickery, no charm, could protect him from the coming clash with Romola. He was being driven forward like a timid beast toward a desperate leap, pursued by the claws and fangs of fear.

A secret uneasiness had long gnawed at him—the idea that clinging to Bardo’s wishes regarding the library had become, under current circumstances, a foolish sentimentality, costing both himself and Romola real advantages. But it was only recently, amid the upheaval of the past week, that this feeling had crystallized into action. The pressure of new motives and the rare chance of a timely opportunity had pushed him forward.

Indeed, it was not until he saw Baldassarre regaining his sanity, until he began to fear he might be forced to flee Florence, that he resolved to exercise his legal right to sell the library—before the chance slipped away, lost to French and Milanese buyers. For if he had to leave Florence, he did not want to depart as a penniless exile. He had grown accustomed to comfort, and he wished to carry that comfort with him. He wanted to take Romola with him—and if possible, to do so without disgrace.

Success had bred in him a hunger for the pleasures tied to status and influence. To vanish under dishonor, even with riches, seemed not temptation but horror—a descent into the life of a fugitive. He could not sever ties with the Florentines who respected him, nor could he truly separate from Romola. She was the wife of his first love—still loved, still essential. She was part of the fabric of his life, something he dreaded losing.

He winced at the thought of her judgment, uncertain how far her feelings might turn against him. The power a husband holds over his wife—power that allows some men to risk betrayal—could not soothe Tito’s anxiety. That was the weight that held his head down, keeping him from lifting his eyes to hers. Her gaze, clear and pure, brought too vividly the vision of the struggle ahead.

But there was no avoiding it. If they were to leave Florence, they needed money. Indeed, Tito could not imagine shaping a future for himself without substantial funds. And this need—to arrange life according to his own desires—was the root of all his missteps. He would have borne any sacrifice that did not bring discomfort.

The magnates came and went, bargains were sealed, and Romola returned home. That night, nothing serious was spoken. Tito was cheerful, animated, sharing stories and scenes from the French court with a liveliness he had not shown before. Romola noticed the effort behind his cheerfulness and, understanding he was trying to mend the wound of the morning, accepted it as an act of contrition. Yet inside, she ached at the growing distance between them—the silence around a hurt neither dared name.

The next day, Tito stayed away until late. It was a significant evening for Romola. Piero di Cosimo, spurred by guilt over any pain he may have caused her, had delivered her father’s portrait. She had placed it against the back of Bardo’s old chair and stood before it, lost in memory, when the door opened and Bernardo del Nero entered.

“It’s you, godfather! How I wish you had come earlier—it’s growing dark,” Romola said, stepping forward.

“I just stopped by to tell you the good news,” Bernardo said. “Tito hasn’t returned yet, so I thought you should hear it first. The French king is leaving tomorrow—not a moment too soon. There was another clash this morning between our people and his soldiers. But now, there’s hope the city may regain order, and trade may resume.”

“That is wonderful,” Romola said. “But it seems sudden. Yesterday, Tito thought the king would linger much longer.”

“He’s been well scolded,” Bernardo replied, smiling. “His own generals opened their mouths wide enough, and then our Signoria sent Fra Girolamo—the city’s mastiff. The Cristianissimo trembled at that thunder and ordered the retreat. We may quarrel among ourselves when he’s gone, but everyone agrees: we’re glad to be rid of the army choking Florence. And the friar barked with purpose this time. Ah, what is this?” he added, as Romola guided him toward the portrait.

“Let us see,” he said, beginning to unroll his scarf as she brought a stool.

“Don’t you need your spectacles, godfather?” Romola asked, anxious he should see exactly what she saw.

“No, child, no,” Bernardo answered, uncovering his grey hair as he sat upright. “For seeing at this distance, my old eyes may be sharper than yours. Old eyes are like old memories—they remember best what lies far away.”

“It’s better than having no portrait at all,” Romola said softly after a pause. “It doesn’t resemble him as closely as the image in my mind, but that image might fade with time.”

She rested her arm on his shoulder, drawn to him by their shared reverence for the dead.

“I don’t know,” Bernardo said. “I almost think I see Bardo as he was in youth—better than this painting shows him in age. Your father had fire in his eyes when he was young. I never understood how someone so passionate, so impatient, could spend his life among books and shadows. But he gave his heart to it.” He shrugged slightly, and Romola heard in his voice a resonance with her own thoughts.

“And he remained disappointed until the end,” she murmured, then quickly added, fearing she might seem to blame Tito, “If only his greatest, most cherished wish could be fulfilled—just as he imagined it!”

“Well, perhaps it may yet,” Bernardo said kindly, rising and placing his cap on his head. “The times are stormy, but fish are caught by waiting. Who knows? When the wheel turns enough, I may yet be Gonfaloniere before I die. And no creditor can touch these things.” He glanced around the room. Then, turning to her and patting her cheek, he said, “And you needn’t fear my dying—my ghost will claim nothing. I’ve seen to that in my will.”

Romola took his hand and pressed it to her lips, silent.

“Have you been scolding your husband for staying away so much lately?” Bernardo asked, wishing to shift the mood. “I see him everywhere except here.”

A flush crept over Romola’s neck and face as she replied, “He has been in great demand—you know how well he speaks. I’m glad his worth is recognized.”

“You’re content, then, Madonna Orgogliosa?” Bernardo asked, smiling, as he moved toward the door.

“Assuredly.”

Poor Romola! There was one thing that would have made her disappointment in her husband even harder to bear: the knowledge that others might see him as the cause of her pain. It was perhaps a woman’s vulnerability—but one closely linked to her nobleness. For the woman who willingly reveals the private wounds of her marriage turns its sacred space into something common, profaned.

The day after the French departure, Florence buzzed with relief and celebration. Romola, like every other citizen, felt the weight of their presence lifted—yet her own joy carried a deeper, more personal edge. There was a quiet hope stirring in her heart, half-felt, half-superstitious: that with the French gone, so too might vanish the dread that had clung to Tito since their arrival. She could not explain why, but she knew the fear had come with them—the image of a prisoner grasping him, as drawn by Piero, haunted her thoughts. And now, with the foreign troops withdrawn, perhaps that shadow would fade too.

Wrapped in her mantle, she stood beneath the loggia at the top of the house, watching for glimpses of the departing army and royal entourage crossing the bridges toward Porta San Piero. When the gates closed behind them, she lingered still, wanting to feel the city’s pulse through the great tolling of the bells. It was dusk when she finally descended into the library, lit her lamp, and resolved to conquer the restlessness that had kept her idle all day. She would work on copying the catalogue—something she had long set aside.

Tito had left early; she expected him later. Before he returned, she planned to move to the saloon, with its dancing nymphs and birds—a place she had retreated to each evening since he’d complained the library was cold and gloomy. But just as she began her task, Tito entered.

Her first thought was how bleak the room must seem to him—dark, vast, lit only by a single oil-lamp at one end, the fire nearly dead. She rushed forward. “Tito, dearest—I didn’t expect you so soon,” she said, nervously lifting her arms to unfasten his becchetto.

“I am not welcome then?” he asked, smiling brightly as he embraced her, holding his head back playfully.

“Tito!” she replied, voice soft with affectionate reproach. He kissed her gently, stroked her hair as he always did, and seemed content to leave his cloak where it was. Romola trembled—not from cold, but from joy. The day’s tensions had sharpened her senses, and now, seeing him return to his old ways, she felt a flicker of hope. *It will come back,* she thought. *The old happiness may yet return.*

But Tito was not truly himself. Beneath the warmth, his heart pounded with anxiety. He had prepared this moment carefully, though it seemed effortless.

“If I’d known you’d come so soon,” Romola said, finally helping him remove his coat, “I’d have made a little feast for this joyful ringing of the bells. I didn’t mean to be here when you arrived.”

“Never mind, sweet,” he said lightly. “Don’t worry about the fire. Come—sit down.”

She took her usual seat on the low stool beside his chair, resting her arm on his knee as she once had on her father’s. She looked up at him, waiting.

He had never noticed the portrait leaning against the wall. She had never mentioned it—though it had grown heavier in her mind with each passing hour.

“I’ve enjoyed the bells tonight,” she began. “They shook me, almost deafened me—I felt like a Bacchante possessed. Are the people really so joyful?”

“Joyful, yes—but in a sour, pious way,” Tito replied, shrugging. “And those who remain in Florence have little reason to celebrate. To me, the true cause for gladness would be getting out of Florence altogether.”

He had struck the note he wanted—without effort, without pretense. His tone was casual, but his eyes held gravity. Romola hesitated. “Why, Tito? Are there fresh troubles?”

“No need for new ones, my Romola. There are already three powerful factions in the city, ready to tear each other apart. If the Frate’s party gains strength enough to silence the others—as seems likely—life will be no more pleasant than a funeral. They’re already planning a Great Council. Soon, the man who sings sacred lauds loudest will be the most eligible for office. And then there’s the crushing burden of the subsidy paid to the French king, and the war to reclaim Pisa. Florence will be drained—its future bleak. For such men as those who find pleasure in lashing themselves in crypts, it may be ideal. But for the rest of us… exile has the better claim.”

He paused. “For my part, I’ve been thinking seriously that we should leave Florence.”

Romola froze. “Tito—how could we leave? Surely you don’t think I could—no, not yet. Not for a long time.”

Her voice trembled. She felt cold, shaken. He must know the reasons she carried in her heart.

“That is all imagination, my love,” he said softly. “Your secluded life has given you false importance to a few things. Remember—I told you before marriage that I wished we were elsewhere. You’ve seen too little of the world to understand what I mean. There’s something in the Florentines that reminds me of their sharp spring winds. I prefer people who take life less fiercely. And it would do you good, my Romola, to see a new world—to bathe in softer waters, to forget.”

He leaned forward, kissed her brow, touched her hair. But she felt nothing—only the chilling distance between them. Her love, once so certain, now felt shattered.

“It is not because I believe Florence is perfect that I wish to stay,” she said quietly. “It is because—because we must fulfill my father’s wish. My godfather is old—seventy-one. We cannot leave it to him.”

“And that is precisely why I wish we were two hundred leagues away,” Tito replied. “Those superstitions cling to your mind like clouds, blinding you. I must protect you—even against yourself. If your eyes, so tender, see falsely, then I must see for you. I must save you from wasting your life on dreams that can never be fulfilled.”

Romola sat silent. She saw where he was leading. He wanted her to accept that the library could be handed over to a monastery, or sold—any means to free them from Florence. And she knew she would never yield. This was not merely duty—it was honor, memory, love.

But the pain of realizing they were divided overwhelmed her. She could not speak. Tito, meanwhile, felt pleased by her silence. Though he feared her passion, he could not imagine how deeply she would feel this betrayal. Confined within his narrow intellect, he believed his arguments were irrefutable. He continued, gentle but firm.

“You know, dearest—your own judgment once showed you—that the idea of isolating books and relics under a single name is not truly beneficial. It’s fragile, easily lost. Look at the Medici collections—what remains? And I find it even wrong to hold such petty claims. Why should Florence be favored over any other city? I understand your feelings for the dead—but wisdom sets limits. Otherwise, lives are wasted in futile devotion—like praising gods who cannot hear. You gave your life to your father while he lived. Why demand more?”

“Because it was a trust,” Romola said, voice low but clear. “He trusted me. He trusted you, Tito. I didn’t expect you to feel as I do—but I did expect you to respect it.”

“Yes, dearest,” he said, “of course I would care if his welfare depended on it. But it doesn’t now. If I believed in purgatory, I’d want masses said. If I believed he suffered now, I’d share your strictness. But philosophy teaches us to break free from these invisible chains—these fantasies we bind ourselves with. Your mind, so quick, can distinguish real good from illusion. Ask yourself—what good do these books do locked in Florence under his name, compared to being shared widely? Is not dispersal a greater service? The rivalry between Italian cities is petty. The fall of Constantinople enriched the whole world.”

Romola listened, heart breaking. His words were smooth, rational—yet they revealed a soul devoid of depth. She felt contempt rising, mingled with grief. This man she had loved, who had once seemed noble, now spoke of faithfulness as a weakness, of love as folly.

“You talk of substantial good,” she said, voice growing stronger. “But what of loyalty? Of love? Of gratitude? What good is it to keep our promises, knowing others depend on our truth? What good is it to honor a just life? Or is it good to harden our hearts against those who trusted us? What kind of man does such a thing—clever, perhaps, but living only with himself?”

Her voice rose, edged with scorn. “I see no good in a world built on such men. I’m not thinking of Italy or the civilized world—I’m thinking of my father. Of my love. Of his claim on us. I would give up anything—leave Florence—anything. But not this. What have I to do with your arguments? It was his longing—and therefore mine.”

Her voice had steadied. She felt she had said all she needed.

Tito saw he could not persuade her. He would have to act. The time for resistance was over. He would make the revelation now—before she could interfere.

“I’m sorry to hear you speak so stubbornly, Romola,” he said calmly. “Because it forces me to cause you pain. But I foresaw your opposition. A decision had to be made quickly. So I acted alone—for your sake.”

She stared at him, breathless.

“I’ve arranged for the transfer of both the books and antiquities. They’ll serve better uses elsewhere. The books have been sold to the Duke of Milan. The marbles, bronzes, and artifacts go to France. Both will be safe under strong powers—unlike Florence, which teeters on ruin.”

Before he finished, Romola rose. She stood over him, hands clenched, eyes blazing. For the first time, fury flashed in her face.

“You have _sold_ them?” she whispered, as if doubting her ears.

“I have,” Tito replied, flinching slightly.

“You are a traitor,” she said, voice grating with disdain.

She turned abruptly. “It may still be stopped. I am going to my godfather.”

In an instant, Tito lunged to the door, locked it, and pulled out the key. The moment demanded control. He wasn’t angry—he simply wanted to contain her.

“Try to calm yourself, Romola,” he said, leaning casually against a pedestal beneath a stern Roman bust. “Understand the facts. Going to Messer Bernardo won’t change anything. He cannot reverse what’s done. Sit down. You wouldn’t wish to expose our private quarrel to a third person.”

He knew he had struck a nerve. But she did not sit.

“Why can’t it be reversed?” she asked, voice steady. “Nothing has moved yet.”

“Because the sale is final. Agreements are signed. The buyers have left Florence. I hold the bonds for the money.”

“If my father had suspected you of treachery,” she said, voice sharp with bitterness, “he would have secured the library beyond your reach. But death came too soon. When his ear was deaf, his hand stiff—you stole from him.”

She paused. Then, with gathering fire: “Have you stolen from someone else—someone alive? Is that why you wear armor?”

The words tore from her like a whip. At first, Tito crumpled under the blow. The shame he had dreaded felt worse than imagined. But then, a reaction surged. He would not be mastered by a woman whose rage seemed like fate itself.

“It’s useless,” he said coolly, “to answer madness. Your obsession with your father has driven you mad. Any reasonable person would see I’ve taken the wisest course. Even Messer Bernardo would agree.”

“He would not!” Romola said. “He lives in hope of fulfilling my father’s wish. We spoke of it yesterday. He will help me. Who are these men you sold to?”

“Men of no consequence. Count di San Severino and Seneschal de Beaucaire are now with the king, heading to Siena.”

“They can be caught,” Romola said, hope rising. “Persuaded to return.”

“No,” Tito said firmly. “They cannot.”

“Why?”

“Because I refuse to let them.”

“But if you were paid—*we* will pay you,” she said, voice pleading. “We’ll repay the money.”

No words could reveal more clearly how far she had fallen from him. Yet she spoke not with anger, but desperation.

“No,” Tito said. “Such thoughts are impossible. You wouldn’t ask your godfather to bury three thousand florins more than he’s already spent. Your pride would not allow it.”

She trembled. Coldness seized her. She sank onto the carved chest beside her.

He continued, voice clear and cold as ice: “Besides, it is not my wish that Messer Bernardo advance any sum—even if the plan were not utterly foolish. And I beg you to consider: what will happen if you oppose me? If you try to disgrace me before him? What purpose will it serve? The event is irreversible. The library is sold. And you are my wife.”

Each word was calculated. He watched her closely.

She turned her eyes from him, staring at the floor. Minutes passed.

When she spoke again, her voice was quiet, flat. “I have one request.”

“Name it—if it does not harm us both.”

“That you give me the portion belonging to my godfather. Let me repay him.”

“I need assurance first—of your intentions toward me.”

“Do you believe in assurances, Tito?”

“From you, I do.”

“I will do you no harm. I will say nothing. I will not pain him—or you. You are right—the event is irrevocable.”

“Then I will do as you ask tomorrow morning.”

“To-night, if possible,” she said. “So we need not speak of it again.”

“It is possible.” He moved toward the lamp. She sat still, eyes distant.

He bent over her, placed a paper in her hand.

“You will receive something in return, you know,” he said gently, already feeling secure. “A token.”

“Yes,” she said, taking it without looking at him. “I understand.”

“And you will forgive me, Romola, when you reflect.”

He touched her brow with his lips. She did not react.

She heard him unlock the door. She heard the court gate open and close.

Then, as if released, she rose. She walked to her father’s chair, where his portrait stood propped. She knelt before it—and broke into sobs.

Baldassarre wandered through Florence, searching for a place to rest—any sheltered corner where he might sleep without spending much. He had grown used to open skies and wide horizons over the years, and now the narrow streets of the city, with their crowded rooftops and limited views, pressed in on him like a cage. His thoughts were heavy, his memory faint, and the silence of the alleys deepened his loneliness.

His steps led him toward a rare spot within the city walls—a hill that rose above the buildings, offering a glimpse beyond the stone and tile into the distant hills and valley. This was the slope behind the Via de’ Bardi, known locally as the hill of San Giorgio. Bratti had told him Tito lived there, and though he hadn’t yet found him, the hill’s quiet appeal drew him upward.

It was sparsely settled, mostly gardens and patches of rough earth. Near the edge of a broken stretch of land—once ruined by a landslide centuries before—stood a squat, square building with fluted tiles, looking like a truncated tower. Beside it was a small outhouse, half-built against a crumbling wall. Under a large mulberry tree, its leaves turning yellow, an old woman was untying a goat with two kids. The outhouse door stood open, revealing only straw and tools inside.

This was exactly what Baldassarre needed.

He approached her, but she was deaf, speaking in low murmurs and shaking her head. Only when he shouted close to her ear did she understand. He explained he was a poor traveler from far away, unable to afford a hostel. All he wanted was a place to lie down, and he’d pay her a few quattrini a week.

She remained doubtful, muttering to herself. Then, as if struck by an idea, she fetched a hatchet and pointed to a thick log half-buried in litter. “Chop this,” she said, “and you can stay one night.”

He agreed.

Monna Lisa watched with arms crossed, a smile playing on her lips. “It’s been there since my husband died,” she whispered. “I might as well have thrown a stone on the fire. He chops well enough, though he speaks oddly and looks strange. I couldn’t have paid less. And if he only wants straw to sleep on, maybe he’ll run errands for me. Who will know? And sin hidden is half forgiven.” She glanced toward the upper window, where a child’s eyes peered through a round hole in the shutter.

Tessa had seen everything. At first drawn by the loud voice, she had stayed at the opening, watching quietly. Now, as Baldassarre entered the outhouse and sat on the straw under the gathering dusk, a sudden urge stirred in her heart. She would take him part of her supper. He wasn’t deaf like Monna Lisa, and she could tell him things she couldn’t say aloud to the old woman.

It was forbidden. Her duty was obedience, and she knew it. But it was also exciting. And she had nothing else to confess—only sometimes dozing over her prayers or being cross with Monna Lisa for being so hard of hearing. There was no fear now. She was safe, free, and idle.

She stepped back from the shutter, her face bright with mischief. She wore her dark-green serge dress, red girdle, silver hairpin, and red glass beads. Around her neck was a new gold ring, a treasure she wore proudly. She sat on her rickety stool, knees tucked close, her mind fluttering between fear and delight.

She had been warned: make no friends, or her new life would vanish like a dream turned to lead. She had obeyed—going to church with her hood pulled low, lips tightly pursed. But this man was not a friend. He was just a stranger, sleeping in the outhouse. And if she brought him food, he wouldn’t betray her.

Monna Lisa would forbid it, so Monna Lisa must not be consulted. It didn’t matter what came after.

Supper was ready—macaroni with cheese, fragrant and warm. Tessa hurried downstairs, pretending innocence. “What was that noise?” she asked, then, before the old woman could answer, gave her a sharp look and sent her upstairs. “I’ll eat down here.”

Three minutes later, she stood at the outhouse door, lantern in one hand, wooden bowl in the other. She kicked gently, and Baldassarre opened the door, startled awake.

“Supper!” she shouted, lifting her mouth to his ear. “Sit down and eat. I’ll stay with you.”

Surprise held him still. Distrust warred with something softer. But he could not push her away. He sank back onto the straw, and Tessa placed the bowl in his lap, set the lantern between them, and smiled as if to say, *You may really eat it.*

He had been gnawing dry bread. The warmth of the macaroni was welcome.

She watched him eat a few bites, then leaned in again. “I like my supper. Don’t you?”

Baldassarre looked at her—not with a smile, but with a quiet tenderness, like a dog touched by kindness. “Yes,” he said. “But I can hear. I’m not deaf.”

“Oh!” she said, hands rising in surprise. “I forgot. Monna Lisa is deaf. I live with her. She’s kind. We have plenty. Nuts, if I want. No work. I used to work—I didn’t like it. But I liked feeding mules. I miss little Giannetta. Now we have a goat and two kids. I talk to the goat. But now… I’ve got something else. Can you guess?”

She tilted her head, challenging him with a grin.

“No,” he said, putting down his bowl. “I don’t know.”

“You like talking to me?” she asked. “But you mustn’t tell anyone. Shall I bring you sausage?”

He shook his head, but his face softened. She felt at ease.

“Well,” she said, “I’ve got a baby. A pretty bambinetto. Little fingers, little nails. Born at Nativita, Monna Lisa says. I was married at Nativita, long ago. Nobody knew. Oh, Santa Madonna! I didn’t mean to tell you!”

She bit her lip, shoulders lifted, eyes wide—expecting him to be shocked.

But Baldassarre seemed untouched.

“You’re a stranger,” she said. “You don’t live anywhere. You don’t know anyone?”

“No,” he replied, more to himself than to her. “Only one man.”

“His name isn’t Nofri, is it?” she asked, suddenly afraid.

“No,” he said, seeing her fear.

“Is that your husband’s name?”

Tessa laughed, clapping her hands. “No! But I can’t tell you about him. You wouldn’t believe what he is. Not like Nofri at all!”

She laughed again, delighted by the contrast—the cruel stepfather and her gentle husband.

“But I don’t see him often,” she said more quietly. “And sometimes I pray the Holy Madonna to send him sooner. Once she did.”

She paused. “But I must go back to my bimbo. I’ll bring it tomorrow. You’ll like to see it. It cries only when it’s hungry. Monna Lisa says it’s fine. She had babies once—they’re all dead men now. My husband says she’ll never die, because she’s so well dried. I’m glad. I love her. You’ll stay tomorrow, won’t you?”

“I’d like to come here to rest,” he said. “I’ll pay. I won’t hurt anyone.”

“No,” she said, “I think you’re not a bad old man. But you look sad. Will you cry when I leave?”

“I think not,” he said.

“That’s right,” she said, smiling. “I’ll bring breakfast. And the baby. Good-night.”

She took her bowl and lantern, closed the door.

To Baldassarre, the visit had been like a fleeting rainbow across a stormy sky. He barely thought of her again until sleep took hold, dreams weaving through his waking mind.

But Tessa thought of him constantly.

As soon as she returned, she told Monna Lisa everything. “Let him stay,” she insisted. “He’ll help. He knows no one. He’s not dangerous.”

The old woman pretended reluctance. “Messer Naldo will be angry,” she muttered.

Tessa didn’t believe it. Naldo had never objected to strangers who had nowhere to go. And this man only knew one person—someone who wasn’t Nofri.

“Well,” Monna Lisa finally said, “if he stays and helps me carry things up the hill, you must keep silent. Tell no one.”

“I’ll only tell the bimbo,” Tessa promised.

“And God may love us enough to keep Messer Naldo from finding out,” Monna Lisa added. “He comes only at night. He was here two days ago. Likely he won’t return till the old man has gone.”
