“Oh me,” Tessa sighed, clasping her hands. “I wish Naldo didn’t have to go so far.”

“Ah, child,” Monna Lisa said, “the world is big. Behind the mountains, people walk night and day to reach Rome, to see the Holy Father.”

Tessa nodded, solemn. Then she began rocking her baby, humming soft, meaningless syllables like a chime.

The next morning, she was eager. She prepared breakfast quickly, wanting to show him her baby and speak again.

But Monna Lisa had already made Baldassarre fetch water. Tessa hurried down, insisting he sit on his straw. “I’ll stay with you,” she said, placing steaming broth and soft bread in his hands.

“I won’t let Monna Lisa give you too much work,” she said. “I don’t like work. I like sitting in the sun, feeding things. Monna Lisa says work is good—but she does it all. So I don’t mind. She’s not cross. You needn’t fear her.”

Then she went to get her baby.

She returned with the tiny bundle, wrapped snugly. The infant had large, dark eyes and a tiny nose. “Look,” she said, seating herself beside him. “This is my baby. You didn’t think it was so pretty?”

“It’s like the little Gesu,” she added. “I think the Holy Madonna will be kinder to me now. I have everything—except seeing my husband more often.”

“You may hold him,” she said, “but don’t kiss him. You might hurt him.”

She spoke gently, almost pleading. Baldassarre took the baby.

“Poor thing,” he murmured, his voice deep and strange. “Poor thing.”

He did not feel comforted. Instead, he felt a fierce protectiveness rise within him. This child, this innocent girl—she was not a solace, but a cause for anger. A reason to fight.

“Oh, don’t be sorry for me,” Tessa said. “Even if I don’t see him often, he is more beautiful and good than anyone in the world. I pray to him when he’s away. You couldn’t imagine what he is.”

She looked at him with wide, mysterious eyes, longing for him to ask.

“Yes,” he said, bitter. “I could.”

“No,” she said firmly. “You couldn’t. You thought he might be Nofri.”

She smiled triumphantly. “But never mind. You couldn’t know. What is your name?”

He rubbed his brow, stared blankly. “Ah, child,” he said slowly, “what is it?”

He did not forget his name. But the question had stunned him. Memory failed him in that moment.

Tessa saw his pain. “Never mind,” she said softly. “You’re a stranger. Names don’t matter. Good-bye. I need my breakfast. Come whenever you like. Monna Lisa says you may. And don’t be unhappy. We’ll be good to you.”

“Poor thing,” Baldassarre said again, holding the baby close, his voice heavy with sorrow.

Messer Naldo returned sooner than expected—on the evening of November twenty-eighth, just eleven days after his last visit—proving he had not ventured far beyond the mountains. And a scene that unfolded that night in the Via de’ Bardi may help explain why his steps turned toward the hill of San Giorgio.

When Tito first found shelter for Tessa upon returning from Rome, more than a year and a half earlier, he told himself he acted out of kindness alone, compelled by the unfortunate incident that had led foolish little Tessa to believe him her husband. It was true that she was charming—pretty, trusting, and so naturally affectionate that it was hard not to want to comfort and pet her. But it was also true that Tito’s kindness was not merely self-serving. Had it been only about pleasure, he might have let her go; for though he enjoyed her presence in idle moments, he was not in love with Tessa. He was in love—for the first time in his life—with someone entirely different: a woman whose mere presence could possess him, whose long tresses brushing his cheek sent vibrations through hours of thought. All the idealism in Tito’s nature had awakened at Romola’s touch. His mind was too refined, his spirit too sensitive, to fall into the vulgar indulgences others embraced. Yet he had woven a web around himself and Tessa—one he now felt powerless to unravel. At first, after their mock marriage, he had deliberately left her under the illusion, calculating that he might need such a refuge one day. But since then, the web had grown on its own, like a vine clinging tighter than he could break. Kindness and self-indulgence were hard to separate in a nature as soft as Tito’s. The annoyance he’d felt when Tessa pursued him on the day of his betrothal, the firm resolve he’d made to reveal the truth—these were proof enough to him that he had yielded to compassion, not convenience. And in those early days of devotion to Romola, he needed justification. He was glad she remained ignorant of certain truths. But every strong emotion creates its own conscience, its own piety—just as a child’s love for a parent can endure even amid decay. Tito could not yet rest easy knowing he carried a secret wound against the woman he loved.

Yet he took care to guard that secret. Monna Lisa, who rarely left her home except to shop or attend confession once a year, knew nothing of his real name or whereabouts. She only knew he paid her well and kept her comfortable, and cared little else—except that she grew fond of Tessa and took pleasure in the tasks she was paid to do. There was mystery behind the arrangement, certainly—Tessa was a peasant girl, Messer Naldo a gentleman—but Monna Lisa might just as easily have believed them truly married. For Tito had frightened Tessa into silence, warning her that if she ever spoke of their marriage, she would never see him again. And Monna Lisa’s deafness, which required careful speech, protected Tessa from accidental revelations—such as she had already made to Baldassarre. For much of the time, Tito’s visits were rare enough that it seemed natural he traveled between them. They came mostly from a desire to check on Tessa, and though each visit always proved more pleasant than anticipated—always renewed the charm of her innocent affection—he had no deep need for her yet. Still, he was determined, if possible, to preserve the simplicity that made her so appealing. He wanted her to remain a genuine country girl, untouched by the world that might dull her grace. He would have been shocked to see her dressed in finer clothes; her speech would lose its charm if her world expanded, her joys matured. Even the squirrel-like delight she took in eating nuts marked the limit of the luxuries he allowed her. In this way, Tito preserved Tessa’s charm—and, by happy chance, spared himself further expense, which weighed heavily on a man whose funds were already strained by his outwardly modest lifestyle.

This, in short, was the story of Tito’s relationship with Tessa until recently. True, once or twice before Bardo’s death, the idea of being with Tessa—of finding an hour of ease away from the old man—had tempted him when weariness threatened. But there had been no real hunger for her until very lately. That moment was unmistakable, as clear as the onset of a sickness that permanently alters sight and hearing. It was the day he first saw Baldassarre and bought the armor. Returning across the bridge that night, coat of mail in hand, he felt an overwhelming urge to avoid Romola. She, too, lived in a world of limited experience, trusted him deeply—but beneath her open eyes, Tito sensed a mind capable of judgment. Her trust wasn’t born of ignorance, but of nobility, and that nobility could become a dangerous mirror. After the agitation of the day, he craved release, a place where he could breathe without restraint. And there was Tessa—sweet, adoring, believing any tale, unshaken by public opinion. So, while Romola waited and listened for him, he climbed the hill.

No wonder, then, that he took the same path eleven days later, after Romola’s first storm of scorn. He did not wish Tessa in his wife’s place, nor could he help wishing Romola would forgive him. For it was Romola—her world, her ambitions, her depth—who belonged to the realm where a man of talent and purpose must ultimately strive. But he needed a refuge from a standard too strict, one he could not escape simply by calling it folly. And Tessa’s small soul offered just such a haven.

It was barely eight o’clock when he ascended the stone steps to Tessa’s door. Usually, she heard him enter and rushed to meet him—but not tonight. When he opened the door, he understood why. A single dim light flickered above the dying fire, revealing Tessa kneeling beside the baby’s bed. Her head had fallen sideways onto the pillow, her brown rosary—normally hung above the Madonna’s image—loosely held in her right hand. She had drifted off mid-prayer.

Tito stepped quietly across the room and sat beside her. She likely heard the door creak in her dream, for she opened her eyes almost immediately—without surprise, without movement—just gazing at him as if his presence silenced all other thoughts. When he lifted her chin and leaned down to kiss her, she whispered:

“I dreamed it, and then I said it was a dream—and then I woke, and it was true.”

“Little sinner,” Tito teased, pinching her chin, “you’ve not finished your prayers. I’ll punish you by not looking at your baby. It’s ugly.”

Tessa frowned, though he smiled. “Ah, no! He’s prettier than anything. You don’t think he’s ugly. You’ll look at him. He’s even prettier than last time—only he’s asleep, so you can’t see his eyes or tongue. And his hair is growing—isn’t that wonderful? Look at him!”

He obliged, pressing a gentle butterfly kiss to the tiny form, then turning her face toward him. “You like looking at the baby better than your husband, you false one!”

She rested her hands on his knee, her eyes wide and angelic—like one of Fra Lippo Lippi’s cherubs.

“No,” she murmured, shaking her head. “I love you best. But I want you to love the bambino too. I used to want only you to love me.”

“And did you expect me to return so soon?” Tito asked, eager to hear her prattle. He still felt the tremors of his recent turmoil—like a man shaken violently—and this was the easiest way to find relief from silence.

“No,” she said, counting on her fingers. “I’ve counted the days—today I started over at my right thumb—since you wore the beautiful chain-coat Messer San Michele gave you. And you’re wearing it now,” she added, peering through the opening in his tunic. “Perhaps it brought you back sooner.”

“Perhaps it did,” Tito replied. “But forget the coat. Tell me—what happened since I was here? Did you see the tents in the Prato? The soldiers and horsemen crossing the bridges? Did you hear the drums and trumpets?”

“Yes,” she answered, voice hushed. “I was afraid they’d come up here. Monna Lisa was too—she said they might take our kids. She says it’s their business to cause harm. But the Holy Madonna protected us—we never saw one. But something has happened… and I hardly dare tell you. That’s why I prayed extra Aves.”

“What do you mean?” Tito asked, suddenly anxious.

“Will you let me sit on your knee? Then I won’t be so frightened.”

He lifted her onto his lap, arms wrapped around her, but his expression darkened. Something unpleasant had followed him even here.

“At first I didn’t want to tell you,” she whispered, as if softness could lessen the fault. “We thought the old man would be gone before you came. But he’s still here, and you’re here. I never disobeyed you before. I want to tell you—and maybe you’ll forgive me. It’s been so long since I confessed.”

“Tell me everything, my Tessa.” He hoped it was nothing serious.

“Oh, you’ll feel sorry for him. I’m afraid he cries when I don’t see him. But that wasn’t why I went to him first. I wanted to talk to him, show him the baby. He was a stranger, living nowhere, and I thought you wouldn’t mind. I think he’s not a bad man—he wanted to sleep on the straw next to the goats, and I made Monna Lisa say yes. He’s gone most of the day, but when he returns, I talk to him and bring him food.”

“A beggar, I suppose,” Tito said sharply. “That was wrong, Tessa. I’m angry with Monna Lisa. He must go.”

“No,” she insisted. “I don’t think he’s a beggar. He tried to pay Monna Lisa, but she said he should work instead. He gets shaved, his clothes are neat—Monna Lisa says he’s decent. But sometimes… I think he’s not right in the head. Lupo at Peretola wasn’t right, and he looks a little like Lupo—like he doesn’t know where he is.”

“What kind of face does he have?” Tito asked, heart beginning to race. The image of Baldassarre haunted him. Already, he saw him sitting on the straw, just yards away.

“Fetch your stool, Tessa,” he said. “Sit there.”

“Will you forgive me?” she asked timidly, stepping down.

“Yes,” he said, “I won’t be angry. Just sit and describe him.”

“I can’t think how to say it. He’s not like my stepfather Nofri or anyone. His face is yellow, with deep lines. His hair is white, but none on top. His eyebrows are black, and he looks at me from under them, saying, ‘Poor thing!’ as if he thinks I was beaten like I used to be. That makes me think he’s not right, doesn’t it? I asked his name once, but he couldn’t tell me. Everyone has a name, isn’t that true? Now he has a book, and he stares at it for ages, like a Padre. But I don’t think he’s praying—his lips don’t move. Ah, are you angry? Or sad for the old man?”

Tito’s eyes remained fixed on her, but he no longer saw her. He saw only what her words evoked. A new possibility rose before him—one that could dissolve the fear and shame poisoning his life. Destiny had placed within reach a chance to redeem the moment on the Duomo steps, when the past had seized him with living hands, and he had denied it. A few steps, and he might stand before his father—alone. He might seek forgiveness, reconciliation. Money from the sale of the library could fund their escape from Florence, undetected, into Southern Italy, where French rule offered protection. Romola need never learn the full truth—she could not identify the prisoner in the Duomo as Baldassarre, nor know what transpired unless he revealed it. If his father forgave, he would agree to bury the past.

But with this hope came another: the possibility that the fierce man might refuse. Well—then things would remain as before. For there would be *no witness*. This was not repentance draped in white cloth, confessing sins before men. This was repentance that would restore peace, hide pain. And Tito’s tender heart, his aversion to harshness, now swelled toward his father. To have him near, to replace hatred with old affection—that was a peace his nature craved. He longed for his world to be cushioned again with goodwill, especially after Romola’s rejection. It was easy for him to smile at those he had wronged, to offer kindness. He could not taste the bitterness of injury, nor grasp the full weight of reparation. The opportunity was here, and it stirred him beyond reason. He rose abruptly, moving toward the door. But Tessa’s cry—beads falling—broke his trance.

“My Tessa,” he said, “bring me a lantern. Don’t cry, little pigeon—I’m not angry.”

They descended the stairs. Tessa reached for Monna Lisa’s ear to shout for the lantern, but Tito stopped her.

“Stay, Tessa. No lantern. Go back upstairs. Be quiet. Say nothing.”

In moments, he stood before the closed door of the outhouse, moonlight glinting on the weathered wood.

In that final moment, a tremor passed through Tito—a sudden instinctive recoil from a tiger’s gaze, a tiger’s leap. Why should a young man, armed, fear an old one, weaponless? Only a moment’s hesitation. Then his hand gripped the door.

Was his father asleep? Was there nothing but this door between him and the voice, the glance that no magic could soothe?

Baldassarre was not asleep. High in the wall of the hovel, a square opening let in moonlight like a pale river. If Tito had looked through it, he would have seen his father seated on the straw, holding something that gleamed like a white star—his poniard. Baldassarre ran his thumb along the blade, seeking solace in its edge, escaping the void between rage and purpose.

He was in one of his worst states—conscious helplessness. He had spent daylight poring over the book beside him, trying to recall the names of his jewels, their symbols. Once, he could remember them. Tonight, they were lost in darkness. His memory had failed him utterly. He was reduced to a mad awareness: a solitary pulse of rage in a world of defiance. He had drawn the dagger, feeling its edge, his mind narrowed to one image—the sensation of driving it into a base heart, a revenge he could not achieve otherwise.

Tito pulled the door. It scraped against the floor, waking Baldassarre from his trance. Startled, he rose from his knees, disoriented, not knowing where he was. Before he could stand fully, the door swung open. Moonlight framed the silhouette of a figure—dark against the glow—curls bright, cheek rounded—*the image of his reverie*, not shadowy, but real, close, like water at the lips after thirst.

No thought could interrupt that hunger. In one instant, before Tito could retreat, the old man surged forward, dagger flashing.

Then—snap. The blade broke. Baldassarre fell backward onto the straw, clutching the hilt, the jagged end lying at Tito’s feet.

Tito staggered under the force, heart leaping with terror. But then came relief—the triumph of survival. His armor had saved him. Vengeance lay helpless. Yet no cruel impulse stirred. Instead, seeing his father near, powerless, made reconciliation easier. Fear was gone. Now he only wanted to be free from the feeling of being hated.

After a long silence, Baldassarre lying motionless in fury, Tito spoke softly, as he had before their parting in Greece:

“_Padre mio._”

A pause. No response.

“I came to ask your forgiveness.”

Another pause—letting the words settle.

“I was taken by surprise that morning. I wish now to be your son again. I want to make your life happy, so you may forget what you’ve suffered.”

He waited. He had said all he could. Until he saw a sign, it was useless. Perhaps his father’s mind was broken. Perhaps the fall had stunned him.

At last, Baldassarre moved. He cast aside the broken dagger, slowly rising, trembling. Tito reached out a hand. And in that instant, as he began to believe acceptance was near, a fleeting thought struck him—the burden of what would follow.

Baldassarre grasped the hand, pulled himself up, and stepped close—faces inches apart.

Then he spoke, voice deep and shaking:

“I saved you. I nurtured you. I loved you. You abandoned me. You stole from me. You denied me. What can you give me? You have made the world bitter to me. But one sweetness remains—_that you shall know agony._”

He released Tito’s hand, stepped back, leaned on a stone, then sank again onto the straw.

The surge of rage had drained him.

For several days, Tito saw little of Romola. The morning after the library’s fate was decided, he approached her gently, telling her it would be best if she removed any personal belongings from the room, as agents were soon to arrive to pack up the antiquities. Leaning forward, he kissed her brow and suggested she remain in her own chamber—where the small painted tabernacle stood, where she now sat—so that she might avoid the commotion of strangers’ footsteps. She agreed quietly, showing no sign of distress. Yet the night had been one long vigil for her; though her body remained strong, sensation had dulled into a persistent ache, as though she were numb and bruised from within.

Tito sensed her illness but dared not press further. When he noticed her hand and forehead were cold as stone, he fetched a soft fur-lined mantle and draped it lightly over her shoulders. Each time he returned, the scene remained much the same: he offered small acts of kindness or tender words, hoping to draw her back, but she seemed unable to speak, or even meet his gaze. “Patience,” he murmured to himself. “She will recover. She will forgive. Our bond must still be stronger than all this.”

It is easy for one who strikes to shift into the role of the wronged when the wounded remains silent and still. The striker feels no pain, only the weight of his own restraint, and begins to believe his forbearance a virtue. But Tito was not prone to such self-pity. His nature inclined toward reconciliation, and he would have endured much just to feel Romola’s hand rest once more upon his head—as it had done that first morning, when he had recoiled from her eyes.

Yet patience came easier now, for life beyond the walls of their home stirred with new purpose. A course of action shaped by deep character often traces its beginning to a single moment—a spark that ignites a flame. For Tito, that moment had come in the Piazza del Duomo, when mounted on bales, he tasted a sharp pleasure in knowing he could charm men with mere words. From then on, his mind turned increasingly toward political life—toward the kind of influence Florence’s turbulent times might demand.

But fear of Baldassarre had risen at the same instant, like a rock blocking his path. That dread had driven him to sell the library—an act meant to prepare for flight should necessity arise—just as Florence began to stir anew in his imagination. Now, however, that fear was nearly gone. He would still wear his armor, guard his wits, and ready himself for whatever trials might come—but he no longer felt compelled to leave Florence behind. His father had refused the offer of reconciliation, forcing Tito into defiance. And an old man, far from home, memory fading, was too weak to resist.

Now, Tito’s quiet desires unfolded into clear resolve. As the fervor of youth softened, life took on the shape of a game—part skill, part chance, and deeply engaging. And Florence promised a game both swift and thrilling: one of revolution, faction, and hidden maneuvering. It was a world where brilliance could thrive without burden—where loyalty to truth was replaced by loyalty to advantage, and where the only creed needed was that “ginger is hot in the mouth.”

No sooner had the French guests departed than Florence trembled with unrest—like an ant colony freed from shadow, scrambling to rebuild its nest. Questions flooded the air: How would they pay the king? How would they fight the stubborn rebels of Pisa? Above all, how could they reshape their government so that magistrates were chosen wisely, laws passed fairly?

Until these were answered, trade faltered, and the vast number of workers—those not counted citizens, denied even a vote—grew restless. Something had to be done.

So the great bell rang out, summoning the people to a parliament in the Piazza de’ Signori. The crowd gathered thickly, hemmed in by armed guards at every exit. Then the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and eight Priors—appeared before the stone lion on the platform of the Old Palace. They proposed that twenty leading citizens be granted dictatorial power for one year: authority to appoint all magistrates and restructure the state. The people roared their approval, declaring themselves the electors of the Twenty.

This form of “parliament” was ancient Florentine tradition—a way to make the will of the few appear the choice of the many. The shouting died quickly, but debate raged inside the palace. Should Florence adopt a Great Council, Venetian-style, where all officials and laws were chosen by a broad assembly of qualified citizens—regardless of rank or party? Or should governance remain narrower, preserving the influence of noble families, diluted less by shopkeepers and artisans?

Doctors of law argued day and night. Messer Pagolantonio Soderini championed the popular model with eloquent reason. Messer Guidantonio Vespucci countered with equal strength for a more aristocratic order. It was a debate over boiled versus roasted meat—already decided by taste, with arguments merely prolonging the delay.

Inside the palace, those in power favored Vespucci’s caution. Change should be measured. Outside, the masses, aware of their lack of voice and their grievances, feared change less—and desired it more.

And there was a growing force outside the palace shaping these vague desires into something firm and decisive: the preaching of Savonarola.

Driven by spiritual duty and aided by politicians who could not pass laws without his support, Fra Girolamo moved swiftly in his sermons—from general exhortations to abandon private passions for the public good—to specific commands: “Choose the Great Council!” “The Great Council is God’s will!”

To Savonarola, these were not separate truths—they were one. The Great Council alone could give voice to the people in a way powerful enough to overcome partisan corruption. It made honest governance possible. And the purer Florence’s government became—the safer it was from men who profited from moral decay—the closer the city would come to becoming a true community, worthy of guiding the Church and the world toward renewal.

Savonarola’s vision never stopped at politics. Every effort served a higher end, grand and moral. He harbored no petty grudges, sought no personal gain. Even in his final hours—when shame, torture, and terror laid bare every secret of his soul—he told his tormentors: “Do not wonder that I have spoken so little. My purposes were few—and great.”[1]

[1] “Se vi pare che io abbia detto poche cose, non ve ne maravigliate, perchè le mie cose erano poche e grandi.”

More than three weeks passed before the last of the library’s contents were packed and carried away. Romola did not shut her eyes or ears to it. Instead, she stood witness to every step—watching, listening, as if the very act of seeing could force the truth into permanence. The exhaustion that follows a violent emotional upheaval often brings with it a dreamlike disbelief in what has happened. Yet each evening, when the workmen had gone, Romola would take her hand-lamp and walk slowly through the chaos of straw and wooden crates, pausing at each empty pedestal, each once-prized object now laid low. She lingered, not for comfort, but to confirm—to assure herself, again and again—that her love was truly gone, and that the world had turned barren beneath her feet.

And still she returned, night after night—not to prove anything anymore, but because the ache of remembering her father, of mourning his memory in this ruined space, was all that remained alive in her heart. On December 23rd, she knew the final load was leaving. She ran to the loggia at the top of the house, so she wouldn’t miss the last sight—the slow wheels crossing the bridge, carrying away what had been her father’s life’s work, his dreams, his hopes.

It was a cloudy day, dusk falling fast. The Arno flowed dark and restless; the hills stood mournful. Florence, wrapped in its stone towers, looked silent, tomb-like from above, cloaked in shadow. Santa Croce, where her father lay, was swallowed by darkness. And over the bridge, crawling slowly into the narrow street beyond, came the white bundle of books and manuscripts—like some cruel, deliberate fate dragging her father’s legacy into an unmarked grave.

Romola felt less like she was watching it happen than that her father himself was aware of it, helpless under the weight of stone, unable to hear her voice say he was not alone.

She stood motionless even after the cart vanished, oblivious to the cold, soothed by the gloom that draped around her like a shroud, shielding her from the noise and laughter of a city that had no room for grief.

Then—suddenly—the great bell in the palace tower rang out, not in alarm, but in wild triumph. A peal of joy, sharp and insistent, echoed across the sky. One by one, every other bell in every tower answered, joining in a chorus that swelled until the air itself seemed to vibrate with sound. Flames flickered between the turrets—tiny, dancing lights—as if the sound had caught fire.

That sudden clang, that leaping light, struck Romola like wounds. It was the victory of demons, celebrating the success of her husband’s betrayal and the ruin of her life. Less than three weeks ago, she had stood enchanted by these same bells, hearing in their song a promise of her own happiness. Now, the city’s joy felt like cruelty. She stood apart, severed from the life below—Florence shouting its exultation into the wind, deafening sorrow with noise.

She could never join in such gladness again. Only those whom joy forgets would remain near her. In her bitterness, all celebration seemed mockery. Men shouted songs with hearts full of sorrow, then glanced at one another’s faces, searching for proof that joy was real.

Romola had lost faith in the happiness she once longed for. It was now a thing hateful—a smiling, soft-handed creature with a selfish, narrow heart.

She ran down from the loggia, hands pressed hard against her ears, hurrying across the antechamber—only to stop short, startled, as she met her husband coming toward her.

His step was light, his face lit with satisfaction, a glow uncommon in him.

“What? Was the noise too much for you?” he asked, noticing how tightly she clutched her ears. He took her wrist gently, drew her arm into his, leading her into the saloon adorned with dancing nymphs and fauns.

“Florence is mad with joy,” he said, “over the new Great Council—it will end all evils, especially the sin of merriment.” He smiled. “You look stunned, my Romola. And you’re cold. You shouldn’t stay so late on that drafty loggia without wraps. I came to tell you I’m being called to Rome on business for Bernardo Rucellai. I leave immediately—I’ll meet my party at San Gaggio tonight, so we can depart early tomorrow. No trouble for you—I’ve already packed my things. I’ll be back soon.”

He expected nothing more from her than quiet acceptance. He didn’t even dare kiss her brow tonight—only pressed her hand to his lips and left.

Tito sensed now that Romola was colder, harder than he’d imagined. Her love wasn’t the sweet, clinging devotion he’d once believed in—the kind that transcends judgment, that makes marriage a true bond. But this frozen silence was better than rage. At least she didn’t resist. Her pride, her ability to see when struggle was useless—these made her easier to bear.

But as soon as the door closed behind him, Romola shed the mask of stillness. The icy calm melted. Inside, she was far from passive. These past weeks had been spent in quiet preparation—planning, gathering strength—for a decision she had made long before.

The first thing she did was summon old Maso.

“Maso,” she said, voice firm, “we leave tomorrow morning. We can catch the first convoy of cloth—they’re waiting at San Piero. See to the two mules tonight. Be ready to set off at dawn, and wait for me at Trespiano.”

She meant to travel with Maso as far as Bologna, then send him back with letters to her godfather and to Tito—telling them she had gone, and would never return. She had planned everything so carefully—so that her departure would be secret, her broken heart unseen by any vulgar eye. Bernardo del Nero had been away at his villa, escaping political suspicion, content to tend his land. Romola had paid him the debt without meeting him. He didn’t know the library had been sold—he’d simply assume Tito had found sudden fortune.

Maso knew only that her journey was to be kept secret. And obeying her commands exactly—that was all he cared for in his old age.

Romola had no intention of sleeping that night. Once the door was locked, she lit her taper and went to the carved chest where her wedding clothes lay—white silk, gold embroidery, the long veil, the circlet of pearls. A sob rose in her throat. They looked like a shroud for a dead joy.

In a tiny gold loop of the circlet, a pink sugar-plum still clung—a relic from the shower of sweets at her wedding. Tito had noticed it first and said it should stay there forever.

At moments like this, memory swept over her—pulling her back into a time of perfect trust. She saw again the man whose love had made the world fresh and wondrous, as though she were a child among sunlit flowers. She heard his gentle voice, saw his honest eyes, breathed again the freedom of soul that comes from believing someone near you is greater than yourself.

And always, at such moments, tears came—because the woman who loved still felt something akin to the mother who bends over a child’s cold body, feeling warmth where there is none.

But there was another thing in the chest—something dark and coarse, tightly rolled. She turned from the white and gold to it, and as her fingers touched the rough serge, her tears stopped. This harshness pulled her back to reality—the world where love and delight had fled.

She untied the thick cord and spread the bundle on the table. It was the grey serge dress of a sister from the Third Order of Saint Francis—someone who lived in the world but devoted herself to piety. The Florentines called such women *Pinzochere*. Romola would wear this as disguise. She decided to put it on now—so if she needed sleep, she would wake ready to go.

She removed her black gown and slipped into the coarse sleeves of the serge mantle. The rope girdle bit into her fingers as she tied it. She welcomed the discomfort—each sensation reminded her of her new scorn for pleasure, for ease, for the way men avoided suffering while others bore burdens too heavy to carry. This garment was a rejection of everything her husband represented.

She gathered her long hair, pulled it tight from her face, bound it into a hard knot at the back. Then she tied a square of black silk across her head and under her chin, and drew the cowl over it. She held up the candle to the mirror.

Yes—her disguise was complete. To anyone who hadn’t known her closely, she might pass. To herself, she looked strangely like her brother Dino—the oval of her cheek needing only to grow thin, the eyes already sad, just needing to deepen in shadow. Was she becoming more like him in other ways? Only in this: she now understood how men could flee earthly delights, how they could choose sorrow over beauty, how they could fix their minds on pain rather than joy.

But she did not linger. She began collecting the relics of her parents—every object too large for her small wallet. Portraits, trinkets, keepsakes—all would go into the chest with her wedding clothes. When she was safely gone, she would leave the chest with her godfather.

She paused. There was one more thing to cast off—something belonging to the past she was turning away from forever.

She touched the betrothal ring on her finger. Her thumb and forefinger rested there—still. She had come so far toward this act—breaking free from a husband who had shattered her trust, severing a bond that no longer meant love. Yet the power of symbols—the way outward signs bind us to our identities—gave the simple act of removing the ring a strange weight. It felt like tearing her life in two.

Was it possible that this impulse, so clear and strong, was actually blindness? That there was something deeper in human bonds than mere illusion?

If the Tito who gave her the ring was not the same man she no longer loved, why return it? Why not keep it as a memorial?

This thought shook her. The act, which should have been a clean break, instead stirred a hidden fear—a sense that she was betraying something sacred.

But then a stronger voice rose within her.

“It cannot be! I cannot be ruled by him. He is false. I recoil from him. I despise him!”

She snatched the ring from her finger and placed it on the table beside the pen she meant to use for her letter.

Again, she felt the truth: there was no law for her but the law of her affections. Love had shaped her life—her patience, her courage, her capacity for sacrifice. But now, that strength had transformed into repulsion. Her hatred of Tito grew in direct proportion to the depth of the love he had betrayed, the devotion to her father he had violated.

All motive had faded—except indignation, scorn, the need to tear herself from him.

She was not following rules or traditions. The stoic philosophy her father taught her—its grand severity—was familiar, even inspiring at times. But she had never used it. She had endured not because of reason, but because she loved. Maxims about detachment, about not clinging, had been powerless against her tenderness—just as they had been against her father’s longing for justice.

She had no theories. She had only felt strong in love. And now, without that strength, life became a problem she had never faced.

Her solution was simple. She would follow only the instinct to leave the man she no longer loved.

Yet even as her resolve burned bright, anguish shifted beneath it—constantly changing shape. Now, with her preparations nearly done, she hesitated. She delayed writing the final words of farewell.

The emotions of the past weeks surged back—cruel, urgent. She reached for the pen—and her hand dropped. Tears came, bitter now, not from remembrance, but from shame. Shame for the words she had spoken to Tito—“Have you robbed someone else who is _not_ dead?” To have said such things—to her husband—felt like a degradation of her whole life.

Hard words between lovers are monstrous in memory—like seeing greatness fall into rags.

The contrast between past and present pressed upon her so fiercely that it became almost physical. She felt numb, hollow, trembling inside. She needed something solid—something harsh.

She gripped the knotted cord at her waist. Then she seized the rough lid of the chest—nothing else to go in? No. She closed it, pressing her palm against the carved wood, and locked it.

Now she remembered: she still needed to finish her disguise. The leather purse, the *scarsella*, must hang at her side—filled with small coins. Her florins and jewels, gifts from her godfather and cousin Brigida, were safely tucked inside her mantle. On the other side, the rosary.

She hung it there.

It never occurred to her that more than clothing was needed to pass as a *Pinzochera*—that her expression, her manner, her very presence, were unlike those of a woman whose eyes bowed in prayer, whose lips moved in silent repetition. Her inexperience shielded her from imagining the dangers ahead. And her pride helped her ignore them.

She didn’t know any Florentine woman had ever done what she was doing. Wives fled to friends or monasteries—but those paths were closed to her. She had forged her own: to go to Venice, to Cassandra Fedele, the most learned woman in the world, and ask how a woman of knowledge could live alone, sustain herself, and find purpose.

She was not afraid of hardship or uncertainty. Her life could never be happy again—but it must not be ignoble.

And in a tender mix of romance and realism, her future vision included not just survival, but heroism—stoic, proud, enduring. She imagined herself writing, someday, something that would rescue her father’s name from oblivion.

After all, she was only a young girl—this poor Romola, who had reached the end of her joys.

There were still tasks to do. A small key sat in a casket on the table—but now, her taper flickered and died. She had forgotten another light.

In moments, the room was dark.

She felt her way to a chair and sat, waiting for morning.

The key had brought back memories—memories that had haunted her since the revelation that split her from Tito. The last meeting with Dino had never left her mind. His dying words echoed in her thoughts:

“The man whose face was blank loosed thy hand and departed. As he went, I saw his face—it was the face of the great Tempter… And thou, Romola, didst wring thy hands and seek water, and there was none… and the plain was bare and stony again, and thou wast alone…”

She couldn’t stop hearing it. The wasted face, the strained gaze at the crucifix, the awe that made her kneel, the broken words, the silence after—everything replayed in her mind like a vision from another world.

But her mind resisted. Just as a strong body fights fumes when they threaten to choke, a strong soul resists phantasms when they threaten to rule.

What did those words have to do with her real grief? They were chance—mere echoes of Dino’s beliefs. He feared pagan learning. He valued celibacy. He remembered their home, the library. From these threads, the vision had been woven.

What reason had she to believe such a thing? None. True as the warning had proved, Romola knew with certainty that acting on it would have been cowardice. Her trust had failed—but she would choose again to have trusted, rather than be led by ghosts.

Yet the memory persisted, linking itself to her present. It gave her a glimpse into lives she had once dismissed—lives shaped by visions, voices, inner revelations.

“If only they were stronger in me,” she whispered, “I might lose sight of what that vision really was—and take it for prophecy. I might become a seer myself, like Suora Maddalena, Camilla Rucellai…”

She shuddered. All her education, all her upbringing, had taught her to scorn such superstition—the weakness of those who sit in darkness, reading fate in flickering flames.

And yet—she felt something deeper than coincidence. The moment with Dino, the connection with his dying mind—it lived anew in her. If such experiences were common, she would want to understand them. Even learn the thoughts of those who wept before images of martyrdom.

There seemed to be more than madness in that communion with suffering. Her springs were dried up. She wondered what other waters existed—what strength men found in the desert.

And the moments in the Duomo, when she had sobbed with a strange blend of rapture and pain as Fra Girolamo offered himself for the people—those came back to her, like a taste of a distant spring.

But again, she recoiled. She refused to let herself be drawn into visions, into fears that twisted natural love, as Dino had done.

She sat in darkness, waiting for dawn—her disguise complete, her heart torn, her path chosen.

Romola awoke to the soft tap of a knuckle against her door. The pale, cold light of dawn seeped through the shutters, casting long shadows across the room. Maso stood there, waiting for the travelling-wallet. He started slightly when she opened the door—no longer the woman he knew, with hair like sunlight and grace in every line, but a figure wrapped in thick grey folds, her face hidden beneath the dark cowl, pale and still as winter stone.

“It is well, Maso,” she said, forcing calm into her voice. “The wallet is ready. You may go on quietly—I shall not be far behind. Once beyond the gates, you may walk more slowly; I may join you before Trespiano.”

She closed the door gently, then reached for the key she had taken from the casket the night before. It was the original key to the small painted tabernacle—the one Tito had forgotten to cast into the Arno. It had slipped unnoticed into the corner of his embroidered scarsella, worn with the purple tunic, and years later, after their marriage, Romola had found it there. She had kept it, not used it, but held it close—knowing it was within reach, a quiet certainty in a life that had grown uncertain.

Now, she turned toward the cabinet where the tabernacle stood. It had been moved near the window, so the morning light fell upon its painted surface just enough to reveal the familiar scenes: Bacchus triumphant, crowned with vines and clusters, embracing Ariadne; the Loves scattering roses, dolphins gliding through rippled seas, all framed by a border of flowers like a paradise bower.

But now, these images struck her with a new bitterness. They were no longer beautiful—they were mocking. Foolish Ariadne, her eyes wide with love, her curls like tendrils entwined in the vines—how she must have believed in joy! How different she would look now, Romola thought, if she could see what lay beneath the surface of this world.

Romola lifted the mirror and looked at herself. The reflection startled her—the pale face, the shadowed eyes, the cowl drawn tight. She set the mirror down quickly, as though repelled. This disguise, this act of concealment, made her feel unclean. Was she becoming one of those fanatics, those weeping nuns she had once despised? The thought filled her with dread.

She thrust the key into the lock of the tabernacle, opened it swiftly, and took out the crucifix without looking at it. With trembling fingers, she threaded a cord through the ring, fastened it around her neck, and hid it beneath the folds of her mantle.

“For Dino’s sake,” she whispered.

There were letters still to write—letters Maso would carry back to Bologna. The first was to Tito:

“Tito, my love for you is dead. And so, so far as I was yours, I too am dead. Do not seek to enforce laws to bring me back—it would only bring you sorrow. The Romola you married will never return. I need explain nothing. The words I spoke to you last—those were not anger, but the truth of an irreversible change.

I ask only that my bridal chest be sent to my godfather, who gave it to me. It holds my wedding garments, the portraits, and relics of my parents.”

She folded the ring inside the letter and wrote Tito’s name on the outside.

The second letter was to Bernardo del Nero:

“Dearest Godfather—If I could have brought comfort to your life by staying, I would not have left. But I have gone. Do not ask why. If you love my father, prevent anyone from seeking me. I could not endure Florence any longer. I cannot speak of why. Let my life remain silent. I have asked that my bridal chest be sent to you. When you open it, you will understand.

Give all my mother’s things to my cousin Brigida, and ask her to forgive me for not saying farewell.

Farewell, my second father. The best thing I have in life is still the memory of your kindness—and gratitude.

Romola.”

She placed the letters beside the crucifix in the depths of her mantle. Everything was done. She was ready.

No sound stirred in the house. She descended the stairs like a ghost, her steps soft on the stone. Outside, the street was empty, the city still asleep. Her heart pounded, yet she felt a strange strength in each step—like a chain broken at last. The urgency of her choice, the fear of delay, pushed sorrow aside. As she reached the Ponte Rubaconte, she noticed less the sight of Santa Croce than the widening streak of yellow light cutting through the grey sky. She quickened her pace—fearful of faces, of recognition.

Her path led straight to Borgo Pinti, then along the city walls to Porta San Gallo. It passed through Piazza di Santa Croce, but she walked through it without pause, without looking toward the church. The idea of being watched—of strangers speculating on her grief—filled her with a physical shiver. She felt degraded even by the act of her husband that had driven her here. Yet no one noticed her—not a glance from a window, not a word from a passerby.

She was alone.

Beyond the gate, the city dwindled. The sky opened wide above her. She stepped into freedom—a life of solitude and hardship, yes, but free. She had broken the chains she had once accepted blindly. From now on, no breath of false affection would touch her cheek, no oppressive mind suffocate her spirit.

The air was sharp, the trees bare, the hills bleak. These were not lands haunted by gods of beauty and pleasure—gods she had abandoned forever.

Then, suddenly, the sun broke through. Light flooded the road, casting long shadows. In that instant, the world seemed alive with divine presence—so strong, so clear, that Romola felt a deeper awe settle within her. This was no mere sunrise. It was a moment of revelation, of transformation. She stood in the golden glow, her own shadow stretching before her, a silent companion she could not escape.

Until now, she had seen only peasants with mules, and the winding road had hidden Maso’s distance. But as she passed Pietra and climbed higher ground, she lifted the edge of her cowl to look ahead.

And froze.

It was not Maso.

Two monks approached, only yards away.

The cowl had shielded her vision—she had been seeing only the path, her shadow, the brightness beneath her feet. Now, the encounter startled her. Monks—her disguise made them dangerous. They might demand prayers, passwords, signs of faith she could not give. She walked on, pretending indifference, her hands clenched, her breath shallow.

They passed, moving downhill. Relief came quickly. She saw a flat stone beside a cypress tree rising from a green bank. She sat down, turning her back to Florence, refusing to look until they were gone.

She raised the cowl again—and saw Maso and the mules ahead, not far off. She could catch up. She might linger.

She sat there, breathing deeply, feeling the cold wind on her face, the weight of the world lifted from her shoulders.

She was free. Alone. And finally, hers.

That evening, Tito had set out for supper with high spirits. The invitation came from Bernardo Rucellai, a man whose name carried weight in Florence—not just because of his wealth or noble blood, but because he embodied the very essence of Florentine grandeur. His family’s fortune had begun centuries before, when a merchant brought from the Levant a rare dye made from a lichen known as _orcella_, which grew on sun-baked rocks in distant islands. This crimson pigment—_oricello_—had become prized throughout Europe, and its success earned the family their name: Oricellari, later softened into Rucellai. Bernardo himself was no mere inheritor of fortune. He had married Lorenzo de’ Medici’s sister, hosted the Platonic Academy after Lorenzo’s death, built a magnificent palace, and completed the striking black-and-white marble façade of Santa Maria Novella. He wrote learned treatises on ancient Rome, collected relics of antiquity, and spoke Latin with purity and grace. To list his achievements was almost to write an epitaph—one that might prompt the Muses themselves to lament the impossibility of such excellence dwelling in one mortal frame.

Tito had received the invitation through Lorenzo Tornabuoni, who emphasized its importance, hinting at political purpose. And indeed, Bernardo was one of the Twenty, the ruling body of Florence for three weeks. The gathering at the Rucellai Gardens—known also as the Selva or Orto de’ Rucellai—was expected to be both sumptuous and significant. Fine wines, delicate dishes, and distinguished company awaited. Yet Tito had reason to be wary. He had attended similar dinners before—banquets where conversation veered toward metaphysics and philosophical systems so intricate they required not only attention but endurance. On one occasion, he had been asked not merely to accept a worldview, but to listen to its full exposition, from creation to final consummation. It was exhausting.

This night, however, promised something different. A dark sky hung over Florence, lit only by occasional lanterns glowing before images of the Virgin. As Tito walked along the Via della Scala, his figure emerged briefly in the light, then vanished again into shadow. Behind him, moving with quiet determination, was another man: thickset, elderly, dressed in a simple serge tunic and felt hat. Baldassarre Calvo.

They reached the garden gate together. The smaller door—the _sportello_—was already open, held by a servant distracted by some other matter. Tito stepped inside swiftly, naming himself as he passed between evergreen hedges illuminated by torchlight. Then Baldassarre followed.

“Your name?” the servant asked.

“Baldassarre Calvo,” he replied without hesitation.

“You are not on the guest list. All guests have entered.”

“I belong to Messer Tito Melema. I am to wait in the gardens.”

The servant hesitated. “Only guests may enter. Are you his servant?”

“No,” said Baldassarre calmly. “I am a scholar.”

There are those whom one cannot refuse simply by speaking with quiet authority. The porter stepped aside. Baldassarre entered, and the gate clanged shut behind him, chaining securely as he disappeared among the gleaming foliage.

His confidence was remarkable—unlike the man who had once stood trembling before Tito, dagger broken in two. That moment had marked a turning point. After Tito left, Baldassarre sat in the wreckage of his rage, limbs numb, hands shaking, yet his mind strangely clear. In that stillness, memories surged—not fragmented, but whole. He saw again the life he had lived, the years of suffering, the betrayal, the secret marriage to Monna Lisa, the hidden wife in the Via de’ Bardi. Now it all came back: the truth, the shame, the injustice.

He looked down at the shattered dagger. Then at the book beside him—a large manuscript of Pausanias, open under moonlight. The page bore the title: ΜΕΣΣΕΝΙΚΑ. ΚΒ′. Once, he had read this work fluently. But earlier that night, it had meant nothing—just ink on parchment, like stains on a wall. Now, the letters burned with meaning. They summoned a world: Messenia, resisting Sparta, enduring tyranny. He saw the stoning of Aristocrates—condemned by a people who cast him out, refused burial, and erected a pillar inscribed with verses of divine retribution. The words echoed in him, stirring long-dormant emotions. He forgot his age. He could have shouted.

The light had returned—mother of knowledge, mother of joy.

Strength flooded back into his limbs. He rose, took up the broken dagger and the book, and stepped out into the moonlit hilltop. The air was sharp with frost, but he felt no cold. Only warmth—of power, of clarity. He wandered across the high ground, gazing down upon Florence, asleep beneath its mountain guardians. The river glimmered palely; the valley stretched toward snow-capped peaks. And he felt himself master of it all.

For years, memory had been a dim echo, a half-remembered dream. Now, it was a living force. He could see cities, understand their rhythms, grasp their secrets. Names, faces, events—his mind raced through them like a man reclaiming an inheritance. He was no longer lost in shadows.

Yet amid this resurgence, one thought remained fixed: Tito.

The man who had stolen his life, his honor, his peace. The serpent who slithered through society with charm and lies. And now, at last, he could reach him. Not for justice—no, not that. For vengeance. For the deepest wound possible: to shatter the illusion of triumph, to make the smug favorite feel the sting of humiliation, to bring him face to face with his own guilt.

Baldassarre had spent days tracking Tito, learning his habits, his allies, his weaknesses. He avoided Nello’s shop, knowing Tito frequented it. He turned away from Piero di Cosimo, fearing any sign of pursuit might alert the target. He had bought a new dagger—spending what little money remained—and lived frugally, feeding only on hunger and thirst for revenge.

Twice, he had felt the return of fog—mental dizziness, loss of form, fading vision. Once, he had tried to enter the Palazzo Vecchio, hoping to confront Tito in council, but failed. The fear gnawed at him: would the light fade again? Would he lose everything?

But tonight, he felt certain. Tonight, the moment was right. Tito was at the height of his favor, surrounded by powerful men, secure in his position. And Baldassarre would not let him escape. He would not seek punishment, nor recognition. He wanted only one thing: to deliver the sharpest pain imaginable—to make Tito know, in that instant, that he was not invincible.

And so, as the garden gates closed behind him, Baldassarre Calvo stepped into the heart of the feast—not as a guest, but as a ghost returning from the past, armed with memory, resolve, and a broken blade reborn.

As Tito stepped into the elegant pavilion, his sharp eyes quickly took in the gathering—its guests, their manner, the air of quiet purpose that hung over them like a veil. He had guessed rightly: this was no mere social affair. Though the evening wore the guise of conviviality, its true aim lay deeper—in the strengthening of political bonds through shared wine and conversation. In Florence, where moderation was prized, such gatherings were rare but potent. Good food, fine wine, and the warmth of fellowship were believed to sharpen the mind’s grasp of principle, especially when the spirits were high and reason softened by pleasure. The Florentines valued thrift, yet even among them, indulgence had its devotees—those who bowed to Madonna della Gozzoviglia, goddess of feasting, and San Buonvino, patron of good cheer. The Rucellai family stood apart, known for their lavish hospitality and refined taste. Tonight, they hosted not just a dinner, but a subtle meeting of minds and ambitions.

There would be no lofty discourse on philosophy here—not tonight. Yet there was no need to remove the bust of Plato from the wall, nor erase the frescoes of the cardinal virtues. These symbols had long watched over more intellectual banquets. This very bust had once presided over the Platonic Academy, brought here after Lorenzo de’ Medici’s death, when the philosophers migrated from his villa to these gardens. On each November 13th—the anniversary of Plato’s passing—a select group of scholars would gather beneath laurel leaves, dining with restraint and debating the master’s teachings with fervor. Among them had been Pico della Mirandola, once a brilliant, reckless youth whose bold theses startled Rome, later transformed into a man seeking inner peace; Marsilio Ficino, nurtured from youth on Platonism until his mind seemed almost too saturated with it; Angelo Poliziano, poet and scholar alike, whose words still stirred readers centuries later; and Leon Battista Alberti, a towering intellect—artist, scientist, inventor, thinker—whose breadth dwarfed even the most gifted of his peers. Others, less famed but no less vital, had shaped the intellectual currents of their age, leaving unseen legacies as essential as the laborers who tilled the land before us.

Bernardo Rucellai himself would have earned a place in that circle long before he became its host. Still in his prime, barely forty-four, he carried himself with quiet dignity, a man of refined Latin speech so pure that Erasmus once noted he could not be tricked into speaking it—even under pressure. He greeted Tito with unusual warmth, placing him between Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci, both young men of the Medicean faction, accomplished and discreet.

At first, the talk remained light. As the brass bowl of scented water passed around, fingers adorned with rings gleamed under wax-lit candles, and the scent of fresh white damask—imported from France—drifted through the room. Someone mused aloud: what would Dante’s old Florentine, stiff in leather belt and bone clasp, think if he could see silver forks at table? All agreed that the customs of later generations would astonish their ancestors—if only those ancestors could witness them.

While the forks danced lightly over appetizers—tiny morsels of liver cooked to melt upon the tongue—conversation turned to the artistry of the table. The enamel designs on the brass service drew admiration, and someone remarked again on the silver dish for confetti, a masterpiece by Antonio Pollajuolo, whom popes had lured away from Florence to Rome, seduced by greater splendor.

“I remember,” said Niccolò Ridolfi, a man of middle years with an air of effortless ease born of lifelong status, “Antonio growling about his metalwork. He took up painting in a rage, saying, ‘The artist who puts his work into gold and silver puts his brains into the melting-pot.’”

“Not unlikely,” replied Giannozzo Pucci. “If this war with Pisa continues, and unrest spreads to our other towns, it won’t just be our silver dishes that vanish. I fear even Antonio’s silver saints at San Giovanni may one day disappear—melted down into coin.”

“The Frate is already preparing us for that,” said Tornabuoni. “He tells the people God will not accept silver crucifixes while stomachs starve. The church, he says, is best adorned with the gems of holiness and the fine gold of brotherly love.”

“A useful doctrine for funding war,” Bernardo Rucellai observed dryly. “But politics come after the confetti, Lorenzo—when we’ve drunk enough wine to wash them down. They’re too solid for roast and boiled.”

“Indeed,” Ridolfi added. “Luigi Pulci would have said this delicate kid must be eaten with an impartial mind. Once, at Careggi, when Luigi was in full flow, he insisted nothing corrupted the palate like opinion. ‘Opinion,’ he declared, ‘corrupts the saliva—that’s why men took to pepper. Scepticism is the only philosophy that doesn’t bring a taste in the mouth.’ ‘Nay,’ poor Lorenzo de’ Medici replied, ‘you’re wrong. Look at Matteo Franco—untainted sceptic—who wants hotter sauce than any of us.’ ‘Because he has a strong opinion of himself,’ Luigi shot back. That’s the original egg of all opinion. _He_ a sceptic? He believes in the immortality of his own verses. He’s like that friar who described the pavement of the bottomless pit. Poor Luigi—his mind was steel, sharp enough to cut anything it touched.”

“And yet gentle-hearted,” Pucci said. “His talk seemed like soap-bubbles. He raved about food and drink—but lived as temperately as a butterfly.”

The feast lingered. After meats came capon and game, then the crowning glory: a peacock, prepared according to Apicius’s recipe—feathers intact, arranged to resemble a living bird at rest. Only the skilled carver dared turn it, exposing the plucked breast to serve delicate slices. Few chose it. Tito quoted Horace and scattered his portion across the plate. Rucellai offered a learned note on ancient peacock eggs but declined to eat. Ridolfi held a piece on his fork, telling a story of a Sienese man who, wanting to impress without expense, bought a wild goose, severed its beak and feet, boiled it in its feathers, and served it as a pea-hen.

In truth, little peacock was eaten. But the spectacle mattered—proof of wealth, of taste, of belonging to a world beyond reach for most. To speak ill of such luxuries now would be folly, especially when Fra Girolamo preached that the rich should not live opulently for the sake of the poor.

Outside, in the cold dark, another man walked in slow circles. Baldassarre, disowned and alone, paused among the trees, watching through the windows. He saw Tito laughing, gesturing, heard his voice rise and fall in the warm chatter. His heart burned with ambition—this moment, this night, was his chance to break into that world. And he took grim pleasure in seeing Tito so carefree, unaware of the trap being set.

Inside, the light masked everything. The glow of candles and laughter hid the pale face in the shadows. Light, like darkness, could be a curtain.

As the meal advanced, the talk grew sharper. The sense of civic duty pressed heavily on all. Fra Girolamo’s sermons, his influence, dominated the thoughts of every guest. Before the stewed fish was removed, and sweets still awaited, the friar’s name surfaced—despite Rucellai’s earlier warning. Politics crept back in.

At first, it was gossip: how the Great Council vote had gone, how hot-tempered Francesco Valori had been, how Soderini’s speeches echoed the friar’s sermons—both, it seemed, fed from the same source.

“My view,” Ridolfi said, “is that the Frate has a longer head for public affairs than Soderini or any Piagnone. Soderini is merely his mouthpiece.”

“No,” Rucellai countered, “the Frate is clever, yes—but Soderini is no fool. He’s studied Venice’s councils. He won’t borrow ideas from a monk. He loads the cannon; the Frate brings the powder and lights the match. The people are growing stronger—and they’re beginning to rule us. Ecco!”

When servants left, wine flowed freely. Then Tornabuoni spoke: “Whether Soderini owes the Frate or not, we owe him the amnesty. We might have done without moral reform voted by black beans—but the fact that our heads remain on our shoulders, and our property spared, is a gift we welcome. And I believe only the Frate’s power could have secured that. I’ve spoken with him.”

A murmur rose. Rucellai simply nodded—no surprise, no comment.

“Yes,” Tornabuoni continued, “I visited him in his cell—an uncommon favor. Even Valori rarely sees him alone. But perhaps he welcomed me because I wasn’t already his follower—I had to be converted. And now I see clearly: the safest path for us Mediceans is to support the Frate’s party. We’re too weak to stand alone. If the Frate falls, who will rise? Nerli, Alberti, Pazzi—the Arrabbiati—men who’d tear us apart like mad dogs, not content until half of us are banished.”

Agreement rippled through the table.

“A wise dissimulation,” Tornabuoni went on, “is the only course for rational men in times of turmoil. I needn’t say my real loyalties here—many share them. But beyond personal ties, I agree with my friends: the only lasting peace for Florence is the dominance of one family. The Frate’s dream—a popular government where no party exists—is fit for some remote island found by Cristoforo Colombo, not for quarrelsome Florence. Change is coming. With patience, we can shape it. For now, let the Frate’s flag fly. If another rises, it will be black for us.”

“It’s true,” Ridolfi said bluntly. “You’re right, Lorenzo. I’m too old to pretend I’ve changed. Some of us—like Bernardo del Nero—would never take another’s shield. But we can lie still, like old dogs. Barking won’t help now. As for these psalm-singers, who vote only for God’s glory and pretend we can all love each other—well, their time won’t last. There are only two kinds of government: one where men show teeth, one where they lick the strongest. They’ll get their Great Council tomorrow—certain. They’ll think they’ve invented something new. But as sure as every lucco in the Council has human skin, their plan will end in snarling or licking. That’s my view. Not that I think noble families should chase reasons like law doctors. I’ll stay true to my alliances. No chalk-mark tells me which reason is true. But Bernardo here—Rucellai—he’s a man of reasons. I’ll listen to fine arguments, so long as they don’t interfere with my duties.”

“If you appeal to me, Niccolò,” Rucellai replied with formal dignity, “I must say: while I am shaped by long-standing ties, I cannot commit to schemes I do not control. I might accept a return to the old order—with changes. But I fully agree with Lorenzo: our best policy is to align with the popular party. I make no pretense. I do not hide my doubts. Of the twenty men I work with, I disagree with every one. No single scheme commands my full assent.”

Ridolfi shrugged. The conversation shifted.

As wine deepened, voices grew bolder. The company split into smaller groups. Tornabuoni and Pucci, aware of this, soon moved toward Tito, seated between them, drawing chairs away from the table.

“In truth, Melema,” Tornabuoni said, crossing one leg over the other, “I know no man in Florence better suited to serve our cause. You see what others are—men who cannot conceal their bias, like dogs that bark in their natural tone. Or those whose loyalty is obvious, and thus suspect. Giannozzo and I—we can overcome suspicion. We have finesse, discretion. But you—except you—there is no one else we can trust.”

“Yes,” Pucci said, laying a hand on Tito’s shoulder, “you could help us more than Ulysses himself. I believe Ulysses often made enemies. To manage men, you need a sharp mind wrapped in velvet. And no one in Florence could undertake a journey to Rome with the safety you possess. Your scholarship gives cover. But your talent—your charm—outshines even that. Macchiavelli might have served us, but not as well. He’s too tied to ideas, lacks your grace. He’s lost a great opportunity. You’ve gained it.”

Tornabuoni lowered his voice. “Play your part well, Melema, and the future is yours. The Medici will keep a foothold in Rome as well as Florence. Soon, they may offer even greater rewards than before. Why not take holy orders? A cardinal’s hat awaits. You wouldn’t be the first Greek to wear it.”

Tito laughed. He understood the flattery, but enjoyed it.

“My joints aren’t stiff enough,” he said, “to run for such a prize. A few abbeys held *in commendam*, without tonsure—now that would satisfy me.”

“I was serious,” Tornabuoni said calmly. “A scholar benefits from ordination. But we’ll speak of that later. First: you must win the confidence of those around San Marco. Giannozzo and I will work on that—but you can go farther, since you’re less noticed. You’ll learn their plans, become a screen for our actions. Nothing can be done before your trip to Rome—Piero’s business with the French nobles must happen now. When you return… well, no need to say more. You could become the favorite of San Marco, if you wished. But you know: effective deception is never excessive.”

“If it weren’t for your need to appear loyal to the popular side,” Pucci said gently, “I’d wish your talents used elsewhere—where they’d shine even brighter. But now, we must find someone to infiltrate the Arrabbiati. We need to know their moves more than the Frate’s, who act openly. Still, it would have been hard for you—given your past ties to the Medici, your wife’s connection to Bernardo del Nero. We need someone unconnected, neutral.”

Tito pushed his hair back, his lips curling slightly.

“No need to look elsewhere,” he said. “I can handle it all. I’ll make myself Dolfo Spini’s closest confidant—know his plans before he does.”

He rarely spoke so confidently. But now, a new path opened—broader, more powerful than he’d imagined. Success had once meant favor; now it meant power—power available to the untethered, the unbound. Each party that saw him as a tool might soon depend on him. His alien status, his lack of belief, became advantages. He felt anew the thrill of skill in play. And the motives that might have made him recoil from deceit—loyalty, conscience—had long since been worn thin by the lies of his life.

Romola sat on the stone beneath the cypress tree, and for the first time in her life, she felt truly alone—free from walls, from streets, from the weight of expectation. The morning air was still; the road stretched before her like a ribbon unspooling into distance. Behind her, Florence receded. Ahead, the path wound toward Siena, where Tito Melema now rode, unaware that his wife had slipped away—not in flight, but in search of something else: silence, perhaps, or escape from a life that no longer held meaning. She had left behind not just a husband, but a world of duties, names, and roles she could no longer bear.

The mountains loomed dark and distant, separated from her by a deep hollow in the land—a gulf between her and the cold quiet of nature. It was as if the earth itself had opened to let her breathe. No one watched. No one claimed her. For an instant, she believed she was free.

Then, a voice—low, firm, yet unmistakable—broke the stillness.

“You are Romola de’ Bardi, the wife of Tito Melema.”

She did not turn. She knew that voice. It had pierced through her thoughts more than once before, when she stood at the edge of decisions too great for her own reason. But this time, it came not from memory, but from the present—close, undeniable.

It was one of those monks in black robes who moved through Florence like shadows, their presence always suggesting a deeper order than the world’s surface allowed. And yet, though she resented the intrusion, Romola felt a tremor pass through her—not fear, not yet, but a kind of awe, as if some unseen force had reached out and touched her soul.

“I have a command from God,” the voice said, “to stop you. You are not permitted to flee.”

Her anger flared. She refused to look at him. Her body remained rigid, seated, but her mind burned with defiance.

“What right do you have to speak to me? To hinder me?”

“The right of a messenger,” he replied. “You wear the garb of a nun, but your purpose is not holy. You have used it as a disguise. Yet you were not meant to pass me unseen. I know who you are. I know what you seek—to hide your name, your place, your duty. You wish to choose a new life, without law, without rule, without obligation. But I am sent to call you back. My daughter, you must return to your place.”

Each word struck her like a blow. She wanted to reject them, to rise and walk away, but something held her—something deeper than pride. She was shaken. Not by the threat, but by the certainty in his tone. This man did not speak from power, but from conviction. He spoke as if he carried a truth that could not be denied.

At last, she rose. Her eyes met his.

It was Fra Girolamo—Savonarola. She had known it all along, even before seeing his face. And now, standing before him, nearly his height, she felt small.

She opened her mouth to speak, but no words came. His gaze held hers—not with judgment, but with a quiet intensity that seemed to see into her very being. There was nothing divine in his features—no halo, no radiant beauty. Only strength, discipline, and a calm that humbled her.

He saw her, not as a woman fleeing, but as a soul in conflict. And in that gaze, Romola sensed something she had never known before: human fellowship—not born of blood or love, but of shared purpose, of a bond forged in truth.

And so she fell silent.

“Your freedom,” Savonarola said gently, “is proud. But tell me—what is more base than a debtor who thinks himself free?”

A chill ran through her. The words cut to the core of her belief.

“You are fleeing,” he continued, “from your debts—the debt of a Florentine woman, the debt of a wife. You turn your back on the lot appointed for you, and seek another. But can any man or woman choose their duty? No more than they can choose their birth or their parents. You are running from God’s presence into the wilderness.”

She tried to argue, but the fire of her resistance cooled. In its place came a strange sense—of possibility. Could submission bring strength? Could obedience mean freedom?

She looked at him again, and this time, she spoke with reverence, though still with hesitation.

“My father,” she said, “you cannot know the reasons that drive me. None can. Only I know them. I have been driven by sorrow. I must go.”

“I know enough,” he replied. “My mind has been illuminated concerning you. You are not happy in marriage—but I am not your confessor. I need not know what lies within your heart. I only know this: you were warned before your marriage, by a message from heaven. You chose the bond. Now you seek to break it. That is a betrayal—not only of your husband, but of the sacred trust between man and man, between citizen and city.”

She shrank as if struck. The thought of Tito—his deceit, his cowardice—came rushing back, and then, suddenly, she saw herself reflected in his sin. Was she not also breaking faith? Was not her flight a form of betrayal?

“And why flee?” Savonarola pressed. “Florence is the home of your soul, not just your birthplace. Your people suffer. Hunger walks our streets. Misery cries out. And you say, ‘I care not—I will go.’ You think yourself above them? But you are not. You have lived apart, high on your hill, blind to the needs of others. You scorned the Church, yet you have no faith of your own. You have become a wanderer, seeking freedom, but finding only emptiness.”

She raised her head. “I was not going for ease. I was going to hardship. I expect no joy. It is gone from my life.”

“Then you seek only your own will,” he said. “But good is not chosen—it flows from obedience. It is a river from the foot of the Invisible Throne. If you forsake your duty, you will find only sorrow without purpose—bitter herbs, and no bread.”

Tears gathered in her eyes. She clasped her hands, pleading. “If you knew—how impossible it seemed to endure!”

He pointed to the cord around her neck. “Draw forth what you carry.”

She hesitated, then pulled out the crucifix.

He nodded. “There, my daughter, is the image of a Supreme Offering—made by Supreme Love, because the need of man was great.”

She stared at the cross, trembling. How far she had come since she first took it from his hands! Once, she had seen it as a symbol of piety, a relic of tradition. Now, it seemed to glow with meaning she had never understood.

“Conform your life to that image,” he said. “Make your sorrow an offering. When the fire of Divine charity burns within you, you will see the needs of others, and you will not call your sacrifice great.”

She looked up, her eyes filled with a dawning recognition.

“You have carried yourself proudly,” he continued, “as one apart, untouched by common blood or thought. But you have been unborn to true life. Your love for your father is gone? Then you are without law, without religion—no better than a beast robbed of her young. But if you had faith, you would not flee. You would stay, and work, and suffer—for Florence, for your people.”

She turned away, unable to meet his gaze. The path ahead was clear, yet terrible.

“If I return,” she whispered, “I must face him. My husband… he is not… my love is gone.”

“He is not a malefactor?” Savonarola asked.

“No,” she said quickly. “I accuse him of nothing.”

“Then you have no excuse,” he said softly. “For if he were guilty, your place would be beside him in prison. But he is not. And so your duty remains. Marriage is not merely flesh and desire. It is a sacrament—a vow made before God. You may not break it.”

She shook her head. “Yet how can I bear—”

“Bear it,” he said. “Make your sorrows an offering. For the work of redemption begins here, in Florence. Sin and sorrow are being made to cease. And you, my daughter, are called to partake in it. You are a child of Florence. Fulfil your inheritance.”

His voice grew stronger, his face alight with passion. He stood with arms folded, yet radiating a power that drew her closer, not by force, but by truth.

“Come,” he said. “Come back to your place.”

And in that moment, Romola felt the old doubts dissolve. The cold fears melted under the warmth of his faith. She was not being commanded—she was being called.

With a low, prayerful cry, she said, “Father, I will be guided. Teach me. I will go back.”

She sank to her knees.

Savonarola reached out his hands over her—but said nothing. For words were no longer needed. The silence between them spoke louder than any sermon ever could.

“Rise, my daughter,” said Fra Girolamo at last, his voice calm and steady. “Your servant waits not far off with the mules. It is time for me to continue my journey to Florence.”

Romola rose slowly from her knees. The silence she had kept was more than mere stillness—it had been a sacred act, a surrender of self to a new way of being. In renouncing her decision to leave her husband, she had felt her will shattered, as if every thread of personal resolve had been severed. Now, even the smallest choices seemed beyond her. She lifted the edge of her cowl, glancing toward the hill’s edge where Maso and another Dominican stood with their backs turned, ten yards away. But her eyes returned to Savonarola, lingering on him in quiet appeal. She did not speak. She waited for him to send Maso back—because to command would have felt like defiance, and she could not yet bear that.

“I will go and call them,” he said, interpreting her look. “And I wish to place you under the care of the brother who travels with me. You have chosen to submit yourself to guidance, to seek wisdom once deemed foolishness. One gateway to such wisdom is confession. You will need a confessor, my daughter—and I entrust you to Fra Salvestro, a brother of San Marco, whom I trust above all others.”

“I would rather have no guide but you, father,” Romola whispered, her voice trembling. Her brow furrowed with anxiety.

“My daughter,” he replied gently, “I do not serve as a confessor. My calling keeps me apart from such duties—those that would draw me into constant contact with the world, distracting me from my mission.”
