“Then shall I never speak with you privately?” she asked, her voice rising slightly. “If I falter—if I—” She stopped herself, her breath catching. A sudden fear gripped her: what if the strength she had found in surrender began to fade without his presence?

“Should your soul need words from me,” Savonarola said softly, “you will let Fra Salvestro know. I will meet you in the sacristy or choir of San Marco. And know this—I will not cease to watch over you. I will instruct my brother well, so that he may lead you along the path of service—to the poor, the hungry, the suffering. For now, you are called not only as a woman, but as a daughter of Florence in these hard times. I wish to see you among the weaker sisters as the apple tree among the forest trees—your beauty and gifts not ends in themselves, but vessels through which the divine light shines more clearly. I must go now. I will call your servant.”

When Maso had moved ahead, Fra Salvestro stepped forward. Savonarola led Romola to him. Before, Romola had felt an instinctive resistance to accepting a stranger as her spiritual guide. Yet to oppose Savonarola’s choice now would be to reclaim independence when she had just abandoned it. Her mind was bent not toward ease, but toward sacrifice. She bowed deeply before Fra Salvestro, her gaze lowered, then raised her eyes.

But when she saw him fully, her hesitation turned to unease.

Some men inspire reverence by their very presence; others require us to bring our reverence already formed. That distinction struck Romola sharply as Savonarola disappeared from view and Fra Salvestro Maruffi took his place. There was nothing overtly unpleasing in the man’s face or manner—no trace of hypocrisy, no coarseness. Indeed, his features were finer than Savonarola’s, his frame taller. He was a well-known confessor to Florence’s elite, seasoned in spiritual direction. Yet his expression lacked depth—a wavering quality, as if his thoughts could not fix upon one great passion or conviction. It was not insincerity, but a shallowness of spirit. Such souls often strive earnestly to fulfill noble roles, reciting lofty truths with solemn faces, only to betray themselves through subtle shifts in expression—like a poet who cannot summon true verse, whose mouth betrays prose instead.

Fra Salvestro was prone to visions, perhaps born of a constitution inclined toward somnambulism. Savonarola believed these visions came from divine sources; Fra Salvestro himself had once doubted them, even rebuking Savonarola for preaching prophecy. This contrast proved again that greatness is not measured by belief in superstition, but by the fire within. Between the two, there was no question: one was a prophet, the other a well-meaning man shaped by circumstance.

The difference became clear to Romola as Fra Salvestro began speaking—offering words of encouragement, exhortation. After her initial rebellion against Savonarola had passed, she had forgotten her old dread of falling into fanaticism, of being drawn into cold, rigid piety. But now, that dread returned—not with force, but like a chill wind stirring through a clearing after sunrise. It could not undo the transformation she had undergone, but it dimmed the glow of her new resolve. Like grey clouds gathering over dawn, it made her return seem long and dull.

And perhaps no path is harder than the one we retrace after walking it with fierce determination.

As they crossed the city gates, delicate snowflakes drifted down around them. The grey sister hurried home from the Piazza di San Marco, stepping across the bridge, turning into the large door on Via de’ Bardi. Her footsteps left dark marks on the thin layer of snow, her cowl heavy and damp against her face.

She climbed to her room, shed her serge cloak, burned the letters she had written to Dino, restored her small treasures to their places, unbound her hair, and slipped into her plain black dress. Instead of embarking on a distant journey, she would sit where she always sat. Snow fell softly against the windows. She was alone.

Yet she did not feel despair. The solitude was sharp, yes—but her spirit burned with purpose. She was beginning again, guided by a new understanding. She had poured her entire will into renunciation. The empty tabernacle remained locked, and she placed Dino’s crucifix outside it, a silent witness.

No sound broke the quiet until night descended like a white ghost at the windowpane.

And yet—for Romola—this Christmas Eve of 1494 would remain the most unforgettable of her life.

It was the thirtieth of October, 1496. The morning sky was clear, and a gentle autumn wind stirred the leaves along the city’s narrow streets. But the Florentines had little thought for such comforts. Their minds were gripped by storms at sea—gales that had scattered ships from Marseilles carrying soldiers and grain, leaving Florence stranded in desperation. Famine stalked her alleys; hunger gnawed at every door. And beyond the walls, enemies gathered. The French king, once hailed as a new Charlemagne, had swept through Italy, seized Naples with ease, and then vanished—some said broken-hearted over the death of his newborn son, others whispering he would never return. In his absence, a Holy League had formed under Pope Borgia to expel the “barbarians” still holding Naples. It sounded noble, but many saw it as wolves plotting to drive out one another, only to turn on the weakest prey. Florence, alone among the great Italian states, refused to join. She clung to her alliance with France, though now it seemed like a fatal choice.

The price was high. Pisa, still fighting fiercely for independence, was backed not only by Venice and Milan but also by Emperor Maximilian, who had been invited by the League. His forces joined those already besieging Leghorn, while Venetian and Genoese fleets blockaded the coast. If Leghorn fell, Florence would be cut off from the sea—her last lifeline severed. Already, she was hemmed in on land by hostile powers: the Pope’s enmity, smaller states’ jealousy, and the growing unrest within her own walls.

Yet Florence did not break. Her government, though strained, showed courage. Taxes were raised, new soldiers enlisted, and embassies sent—not just to plead, but to reason, to conciliate. Even more remarkably, they chose not to bar the gates against starving peasants and beggars fleeing other cities. These poor souls poured into Florence like birds seeking warmth after winter. It was a bold act—especially since Savonarola’s followers held strong sway in the council—and it drew sharp criticism. Many argued that Florence should have joined the League, united with her neighbors, rather than risk isolation. They said she should protect her own people, not invite disease and despair with every ragged stranger.

But each day brought deeper suffering. Each day, the murmurs grew louder. And now, for over a month, Fra Girolamo had not preached—his voice silenced by a papal decree. Yet when news came that the grain ships were lost, the people clamored for him again. Faith, they believed, was their only weapon. Against Rome’s orders, the Signoria summoned Savonarola back to the Duomo. Two days prior, he had stood once more in the pulpit and spoken with fire and calm: *Wait. Be steadfast. God will come.* He even offered to let them strip his robe from him if divine help did not arrive—proof enough of his conviction.

But on this morning, there was no sign of deliverance. Only cold winds and empty granaries.

Still, hope flickered. For centuries, the Florentines had turned to the Madonna dell’Impruneta in times of crisis—drought, plague, war, famine. Her image, hidden within a sacred tabernacle, had been carried into the city, and each time, relief followed. The faithful believed she was a mother who answered their pleas, who bent her ear to their sorrows. The saying went: *Florence has a Madonna who will do what they please.* Now, more than ever, they needed her.

And so, the night before, the tabernacle had been brought from L’Impruneta—six miles outside the city gate toward Rome—with solemn procession and guarded escort. It now rested in San Gaggio, awaiting the dawn ceremony when all the fraternities, guilds, and officials would carry it through the streets to the Duomo. But the Mother had not yet entered the city. The morning broke as it had every day: with hunger, fear, and silence.

Pestilence crept close behind famine. Hospitals overflowed. Courtyards of private homes became makeshift infirmaries. Still, the desperate remained unsheltered. And early that morning, as always, members of the burial brotherhoods walked the streets, collecting bodies left by the roadside—those too weak or forgotten to be found.

Among the quiet figures moving through the city was Romola de’ Bardi. Dressed in plain black serge, her head covered with a simple veil that concealed her hair except for the golden bands framing her brow, she walked from the Ponte Vecchio toward Por’ Santa Maria. Her steps were steady, her presence calm—like a quiet stream flowing through dry ground.

Her path was blocked by a bier being carried by members of San Jacopo del Popolo. The men paused, bending to examine something on the pavement. A crowd of idle workers gathered around, their faces drawn thin by hunger.

“He’s dead,” one declared. “Messer Domeneddio has taken him.”

“Ah,” said another, “if we could all stretch our legs and lie down, heads forward, it would be better than standing upright on empty stomachs.”

“Poor old man,” someone else muttered. “Death got a bad bargain. Life won.”

“No Florentine,” snapped a third. “A beggar from Siena. San Giovanni save us! They don’t need armies to fight us—they send us armies of starving men.”

“No,” said another, “this one’s from the Stinche. I know the grey patch where the prison badge used to be.”

“Quiet!” called a brother. “Help lift him. We’re wasting time.”

“But is he really dead?” asked one. “He might just be faint from hunger.”

Romola stepped forward. “Let me try wine,” she said softly.

She opened a small flask at her belt and, with practiced hands, slipped a tiny ivory tube between the man’s teeth. A few drops of wine trickled in. Then more. Slowly, the old man stirred. His head turned slightly. His eyes opened—dark, deep-set, clouded with exhaustion.

And then Romola froze.

Those eyes—wild, lined, shadowed by age—were unmistakable. She had seen them before: in the Duomo, the day Tito wore armor for the first time. In Piero’s sketch, where Tito flinched from a hand reaching out. This was the prisoner who had escaped—Baldassarre, the man whose fate had haunted her dreams.

A shiver ran through her. Was this the moment she would learn the truth? The secret that might unravel everything?

She felt the urge to flee—but then, stronger, the duty to stay. Her husband had harmed this man. She could not turn away.

She kept her right hand ready with the flask, her left supporting his neck. Her fingers trembled, but her instincts moved without thought.

Baldassarre looked up at her. For the first time, he saw the woman who lived in the Via de’ Bardi—the wife he had longed to see, denied by the walls of grief and silence.

“Shall we take him to San Stefano?” Romola asked gently. “We can move on, and you can continue your task.”

They walked thirty yards to the church steps. By then, Baldassarre could prop himself against the doorway. The brothers moved on, but the crowd lingered—curious, restless, hungry.

“Ah,” someone said, “he ate well in the Stinche. That’s why he’s weak now.”

“_Gnaffè_,” grumbled another. “That’s why the Eight turned some prisoners loose—to make room for honest folk. But if every thief gets revived with wine and bread, maybe we Ciompi should drown ourselves in the Arno while the water’s still running.”

Romola sat beside the old man. “Can you eat a little bread?” she asked. “I must go to the hospital, but I’ll return. Will you wait?”

He stared blankly, repeating her words: “Come back.”

She understood—he was fading. But she hoped he grasped the promise. She opened her basket—soft loaves inside—and placed one in his hand.

“Madonna,” said a rough man in a red night-cap, pushing through the crowd, “you save your bread for sick women and children. Why give it to a thief who won’t eat?”

“If he doesn’t want it,” replied another, “let him be. He’s better off than us—stomach full of cravings, no food.”

“Yes,” said a young man in fine cloth, “if a man wants to die, we shouldn’t revive him. Dead men don’t need trenchers.”

“Oh,” laughed the first, “you don’t understand the Frate’s charity. He preaches to the birds, like Saint Anthony. He says hawks are made to feed sparrows—just as good Florentines should feed six beggars from Arezzo or Bologna. That madonna there? She’s a pious Piagnone. She won’t waste her bread on honest citizens who’ve got nothing but prophecies to swallow.”

“Come, madonna,” said the man in the cap, “the old thief won’t eat. You’d better give it to us. We fast so much—we’re half saints already.”

The circle closed tight. Romola reached into her basket, dipped bread in wine, and prepared a soft sop. She did not look at them.

Then she rose.

Her gaze swept the faces before her. Pale, hollow-eyed, gaunt. Some watched her with hunger, others with defiance.

She held out the basket.

“Hunger is hard to bear,” she said quietly. “You have the power to take this bread. It was meant for sick women and children. You are strong. But strength does not excuse taking from the weak. You may take from me—but not from him.”

Silence.

No one moved.

The man in the night-cap shifted awkwardly, bumped his neighbor with an elbow. Others stepped back. The young man in fine clothes disappeared.

Then, the armed guards of the Signoria arrived—patrolling ahead of the coming procession. They moved in, dispersing the crowd.

Cecco, the man who had spoken last, retreated up the steps, avoiding a threatening mace. Then he turned to Romola.

“Madonna,” he said respectfully, “if you wish to go, I’ll watch the old man.”

He was a wild figure—ragged tunic, bare arms, bristling beard, a face like it had been pressed flat. A scrap of red cloth tied over his ears.

Romola hesitated.

“I’m not so handsome as you,” Cecco said, understanding her doubt, “but I’ve got an old mother who eats my porridge. There’s a heart in me—believe it. And see? The old fellow’s eating. He’ll walk soon enough.”

Romola smiled faintly. “Thank you,” she said. “I’m sorry I doubted.”

She leaned toward Baldassarre. “Wait for me,” she whispered. “I’ll return.”

He nodded—just a slight motion of head and hand.

And Romola walked on, toward the hospital of San Matteo in the Piazza di San Marco.

Romola returned from the hospital later than she had intended, her steps lingering as she took a longer route toward the river, veering away from the Ponte Vecchio and reaching it at a distance. She turned toward the bridge, determined to hurry to San Stefano in search of Baldassarre. Though she dreaded what she might learn, a deep sense of obligation held her—forsaking him would feel like abandoning a bond too close to ignore. But as she neared the crossroads where the Por’ Santa Maria lay to her right and the Ponte Vecchio to her left, she found herself swept into a crowd that suddenly dropped to their knees. Instinctively, Romola knelt with them. The Great Cross of the Duomo was passing—the head of the procession. She had arrived later than expected and now must wait until the solemn train passed by.

When she rose again, the stillness and standing posture made her fatigue more acute. Her limbs ached, and the silence pressed upon her. A shopkeeper beside her spoke gently: “Madonna Romola, you must be weary standing. Gian Fantoni will gladly offer you a seat in his home. His door is just here—let me open it for you. He loves God and the Frate as we do. His house is yours.”

Romola had grown accustomed to such warm familiarity from ordinary citizens whose faces she knew from the Duomo. For her, home no longer meant only the quiet house in the Via de’ Bardi, where loneliness often settled like dust. It was now the city itself—the towers, the winding streets, the people who greeted her not with cold recognition but with eyes full of trust or silent appeal. She welcomed the invitation and stepped through the open doorway, guided by the kind hose-vendor to an upstairs window. There, a stout woman with three children, all dressed plainly in the fashion of the Piagnoni, made room for her with reverence, drawing aside bright draperies to give her a clear view.

From this vantage, Romola could see the procession moving slowly across the Ponte Vecchio, its solemn rhythm echoing between the houses. Beyond, the Arno flowed quietly toward the bridge of Santa Trinita. The air was heavy with silence. No chant broke the hush—only the steady tramp of feet and the soft whisper of woolen robes.

At first, young men passed by—Florentine youths bearing aloft a white image of the youthful Jesus, crowned with golden light, standing beside a tall cross where thorns and nails waited. Then came the Companies of Discipline—men bound by secret vows of penance and devotion. Their garb concealed their heads and faces entirely, leaving only their eyes visible. These were Florentine citizens—merchants, artisans, scholars—now stripped of identity, walking as symbols of shared faith. Each company wore its own color and emblem, yet all were wrapped in shrouds that erased individuality, leaving only unity in purpose.

In contrast, the monks stood out as distinct figures, despite their common tonsures and habits. First came the reformed Benedictines in white, then the long line of Franciscans in grey, their cords knotted at the waist, some barefoot in wooden sandals. They were the most numerous order in Florence, known for their compassion and opposition to the Dominicans. After them followed the Augustinians of San Spirito, their dark robes topped with thoughtful, cultured faces—men who had inherited Boccaccio’s library and formed the most learned community in the city. Then came the Carmelites, their white over dark garments marking their spiritual lineage; and finally, the Servites, black-clad, founded by seven merchants who abandoned wealth to worship the Divine Mother.

Then came the moment that stirred hearts—black mantles over white scapularies, the unmistakable sign of the Dominicans. Those from Fiesole led the way, one after another, their coarse cloaks worn thin in places, their humility evident. The Prior of San Marco had enforced strict poverty among his followers, and their garments bore the mark of that discipline.

Yet amid the plainness, one figure stood apart—a mantle slightly less ragged than the rest, a tonsured head beneath it that, though unremarkable at first glance, carried a weight of presence. To those who knew him, it was Savonarola. His face had been seen on bronze medals bearing the sword of God, or flanked by armed guards on his way to the Duomo, or illuminated with fire when he preached to rapt crowds. Now, as he approached, no one dared break the silence with a cry or shout. Yet the air thrummed—there was a low hiss, half curse, half breath of awe, mingled with murmurs that yearned to be prayers. A few of his disciples fell to their knees as if something sacred passed. That gesture spread like a ripple along the street—some remained still, others bowed deeply. But hatred, too, burned fiercely. As Savonarola walked up the Por’ Santa Maria, Romola saw someone at a high window spit toward him.

Monks followed—Frati Umiliati from Ognissanti, proud of their ancient role in the wool trade; then Vallombrosan and other Benedictines, their habits subtly different, reminders of past reforms that had reshaped the spirit of the Church. Finally, the secular priests, untonsured, walked in quiet dignity.

Next came the twenty-one Arts of Florence, their banners waving proudly—bakers, tailors, judges, notaries—all asserting their place in the city’s life. Then the lesser officers of State, rising in rank, until the line broke with the Canons of the Duomo carrying a sacred relic: the head of San Zenobio, bishop of Florence, encased in silver. He was said to have saved the city centuries before, and now his presence anchored the procession.

Behind the relic came the archbishop, robed in splendor, under a canopy. And then—the hidden Image. It was veiled first by brocade curtains, then by an older, inner tabernacle never opened in living memory. Within it rested the unseen Madonna—found long ago in the soil of L’Impruneta, crying out as the spade struck her. This image had rarely left its shrine without rich gifts preceding it—veils of silk, mantles of gold, offerings from noble families and monasteries. The most famous gift, legend said, had come from a poor abbess and her nuns, who wove a golden brocade mantle with their prayers, embroidered it with devotion, and watched it carried to the Virgin by two winged youths who vanished into the sky.

But today, no gifts preceded the tabernacle. Only the poor were to receive offerings. That was the decree of Fra Girolamo, who preached not for the glory of relics but for the relief of suffering. Altars had been set up outside churches, where alms were collected for the hungry. Not even a torch lit the path. The unseen Mother, it seemed, cared less for finery than for the cries of the starving.

Florence was at her end. She had given all she could. Only divine help remained. Savonarola had promised it would come—and many clung to his words more than to the miracle of the image. Others, hardened by doubt, hoped he would be proven wrong.

Slowly, the tabernacle moved forward. Knees bent. Silence deepened. The procession drew to a close—the Priors and Gonfaloniere awaited their turn. The last of the symbolic trains faded from sight. A faint hope flickered, struggling against despair.

Romola, her heart swelling with both dread and the familiar warmth of communal faith that had become part of her soul, let out a deep sigh. She remained on her knees, drained. Then—suddenly—a flash of bright color burst from between the buildings onto the distant bridge.

She leapt up, arms outstretched, leaning from the window. Her black veil slipped back, revealing the golden gleam of her hair and the flush of sudden emotion on her face. A shout rose instantly. The final ranks of the procession paused. All eyes turned toward the bridge.

But the rider was already past it—galloping swiftly along the Arno. His bay horse, streaked with foam, seemed almost white from speed. His cap flew loose beside his red becchetto, and in his hand he waved an olive branch.

A messenger—bringing good news!

The blessed olive branch spoke from afar. But the people could not wait. They surged forward, grabbing the horse’s reins, pushing and trampling.

Romola saw then that the rider was Tito—her husband—sent to Pisa days earlier on a private mission. Recognition brought no new joy to her eyes. She had already checked her impulse to reach out. Her only thought was the news—what relief had come for Florence?

“Good news!” “Best news!” “News to be paid with hose!” (_novelle da calze_)—Tito answered the crowd’s clamor, barely able to speak until he reached the meeting point where the Gonfaloniere and Priors waited.

He paused, bowed low, and said: “Magnificent Signori! I bring the joyful tidings that the French galleys, laden with corn and men, have arrived safely at Leghorn, thanks to a strong wind that kept the enemy fleet at bay.”

His words echoed down the streets. A great roar erupted, rolling along the river, then returning in waves from the procession toward the Duomo. Shouts multiplied, spreading like ripples across a lake stirred by one force. For minutes, no one spoke. The Signoria lifted their caps, standing bare-headed before a rescue that came not from their own power, but from beyond—from the realm of faith and divine grace.

At last, as the signal to move forward was given, Tito smiled and added: “I should say, any reward of hose offered by the Magnificent Signoria should go not to me, but to another man—Meo di Sasso—who rode hard to bring these tidings. His horse broke down just before Signa. He’ll arrive within the hour, and rightly deserves the honor, having borne the greatest labor and missed the greatest joy.”

It was a graceful acknowledgment. After a brief reply from the _Proposto_, the procession continued. Tito turned his horse and followed. The great bell of the Palazzo Vecchio began to toll, amplifying the people’s joy.

Romola, instead of seeking recognition, pulled her veil back over her head and stayed still. Yet she felt certain Tito had seen her. He had that look—seeing everything without seeming to see anything at all.

The crowd had barely moved on when Romola stepped down from the church steps and hurried toward San Stefano. Cecco had followed the throng into the Piazza, leaving Baldassarre alone by the doorway, clutching his horn cup as he waited for her. There was a marked change in him—his earlier vacant stare, like that of a man half-awake from a dream, had vanished, replaced by an intensity so sharp it seemed to burn. As Romola approached and spoke, his eyes locked onto hers with sudden fury, a wildness that suggested a beast seeing its prey just beyond reach. She flinched at the look, but instantly thought: *He has seen Tito.* And in that moment, a flicker of hope stirred within her—perhaps this man was the one who had wronged her husband, not Tito. If she could confront them both now, perhaps she could find truth, and peace.

“If you will come with me,” she said gently, “I can give you shelter and food until you are strong again. Will you come?”

“Yes,” he replied, voice low and strained. “I shall be glad to get my strength.” He repeated the words as if rehearsing them, more to himself than to her.

She gestured for him to walk beside her, choosing the quieter path along the Arno toward Ponte Rubaconte. As they crossed the bridge, she ventured, “I think you are not from Florence.”

He turned sharply, silent, wary. The fog of confusion still clung to him, thickened by weakness, and suspicion guarded every word. But Romola’s gaze held a softness that finally broke through. After a pause, he answered cautiously: “No. I am no Florentine. I am a lonely man.”

She noticed his reluctance and dared not press further—fear that he might turn away. Yet as they walked, her thoughts raced. If this man were innocent, why the secrecy? Why the dread? Her fragile hope began to fade. Silence stretched between them until they reached the entrance to Via de’ Bardi. Then, suddenly, he stopped and looked at her as though struck by a memory.

“Ahh!” he said, before she could speak. “You are his wife.”

“Whose wife?” she asked.

He could not name him. The image of Tito filled his mind so completely that words failed. He only stared at her, eyes fixed, unblinking.

Romola opened the gate to her courtyard. Inside, straw covered the ground, where several sick lay resting. Children crawled among them, tiny and pale, nibbling straws, babbling softly. She stepped forward. “Come in,” she said, voice trembling. “I’ll find you a place to rest and bring you food.”

“No,” he said, stepping back. “I will not come in.”

But he did not move. He stood rooted, overwhelmed by sensations too chaotic to process. She reached into her purse and offered him several _grossi_. “Let me give you money. You can buy food. It will be easier soon.”

He studied the coins, then took them. “Yes,” he said quietly. “I will take them.”

She poured the coins into his hand. He gripped them tightly, as if they were already a weapon.

“Tell me,” she pleaded, “what shall you do?”

But he turned and walked away, heading back toward the bridge. He passed it without hesitation, continuing straight up Via del Fosso until he came upon Niccolò Caparra’s shop. The master was gone—marching with the armourers—but an apprentice remained. Weapons hung everywhere. Baldassarre’s eyes found what he sought: a small, sharp hunting knife. The boy sold it for three _grossi_, no questions asked.

Baldassarre slipped the blade into his tunic. It felt alive against his chest—a companion, a power. Even if it failed, it was better than nothing. It might shatter on armor, but would armor always be there? In prison, during those long months of waiting, pride may have grown careless. The knife had been bought with the traitor’s own money—that was justice.

Before taking the coin, he had known what he would do: buy a weapon. And food—yes, food too. To strengthen the body that carried vengeance. When he had eaten enough, he would think. First, how to hide—lest Tito have him seized again.

With that thought, he turned into narrow alleys, bought bread and meat, and sat beneath a loggia to eat. The bells rang louder, swelling with joy, shaking the air. To him, they were only part of a world that mocked him.

Romola watched until he disappeared around the corner into Piazza de’ Mozzi. Part of her felt relief—another part reproached herself for not pressing harder, for not knowing whether this man’s suffering was innocent, or whether she had failed him. What could she have done if the truth revealed something terrible about Tito? Could she bear it? Was she not allowed to wish ignorance, when silence was her only defense?

Yet the question stirred too many emotions. She pushed it aside. Now was a time for joy—the city was saved. She entered the courtyard, closed the door to block out the bells, and removed her black veil. Standing in the center, she told the sick and children that grain was coming, that Florence rejoiced.

They sat up, children scrambling to her side, pulling at her clothes, their small hands reaching for her face. She sat down, exhausted, letting them tug at her hair, peer into her basket. Around her, weak voices murmured: “Praised be the Holy Virgin!” “It was the procession!” “The Mother of God has taken pity on us!”

At last, she rose, too weary to smile. “I will return soon with your dinner,” she said, climbing the stone steps.

“Bless you, madonna! Bless you!” they called, in the same tone they’d used for the unseen Madonna moments before.

Romola cherished that sound. She did not naturally delight in caring for the sick or mending rags. Her upbringing had kept her apart from such duties. Without deeper purpose, these tasks would have felt burdensome. But now they were her anchor, the only place where clarity remained.

When doubt crept in—when the distance between her and Tito grew wider, when conversations with Fra Salvestro or followers of Savonarola left her feeling suffocated by narrowness, when old contempt for superstition surged—she returned here. Among the poor, her faith renewed itself. Helping others became proof that Savonarola had been right to call her back. Her place had never been empty. It had been filled with love and labor. Florence needed her. And the more her sorrow deepened, the greater her joy in remembering the countless hours she had eased another’s pain.

Her passion, once spent on family affection, had transformed into a fierce compassion for all humanity. She no longer believed her own life could be happy—nor did she seek happiness. Her purpose was simpler: to lessen sorrow.

Savonarola inspired this fire. Though she recoiled from his repetitive visions and allegories when others spoke them, his voice stirred her soul. His anger at corruption, his zeal for justice and reform, ignited her own spirit. His vision of Florence’s liberty tied to a broader renewal gave her a sense of belonging in history. Through daily contact with the suffering, this vision hardened into action—not sentiment, but resolve.

She rarely questioned doctrine. She avoided pondering his prophecies of divine judgment and rebirth. Instead, she trusted him. Not because of intellect, but because of emotion—because his nature moved her deeply. She listened patiently to his teachings, not for logic, but for the fire behind them. No soul is truly desolate as long as it holds trust in another. For Romola, Savonarola was that figure—a rope suspended across her path, steady and strong. Without it, even solid ground might not save her from falling.

And so, she continued—walking through sorrow, guided by compassion, sustained by faith.

CHAPTER XLV. At the Barber’s Shop.

After his unexpected triumph as the bearer of the olive branch—a gift from fortune not sought but gratefully received—Tito had other duties to attend to, carefully planned and deliberately executed. He paused at the Palazzo Vecchio, waiting for the return of the Ten, the council entrusted with foreign affairs and matters of war, so that he might deliver the results of his private mission to Pisa. This mission was meant as a preliminary step toward an official embassy, one in which Bernardo Rucellai would lead the delegation, aiming to establish peace between Florence and Emperor Maximilian and the League. Tito’s talents in diplomacy were well known, and when he presented his findings with clarity and precision, even Bernardo del Nero, then one of the Ten, could not suppress his admiration—though he wished he could.

Bernardo’s initial dislike of Tito had resurfaced, intensified by the sale of the library. Romola had said nothing to him about it, and he took her silence as a sign she did not wish to speak of it. Yet he was certain the act had deeply wounded her, violating her father’s last wishes. The old man watched her closely and saw signs that her marriage was not fulfilling. “Ah,” he mused inwardly, “this must be why she has taken up with Fra Girolamo and the Piagnoni—something I never imagined of her.” Women without children, he thought, often turned either to folly or to excessive piety, believing themselves burdened with the salvation of souls. As for Romola, he reflected, she had been over-educated—filled with Latin and Greek until she seemed no more truly feminine than if she’d spent her days only threading needles. And her husband—so smooth, so useful, so universally employed because he was so pliable—he hoped Tornabuoni and the others would not regret their trust. “Well, well,” Bernardo muttered, “a crooked furrow may still yield a full sack; and whoever insists on having only honest men in service will find himself paying little.”

This long-held belief—that political agents could not be judged solely by moral standards—kept Bernardo from interfering in Tito’s disfavor. Aside from what must remain sacred for Romola’s sake, Bernardo had no real accusation against the Greek scholar beyond the fact that he was Greek—and that Bernardo simply did not like him. The duplicity of pretending loyalty to the popular government while secretly favoring the Medici was common among half the Medicean faction. But Tito practiced it with greater skill than most. That was all. So Bernardo remained cold toward him, and Tito returned the coldness with courteous distance.

Still, in Florence, the old bond between Bernardo and Bardo endured. Any service done to Romola’s husband was seen as an honor paid to her godfather.

Having delivered his report at the Old Palace, Tito felt the formal duties of the day completed. He was weary, dust-covered from long riding, yet he did not go home. There were matters in his satchel and in his mind that needed settling—quickly, discreetly, without drawing attention. He walked slowly from the Palazzo toward the Piazza del Duomo, moving with a casual air, though his eyes scanned the surroundings with quiet purpose.

The procession had ended, but the bells still rang, and the streets buzzed with restless energy. People craved a deeper expression of joy, something more tangible than prayers or processions. If the Frate had stood in the square and preached, they might have found contentment. But now, despite the new rules declaring Christ the special king of Florence and demanding all pleasures be Christian in nature, many young men who shouted “Viva Gesù!” secretly longed for a bit of stone-throwing—just to release their exuberance.

As Tito passed, some recognized him as the olive-branch messenger and pressed around him with eager questions. To escape the unwanted attention, he told them Meo di Sasso, the true envoy from Leghorn, was likely arriving soon and might be met near Porta San Frediano. That explanation, cleverly plausible, freed him from further pursuit.

He reached the Piazza del Duomo, glancing about with feigned indifference, though he was actually searching for someone specific. The end of the procession had drawn crowds to the square, especially near Nello’s barber shop. His instinct proved correct: a group leaned against the railings near the north gate of the Baptistery—exactly the kind of gathering he sought.

He approached, appearing to notice the tallest figure in the group only by chance, turning slightly to greet him with a nod as he tossed his hat back over his shoulder. Yet this man—broad-shouldered, dressed in a richly embroidered tunic, linen displayed in the latest French fashion, sword and dagger at his belt, a red plume in his hat—was no ordinary citizen. His bearing was bold, almost defiant, a mockery of Florentine sobriety under the influence of the Piagnoni. Youthful features gave way to coarseness in his wide face and thick waist, and his eyes held a crude, intrusive gaze that unsettled refined minds like a harsh light or foul smell.

Beside him stood others—equally young, expensively dressed, armed—laughing loudly, as if to prove their amusement was genuine, even if some doubted it. Their laughter echoed anew as Tito joined them.

Leaning against the doorpost was Niccolò Macchiavelli, a sharp-eyed young man already adept at reading the hidden motives behind public words. “Messer Dolfo’s head,” he remarked, “is more pumpkin than I thought. I judge a man’s dullness by the tricks he uses to fool others. The dullest are those who grin after being kicked in the shins. If I were just a little slower,” he added, smiling as the circle opened for Tito, “I’d pretend to love Melema—now that he’s got a secretaryship perfectly suited to me—as if Latin poorly paid could ever love Latin better paid! Melema, you’re a pestilently clever fellow, very much in my way. I’m sorry to hear you’ve had another stroke of luck today.”

“Questionable luck, Niccolò,” Tito replied, tapping him lightly on the shoulder. “All I’ve gained is being grabbed and breathed upon by wool merchants, as filthy and battered as a letter-carrier from Bologna.”

“Ah! You need my art, Messer Oratore,” said Nello, stepping forward at the sound of Tito’s voice. “Your chin bears yesterday’s stubble. Come, come—submit yourself to the priest of all the Muses. Sandro, lather quickly!”

“In truth, Nello,” Tito said, seating himself, “that is precisely what I desire. It was why I came here instead of home after my business at the Palazzo.”

“Yes indeed,” Nello agreed. “It wouldn’t do for you to appear before Madonna Romola with a rough beard and tangled hair. Nothing coarse should approach the Florentine lily—though I see her walking daily like a sunbeam through our rags—if indeed she’s not more like a moonbeam now. Yesterday, when I met her, she looked pale and worn, like that fainting Madonna in Fra Giovanni’s painting. You must see to it, my learned friend: she fasts too often, keeps vigils too long in your absence.”

Tito sighed. “It is true, Nello. She denies herself half her food every day during this famine. What can I do? Her heart burns with devotion. A husband’s influence is powerless against the Frate’s.”

“As every other influence likely is,” said Domenico Cennini, who had entered with Tito. “Have you heard anything at Pisa about how things stand in Rome, Melema?”

“Secrets of the council chamber, Messer Domenico,” Tito replied, palms raised in mock innocence. “An envoy must be as silent as a confessor.”

“Of course,” Cennini said. “No breach of that rule. Still, I believe that if the Holy Father pushes the Frate too far, the Frate would stir heaven and earth to call a General Council—and succeed too. And I, for one, would not mind, though I’m no Piagnone.”

“With respect to your experience, Messer Domenico,” Macchiavelli interjected, “I differ—not in wishing for reform, but in thinking the Frate can outmaneuver the Pope. His game is impossible. Had he limited himself to preaching against Rome’s sins and predicting Italy’s scourge without naming how, Pope Alexander would have let him preach as long as he had listeners. Such spiritual storms don’t topple walls. But the Frate wants more than a trumpet—he wants leverage. And he *has* leverage. He seeks to spread Christ’s word by supporting the popular government in Florence, while the Pope, as I know from reliable sources, desires the opposite.”

“Then Florence will stand by the Frate,” Cennini insisted. “I myself wish he’d stop prophesying—but if our right to choose our own government is attacked, I am a son of the Church, yes—but I’d vote to resist Pope Alexander VI, as our ancestors resisted Gregory XI.”

“But forgive me, Messer Domenico,” Macchiavelli said, hands tucked into his belt, speaking with calm authority, “have you grasped the Frate’s true position? How did he become such a lever? Because he holds the people’s ear. He offers them threats and promises they believe come straight from God—not just about hell and paradise, but about Pisa and the Great Council. But if events turn against him, shaking the people’s faith, his power becomes his downfall. He draws three kinds of hatred: the common man’s resentment of strict virtue; the jealousy of stronger Italian powers who want to control Florence; and the people’s anger, whom he promised earthly blessings, not just heavenly ones. A prophet must have an army behind him, like Mahomet, to revive faith when it falters.”

“Better to sum it all into one,” Francesco Cei broke in hotly. “He has earned the hatred of every sensible and honest man by spreading hypocritical lies. His place is with the false prophets in Hell, walking backward.”

“You’re too angry, Francesco,” Macchiavelli said gently. “Poets cut clouds in wrath. I’m no follower of the Frate, nor would I risk a finger for his truth. But truth is a flower of paradise—its seeds rarely bloom outside its walls. You, too, tell poetic lies—some from passion, some to please. But you object to lies in prose. The Frate differs only in where he draws the line between poetry and fact. In the pulpit, he has fervor—and an audience to satisfy. Ecco!”

“You’re somewhat lenient there, Niccolò,” Cennini said gravely. “I believe in the Frate’s integrity, though not his prophecies. As long as that isn’t disproved, we have a strong popular party to protect him and resist foreign meddling.”

“A party that seems strong,” Macchiavelli replied, shrugging, his glance barely flickering toward Tito, who was now enjoying Nello’s combing and scented oils. “But how many Mediceans are among you? How many won’t shift allegiance for personal grudges?”

“As to the Mediceans,” Cennini said, “there’s little genuine loyalty left to the Medici. Who would risk much for Piero de’ Medici? A few old friends, perhaps—like Bernardo del Nero. But even those tied to the family support the popular government and would defend the Frate. I spoke to Giannozzo Pucci earlier. I’m convinced he’d oppose any attempt to overturn the new order.”

“You’re right, Messer Domenico,” Tito said, rising from the chair with a smile in his eyes. “And I suspect tender feelings helped hard theory there. I think jealousy sparked Pucci’s break with Piero. Else, a man as gentle as he is wouldn’t show such bitterness. Was he in the procession?”

“No,” Cennini replied. “He’s at his villa—left three days ago.”

Tito adjusted his cap, pretending not to care, but in truth, he had just secured vital information. In his satchel lay a crushed gold ring, entrusted to him by an envoy of Piero de’ Medici, whom he’d met by chance on the Siena road. Since Pucci was absent, Tito would send the ring via Fra Michele, a Carthusian brother serving the Medici. Its receipt would bring Pucci back to hear the rest of the message.

“Behold him!” Nello declared, waving his comb. “The most handsome scholar in the world—or the marshlands! A trifle thinner than when he first came to Florence—eh? And I swear, there are faint lines forming around your mouth, Messer Oratore! Ah, the mind is beauty’s enemy! Once, I was considered handsome by women—when I wore swaddling clothes. Now—oimè! My unwritten poems are ciphered across my face!”

Laughing with the others, Tito waved farewell and left.

“I’m with Piero di Cosimo,” Francesco Cei said. “I don’t fully trust Melema. That smile grows stronger—no wonder he has lines at the mouth.”

“He’s too successful,” Macchiavelli teased. “There must be something wrong with him—else why would he have that secretaryship?”

“He’s capable,” Cennini said fairly. “My brother and I have always found him useful with our Greek manuscripts. He satisfies the Ten. I like to see a man rise by merit. And Secretary Scala, who helped him from the start, still speaks highly of him.”

“Certainly,” a notary murmured from the background. “He writes Scala’s letters—or corrects them—and earns well for it.”

“I wish Bartolommeo would pay me to cure his gouty Latin,” Macchiavelli said, shrugging. “Did *he* tell you about the pay, Ser Ceccone, or was it Melema?”

“Melema? No,” the notary answered. “He’s as tight as a nut. Never brags. That’s why he’s everywhere. They say he’s growing rich doing underhand work.”

“It *is* a bit unfair,” Macchiavelli said, “with so many skilled notaries idle.”

“Well,” Cei said, “I remember a nasty story from a year or two ago—the man who claimed Melema stole jewels. It was hushed up, but Piero di Cosimo said he believed it, for he saw Melema’s face when the man grabbed him—‘painted with fear,’ as Dante says.”

“Enough venom, Francesco,” Nello snapped. “Else I’ll consider it my duty to cut your hair crooked next time I hold your scissors. That jewel story was a lie. Bernardo Rucellai and the Magnificent Eight knew the truth. The man was a madman—properly locked away. And Piero di Cosimo’s mind runs wild—he’d mistake a lizard for a crocodile. That tale is buried deep—our noses revolt at it.”

“It is true,” Macchiavelli said. “You forget the danger of precedent. Next, a beggar might accuse you of stealing verses—or me, God help me, of stealing his coppers. Ah!” he added, turning to the door, “Dolfo Spini has carried his red feather out of the square. That swaggering captain would rejoice if Florence lost Pisa—just to see the people tear the Frate’s robes off. With your pardon, Francesco—I know he’s your friend—but few things would please me more than to see him play Capo d’Oca, who blew his trumpets to the tournament and returned with them in a bag.”

That evening, as darkness settled and the sky threatened rain, Romola returned from the hospital of San Matteo, her lantern glowing softly beside her, accompanied by Maso. She had gone there after vespers, drawn by her quiet devotion to the sick and suffering. Just as they neared their home, she encountered Tito emerging from the monastery of San Marco. He had left the house earlier that day and had spent little time with her, his presence marked only by fleeting moments and formal gestures. Now, he offered to walk her home, dismissing Maso without hesitation—his short steps were an annoyance Tito could not bear.

It was customary for him to accompany her only when duty demanded it. Their marriage had long since become a ritual of mutual respect and silence, not intimacy. They spoke of matters great and small, but always with careful detachment, each adhering to a shared pretense of harmony. If Tito wished for supper in the old library—now transformed into a pleasant banqueting hall—Romola would see to it without question. In return, he allowed her full freedom in her daily life, accepting her help in copying manuscripts or organizing notes, and even providing supplies for her charitable works. Yet between them lay a gulf so deep that even glances were avoided. Tito often pretended she was not at home, refusing to seek her in her room, and joked that she preferred solitude to his company.

In the early days after her decision to stay, Romola had tried—tentatively, painfully—to rebuild trust. But such efforts required honesty, clarity, a willingness to confront their differences openly. Tito, however, saw no value in such truths. To him, affection was the only remedy, and affection, in his view, meant forgetting the past. But Romola could not forget. Her tenderness had died with her illusions. She could be gentle, obedient, even kind—but love was beyond her reach. And so, though she remained his wife, she was also a stranger within his walls.

This night, as they walked together, Tito said, “The day is not yet done for me. I’ll escort you home, my Romola, then I must attend to another matter—one that will take an hour, perhaps more. I need rest, and I shall return soon.”

He began to speak lightly of his visit to Pisa, of the sights and people he’d seen, until they approached a loggia near a street lamp that cast its dim light over a painted image of the Virgin. The street was quiet, but now came the sound of approaching footsteps and voices growing louder.

“We’ll be caught in the rain unless we take shelter,” Tito said quickly, guiding Romola up the step of the loggia with a slight urgency.

“Surely this drizzle isn’t worth waiting for,” Romola replied, surprised.

“No,” he answered, “it’s growing heavier. Let us wait a moment.”

But beneath his calm words was a keen alertness. The flicker of the lamp revealed something—a red feather on a hat, a gleaming sword hilt. A man wearing those signs was the last person Tito wanted to meet tonight, especially with Romola beside him.

Earlier that day, he had met Dolfo Spini, captain of the Compagnacci, and the task he now spoke of was a second meeting with him—part of a plan set in motion after his visit to San Marco. Tito had been entrusted with forged letters addressed to Savonarola, one bearing the seal and signature of the Cardinal of Naples, who had long supported the Friar. These letters claimed the Cardinal was traveling from Pisa and, due to pressing reasons, would not enter Florence. Instead, he intended to pause at San Casciano, ten miles out, and ride to meet Savonarola the next morning on the road to Florence, dressed as a simple priest.

The real purpose? A trap. Spini and his men were to lie in ambush five miles from the city gates. When Savonarola emerged, accompanied only by a Dominican brother, they would seize him and hand him over to Milanese horsemen waiting near San Casciano, who would carry him into Roman territory. It was a dangerous gamble—Savonarola was shrewd, cautious, and wary of leaving the city. His friends insisted he travel with armed guards; here, he would be alone. But Tito calculated that the Frate’s passion for reform and his hope of securing papal support might override caution. Time was limited, and the letter was designed to act swiftly.

Tito had personally delivered the letters to Savonarola, declining to let anyone else handle them. With skillful subtlety, he hinted at their contents, suggesting they involved a rendezvous outside the city, and urged the Friar to request an armed escort from the Signoria. He offered to deliver the plea privately. Savonarola had refused: “An armed guard defeats privacy.” His eyes flashed with resolve. Tito knew then—the Frate would go.

Yet Tito cared little about the outcome. He played both sides, always ensuring his own advantage. If Savonarola fell, he stood to gain diamonds and papal favor. If not, he had strengthened his position with both Savonarola and Spini, and any secrets he uncovered made him more valuable to the Medici. But Spini was reckless. He enjoyed intrigue but lacked discretion. He drank too much, laughed too loudly, and sometimes forgot himself. Though they had agreed never to show open familiarity, Spini might still betray them with a sudden embrace or careless word.

Now, Tito hoped Spini hadn’t seen him. But fate was cruel. Hidden in shadow, Spini had glimpsed Tito under the lamplight—clearly visible, unmistakable. Romola, wrapped in black, remained unnoticed, standing behind her husband’s shoulder in the corner of the loggia.

Spini’s voice cut through the air like a blade:
“Ha! My carrier-pigeon!” he rasped, gripping Tito’s shoulder. “What’s this? Hiding from your comrades? Well, I found you. Saves time. How’s the hunt tomorrow? Will the bald-headed bird rise? Are the falcons ready?”

If rage had been in Tito’s nature, he would have felt it. But instead, he faced a crisis of choice. To silence Spini might raise Romola’s suspicion—she was sharp, and once alarmed, impossible to deceive. To push him away might provoke violence. So he chose a third path—one born of fear: the fear of her.

He gently pressed her hand, as if signaling, and smiled easily.
“Yes, my Dolfo,” he said, “you may prepare. But leave the trumpets behind.”

Spini bristled. “Don’t play Ser Saccente with me. I know where the devil keeps his tail. Did he swallow the bait whole? No scent of hook? The prophet’s nose didn’t work?”

Tito thought, *The brute won’t stop till he empties the bag.* But aloud, he said, “Swallowed it all—like you drink Trebbiano. Ha! Look—torches! A dead body must be coming. I hear the pestilence spreads.”

“Santiddio!” Spini muttered, turning abruptly. “I hate those biers. Good-night.”

The torches were real, but they led not to a corpse, but to a church official returning home. Tito’s lie had worked—Spini had left.

Only then did Tito turn to Romola.
“Do not worry, my Romola,” he said calmly. “That beast said nothing of consequence. We can go now. The rain hasn’t worsened.”

But Romola trembled—not with fear, but with fury. His tone was too easy, too cold. She trusted nothing he said.
“I will not move,” she declared. “Not until I know this treachery will not happen.”

“Wait,” he said, control steady, though irritation stirred. “Let these torches pass.”

They passed—Vicario dell’Arcivescovo—and Tito bowed respectfully. But Romola saw nothing. In that moment, she would have leapt upon him, dragged him down a cliff, if it meant stopping the plot. Union with this man? Impossible. The discipline she had mastered over two years crumbled. She felt only separation.

They stood in near darkness, faces barely visible.

“Tell me the truth, Tito,” she whispered, voice trembling. “This time—tell me the truth. It will be safer for you.”

“Why should I lie?” he replied, a hint of contempt in his voice. “The truth is what you should rejoice in—that I know Spini’s plot, and I’ve ensured the Frate won’t fall victim to it.”

“What is the plot?”

“I decline to say,” Tito replied. “But know this—the Frate will not leave the city.”

She was silent for a moment. Then, in anguish:
“Tito, it’s no use. I don’t believe you.”

She saw him shrug, palms raised—silent, cold.

“If the Frate leaves—or if harm comes to him,” she said, voice rising, “I will tell the Signoria. You will be disgraced. What if I am your wife? I will be disgraced with you. If we are united, I am the part of you that will save you from crime. Others shall not be betrayed.”

“I know what you’d do, _anima mia_,” Tito said, coolly. “So think: if you believe me in nothing else, believe this—I will protect myself. I won’t give you the power to ruin me.”

“Then you promise me—he will not go beyond the gates?”

“He shall not.”

A pause. But doubt remained.

“I will go back to San Marco,” Romola said, stepping forward.

“You shall not!” Tito snapped, seizing her wrists with force. “I am master here. You will not oppose me.”

Passers-by approached. That was why he whispered.

Romola did not struggle. She felt mastered—physically, mentally. But she heard the footsteps, and her pride compelled her to yield, to follow him from the loggia.

They walked in silence, the drizzle soaking their cloaks.

At first, her anger had burned bright—opposition seemed noble, even necessary. But now, memories surfaced: the restraint she had learned, the dignity she had preserved. The grip on her wrists had not enflamed her—it had chilled her. This was not a fight of passion, but of power. And she remembered her flight, her return, the self-control she had regained. That memory held her back.

Now, her mind turned to probabilities. Tito would not risk exposure. He knew she knew. And perhaps—yes, she clung to this possibility—he had never meant to betray Savonarola. Perhaps he had only used the plot to secure his own position.

Tito, meanwhile, was calculating. He waited until they neared home before speaking again.

“Well, Romola,” he said, “have you calmed yourself? If so, consider this: if I truly meant to aid Spini, would I now carry a wife who knows the secret? Such a woman would be a liability. You must see that.”

He assumed the tone of reason—cool, persuasive, confident.

“Yes, Tito,” she said quietly. “You believe I would protect the Republic. You are right. If the Frate is harmed, I will denounce you.”

She paused. Then, with effort:
“But it was not so. I may have spoken too hastily. You never meant to betray him. Only—why must you seem so close to that man?”

“Such alliances are inevitable,” Tito replied, pleased to see her struggle. “Practical men must walk with wolves. You fair creatures live in clouds. Go to bed. Rest well.”

He opened the door for her.

Tito’s carefully laid plans had unraveled not through any flaw in his intellect, but by the capricious hand of chance—those small, unforeseen turns of fate that no strategy can contain. It was rare for him to walk with Romola in the evenings, yet tonight, when her presence could not have been more ill-timed, he found himself beside her. Life, he reflected, was a game so intricate that even the most calculated moves could be undone by the lightest breath of wind, like thistle-down carried on a sudden gust.

He did not grieve over Spini’s failed scheme; what troubled him was the delicate balance he now faced. How could he warn Savonarola without arousing suspicion? And how could he inform Spini without revealing his own shift in allegiance? If he rushed to the monastery before the monks retired, it would appear as though he had betrayed Spini’s plot at the very moment he delivered the forged letters—a connection too obvious to ignore. Yet if he delayed until morning, he risked losing the chance to alert Spini that Savonarola had changed his mind. The Compagnacci, enraged by their disappointment, might turn against him. Still, Tito chose the delay, trusting that he could calm Spini with the explanation that the Frate had simply grown cautious.

But the decision left him restless. He had no desire to see Romola again and spent the night wandering the city streets, avoiding home. Meanwhile, Romola remained awake, dressed in her night clothes, listening to the rain grow heavier. She welcomed the storm—the thunderous sky seemed to silence human schemes, forcing men to stay within. But her mind was far from still. Doubt gnawed at her: Was Tito lying to her? What hidden purpose might he harbor? Every person who trusted him was at risk. She tried to convince herself otherwise, but the truth was unbearable.

And then came the deeper question: Was she being selfish, driven by pride, in refusing to expose her husband? If he were guilty, should she not stand beside him in prison? That she could accept. But was it not her duty, as a wife, to prevent harm before it was done? Could she allow him to commit acts that would make him a criminal, when she might stop him? Prayer felt impossible—her thoughts were too fierce, too active, for quiet submission. The tension grew, feeding her imagination with visions of ways Tito might have evaded her warnings.

At last, the rain eased, the clouds broke, and dawn spilled across the city in clear, cold light. The clarity only deepened her unease. She wrapped her mantle tightly and ran to the loggia, scanning the landscape as if some sign might emerge from the hills or rooftops. But all she saw were buildings—no clue, no certainty. Perhaps she could go to her godfather, urge him to act without revealing everything, to block Savonarola’s passage through the gates. But time was slipping away. She realized, with fresh anguish, that she had learned nothing concrete from Tito. It was past seven. There was no choice: she must go to San Marco.

She descended the stairs swiftly, ignoring her sick patients, and stepped into the street. She moved quickly along the Via de’ Bardi toward the Ponte Vecchio, choosing the heart of Florence—the shortest route, and one where she might still meet Tito. Perhaps he would confirm the Frate’s safety, ending her torment.

At the Palazzo Vecchio, she searched the colonnaded courtyard, then swept her gaze across the Piazza. But there was no sign of him—not the figure once cherished in her youth, now etched into her soul with pain. She pressed on to the Piazza del Duomo, where the morning bustle had already begun. Worshippers climbed the marble steps, merchants carried goods, and voices rose in conversation. Then, among the crowd, she saw him.

Tito stood outside Nello’s shop, leaning casually against the doorpost, hat in hand, hair freshly combed, talking with ease. His relaxed demeanor struck her like a blow. This man, who had stirred such dread in her, now appeared untroubled, almost carefree. A wave of fury surged through her. She recognized Cronaca and two others from San Marco nearby. In that instant, an idea flashed: *I will force him to speak before them.*

She approached silently, her step light but swift. As she neared, Cronaca turned and said, “Here comes Madonna Romola.”

A jolt passed through Tito’s body. He met her eyes—hollowed by sleepless watching, yet blazing with something fiercer than fear.

“Is the Frate gone beyond the gates?” she asked, voice low but sharp.

“No,” he replied, struggling to keep his composure. He felt utterly powerless before her.

“And you are certain he is not going?” she pressed.

“I am certain he is not going.”

“That is enough,” she said, turning abruptly and ascending the steps into the Duomo.

Tito watched her retreat, a feeling rising in him colder than hatred—something closer to revulsion.

In the square, others stood near: true followers of Savonarola, yes—but also Ser Ceccone, the notary, a secret agent of the Medici, like Tito himself. Ser Ceccone was no man of grace. Unlearned, unattractive, unkind, he lacked charm even in betrayal. And so, he held no affection for Tito Melema.

It was late afternoon when Tito returned home. Romola sat across from the cabinet in their narrow room, her pen moving steadily over parchment as she copied documents. The light was fading, and she was about to pause when he entered—silent, unannounced, with a purpose that seemed carved into his posture. He did not remove his cap or mantle, but leaned one arm on the cabinet, standing directly before her, his gaze fixed.

Romola had spent the day certain of the Frate’s safety, and now felt a quiet remorse for the anger and distrust that had driven her to confront her husband publicly—a matter he had always wished kept private. She replayed the moment in her mind: her outburst, her accusations, the way she had spoken before others. Had she been wrong? Could the political machinations she’d heard even her godfather speak of—those cunning shifts of loyalty, those alliances born of necessity rather than truth—explain Spini’s connection without implicating Tito in any deeper plot?

She wanted to confess her haste, to ask forgiveness. For hours, she had hoped this moment might open a door—perhaps not only to apology, but to words long buried, truths never spoken. Two years of silence had passed between them, so complete that Tito did not know she had once fled from him, nor that she had returned. Their past—the tenderness of youth, the sudden fracture—remained sealed away, like a feast interrupted by death, its table still set but no longer touched.

Now, she looked up at him with softness in her eyes, the kind born of regret. But something in his face stopped her. A subtle shift—tighter lips, colder eyes—made her breath catch. They stood in silence, staring at each other, the air thickening.

Tito knew this was a turning point. Beneath his usual calm, beneath his gentle manner, lay a will that had surfaced permanently now. It showed in the rigid line of his jaw, in the way his body seemed to hold itself back—not from violence, but from yielding. He was not angry. He was decisive.

“Romola,” he said, voice cool and clear, like water over stone, “it is time we understood each other.”

“Yes,” she whispered, her voice barely audible. “That is what I most desire.”

Her face, pale and composed, bore no trace of the fury she had worn earlier. Only sorrow and doubt remained. In contrast, Tito’s presence loomed larger—strong, unyielding. He continued.

“You took a step this morning,” he said, “one that was useless. It drew attention. And it may bring trouble upon me.”

“I acknowledge it,” she said, speaking more firmly now. “I was too hasty. I am sorry if I wronged you.” She hoped this would soften him. Then, perhaps, she could say more.

But Tito did not relent. “I wish you to understand once and for all,” he said, tone unchanged, “that such actions are incompatible with our marriage. Think carefully about how you came to act. Reflect so this does not happen again.”

“That depends chiefly on you, Tito,” she replied, surprised by the heat in her own voice.

He smiled faintly. “You would say, then, that nothing should arise to stir your suspicions? You were frank enough last night to admit you do not believe in me. I am not shocked by your conclusions—only by the consequences they may bring. Do you hear me?”

“Yes,” she answered, flushing with irritation at his cold superiority.

“Then listen. It may not be long before some word or incident sets your imagination running again—imagining crimes, plotting disasters. You might rush to the Palazzo Vecchio, alarm the Signoria, throw the city into chaos. Let me tell you what might follow. Not just my disgrace—something you seem ready to face—but the arrest, the ruin, of many men in Florence. Including Messer Bernardo del Nero.”

Tito had made his move. And it struck deep.

The color drained from Romola’s face. Her lips turned white—an unusual pallor for one so resilient. Tito saw it. He had won.

“You may think yourself noble,” he went on, “a heroine saving the city. But you might as well try to unlock doors with prayers as expect your emotions to shape Florentine politics. The question is not whether you can trust me—but whether, having been warned, you will still charge forward blindly into affairs you do not understand.”

Romola felt her mind trapped. The possibilities he painted rose before her—clear, terrifying. She could see the scaffold, the arrests, the collapse of families, all because of her impulse.

“I am rash,” she murmured. “I will try not to be.”

“Remember,” Tito said, voice sharp now, “your act this morning might have caused worse harm than the sacrifice you’ve learned to face without flinching.”

“Tito,” she burst out, rising and stepping closer, her hand reaching for his arm, “it is not true that I would willingly destroy you. I have fought harder than I ever thought possible to stay with you. I left two years ago in anger. I came back because I loved you more than anything. But it is useless. You shut me out. You treat me as someone too irrational to share in your world. You reveal nothing. Nothing.”

She looked like an angel pleading—eyes wide, voice trembling. But Tito did not feel tenderness. He felt only distance. The man who had once found joy in her presence now found it hard to endure her nearness. His nature was gentle, yes—but his intellect was sharp, his purpose firm. And Romola resisted him. No man, unless weak beyond measure, endures being thwarted by his wife. Marriage must be either harmony or dominion.

No emotion flickered across his face when she spoke of fleeing. Only a slight tightening of the mouth, a faint smile.

“My Romola,” he said, “when conditions are known, we must accept them. Wishes cannot fill the Arno, nor turn plums into oranges. I have seen no prayer change fate. You carry strong impressions—ones reason cannot touch. I cannot share them. You withdrew trust. So I have withdrawn myself. There is no need to look back. We must adapt.”

“But Tito,” she pleaded, “we could speak! We could be honest!”

“It was deception,” she insisted, “that changed me. And it has kept us apart. And it was not I who changed first. You changed the night you wore that chain-armour. You hid something from me—about that old man. And I saw him again yesterday.”

“Tito,” she whispered, voice breaking, “if you would only tell me everything—no matter what—I would bear pain, anything. Just let there be no wall between us. Can’t we begin again?”

This time, a flicker crossed Tito’s face—swift, almost imperceptible. He stood motionless, but his skin seemed to whiten. Yet he did not answer her plea.

After a pause, he said quietly, “Your impetuosity, Romola, has a freezing effect. It could chill the baths of Nero.”

At those words, Romola recoiled. She straightened, returning to her usual self-contained poise.

Tito continued. “If by ‘that old man’ you mean Jacopo di Nola—the madman who tried to kill me and accused me of something I did not tell you, because it would have alarmed you—he is dead. I saw his name on the list of the deceased.”

“I know nothing of his accusation,” Romola said. “But I know who he is. The man I saw in the Duomo, with the rope around his neck—the one Piero di Cosimo painted, grasping your arm as he did the day the French came in, the day you first wore the armour.”

“And where is he now?” Tito asked, still pale, but controlled.

“He lay dying in the street,” Romola said. “Starved. I gave him bread and wine. I brought him to our door. He refused to come in. I gave him money. He left without speaking. But he knew I was your wife. Who is he?”

“A fool, half-mad, half-idiot,” Tito said. “Once my father’s servant in Greece. I dismissed him for theft. He hates me. That is the whole story. And now you know why I wear the armour—and why I feared him. Did you think that sight led you to seek him out? To invite him into our house?”

Romola said nothing. Speech felt futile. Like throwing herself against a shield.

Tito stepped back, removed his cap and mantle, pushed his hair from his forehead. He was preparing for final words.

“We need not speak of this again,” he said, voice steady, as before. “But remember—if your passion leads you to interfere in politics again, you may not save anyone. You may raise scaffolds, burn houses. You are not yet so devoted to the Piagnone cause that you believe Bernardo del Nero is the prince of darkness, and Francesco Valori the archangel Michael. I do not need a promise from you.”

“I have understood you too well,” Romola said.

“Enough,” he replied, turning and leaving.

She sank into her chair, face shadowed with despair.

“Oh God,” she whispered, “I have tried. I cannot help it. We shall always be divided.”

Then, suddenly, aloud—“Unless… misery should come and join us.”

Tito, too, had new thoughts after closing the door. He had long planned to leave Florence—once his position here had served its purpose, to move on to Rome or Milan. But now, for the first time, he felt a desire to leave Romola behind. She no longer fit into the life he wanted. An easy relationship required honesty. Honesty meant confession. Confession meant changing course. And Tito, like a leopard with grown teeth, had no taste for milk.

He avoided discomfort. Why cling to what strained him? And Romola had already complicated things with others—Dolfo Spini, furious after a fruitless ambush, waiting in the rain. The scene at Nello’s door, once reported, could grow into something worse than suspicion.

Yet now, Tito believed he had mastered her—not through force, but through fear. He had touched her deepest instincts: her love, her conscience, her sense of responsibility. He had shown her the vast, tangled web of power where moral judgment was useless. He had silenced her mind.

Still, he was not at peace. The world was not yet soft enough. And even if it were, he could not rest fully in comfort. Before he left that evening, he put on his coat of chain-armour.

The winter days slipped by for Romola like white ships gliding past a solitary watcher on the shore—silent, unchanging in their passage, yet each carrying within it the hidden weight of what was to come. Tito’s words had stirred in her a dread that now mingled with her concern for the shifting currents of Florentine politics. She found herself avoiding knowledge, seeking refuge in ignorance rather than face the turmoil ahead. The German Emperor had withdrawn once more, and Florence’s position seemed less strained. Yet hardship lingered, and Romola’s duties remained as demanding as ever. In this work, as always, she found shelter from the unease that gnawed at her spirit.

She could not rejoice when the relief that had come in extremity—relief that seemed to validate the Frate’s party—brought them such triumph. Francesco Valori, fiery leader of the Piagnoni, had been elected Gonfaloniere at the start of the year, and he moved swiftly to assert his liberal vision during his brief two-month term. For the moment, it appeared as though the cause of freedom had gained strength, and Savonarola’s influence deepened. But Romola, now attuned to every whisper of foreboding, sensed something deeper: that no matter how stable things seemed, they were merely incubating the seeds of change—seeds that might one day burst forth in tragedy.

By Carnival time, just after mid-February, her fears were confirmed. The Mediceans were no longer passive. They had begun openly campaigning for Bernardo del Nero’s election as the new Gonfaloniere. On the final morning of Carnival, between ten and eleven o’clock, Romola set out as promised toward the Corso degli Albizzi to fetch her cousin Brigida. Together, they would depart early from the Via de’ Bardi and take their places at a window in the Piazza della Signoria, where Tito had secured a view of an event so unusual and dramatic that all Florence would want to witness it.

For the Piagnoni were determined to reshape the Carnival entirely. Despite Dolfo Spini and others' efforts to revive the old traditions—masked revels, bawdy jokes, and ribaldry—the city had declared Christ its king. Such frivolities were no longer welcome.

Romola walked with a quiet resignation, the kind people wear when they endure long days of spectacle only to please a child or a cherished friend. This Carnival, she knew, marked a turning point—a reform so bold it redefined festivity itself. But though it was a milestone in Florentine life, it did not stir her heart. She did not know that it would also mark a turning point in her own life, when another fate would cease to be hidden and begin to intertwine visibly with hers.

She chose to pass through the great Piazza first, wanting to see the spectacle alone before joining others. Entering from the south, she beheld something monstrous and dazzling—a towering pyramid, or rather a colossal fir-tree, sixty feet high, with shelves branching outward, widening toward the base until it spanned eighty yards. The square teemed with life. Young figures in white robes, crowned with olive wreaths, darted about the base, bearing baskets filled with brightly colored objects. Older men—some in monastic habits, others in artists’ tunics and dark-red caps—assisted, inspected, or stepped back to survey the immense structure. A cluster of onlookers, among whom Romola recognized Piero di Cosimo standing on the marble steps of Orgagna’s Loggia, watched with evident disdain.

Drawing closer, she paused to examine the items arranged in tiers from bottom to top. There were tapestries and brocades of indecent design; paintings and sculptures deemed too tempting to virtue. Game boards, dice, playing cards and printing blocks lay scattered. Musical instruments—lutes, drums, cymbals, horns—were piled beside books of worldly music. Masks and costumes from past Carnivals stood alongside copies of Ovid, Boccaccio, Petrarca, Pulci, and other works considered vain or impure. Then came the tools of feminine vanity—rouge pots, false hair, mirrors, perfumes, powders, and veils meant to entice curious eyes. At the very peak stood a grotesque effigy of a Venetian merchant—perhaps mythical—who had supposedly offered a large sum for this collection of “marketable abominations.” Above him, soaring in ugliness, was the symbolic figure of the old, debauched Carnival.

This was the centerpiece of a new kind of bonfire—the Burning of Vanities. Within the pyramid’s core lay dry fuel and gunpowder. That evening, as the festival drew to a close, the pile would be lit with trumpet blasts, and the old Carnival would crash into flames amid songs of moral victory.

Such a spectacle could not have been possible without a movement born two years earlier under Savonarola’s guidance. No longer left to their own mischief, the youth of Florence were being shaped into disciplined agents of piety. Under Fra Domenico, Savonarola’s lieutenant, boys and young men—seen as the future hope of the city—were trained to speak only pure words, to cultivate zeal for spiritual good, and to find joy only in angelic pleasures: singing hymns, walking in white robes, worshipping God.

These youths had built the tiered seats against the Duomo’s walls, and had gathered daily to hear Savonarola address them as the future glory of a city chosen by God. Now, they were the living force behind this transformed Carnival—a sacred parody of the old.

In ancient times, there had been bonfires? Then now, there would be one—to burn impurity from the earth. Had there been processions? Now, there would be processions—but with symbols of peace: white robes, red crosses, olive crowns. The banners would proclaim virtue, not vice. Had there been dancing beneath open skies to lustful songs? Now, monks and laypeople would dance in brotherhood, their music hymns of divine joy. And the collections? They would be greater than ever—not for feasts, but for the poor. And then, there was the gathering of the *Anathema*: the vanities destined for the pyre.

Young inquisitors, organized in small regiments, went door to door with solemn purpose, asking households to surrender their forbidden goods. Perhaps a woman, after giving up her masks and wigs, still kept tiny red balls from the Levant—meant to flush a pale cheek with artificial color? Let her bring them down and cast them into the basket of judgment. Or perhaps she wore coils of “dead hair”? Let her bring them to the street, not on her head, but in her hands, and publicly renounce the lie. In return, she would hear the voices of youth bless her home.

These boyish enforcers likely took their task with eagerness. To shame others into surrendering cherished possessions—that was a form of piety children embraced readily. When some defiant men threatened violence, it only added excitement. Savonarola himself, though noble in intent, struggled with the means. He saw the need for discipline, but also feared the danger of forcing faith through coercion. Once, the boys had shouted “*Viva Gesù!*” with fervor—it seemed a miracle.
