But soon he warned from the pulpit: “There is too much shouting of ‘Viva Gesù!’ These sacred words grow cheap. Let us have no more till the next Festa.”

Still, that morning, as the long procession of white-robed youth, adorned with red crosses and olive wreaths, filed into the Duomo at dawn to receive communion from Savonarola’s hands, it had been a sight of beauty. Many of those souls, doubtless, carried memories of awe and hope that would sustain them beyond mere civic duty. No obedience, however imperfect, is not progress from lawlessness. These boys would grow into men who would fight and endure in Florence’s final defense.

Now, between communion and dinner, they were making their final rounds—collecting alms and vanities. Hence, the slim white figures Romola saw moving around the base of the great pyramid.

“What think you of this folly, Madonna Romola?” a sharp voice asked beside her ear.

“Your Piagnoni will make *l’inferno* seem pleasant if they carry on like this,” said the speaker, a man whose tone held both scorn and bitterness. “It’s enough to send a cudgel over the mountains to see painters like Lorenzo di Credi and young Baccio helping to burn color out of life.”

“My good Piero,” Romola replied, lifting her eyes and smiling at the stern-faced artist, “even you must be glad to see some of these things destroyed. Look at those gewgaws, wigs, rouge pots—I’ve heard you speak against them as fiercely as Fra Girolamo himself.”

“What then?” Piero snapped, turning sharply on her. “I never said a woman should hide her beauty behind black patches. Go on, Madonna Antigone—what shame for a woman with your hair and shoulders to fall for such nonsense! Leave it to women unworthy of being painted. And consider—has not the Most Holy Virgin always been clothed beautifully? That is Church doctrine. Talk of heresy!”

“And what,” he continued, “would the excellent Messer Bardo have said if these friars burned the divine poets? Are they any more human than onions with bulbs turned upward? Look at that Petrarca beside a rouge pot—do they truly believe the heavenly Laura was a painted harridan? And Boccaccio—do you mean to say, Madonna Romola—you who could model a wise Saint Catherine of Egypt—do you really claim you’ve never read the tales of immortal Giovanni?”

“It is true I have read them, Piero,” Romola said calmly. “Some of them many times when I was a child. I used to take the book down when my father slept, so I could read in secret.”

“*Ebbene?*” Piero demanded, his voice harsh.

“There are some things in them I do not wish to forget,” Romola answered gently. “But you must admit, Piero, that many of those stories are about petty deceit for base ends. Men do not need books to teach them to mock vice as if life were nothing but a vulgar joke. I cannot blame Fra Girolamo for teaching that our time belongs to something higher.”

“Yes, yes,” Piero muttered bitterly, turning away. “Easy to say now that you’ve read them.”

Romola walked on, smiling faintly at his jab, touched by the old painter’s anger—she understood it well, for she knew her father would have felt the same. For herself, she felt no inner conflict with the austere view of pleasure that sought to suppress poetry in order to quell vice. Sorrow and joy each have their limits. A religious passion like Savonarola’s—though it may appear to deny life—ultimately blesses humanity by driving the soul toward empathy, justice, and self-denial. It inevitably draws criticism for its negation.

Romola’s life had shaped her soul to lean toward sadness, making her naturally unjust to merriment. The refined sense we call Taste had been subdued by deeper needs—just as hunger silences the taste for delicacies. Living daily among suffering, carrying the weight of profound personal loss, she found no dissonance in severity that came from strength, sacrifice, and compassion.

A familiar figure, dressed not in mourning but in the bright hues of red, green, and white, made her way toward the Piazza that morning to witness the Carnival. She came from a different quarter now—Tessa no longer lived on the hill of San Giorgio. After the events that had unfolded there with Baldassarre, Tito had deemed it wise, for both practical and emotional reasons, to move her to a quieter, more airy part of the city—a house near the wide garden grounds north of Porta Santa Croce. Though she was not permitted to go out without permission, Tessa had waited until the evening before to speak her wish. That night, Tito had visited her, and she held back her plea until she saw him relaxed and joyful, one arm around sturdy little Lillo, the other gently resting on her shoulder as she tried to steady the wobbly Ninna on her feet. Only then did she feel certain that the weariness he’d brought home with him had truly vanished from his face and voice.

Tessa had learned over time how to navigate the delicate balance between obedience and desire. She could not read, but she had become an expert in reading expressions—the subtle shifts in Tito’s eyes, the warmth of his smile, the softness of his tone. And in this moment, she saw again the same gentle, radiant man who had once awakened beneath the Loggia de’ Cerchi on a Lenten morning, untouched by deceit or suspicion. That spirit, though dimmed by years, flickered brightly in Naldo, seated comfortably in the carved armchair he had brought for his visits. Tito himself was surprised by the quiet relief he felt in these moments—no need for pretense, no fear of exposure. Tessa was too innocent, too trusting, to suspect anything. The children’s voices calling him “Babbo” were music to his ears, fleeting but sweet. He would never leave Florence without them. These round-cheeked, wide-eyed creatures clung to him, knowing no harm, and their affection, pure and unburdened, reminded him that love could bloom even in the most barren soil.

Poor Romola, with all her selfless devotion, seemed only to harden Tito’s heart—her coldness, once unthinkable in him, now a growing presence. But Tessa kept the springs of kindness open, untouched by bitterness.

“Ninna is very good now,” Tessa began, her request rising like a tide in her throat. She let the child sit on the floor. “I can leave her with Monna Lisa anytime. If she cries in the cradle, Lillo knows just what to do—he goes and thumps Monna Lisa.”

Lillo, whose dark eyes seemed deeper against his light-brown curls like his mother’s, jumped down from Babbo’s knee and immediately proved his wisdom by marching over to deliver a firm thump to Monna Lisa, who sat spinning at the far end of the room, shaking her head slowly.

“A wonderful boy!” Tito laughed.

“Isn’t he?” Tessa said eagerly, drawing closer. “And might I go see the Carnival tomorrow—just for an hour or two?”

“Oh, you wicked pigeon!” Tito teased, pinching her cheek. “That’s what you’re longing for? What do you need with carnivals now you’re an old woman with two children?”

“But old women like to see things,” Tessa replied, her lower lip trembling slightly. “Monna Lisa said she’d like to go—but she’s so deaf, she can’t hear what’s behind her, and she thinks we couldn’t manage both children.”

“No, indeed, Tessa,” Tito said, his tone turning serious. “You mustn’t think of taking the children into the crowded streets. I’d be angry if you did.”

“But I’ve never gone into the Piazza without leave since Holy Saturday,” Tessa pleaded, her voice small. “And I think Nofri is dead—his poor _madre_ died, didn’t she? I’ll never forget the Carnival I saw once—it was so beautiful: roses, a king and queen under them, singing. I liked it better than San Giovanni.”

“But there’s nothing like that now,” Tito said gently. “They’re going to burn a bonfire in the Piazza—that’s all. And I can’t let you go out alone in the evening.”

“Oh no, no! I don’t want to go in the evening. Just to see the procession by daylight. There *will* be a procession, won’t there?”

“Yes, after a sort,” Tito said, “as lively as a flight of cranes. Don’t expect roses or glittering kings and queens, my Tessa. Still, any string of people might please your blue eyes. They’ve raised something in the Piazza de’ Signori for the bonfire—you may like to see that. But come home early, and look like a grave little old woman. And if you see men with feathers and swords, keep away from them—they’re fierce and like to cut old women’s heads off.”

“Santa Madonna! Where do they come from?” Tessa gasped. “Ah! You’re laughing. It’s not so bad. But I’ll stay clear. Only,” she whispered, pressing her lips close to Naldo’s ear, “if I might take Lillo with me! He’s very sensible.”

“But who will thump Monna Lisa then?” Tito asked, struggling not to laugh but keeping a stern expression. “No, Tessa—you couldn’t care for Lillo in a crowd, and he’s too heavy for you to carry.”

“It’s true,” Tessa said, sadly. “He likes to run away. I forgot. Then I’ll go alone. But now—look at Ninna! You haven’t looked at her enough.”

Ninna, a blue-eyed child at the age of tottering and falling, was a solid little creature—like a loaded die, always landing the same way. Tessa picked her up, and while Babbo admired her new teeth and other wonders, she whispered, “And shall I buy some confetti for the children?”

Tito pulled coins from his scarsella and placed them in her palm.

“That will buy no end,” Tessa said, delighted. “I won’t mind going without Lillo so much if I bring him something.”

So Tessa set out in the morning toward the great Piazza where the bonfire would be lit. The February breeze felt warm enough to her; she wore only her green wool dress. A mantle would have hidden her new silver necklace and clasp—gifts from Tito, the only ornaments he had ever given her. She wasn’t thinking of showing off her figure—no one had ever told her she was pretty—but she was certain these trinkets were finer than anything worn by the richest peasant woman. She arranged her white hood carefully so the front of the necklace would catch the light.

She walked cheerfully through the sunlit streets, her thoughts full of sweets for the children, unaware that anyone watched her. Yet her descent from her upper room had been observed. She was being followed by someone who had long hoped to see her—someone who had trailed Tito many times before. Baldassarre carried a bundle of yarn, earning his meager living while nursing a fire of vengeance. He had come out of his way again, drawn to the house where Tito had once led him. His imprisonment had deepened his fears and twisted his belief that some dark fate protected Tito. He dared not approach openly—only in crowds or darkness, lest Tito’s gaze fall upon him and drag him once more into disgrace, stripping him of his knife and casting him into prison. His rage had turned stealthy, like a serpent waiting for its one fatal strike. Justice was weak, and the voice that once thundered from the Duomo promising vengeance had fallen silent, drowned by cruelty and cunning.

For a time, Baldassarre’s only goal was to confirm whether Tito still wore the armor—now, even death would satisfy his thirst. But he would never risk his knife again. Waiting had worn him thin. The thought of winning one of Tito’s wives to aid him haunted him—then slipped away. The wife on the hill was gone. If he could find her again, perhaps he could weave a plan. This morning, he had succeeded. He now knew where she lived. As he walked, bent under his burden, he kept his eyes on the green-and-white figure ahead, his mind tracing her life like a blind man reading lines of light.

Tessa passed through long streets, seeing only groups of country folk in their best clothes, chatting and lingering—the usual signs of a holiday before the spectacle began. Then, disappointed in her search for marvels, she noticed a man with a pedlar’s basket selling nothing but small red crosses.

A cross would be lovely above her bed—protection, strength for Ninna. But four white quattrini might be too much. She crossed the street to ask the price, afraid of spending too much on sweets.

The pedlar had turned his back, but when she approached, she recognized Bratti Ferravecchi, a familiar face from the Mercato. Accustomed to avoiding old friends, she turned away.

But Bratti’s eye was sharp. He tapped her arm with a red cross.

“Young woman,” he said, “you come from some distant castello, else you wouldn’t walk about this blessed Carnival without a cross in hand. Santa Madonna! Four white quattrini is a small price for your soul—prices rise in purgatory.”

“Oh, I’d like one,” Tessa said quickly, “but I can’t spare four quattrini.”

Bratti, who had first seen her only as a customer, now exclaimed, “By the head of San Giovanni, it’s the little Tessa—looking as fresh as a ripe apple! Did you escape Nofri? You were right—he’s on crutches now, and a crabbed man with crutches is dangerous. He can reach across the house and beat a woman sitting.”

“I’m married,” Tessa said demurely, remembering Naldo’s warning to behave gravely. “My husband takes great care of me.”

“Ah! You’ve fallen on your feet! Nofri called you vermin—but what does that matter? An ass may bray long before he shakes the stars down. I always said you did well to run. And now you’ve got a husband and plenty of money?”

“I’ve never wanted for anything,” Tessa said, still cautious.

“Then you can afford a cross. I had a Padre bless them. You get blessing and all for four quattrini. Not for profit—I barely earn a danaro. But they’re holy wares, and the path to Paradise grows harder. Even the Carnival feels like Holy Week. The least you can do is buy a cross. God guard you—think of the Devil’s tooth! Have you seen him biting the man in San Giovanni?”

Tessa grew uneasy. “Oh, Bratti,” she said, distressed, “I want to buy confetti for Lillo and Ninna. Sweet things cost so much. And they won’t like the cross, though I know it’s good.”

“Come then,” Bratti said, fond of imagining extortions and then renouncing them. “Since you’re an old acquaintance, I’ll give it to you for two quattrini. A gift, with the blessing.”

Tessa reached for her coins, trembling, when Bratti stopped her. “Wait. Where do you live?”

“Oh, far off,” she answered, distracted. “Beyond San Ambrogio, in the Via Piccola, at the top of the house where the wood is stacked below.”

“Very good,” Bratti said patronizingly. “I’ll let you have it on trust. I’ll call later. You live inside the gates? Well, I’ll pass by.”

“No, no!” Tessa insisted, fearing Naldo’s anger. “I can pay now.”

“No,” Bratti said firmly. “I’m not a hard-hearted pedlar. I’ll come and see if you have rags to trade. Here’s your cross. And Pippo’s shop is just behind you—go fill your basket. _Addio, piccina._”

Bratti walked on. Tessa, eager to spend her money before trouble struck, entered Pippo’s shop, her heart fluttering. She had let Bratti know more than she should. There were dangers in leaving home—and she hadn’t noticed the man with the yarn bundle.

But the joy of having treats for the children lifted her spirits. As she stepped into the Via de’ Libraj, her face glowed with childlike contentment. Now, she thought, the procession must be coming—she saw white robes and a banner. Her heart quickened.

She stood aside, but in the narrow street, she had to look closely. The banner showed the Holy Mother with the Babe—Tessa had grown to believe in her love ever since her own babies were born. The figures wore green wreaths and small red crosses—much like her own. They looked like angels on clouds, and to Tessa, everything in life seemed wrapped in cloud. How they came, she didn’t care. But some carried baskets—what for?

Then they turned toward her. She trembled, as if Saint Michael had shaken his head at her. All she felt was terror—until she saw a round boyish face and heard a high voice:

“Sister, you carry the Anathema. Yield it to the blessed Gesu, and He will adorn you with His grace.”

Tessa panicked. Was it her basket? Oh dear! She looked down.

“No, sister,” said a taller youth, pointing to her necklace and belt clasp. “It is those vanities—the Anathema. Take them off. Let them burn in the Bonfire of Vanities, and save yourself from burning.”

“It is the truth,” said another, taller still, clearly the leader. “Listen to divine voices. You already have a red cross—let that be your only adornment. Give up your necklace and belt, and you shall obtain grace.”

Tessa could not say no. Tears welled up. Her lips quivered. A single drop fell. She saw nothing but horror.

Suddenly, a gentle hand touched her arm. A soft, angelic voice said, “Do not be afraid. No one shall harm you.”

Tessa looked up and saw a lady in black, with a young, serene face and kind hazel eyes. She had never seen anyone like her. Under other circumstances, she might have feared such beauty—but now, only safety mattered. The tears flowed faster as she looked up, clutching her necklace.

“I can’t give them to be burned,” she sobbed. “My husband bought them for me. They’re so pretty. And Ninna—oh, I wish I’d never come!”

“Do not ask her,” Romola said softly to the boys. “It serves no purpose to take things against will. Fra Girolamo would not approve. Such things should be given freely.”

The boys obeyed. They moved on quickly, as if drawn to something else. Tessa breathed deeply—safe. Her treasures were still hers.

“Oh, I’ll go back,” she said, still shaken. “I’ll go nowhere else. But if I meet them again, and you’re not here?”

“Stay a little,” Romola said. “Come under this doorway. We’ll hide your necklace and clasp. Then you’ll be safe.”

She led Tessa under an archway and said, “Can we fit them in your basket? Ah—your basket is full of crisp things. Be careful. Lay the necklace beneath.”

To Tessa, it felt like waking from a nightmare into a dream of peace. This lady—so lovely, so strong, so gentle—was protecting her. She let Romola unfasten her necklace and clasp, watching silently.

“These are sweets for Lillo and Ninna,” she whispered.

“Those are your children?” Romola smiled.

“Would you rather go home than see more of the Carnival? Or would you like to see the bonfire pile in the Piazza de’ Signori?”

“No, oh no!” Tessa said eagerly. “I’ll never like bonfires again. I’m going home.”

“You live at some castello, doubtless,” Romola said, not waiting for an answer. “Which gate do you go toward?”

“Por’ Santa Croce.”

“Then come,” Romola said, taking her hand and leading her to a nearby corner. “If you go down this street, you’ll soon be on a straight road. I must leave now—someone waits for me. You will not be frightened. Your things are safe. Addio.”

“Addio, Madonna,” Tessa whispered, not knowing what else to say. In a flash, the heavenly lady was gone.

Tessa turned to watch—but only saw the tall figure vanish around the stone corner. She walked on, lost in wonder, longing only to return to Monna Lisa and her children. Forever, she decided, she would avoid carnivals.

Baldassarre had watched Tessa until she parted with Romola. Then he left, his yarn bundle in hand. For the first time, he felt a clue—something tangible. He had seen the two wives together. The vision had sharpened his thoughts. The tall one—Romola—was noble, rightful. She had blood that could be stirred to wrath. She understood learning, and might grasp the truth locked within Tito’s silence, like treasure buried by earthquake. She could believe him. She would *want* to believe him—if he could prove Tito’s betrayal. Women cared about that. They would take vengeance. If Romola loved Tito, her pain would be a weapon—another will, another mind to shape plans.

Both wives had been kind to him, and their kindness lingered in his memory. But pain was not a check for him—it was the natural order. Only the hard and base escaped suffering. If anyone was innocent, if anyone was noble, where could their greatest joy lie? Not in happiness—but in hatred, in triumph over injustice.

Yet he must be cautious. He would watch this wife in the Via de’ Bardi. Learn more. For even here, failure was possible. His only power now was patience.

CHAPTER LI. Monna Brigida’s Conversion.

When Romola said that someone else awaited her, she meant her cousin Brigida—but she had no idea how deeply that gentle woman was in need of her presence. As they walked back toward the Piazza, they saw a group of young men halt before Tessa, and when Romola, close enough now to see the poor girl’s distress, called out, “Wait for me a moment, cousin,” Monna Brigida quickly replied, “Ah, I will not go on—come for me at Boni’s shop. I shall return there.” The truth was, Monna Brigida carried within her a quiet unease: on one side, the familiar comforts of vanity—rouge, false hair, pearls—clung to her like old habits; on the other, a growing dread that the Piagnoni were right in saying such things weighed upon the soul. Their solemn way of seeing the world had seeped into everything like a scent—nothing tasted quite the same anymore. And then there was Romola, so young and radiant, living a life that made every ordinary indulgence seem like a sin. A widow of fifty-five who had long found joy in her appearance, and in what others thought of it, needed more than mere comfort to keep her spirit lifted. And Monna Brigida was beginning to struggle each morning at her mirror.

If her soul would flourish better without them, was it truly worth wearing the rouge and the braids? But when she looked into the glass, she saw a pale face with heavy cheeks and lines around her eyes that no smile could hide. When she let her grey hair fall simply about her head, as the Piagnoni did, her courage wavered. Monna Berta would surely laugh, call her an old hag—and though Monna Berta was only fifty-two, that age difference stung. Every woman not of their sect would glance away in pity; men would treat her like a grandmother. Yet at fifty-five, a woman was not so very old—only in need of a little care. So the rouge went on, the braids were pinned tight, the embroidered berretta placed firmly atop her head. Her neck, covered by layers of lace and jewels, looked better than Monna Berta’s, if anything. That very day, preparing for the Piagnone Carnival, this inner battle had flared up again. Overwhelmed by fear and longing, she turned back, seeking refuge in the druggist’s shop rather than face the collectors of the Anathema alone.

But she was too slow. Before she could vanish, the white-robed boys at the rear of the procession noticed her. Perhaps Tessa’s surrender came more easily because part of the troop had already confronted someone burdened far more visibly by the sins they sought to purge.

It happened that some of these youths were among the youngest taken into special training. One small boy, ten years old, wore an olive wreath above his cherubic cheeks and wide brown eyes. His imagination was filled with awe—the weight of eternity pressing on every choice between good and evil—yet his heart still yearned for mischief and power. He was the first to reach Monna Brigida, stepping boldly into her path.

She felt anger rise, searched for a doorway to escape—but none was near. To flee now would only worsen the shame. Still, it was not he who spoke first. It was a fifteen-year-old youth holding one handle of a broad basket.

“Venerable mother!” he began, voice clear and earnest, “the blessed Jesus commands you to give up the Anathema you carry. That cap adorned with pearls, those jewels fastening your false hair—let them be surrendered and sold for the poor. Cast the hair itself aside, as a lie fit only for fire. Doubtless, too, you hide other jewels beneath your silk mantle.”

“Yes, madonna,” added the youth at the other end, repeating phrases from Fra Girolamo’s sermons by heart, “they are too heavy for you—like a millstone dragging you down to perdition. Will you adorn yourself instead with the hunger of the poor, and proudly wear God’s curse upon your brow?”

“In truth, you are old, buona madre,” chimed in the cherubic boy in a sweet soprano, “and you look very ugly with the red on your cheeks and that black, glistening hair. Those fine things—only Satan could find pleasure in you. Your angel is sorrowful. He wants you to rub away the red.”

He snatched a soft silk scarf from the basket and held it forward, offering it as a sacred tool for repentance.

Monna Brigida’s anger melted into spiritual dread. Monna Berta and all the fashionable world were not watching—she was surrounded only by these young monitors, dressed in white robes, crowned with olive branches, marked with red crosses, their candor terrifying in its purity. Her Franciscan confessor, Fra Cristoforo of Santa Croce, was not there to steady her against Dominican zeal. She stood helpless, gripped by a sudden, overwhelming sense that divine judgment had come upon her.

With no alternative in mind, she took the scarf and gently rubbed her cheeks, trembling, obedient.

“It is well, madonna,” said the second youth. “A holy beginning. When you remove those vanities from your head, the dew of grace will fall upon it.”

The boy’s mischief grew stronger. Reaching up, he tugged at one of the jeweled pins securing her braids. The heavy plait fell forward, covering her face and pulling the rest of her headgear down with it. There was no longer any hesitation. She raised her hands, unfastened the rest, and threw her beloved crimson-velvet berretta—its seed-pearl embroidery unmatched—into the basket of doom. She stood bare, unrouged, her grey hair pulled back, her face showing the deep lines of age that no powder could conceal.

But the berretta was not left behind. The boys lifted it up, displaying it with cruel delight, the false hair dangling like a grotesque puppet’s wig.

“See, venerable mother,” said the taller youth, “what ugly lies you have freed yourself from! Now you look like the blessed Saint Anna, mother of the Holy Virgin!”

Thoughts of entering a convent immediately, of never showing herself again, rushed through Monna Brigida’s mind. There was nothing left but to save her soul. Of course, people laughed—she didn’t need to turn to know it. Well, perhaps it was better to be forced to think more of Paradise.

Yet at the thought that her familiar world was slipping away forever, tears welled—those quiet, stubborn tears that come to older eyes. She could barely see as a large, rough hand thrust a red cross before her, over the shoulders of the boys. A gruff voice barked:

“Only four quattrini, madonna—blessing and all! Buy it. You’ll find comfort now your wig’s gone. Deh! What are we sinners doing all our lives? Making soup in a basket, and getting only the scum for our stomachs. Better buy a blessing, madonna! Only four quattrini—the profit is less than the smell of a danaro, and it all goes to the poor.”

Still dazed, Monna Brigida reached into her embroidered purse, hidden beneath her silk mantle, when a figure appeared—more welcome than an angel freeing a prisoner.

“Romola, look at me!” she cried, her voice broken, stretching out both hands.

The white-clad troop began to move on, sensing their zeal had gone too far—perhaps a dispensation granted, no further offerings required.

“Dear cousin, don’t be distressed,” Romola said, struck with pity, though she couldn’t help a faint smile at the sight of her kinswoman—plain, natural, utterly unlike the image she’d always known. She removed the dark cloth from her own head and draped it over Monna Brigida.

“There,” she murmured soothingly, “no one will notice you now. Let us turn down the Via del Palagio and go straight home.”

They hurried away, Monna Brigida clutching Romola’s hand tightly, as if to confirm she was real.

“Ah, my Romola, my dear child!” she panted, struggling to keep pace with the tall, graceful figure beside her. “What an old scarecrow I am! I must be good—I mean to be good!”

“Buy a cross!” shouted the gruff voice again, the rough hand thrust forward once more. Bratti was not deterred by Romola’s presence—he followed quietly, determined to make a sale.

“Only four quattrini, blessing and all—and if there was any profit, it would all go to the poor.”

Monna Brigida could not stop, even if she had wanted to. She raised one hand to silence Romola’s protest, and with the other, offered a grosso—worth many white quattrini—saying softly, pleadingly:

“Take it, good man, and begone.”

“You’re in the right, madonna,” Bratti said, taking the coin swiftly and placing the cross in her palm. “I won’t give change—might as well rob you of a mass. We all must be scorched a little, but you’ll come off easier. Better fall from the window than the roof. A good Easter and a good year to you!”

“Well, Romola,” Monna Brigida said, sighing with a touch of despair as Bratti vanished, “if I’m to be a Piagnone, it doesn’t matter how I look!”

“Dear cousin,” Romola replied, smiling warmly, “you don’t know how much better you look than ever before. I can see now how kind and gentle your face really is—just like you. That rouge and finery used to push themselves forward, hiding your expression. Ask Piero or any painter—if they’d paint you now, they’d choose this face over the old one. Every line on a human face has dignity or beauty, unless it comes from base passions. How noble old men are—like my godfather! Why shouldn’t old women look grand and simple?”

“Yes, when one reaches sixty, my Romola,” Brigida murmured, hesitating slightly, “but I’m only fifty-five. Monna Berta and everyone—but it’s no use. I will be good, like you. Your mother, if she’d lived, would be my age—we were cousins. One must either die or grow old. But if I’m a Piagnone, it doesn’t matter.”

The Carnival day had passed, leaving behind no immediate mark on Romola’s life save the quiet burden of caring for her cousin Brigida, whose frailty and fading youth now seemed to weigh upon her like a solemn duty. Yet beneath this surface calm, a deeper current stirred—a Lenten season that brought with it not rest, but relentless mental strain and ceaseless effort. Bernardo del Nero had been chosen Gonfaloniere, a victory for the Medicean faction, though one that only deepened Romola’s unease. She felt a growing certainty—almost a premonition—that some hidden scheme was unfolding, poised between triumph and betrayal during these two months of her godfather’s authority. Each morning, as dawn crept into her chamber, she was met again by the same haunting dread, clinging to her like a shadow. It followed her through the narrow streets toward the Duomo, where she went each day to hear the early sermon. But once inside, amid the throng and the rising voices, the chill began to fade—like fear dissolving in the heat of battle.

For Savonarola was preaching. These were his final Lenten sermons, delivered under the shadow of excommunication, which he now openly defied. With unflinching clarity, he held up the Church to public scrutiny, naming its corruption without disguise or mercy. He spoke of renewal—not merely reform, but a revolution born of divine will. He declared that a time was coming when the world would rise against moral decay, and the dead body of Christendom would tremble back to life, as Lazarus did at the call of God. At times, he saw himself leading that awakening, his voice echoing across Europe, stirring nations from spiritual slumber. At other moments, he foresaw only persecution and death—this life as a vigil, and salvation only beyond the grave.

Even those who doubted his sincerity could not deny the power of his presence. To Romola, who believed deeply in his purpose, the moment was electrifying—less as a political event than as a personal reckoning. His words became part of her daily strength, woven into the labor of tending the sick, feeding the hungry, and comforting the despairing. Famine still clung to Florence, disease spread through crowded homes, and whispers of the Plague grew louder. In such days, pity alone was not enough. She needed something greater—an ideal to sustain her, a vision of justice rising from suffering. And Savonarola gave her that: a belief in a higher struggle, one in which her small acts of mercy were not meaningless, but seeds sown in darkness for a harvest yet unseen.

Yet even in this grand music, dissonance emerged. The early days of hope—when the Frate preached peace, charity, and unity—had given way to sharper tones. Political factions refused to yield; the French deliverer, so long awaited, had turned away from his promise; and enemies, both petty and powerful, now attacked Savonarola with new fury. As the pressure mounted, so did his rhetoric. Once he had scorned miracles, insisting faith needed no supernatural proof. Now he declared that if the truth of his message were not acknowledged, God Himself would intervene with a miracle. And in the fire of his passion, threats of divine vengeance against tyrants and corrupt priests took on an edge sharpened not just by zeal, but by personal anger.

To Romola, this shift was painful. She sensed the conflict within him—the tension between selfless prophecy and human pride—laid bare in every word. But there were others who found in those harsh notes a kind of ecstasy. Baldassarre, the old man whose past remained shrouded in mystery, had discovered Savonarola’s sermons again. He attended them whenever he could, drawn not by piety, but by the fierce justice they promised. He watched Romola too—saw her face among the crowd—and waited for the right moment to speak with her. Twice she had glimpsed him in the Duomo—once, his dark eyes fixed on hers. She wished to avoid him, yet she looked for him, as one might search for a sign.

But now, more pressing than any memory of him was the present. The Lenten weeks passed. April, the final month of Bernardo del Nero’s rule, neared its end. Still, nothing happened to confirm Romola’s fears. Rumors had circulated from Rome—of Piero de’ Medici preparing a return—but Bernardo’s term would soon be over. Romola tried to find comfort in the fact that her anxieties had proven unfounded. Then, on the twenty-seventh, as she walked through the city on her usual rounds of mercy, a messenger from Camilla Rucellai intercepted her. The woman, known throughout Florence as one of its most fervent seers, demanded her presence immediately—on matters of utmost urgency.

Romola hesitated. She detested the shrill, ecstatic manner of such women, and lately had heard that Camilla had claimed visions condemning Bernardo del Nero. The thought of hearing more such nonsense made her recoil. Why should she open herself to knowledge that could only deepen her turmoil? Yet, by now, habit had shaped her responses. She had learned to suppress her impulses, to obey external demands rather than inner inclinations. Chiding herself for cowardice, she agreed to go.

She found Camilla in a room arranged like a monk’s cell—bare, dim, and filled with the scent of incense and anxiety. The woman seized Romola’s arm with trembling hands, speaking in urgent whispers that rose quickly to a shriek. She had seen a vision—revealed by Romola’s own angel—that Romola knew secrets about Bernardo del Nero, secrets that, if revealed, could save the Republic. She must act. She must break from the enemy of God.

Romola, usually calm and composed, reacted with sudden force. She pulled her arm free, but Camilla clutched tighter, like a crab refusing to let go. Her voice soared into frenzy, claiming that Christ Himself had appeared to her and commanded certain citizens to throw Bernardo del Nero from the window of the Palazzo Vecchio. Fra Girolamo knew of it, but dared not say the vision was false. “And since then,” Camilla wailed, eyes wild, “the Blessed Infant came to me and placed a sweet wafer on my tongue—proof that I have done His will!”

“Let me go!” Romola thundered, her voice low but sharp with rage. “God grant you are mad! Else you are abominably wicked!”

Her strength broke Camilla’s grip. Romola fled, rushing down the street until she reached the church of the Badia. There, behind the heavy curtain under the ancient stone arch, she found a space apart—a sanctuary of stillness. She sank onto the step before Filippino Lippi’s painting of the Virgin appearing to Saint Bernard, a scene of serene grace in a world of chaos. For a moment, she hoped the storm within her might subside.

But the question burned brightest: how could Savonarola allow such visions to stand unchallenged? Could he truly condone the idea of murder in the name of divine justice? Romola knew he wouldn’t. He valued law, order, and reason above all. Yet why did he not denounce Camilla’s claims publicly? Why remain silent?

The answer came, clear and painful: because he, too, walked the line between revelation and imagination. He feared that to condemn such visions would be to condemn his own. He was bound not only by conscience but by politics—by the risk of being seen as a suppressor of divine inspiration, especially when he himself was its chief mouthpiece. His silence was not weakness—it was entrapment.

Kneeling there, face buried in her hands, Romola felt the collapse of her faith. The passion that had once lifted her now seemed entangled with fanaticism. She thought of her godfather—his practical wisdom, his dignity, his refusal to pretend loyalty to the Medici. He was no villain. Yet the very people who called him a traitor were fighting for what she believed was right: a pure government, free from tyranny. And yet, in their zeal, they blurred the line between justice and cruelty.

Her heart pulled her toward Bernardo, toward the memories of her father—men who judged not by symbols, but by character. But her mind warned her: there was likely a plot. A secret movement to restore the Medici. Was that crime? Or merely resistance?

And then came Tito’s warnings—the dread that she might one day know the truth, and be torn between loyalty and duty.

Calm did not come. Not even in the hush of the chapel, not even before the peaceful image of the saint receiving heavenly visitors. The rocky solitude of the painting was too distant. She rose, determined to return to her work—to the sick, the poor, the dying. Only in action, in tangible kindness, could she reclaim a sense of meaning.

As she turned, she saw him standing just two yards away.

It was Baldassarre.

“I would speak with you,” Baldassarre said, his voice low and urgent, as Romola turned to him with quiet expectancy. It was clear he had followed her, waiting in silence until she paused. At last, the truth she had long suspected—yet dreaded—was about to be revealed.

“Yes,” she replied, her tone subdued, almost like one accepting a sentence of penance. “But you wish to go where no one can hear us?”

“Where _he_ will not come upon us,” Baldassarre murmured, glancing behind him with a flicker of fear. “Out—into the open air—away from the streets.”

“I sometimes go to San Miniato at this hour,” Romola said. “If you wish, I will go now. You may follow. It is far, but we shall be alone.”

He nodded, and she set off without hesitation. To another woman, such a journey might have seemed reckless—alone with a man whose strange demeanor hinted at madness, as Tito had claimed. But Romola did not tremble before danger. And though her heart was heavy, she welcomed the distance, the delay it offered before the next blow struck.

The afternoon sun dipped low, casting long shadows across the hills, when she stopped on a rough patch beneath cypress trees. She looked around for Baldassarre. He was near, but when he reached her, he sank down onto the stony ground, breathless. His once-strong frame had weakened; the vigor that marked him in the Duomo was gone. A shiver ran through him, and his eyes held a hollow vagueness, as if the world were slipping away.

“The hill is steep,” Romola said gently, seating herself beside him. “And I fear you are worn by hunger?”

He turned his head slowly, fixing his gaze on her. Words failed him—the moment had come, yet he could not shape them. She sat still, motionless, giving him space, afraid to show impatience. He looked like a broken man, forgotten by time and fortune. Yet Romola had learned to see beyond appearances, to feel the weight of lives buried beneath neglect.

At last, his eyes sharpened, and he spoke with sudden force:

“Ah! You would have been my daughter!”

A flush swept over Romola’s face—then vanished, leaving her pale, lips parted like stone carved in sorrow. In that instant, the truth burst upon her. The fragments fell into place: Tito’s lies, his coldness, the way he spoke of his father’s death. She saw it all—how he had stolen this man’s life, his memory, his love.

She did not move. But her mind raced, shaped by years of knowing Tito’s nature. Her face betrayed her shock, and that was enough to stir Baldassarre. For the first time, his words had touched something real.

“You are a woman of noble blood—is it not true?” he pressed on, gripping her arm. “You go to hear the preacher. You despise falsehood—especially when it wears a smile. You hate your husband?”

“Oh God,” Romola whispered, overwhelmed by memory. “Was he truly your son? Or was it as he said—did you take him when he was small?”

“Ah, you believe me!” Baldassarre exclaimed, his voice rising. “You know what he is!” He clutched her arm tighter, as if her touch gave him strength. “Will you help me?”

“Yes,” she said, not understanding the depth of his meaning. She placed her hand gently over his rough fingers, and tears welled up. “Oh, it is pitiful. Tell me—you were a great scholar. You taught him. How did it end?”

She hesitated. Tito had called him mad. Where were the signs of learning now? But she kept her hand still, listening.

“It is gone,” Baldassarre said, voice breaking. “All of it. They wouldn’t believe me. He lied—he said I was mad—and they dragged me to prison. I am old. My mind does not return. And the world turns its back.”

He paused, his shoulders slumping under a wave of despair. Then, lifting his head again, he said with fierce urgency:
“But _you_ are not against me. He made you love him—then broke your trust. You hate him. Yes, he made _me_ love him. He was beautiful, gentle—a child in need. I found him beaten. He slept in my arms. I watched him grow. I gave him everything—my knowledge, my books, my treasures. He had my gems—sold them. Left me in chains. Never came back. When I returned, poor and broken, he denied me. Said I was mad.”

“He told us his father drowned,” Romola said softly. “Surely he believed it then. Oh, he could not have been so cruel then!”

A vision rose—Tito as she once knew him: tender, radiant, untouched by darkness. The ache of that lost innocence brought a sob, and tears spilled freely.

“You are young,” Baldassarre said impatiently. “Tears are useless. They smother the fire within. It is the fire that moves things forward. Listen.”

Romola turned sharply. Doubt flickered—was this man merely mad? An assassin driven by delusion? But the truth she had glimpsed refused to fade. She chose compassion over fear.

“Tell me,” she said gently, “how did you lose your memory? Your learning?”

“I was ill,” he answered. “I don’t know how long—only darkness. Then, one day, I sat among stones, bathed in sunlight. Nothing else existed. Slowly, a longing grew—something I couldn’t name. On the ship, drifting over water, I understood: I longed for the Boy. To find my thoughts again. I was locked out. Still am. All I feel is a wall… and darkness.”

He fell silent, hands clasped around his head. Romola’s belief deepened. She thought of her own father—how he mourned Dino, the future he’d imagined. This grief was familiar.

“It came back once,” Baldassarre continued after a while. “I was master of all. I saw the world again—my books, my jewels. I thought I had him in my power. I went to expose him—where the lights were, where the trees stood. But he lied again. Called me mad. They dragged me away…”

His voice hardened. “Wickedness is strong. He wears armor.”

Again, he gripped her arm. “But you will help me. He has wronged you too. He has another wife—children. He makes her believe he is her husband. She is foolish, helpless. I will show you where she lives.”

Romola recoiled—not from fear, but from outrage. The violation of dignity, of truth—this was unbearable. Baldassarre sensed her anger, mistaking it for alliance.

“You hate him,” he said. “I know women can hate. You have pride. You despise deceit. And you can love revenge.”

She sat frozen, torn between horror and pity. She felt the pressure of his grip, but did not pull away.

“You shall plan it,” he whispered, eager. “I have learned that you are his rightful wife. A noble woman. You listen to the preacher of vengeance. You will help justice. But think for me. My mind falters—everything slips—but the fire remains. The fire is God. It is justice. It will not die. You believe that—do you not?”

“If they won’t hang him for stealing from me,” he went on, “you will strip him of his armor. I will stab him. I have a knife. My arm is still strong.”

He slipped his hand beneath his tunic and drew forth a blade, running a thumb along the edge as if to steady himself.

Romola felt the weight of every hour ahead growing heavier. She knew better than to plead or deny. Instead, she asked quietly:
“You say she is helpless—that other wife—believes him her husband. Perhaps he married her first. Before me.”

“I cannot remember,” Baldassarre said, pausing. “Only the place. You shall see her. I will take you—but not now.” He added hastily, “_He_ may be there. Night falls.”

“It is true,” Romola said, rising suddenly. The sky had darkened. “But you will come—when?”

“In the morning,” he said, already dreaming of her swift revenge. “Come to me—where you came today. In the church. I will wait at ten. If you do not come, I will return at mid-day. Can you remember?”

“Mid-day,” he repeated, as if needing to fix it in his mind. “Same place. Mid-day. And after…” He rose, gripping her arm again with his left hand, the knife still held tight in the other. “We will have our revenge. He shall feel the edge of justice. The world is against me—but you will help me.”

“I would help you in other ways,” Romola said, making a small effort to break his illusion. “I see you suffer. You must work hard and earn little. I could bring you comfort—make you feel someone cares.”

“Do not speak of that,” he snapped. “I want nothing but vengeance. One drop of justice before I die. I have only my knife. It is sharp. But there is a moment after the thrust—when men see the face of death. Let it be mine he sees.”

He released her and sank back, exhausted.

Romola felt powerless. There was nothing to do but wait.

“Mid-day, then,” she said clearly.

“Yes,” he replied, voice weak. “Go. I will rest here.”

She hurried away. Turning once, she saw him still seated, a solitary figure against the fading light.

Romola moved swiftly through the dimming streets, her purpose growing stronger with each step—like a hidden stream swelling beneath the surface of a river, rising steadily alongside the main current. It was not so much a decision as an inner compulsion, something that could no longer be held back. She paid no mind to the shadows lengthening across the cobbles or the quiet fear that might come from walking alone. She crossed the bridge where the Arno lay dark and still, reflecting only the faint glow of dying light on the horizon, and took the shortest path to the Old Palace. If she met her husband there, it mattered little. She could not calculate risks; she had to speak, to release what was burning within her.

She did not notice the columns of the courtyard or the grand staircase leading upward. Her thoughts were fixed on one thing: to reach the Gonfaloniere. When she reached the hall, she asked an usher for Bernardo del Nero, named herself, and begged to be shown into a private room. She waited only a moment before the door opened and Bernardo entered, his white head held high above the silk lucco draped over his shoulders.

“Romola, my child, what is this?” he said, closing the door behind him with a tone of startled concern.

She had removed her veil and stepped forward without speaking. He placed a hand gently on her shoulder, drawing her slightly away so he could look at her face. Her features were worn by fatigue and long turmoil—her hair loose and tangled—but in her eyes burned a fierce, unyielding energy, as if spirit had overcome body.

“What has he done?” Bernardo asked sharply. “Tell me everything, child. Cast aside pride. I am your father.”

“It is not about myself,” Romola answered quickly. “Not at all. Dearest godfather, it is about you. I have heard things—some I cannot name. But you are in danger here. In the palace. Everywhere. There are men who hate you, fanatics who would see you harmed—and others, traitors, who wear friendly faces. Trust no one. If you trust, you will be betrayed.”

Bernardo smiled faintly. “Have you become so agitated, my poor child,” he said, raising a hand to stroke her brow, “that you bring such ancient truths to an old man like me?”

“No, no!” Romola cried, pressing her hands together tightly as if to silence the words she could not fully speak. “These are not old truths. They are new, fresh, real—things I know but cannot say. You know I am not foolish. I would not come to you unless I had reason. Is it too late now to warn you? To tell you to leave this place? To go to your villa, to wait out these last three days in safety? Oh God—perhaps it is already too late! And if harm comes to you… it will feel as though I had caused it!”

The words escaped her without thought, born of a long-suppressed dread. Then she stopped, startled by their own weight.

“I mean—I know nothing certain,” she added, faltering. “Only fears. Deep, terrible fears.”

“Poor child,” Bernardo murmured, studying her with quiet intensity. After a pause, he said, “Go, Romola. Return home and rest. These fears may be nothing more than shadows cast by small things. Even traitors act from self-interest—the rats run where they smell cheese. We do not yet know which way the scent blows.”

He turned away, gazing into space for a moment, then shrugged slowly. “As for warnings—they serve no purpose for me, child. I do not join plots. But I never abandon my cause. If I march beside stubborn men who charge into guns and pikes, I must bear the consequences. Let us not speak of it further. I have few years left in my sack; let them take what they can. Go now. Rest.”

He touched her head again, softly, tenderly. And she could not help clinging to his arm, pressing her forehead against his shoulder. His touch felt like the final remnant of a life once full of warmth and belonging—a life now seen in memory as pure, even in its sorrow, because it had known no bitterness.

“Is silence best, my Romola?” he asked.

“For now,” she whispered, lifting her head with a pleading glance. “But I cannot say if it will always be.”

“Well,” he replied, lifting the black drapery and folding it carefully around her head, “you have a father’s ear while I live—and a father’s home. Remember that.”

Then he opened the door. “Now go. Hasten. You look like a shadow—dark and swift—and that will keep you safe enough.”

That night, Romola slept deeply. The strain had reached its peak; her body needed rest before it could endure more. Though her habits were strict, she slept on past dawn.

When she awoke, the sound of gunfire echoed through the city. Piero de’ Medici, backed by thirteen hundred men, had arrived at the gate facing Rome. Maso brought her the news, adding many details of uncertain truth. A countryman had warned the Signoria just before daybreak, or Florence might have fallen without warning. Her husband had been summoned to the Palazzo hours earlier and was not at home.

She sent Maso out again to gather information, while she climbed to the loggia to watch for signs of invasion—or resistance. The great Piazza was crowded with armed men. Many citizens suspected of favoring the Medici had been called to the palace and detained. Some people seemed indifferent to Piero’s arrival; others claimed the Signoria had invited him. But whatever the truth, the reception was harsh. Soldiers from Pisa were advancing against him.

In Romola’s memory of those morning hours, few external events stood clearly apart from the storm of thought and dread. She remembered walking to the Badia at the appointed time, despite alarms in the streets. She remembered waiting in vain. And she remembered vividly the scene outside the church when she stepped onto the steps as the doors closed for the afternoon.

The faces of the people—armed and unarmed—had changed. Their voices carried a new tone. The guns fired again, but instead of fear, laughter rose among the crowd. Soon she learned why.

Piero de’ Medici and his riders had turned away from Florence, galloping hard toward Siena.

She heard this from a sturdy shopkeeper from Piagnone, who still held his pike. “It is true,” he said, with a bitter edge in his voice. “Piero is gone—but those who helped him remain. We all know that. And if the new Signoria does its duty, we shall soon learn who they are.”

The words struck Romola like a sudden pain. But the threat they hinted at was not yet upon her. As she returned home, her greatest worry was whether she had lost sight of Baldassarre for too long.

The days stretched on, long and golden, yet they brought neither the hope Romola most cherished nor the dread she feared most deeply. No word came from Baldassarre. And though the government kept a vigilant eye, no trace of the suspected conspiracy emerged. Yet other events unfolded—events that drew her into the heart of Florence’s turmoil, transforming her quiet anxiety into active compassion. The plague spread through the city like a shadow, and Savonarola was excommunicated by the Church. These twin tragedies did not distance her from the friar; instead, they pulled her closer to him, rekindling the moral fire he had once lit within her.

Romola could not walk among the sick and dying, tending to their suffering with a soul stirred by purpose, without remembering who had awakened that purpose in her. It was Fra Girolamo who had opened her eyes to a life beyond self, to one where duty and compassion were not mere ideals but urgent realities. And now, as he stood alone against the tide of corruption and fear, she felt his strength as her own. She could not watch the silence fall upon him—the formal reading of a sentence already posted in the churches, the dimming of candles, the slow retreat of monks—without feeling her allegiance deepen. This was not just a man being condemned; it was a moral force being silenced by greed and power.

She went to the Duomo to witness the excommunication, not out of curiosity, but to strengthen herself. To see the faces of those who rejoiced in his downfall—hardened, coarse, triumphant—only sharpened her resolve. And to see the fear in the eyes of his friends, their voices trembling with confusion, only confirmed her belief: this was not justice, but tyranny dressed in holy robes.

To many Florentines, the Church was not an institution to be questioned—it was a living presence, divinely ordained, capable of blessing or cursing with invisible might. To them, Savonarola’s defiance of Rome was dangerous, almost blasphemous. But Romola had never known the Church as an inherited truth. Her faith had been shaped not by tradition, but by the man who preached against hypocrisy and lived with unwavering integrity. For her, the Church existed only insofar as it mirrored his example. His excommunication was not a spiritual crisis—it was an attack on virtue itself.

She saw clearly what others could not: on one side, a man devoted to purity and public good; on the other, a corrupt old man—Rodrigo Borgia, now Pope Alexander VI—whose lust for power and wealth had driven him to destroy a voice of conscience. The complexities of theology, the nuances of obedience and resistance, meant little to her now. What mattered was the stark contrast between right and wrong, between sacrifice and selfishness.

And so, in the face of this injustice, Romola found renewed strength. Savonarola was no longer merely a preacher challenging Florentine vices—he was now a martyr standing against the very heart of ecclesiastical corruption. He had leapt into the breach, bearing a banner not just for Florence, but for all who believed in truth. In such moments, when courage is seen in action, life seems simpler. The soul feels certain of its highest calling, and even the weakest can believe in heroism.

This fire sustained her through the sweltering summer days. Yet, despite her outward resolve, she remained silent with Tito. She had told him nothing of her meeting with Baldassarre, of the revelation that shattered her world. What would he say? Not understanding, not sorrow—but mockery, perhaps, at her sympathy for the man who had tried to kill him. Baldassarre was likely powerless now. The danger was not that he would harm Tito, but that Tito, discovering his trail, might act again—perhaps with greater cruelty.

Romola waited. She needed to see Baldassarre again, to learn the truth about the “other wife.” Was she the woman Tito had first married? If so, the wound to her pride would be unbearable. That suspicion, born of grief and doubt, mingled with her fading memories of love, eroding every tender feeling she once held for her husband. She believed in the rest of what Baldassarre had said—and that belief made Tito repulsive to her. She recoiled from him, not with anger, but with horror. Only her silence protected her from speaking words she could not control.

Tito, meanwhile, spent much of his time away from home. Like many wealthy citizens during the plague, he retreated to country villas. The Melema estate welcomed him often, and since Romola refused to leave Florence, there was no need for him to maintain a separate residence. So he drifted in and out, leaving her alone with her thoughts.

But by late July, the public crises began to ease. The plague receded. The magistrates, sympathetic to Savonarola, wrote urgent letters to Rome, pleading for the excommunication to be lifted. With less urgency pressing upon her, Romola turned inward. Her body, worn by constant labor and worry, began to falter. Languor settled over her, a natural reaction after months of strain. Yet her mind refused stillness. She could not stay indoors unless the heat pressed down like a weight.

So she walked—morning and evening—through the streets of Florence, changing her path each time, hoping against hope that she might meet Baldassarre. Perhaps illness had robbed him of memory. Perhaps he had forgotten her name, her house, even her existence. That was her faintest hope. But the deeper truth, the one she could not escape, was that he might be dead—consumed by the plague, lost to her forever.

The morning air was already thick with warmth when Romola, returning from San Marco, turned toward the intersecting streets near the gate of Santa Croce. The Borgo La Croce lay quiet and still, so hushed that she could hear the soft thud of her own footsteps on the sunlit pavement. As she neared a bend in the narrow road, a small child—no more than three years old, bare except for a white shirt—caught her eye. He had paused mid-waddle, his head turning slowly as if searching for something lost.

At first, all she saw was his back—a sturdy little boy’s back, broad and firm, topped by a cloud of reddish-brown curls. Then he turned, and she saw his face: dark eyes wide with tears, lower lip trembling, fat brown fists gripping his shirt helplessly. A shadow fell over him—the sudden loom of a tall figure—and at once his fear broke into full cry, great sobs shaking his small frame.

Romola, moved instantly by the instinctive tenderness that lived beneath her composed nature, uncovered her head and knelt beside him. She wrapped her arms around him, pressing her cheek to his, speaking softly, soothingly. At first, the sobs grew louder, but he did not struggle. Then, as abruptly as grief can end in a child, the crying ceased. His face relaxed, his mouth opened in wonder, and he stared up at her.

“You’ve wandered away, little one,” she said, kissing his forehead. “Don’t worry—we’ll find your home again. Perhaps your mother will come to meet us.”

She sensed at once that he had slipped out while his mother’s attention had been elsewhere. She thought it likely he would soon be missed.

“Oh, you’re such a heavy boy!” she teased, trying to lift him. “I can’t carry you. Come then, let’s walk together.”

He remained silent, lips parted in awe, one fist still clenched tight against his shirt. But the other hand reached out without hesitation, grasping her pale, strong fingers.

“You have a mother?” Romola asked gently as they began to move, looking down at him with a quiet yearning. He didn’t answer. A girl might have chattered endlessly under such circumstances; but this little man, square-shouldered and curly-haired, was reserved.

Yet he was alert. At the corner near San Ambrogio, he pulled her arm, pointing ahead.

“That’s the way home, is it?” she smiled. He only tugged harder, urging her forward.

Another turn came, and then Romola found herself in a short street leading to open garden ground. At the end stood a house, and there the boy stopped, pulling her toward stone steps. He clung tightly to her hand, unwilling to let go. She wouldn’t leave him unless she knew he was safe.

They climbed the stairs, stepping into dimness after the bright sunlight. At the top, light poured through an open door. They passed into a small lobby, then into a room where Romola paused—her presence unnoticed.

On a low chair at the far end sat Tessa, one hand resting on the edge of a cradle, her head slightly tilted, fast asleep. Near a window, with her back to the doorway, Monna Lisa worked quietly, preparing salad, deaf to everything. For just a moment, Romola took in the scene before her—then Lillo wrenched his hand free and ran to his mother’s side. He didn’t wake her, only leaned his head against her arm and looked at Romola with solemn curiosity.

Tessa stirred, opened her eyes, and gasped in surprise. Her gaze landed on Romola, and she jumped up, blushing deeply, trembling, unable to speak or move.

“Ah! We’ve met before,” Romola said, smiling. “I’m glad it was your son. He was crying in the street—I suppose he’d run off. So we walked together, and then he remembered the way. You hadn’t missed him? That’s good—else you’d have been frightened.”

The shock of realizing Lillo had escaped overwhelmed Tessa. Her color faded, and she grabbed his arm, rushing to Monna Lisa.

“Oh, Lisa, you’re wicked! Why do you stand with your back to the door? Lillo ran so far!”

“Holy Mother!” Monna Lisa murmured in her soft, thick voice, dropping her spoon. “Where were *you*, then? I thought you were watching him.”

“But you know I fall asleep when I rock,” Tessa snapped, petulant. “I can’t help it.”

“Well, well,” said Monna Lisa, “we must shut the outer door—or tie him up. He’ll be as cunning as Satan before long, and that’s the truth. But how did he get back?”

That brought Tessa’s attention back to Romola. She turned toward her, blushing again, timid. Monna Lisa followed her gaze and bowed low.

“Doubtless the most noble lady brought him back,” she said. Then, stepping closer, she added, “It’s my shame he was found in only his shirt. He kicked this morning—he wouldn’t wear his clothes. And the mother, poor thing, won’t let him be beaten. What can an old woman do without a stick when the boy’s legs grow so strong? Let your nobleness look at them.”

Lillo, aware he was being discussed, lifted his shirt slightly and studied his olive, round limbs with calm interest. Romola laughed, bent down, shook him gently, kissed him. This small gesture calmed Tessa, who had already begun to feel reassured by Monna Lisa’s respectful words.

Naldo had once told her about the Carnival incident—how a heavenly woman had appeared just when she needed help, then vanished. Tessa had asked if she might have been the Holy Madonna. Naldo had replied, “Not exactly—only one of the saints.” So in her simple mind, Romola had become mingled with sacred images. When she returned now, the memory of her kindness was tinged with quiet awe—not deep fear, for Tessa feared only ugly spirits like Nofri and the devil. Good beings, she believed, wouldn’t punish her.

Now, with Monna Lisa speaking plainly and Romola laughing, Tessa felt easier.

“Ninna’s in the cradle,” she said. “*She’s* pretty too.”

Romola approached the cradle. Monna Lisa, one of the rare deaf women who never expected to be addressed, returned to her work.

“Ah! She’s waking—look, her blue eyes are open,” Romola said. “Take her up, and I’ll sit here—may I?—and hold Lillo. Come, Lillo!”

She sat in Tito’s chair, arms outstretched. The boy hesitated, then pointed at her with a puzzled frown.

“That’s Babbo’s chair,” he said.

“But Babbo isn’t here,” Romola replied. “And I’ll be gone soon. Come, let me hold you as he does.”

She wondered silently what kind of man Babbo was—one whose wife dressed like a peasant yet carried herself with quiet grace, hinting at ease and comfort. Lillo agreed to be lifted, and once settled in her lap, began to examine her hands and dress, searching for ornaments beyond the rosary.

Tessa, who had been coaxing Ninna from her grumpiness, now sat nearby, arranging the baby carefully, anxious that the strange lady not favor the boy more than she did.

“Lillo was angry because I sat in Babbo’s chair,” Romola said, leaning forward to kiss Ninna’s foot. “Will he come soon and want it?”

“No,” Tessa answered. “You can stay a long time. I’ll be sorry when you go. When you came to care for me at the Carnival, I thought it was wonderful—you came and went so quickly. Naldo said perhaps you were a saint, and that made me tremble, though saints are good. You were good to me, and now you’ve helped Lillo. Maybe you’ll always come to take care of me. That’s how Naldo used to be—long ago, when I was scared on San Giovanni. I couldn’t tell where he came from—he was so beautiful, so kind. And so are you,” she finished, gazing up at Romola with devout admiration.

“Naldo is your husband,” Romola said, studying the boy’s dark, finely drawn eyebrows—unusual for his age. “His eyes are like Lillo’s.”

She spoke with quiet certainty, not asking—knowing.

“Ah! You know him!” Tessa whispered, amazed. “Perhaps you know Nofri and Peretola, our house on the hill, everything. Yes, like Lillo’s—but not his hair. His hair is dark and long—” she continued, growing excited. “Ah! If you know, *ecco!*”

She touched a thin red silk cord around her neck and drew out a tiny parchment _Breve_, a piece of red coral, and a long, dark curl tied neatly at one end. She held it toward Romola, away from Ninna’s reaching fingers.

“It’s fresh,” she said, placing it against Romola’s pale fingers. “I cut it lately. See how bright it is! They fade, and then he lets me cut another when his hair grows. I keep it with the _Breve_—sometimes he’s gone so long, and I think it helps to protect me.”

A shiver passed through Romola as the curl touched her skin. When Tessa first mentioned her husband coming mysteriously from nowhere, Romola had felt a sudden surge of possibility—something that quickened her pulse. To one seeking answers, even faint clues burn with significance. Now, holding the lock, it seemed for a fleeting second like a mocking echo of the one she herself had cut years ago, winding it with her own hair. Yet she kept her composure. She wanted truth—but not at the cost of hurting this innocent, trusting soul.

“It’s a beautiful curl,” she said, resisting the urge to pull her hand away. “Lillo’s curls may be like it—his cheek is dark too. And you don’t know where your husband goes when he leaves?”

“No,” Tessa said, tucking the treasures safely away. “But I know Messer San Michele watches over him—he gave him a coat made of little chains. If he wears it, no one can kill him. And perhaps—if you *were* a saint—you’d watch over him too, since you’ve watched over me and Lillo.”

A flush rose on Romola’s cheeks—her first sign of certainty. But she pressed her face against Lillo’s head, hiding her emotion. A strange joy surged within her—the thought that her burden might soon be lifted, that this simple woman might be Tito’s lawful wife. A feeling both exultant and humbling for a proud woman to experience. Yet she heard Tessa’s plea, and raised her head.

“I’ll always come if you need me,” she said clearly. “But that chain-coat—your husband didn’t wear it when you married? Perhaps he wasn’t gone so long then?”

“Ah, yes,” Tessa replied. “Much longer. So long, I thought he’d never return. I used to cry. Oh me! I was beaten then—so long ago at Peretola, when we had goats and mules.”

“And how long had you been married before he got the chain-coat?” Romola asked, her heart pounding.

Tessa counted on her fingers. Romola watched them closely—as if they might reveal her fate.

“The chestnuts were ripe when we married,” she said. “Then again when he came back at Peretola. Then again on the hill. Then the soldiers came, the trumpets sounded—and Naldo got the coat.”

“You’d been married more than two years,” Romola said, her mind racing. “In which church were you married?”

Tessa flinched at the tone. Until now, she’d spoken freely, unaware she was revealing anything significant.

“Naldo said I was never to tell,” she said, hesitant. “Do you think he’d be angry if I told you?”

“It’s right that you should,” Romola said, fixing her with quiet authority. “Tell me everything.”

If Naldo’s warning had been fresher, Romola’s presence might have silenced her. But the weight of telling something never shared before made her begin in a low voice.

“It wasn’t in a church,” she said. “It was at the Nativita, during the fair. Everyone went overnight to see the Madonna in the Nunziata. My mother was ill, so I took her cocoons. Then he came to me in the church—I heard him call ‘Tessa!’ I knew him—he’d taken care of me on San Giovanni. We went to the piazza where the fair was. I had some _berlingozzi_—I was hungry, and he was kind. At the end of the piazza, there was a holy father and an altar like the ones in processions. He married us. Then Naldo took me back into the church and left me. I went home—my mother died. Nofri beat me worse. Naldo never came back. I used to cry. Once at the Carnival, I saw him and followed him. He was angry, said he’d come someday—I had to wait. So I waited. It was a long time, but he would have come if he could, for he was good. Then he took me away—because I cried and said I couldn’t stay with Nofri. Oh! I was so happy. Since then, I’ve been always happy. I don’t mind the goats and mules—I have Lillo and Ninna. Naldo isn’t angry often, but I think he doesn’t love Ninna as much as Lillo. And she *is* pretty.”

She forgot her earlier seriousness and began showering kisses on Ninna. Romola sat in silence, eyes distant. In that moment, she saw them not as individuals, but as pieces in her own life’s puzzle. The chill of disappointment settled over her—her troubles wouldn’t be solved by law. She had let go of Lillo, rested her cheek on her hand, seeing nothing.

Lillo noticed the change. He hadn’t responded to her affection, but now he objected to its absence. He raised both arms, pulled her head toward him.

“Play with me again!”

Romola roused herself, clasped him close, and looked at Tessa, who had paused from her kisses and now gazed at Romola with quiet reverence. Romola’s face softened, as if bathed in gentler light. Then she took her scissors from her purse and cut a long, wavy strand of her own hair. The three pairs of wide eyes watched, curious as kittens.

“I must go now,” she said. “But I leave this lock so you’ll remember me. If you’re ever in trouble, think God may send me again. I can’t tell you where to find me, but if I learn you need me, I’ll come. Addio!”

She set Lillo down quickly, offered her hand. Tessa kissed it—half in awe, half in sorrow. Romola’s mind was heavy with thoughts. She needed solitude. Yet, true to her nature, she turned to touch Monna Lisa’s shoulder in farewell. Before the old woman finished her bow, Romola had vanished.

Monna Lisa and Tessa moved toward each other, drawn by mutual impulse. The children clung to their mother’s skirt, sensing the hush.

“Do you think she *was* a saint?” Tessa whispered, showing the lock.

Lisa shook her head firmly, then stroked the golden hair.

“She’s a great and noble lady. I’ve seen such in my youth.”

Romola returned home and sat alone through the sweltering afternoon, the weight of certainty crushing her. Her path remained unchanged. She was caught between the demands of outward law—widely binding, sacred—and inner truths growing ever more urgent. She had absorbed Savonarola’s teaching: that duty was not merely obligation, but the expression of moral order. The sanctity of bonds, especially marriage, was the outward form of deeper human virtue. To break ties lightly, as Tito had done to Baldassarre, was to destroy social and personal integrity.

Savonarola had taught her that her life was part of a greater whole—that even small duties became sacred. She was marching with an army, bearing the weight of a shared destiny. If sacrifice was needed, she would stand ready. She had endured long, struggled hard. But the conditions that once made her bond possible had eroded. All her efforts had only revealed the impossibility of union. The marriage had become a degrading servitude.

The law was sacred. But rebellion might be sacred too. It struck her—this was the same dilemma Savonarola faced: where obedience ended, and rebellion began. To act on one’s own conviction, not guided by law, but facing a law armed with divine judgment—lightning that might strike if the conviction was false.

Before sunset, she made her decision. She would seek no counsel from her godfather or Savonarola until she had spoken directly to Tito. She would ask his consent to live apart. She would not flee secretly or abandon Florence. She would tell him that if he ever truly needed her, she would return. Wasn’t that the highest faithfulness?

She shuddered at the thought he might mock her, suggesting she enter a convent—the only respectable way to leave him. But she knew she could not accept that. Not only did she reject the narrow rule, but her father’s memory forbade it. He had despised monastic life, and she could not honor him by embracing what he abhorred.

Tito had said he’d return that evening. She would wait. She would speak at once—difficult to reach him during the day. When she sent Maso to inform him, she noticed the old man hesitate, his eyes full of concern. But she asked no questions. Perhaps he already knew.

It was late when Tito arrived. Romola had paced the long former library, windows open, wearing a loose white robe instead of her usual black. The change brought relief after hours of heat and stillness, but also sharpened her awareness. Holding a lamp, she opened the door.

He stepped in, startled by the intensity in her eyes—the fierce resolution replacing her usual quiet restraint. But he seemed to expect it.

“Ah, it’s you, Romola,” he said, closing the door behind him. “Maso has gone to bed.” He turned, studying her. “You’ve heard it all, I see.”

Tito had good reason to believe he was safe. Over the past three months, as he anticipated the exposure of the Medici conspiracy, he had used every moment to fortify his position. He had deepened his influence in Rome and Milan by acting as a conduit for secret intelligence—information aimed at undermining both the Friar and the popular faction. At the same time, he had cultivated the favor of the very party he was secretly betraying, carefully presenting himself as a loyal ally of their cause. And throughout this intricate balancing act, he had not withdrawn from the Medici circle; instead, he had grown more trusted, more indispensable to them.

It was easy for Tito to maintain such a triple role. The Medici themselves operated under a code that accepted deception as legitimate when directed against enemies—yet they saw no honor in betraying their own. Because Tito lacked their inherited loyalties, family ties, and long-standing prejudices, they never questioned whether he could be trusted. To them, his detachment made him ideal: a man without roots, unburdened by tradition, free to serve their ends with total flexibility. They failed to see that the very quality that made him useful—the absence of fixed allegiance—was also the absence of moral constraint.

Meanwhile, the Roman and Milanese supporters of the aristocratic faction, known as the Arrabbiati, were locked in constant covert warfare against Savonarola. In their world, duplicity was not only expected but rewarded. The deeper the lie, the higher the price. So, if Tito played a part with conviction, it would have been absurd to suspect him of insincerity—just as it would have been foolish to think any agent in such circles was anything but a master of disguise.

On the other side, the Piagnoni—the popular party—were men of strong convictions and direct speech. They admired Tito’s political alignment with them, especially since he showed no religious fervor. His lack of spiritual zeal actually worked in his favor; it made his support seem more genuine, less performative.

Under these conditions, Tito’s last three months had been marked by remarkable success. What mattered most to him was securing a future beyond Florence—a place where life was smoother, rewards greater, and talent properly honored. Rome and Milan offered such prospects, though currently Milan held the edge. If he could deliver crucial services to Duke Ludovico Sforza within the year, he stood to gain a prestigious post at the Milanese court—one that surpassed even what Rome might offer.

The revelation of the Medici plot had been something Tito had prepared for, though not its exact form. The arrest of Lamberto dell’Antella, found with a damning letter and a heart full of hatred for the Medici, was an unforeseen blow. Tito knew he could not escape suspicion—he had always known that. But his mind moved swiftly. He devised a path to safety that would spare him the worst consequences.

There is comfort in preserving one’s skin—but even a preserved skin feels the air around it. His calculations proved correct. Before returning home that night, he secured three vital outcomes: he would be cleared of all charges related to the Medici conspiracy; he would keep his secretaryship for another year unless he resigned; and, most importantly, the price he paid for these assurances would remain a state secret.

That price would have seemed steep to most men—and Tito himself would have preferred not to pay it. But necessity dictated otherwise. First, he turned to Francesco Valori, a member of the Ten who oversaw his position and also sat on the special council investigating the conspiracy. Valori, as we’ve seen, was a leader of the Piagnoni—a man of sharp intellect, fierce partisanship, and bitter personal grudges, especially toward Bernardo del Nero.

In a private meeting, after swearing Valori to secrecy, Tito confessed his role as a Medici agent—not out of loyalty, but because he believed their efforts doomed. He declared he had always favored the popular government and now sought justice. He even confided his dislike for Bernardo del Nero, framing it as a personal matter. Then came the key revelation: there was another Medici plot—this one aiming to restore Piero de’ Medici to power through a coup scheduled for mid-August.
