With certain guarantees, Tito claimed he could travel to Siena and Romagna, where Piero was gathering forces, and return with documentary proof.

To do so, however, he needed protection. His status as a Medici informant must remain hidden—even from those who suspected him. Otherwise, he would be compromised, unable to operate among them. He demanded immunity from prosecution and the right to retain his office for one year. Without these, he could not proceed.

Valori listened. But his thoughts were not on Tito’s morality. His focus was on ensuring the five accused men received the harshest punishment possible. There would be frantic efforts to save them. The evidence needed to be overwhelming—strong enough to deter others from sympathizing with mercy. In that era, justice often required dirty hands. Torture had already extracted confessions from Lamberto, but more was needed—especially against Bernardo del Nero, who had merely refused to betray the plot after failing to stop it. For Florence’s sake, Valori believed Bernardo must be shown in the worst possible light. Perhaps he didn’t realize how much his judgment was shaped by personal animosity.

Still, he approved Tito’s plan. He presented it to the council anonymously, persuading them of its value. By late afternoon, Tito was summoned before the Special Council. Standing calmly before twenty solemn Florentines, he revealed the new conspiracy—its timing, its aims, its intended execution. He spoke with quiet precision, using measured language that masked the gravity of his words. Yet there was something unsettling about his demeanor: the ease with which he admitted his motives, the way he seemed to acknowledge his own moral shortfalls without shame.

He delivered a commission to journey to Siena at dawn. And he left the chamber with a written guarantee of immunity and continued office.

Among those watching him, several formed a new opinion. This clever Greek was no longer fit for friendship—only for service. Unprincipled men were useful tools. They allowed those with scruples to remain clean while still getting things done. As the poet Pulci once said, “Many love the fruits of treachery, but no one loves the traitor.” Society had built gallows for murderers and martyrs alike—hissed at cowardice and at truth-tellers alike. Such contradictions were not signs of chaos, but of struggle—an attempt to order a world where virtue and vice walked hand in hand.

Tito was aware of the shift in how he was viewed. He possessed a clear sense of self, born of constant reflection and calculated action. He had never wanted disapproval. He had aimed to act so skillfully that others would never know he was acting against their standards. Only Romola had seen through him—because she refused to accept lies. That barrier between them had been erected by the impossibility of concealment.

Now, he felt the sting of judgment. Not because he had done wrong—though he knew he had—but because his actions could no longer be fully hidden. This was a revelation, not just to others, but to himself. He winced at the thought of being despised.

Yet he pressed on. He did not hate Bernardo del Nero. He liked Lorenzo Tornabuoni and Giannozzo Pucci. He had served them well—so well that if they had won, he would have been richly rewarded. But now, with their fall, could he abandon the pleasures of life? His offer of further evidence would likely change nothing for them—he was convinced they would survive. But without it, he would lose everything.

Was there any motive but self-interest? Perhaps some men tied their fate to family pride or ancient feuds. Others, like fanatics, believed in divine reward or apocalyptic renewal. But clear-minded men—like Pontanus, the Latin poet who welcomed the French king to overthrow his own patron—understood that survival meant compromise. Power shielded men from blame. Weakness invited scorn.

Tito sensed the first whispers of that scorn during his audience. It was faint, but real. And it marked a turning point. He was not stepping deeper into sin—he had no bond to the Medici stronger than his duty to his father. But his father’s betrayal had been hidden by lies. This act could not be concealed. It was public. It was a confession.

And Tito resented the cost.

Still, he looked ahead. In a year—or perhaps half—his life could begin anew. He had money safely stashed outside Florence. He was twenty-eight, at the peak of his abilities. Could he not shed the past like old clothes, cast aside the weight of mistakes, and step forward into a brighter scene?

As he rode toward Siena, reins loose, the wind in his face, he imagined a future free from regret. A future where he was no longer haunted by love gone wrong or unexpected returns.

But Tito did not know that, as he left the council chamber, he had brushed past a man in the dim lamplight—a man whose face he didn’t recognize. It was Ser Ceccone, also ready to sell information, also willing to betray those who had failed.

Tito returned from Siena quickly, only to depart again on another journey, from which he did not come back until the seventeenth of August. Nearly two weeks had passed since the arrest of the accused, and still they remained in prison, their fate undecided. For Romola, all other concerns had faded into insignificance during this time—her world reduced to watching the shifting tides of possibility that might determine their outcome. At times, hope seemed strong: delay worked in their favor, and those in power hesitated to act, fearing the backlash of a hasty decision. On one side, voices clamored that the Republic was under threat, and mercy toward the prisoners would invite attack from its enemies. On the other, there was a clear understanding that condemning five prominent citizens to death and confiscating their property would ignite bitter enmity among their families, turning every influential household against those who signed the sentence.

The final judgment rested with the Eight—the body responsible for criminal justice—and required six votes to pass. But the Eight shrank from responsibility, asking instead that the Signoria—the Gonfaloniere and eight Priors—share the burden. The Signoria, in turn, declined, proposing an appeal to the Great Council. This was possible thanks to a law enacted nearly three years earlier at Savonarola’s urging: any citizen condemned by the “six beans” (the fatal six votes) had the right to appeal to the Great Council. Yet now, the accused’s allies resisted this appeal, chiefly because it would hasten a verdict; legally, the sentence must be passed before an appeal could be filed. Their resistance succeeded, and a compromise was reached: the case would be referred to a large assembly scheduled for the seventeenth, composed of all higher magistrates, the Senate of Eighty, and a select group of citizens.

On that day, Romola had been granted permission to see her godfather for the second time, though only in the presence of witnesses. She returned to the Via de’ Bardi with her cousin Brigida, still unaware whether the council had reached a conclusion. Monna Brigida left immediately to await news at the home of a friend connected to one of the magistracies, hoping to bring back word as soon as it came. Romola sank into the first seat in the bright saloon, too overwhelmed to care about her surroundings or her position. Her back faced the door, her head resting in her hands. Time stretched long. She waited for her cousin’s return.

But when the door opened, it was not Monna Brigida who entered.

Since the night she had last seen Tito, Romola had no tangible proof that he had betrayed his oath—no evidence to confirm her worst fears. On the contrary, she had heard that he was still trusted by the Medici, believed to be carrying out secret missions in Romagna under the guise of fulfilling official duties. The confusion surrounding the conspirators' case allowed him to remain unimplicated. Yet her suspicion refused to fade. The horror she felt at his treatment of Baldassarre haunted her, coloring every thought of him. It was as if she had witnessed a murder—her imagination forever stained by the image of blood upon his hands.

As she heard his footsteps on the stone floor, a cold tremor ran through her. She could not turn. She could not rise. He said nothing at first. After a brief pause, he sat across the table from her. Then she lifted her eyes and looked at him—but she remained silent.

He did not seem irritated. Calmly, he spoke.
“This meeting mirrors our parting, Romola. But I know you are in great distress. I have come—if you will listen—to bring you hope.”

She started, shifted her posture, but her gaze remained wary.
“You speak even though it is you,” she murmured, “and yet I do not reject the news.”

“It is true,” he said. “The council has been postponed until the twenty-first. The Eight have finally issued a sentence of condemnation—but now the condemned have formally requested the Appeal to the Great Council.”

Her expression softened. Hope surged.
“And when will it be granted?”

“It has not yet been decided. But it may be. The Special Council will meet again on the twenty-first to decide whether the appeal should proceed. In the meantime, three days remain—a window in which things might change, in which influence might be brought to bear.”

Romola rose abruptly. Color flushed her face, her hands shook. In that moment, everything else—her anger, her doubt—was forgotten.

“Perhaps,” Tito said, rising too, “you already know what I am about to suggest. You are thinking of the Frate.”

“I am,” she said, looking at him in surprise. “Has he done something? Is there news?”

“Only this. Messer Francesco Valori’s fury and harshness were decisive today. Half those who voted against the prisoners did so only out of fear. And there are many supporters of Fra Girolamo here—Piero Guicciardini, for instance, who resisted strongly within the Signoria; and Giovan Battista Ridolfi, though he is a Piagnone, will not easily forgive the death of his brother Niccolò.”

“But how can they deny the appeal?” Romola asked, indignant. “It is the law. It was one of the greatest triumphs of the popular government!”

“They call it an exceptional case,” Tito replied. “There are arguments, yes—but more bluster about the danger to the Republic. Still, the council agreed to postpone. That means the next three days matter. A powerful voice, well placed, could sway the uncertain minds who wish to allow the appeal—and perhaps even check Valori’s rage. I’ve learned that the Frate himself has sent a message to Valori in favor of Lorenzo Tornabuoni. You know you sometimes have access to him. It might be worth trying now.”

“Yes,” Romola said quietly, as if speaking to herself. “I cannot believe the Frate would support denying the appeal.”

“I heard several men in the Palazzo say that it would be a lasting disgrace to Fra Girolamo if he allowed a government made up mostly of his own party to block the appeal—without protesting—when he himself has boasted in sermons and writings that he secured the law.” He paused. “And between us, Romola, though I respect your Frate deeply, he has grown accustomed to preaching about human sacrifice—killing tyrants, rooting out wicked malcontents. Some of his followers may find such rhetoric hard to reconcile with mercy now.”

“I know,” Romola said, her voice heavy with pain. “But he is driven to extremes. Once, it was different. I will ask for an audience. I cannot rest without it. I trust in the greatness of his heart.”

She did not look at Tito. Her eyes wandered downward, lost in thought, unaware that the words she spoke were addressed to her husband.

“Then waste no time,” Tito said smoothly, adjusting his hat as if preparing to leave. “And now, Romola—you may begin to see, beyond prejudice, that my wishes align with yours. You need not see my safety as a fault.”

A jolt ran through her—like lightning. The full awareness of his presence returned. She looked at him, silent.

“At least,” he continued, his tone slightly harder, “you will try to base our relationship on something more than the assumption that because evil is possible, I must have committed it. Am I alone to be excluded from your mercy?”

The emotion that had been suppressed for two weeks surged forward, unstoppable. She spoke with fierce clarity, indifferent to consequences.
“It is too late, Tito. Once suspicion takes root, it cannot be killed. Now I know everything. I know who that old man was—he was your father, the man to whom you owe your life, your standing, more than if you had been born of his blood. Compared to that, the betrayal of my trust, of my father’s trust, is small. As long as you refuse to admit the truth about him, a horror grows between us. The law that binds us cannot be obeyed. I am not a machine. I have a soul that recoils from your actions. Our marriage is a lie—a farce built on perpetual deception.”

Tito did not answer at once. When he did, his voice was careful, edged with alarm.
“So you mean to leave me, then? To abandon what the law gives you a claim to?”

“I want to leave you,” Romola said sharply.

“And suppose I refuse?” he challenged. “Suppose I insist on keeping what is mine by law? You will then, of course, make your accusations public. You will summon your mad assassin—no doubt ready to obey your command—and tell Florence that you believe his testimony because he desires to kill you. First, you’ll accuse me of calling you a Medicean conspirator. Then you’ll accuse the Medici of betraying you. And in both cases, you’ll offer the perfect proof: that you believe I am capable of anything. What a spectacle—what a role for a wife! And if you succeed in making yourself a laughingstock, you will surpass every heroine of ancient tragedy.”

He paused. She said nothing.

He pressed on, feeling the advantage.
“I think you have no real grievance except that I failed to live up to some vague, lofty ideals you set for me when you gave me your love. By withdrawing that love, you’ve reduced me to merely supplying your needs—as a respectable Piagnone, generous and pious. I doubt you’ll succeed in destroying me. But perhaps you’ll start by gaining the ear of Messer Bernardo del Nero?”

“Why do I speak of myself?” Romola cried, overcome with anguish, sinking back into her chair. “It is hateful to think of me at all.”

She did not notice when Tito left the room. She did not know how long passed before the door opened again, admitting Monna Brigida. But at that moment, she rose abruptly.
“Cousin—we must go to San Marco at once. I must see my confessor, Fra Salvestro.”

The morning light was still fresh and clear when Romola set out once more for San Marco, her heart heavy with purpose. The previous evening, through the intercession of Fra Salvestro, she had secured an audience with Fra Girolamo in the chapter-house of the convent. Such meetings were rare—Savonarola guarded his privacy with rigid care, fearing even the faintest shadow of scandal. When granted, they were conducted with deliberate openness, never shrouded in secrecy. That was why this meeting was scheduled at a time when others might be present in the outer cloisters—so that no suspicion could cling to their conversation.

Romola chose the main route through Florence, not merely for convenience, but to gauge the mood of the city. Every loggia, every corner of the piazza, every shop that served as a gathering place for gossipers, buzzed with talk. Trade had slowed, and in its absence, politics had taken over. The people were divided—some demanding swift justice for the conspirators, others insisting on the right of appeal under Florentine law. Three days remained before the sentence was to be carried out, and both sides were using the interval to sway public opinion.

Handbills had already begun to circulate. Some bore bold declarations: “Justice or Ruin!” Others proclaimed, “Observe the Law! Uphold the Appeal!” Around these stark headlines, smaller print swam with arguments—arguments that few would read, for it was believed, in those early days of printing, that large type alone could move the masses. Yet Romola cared less for the slogans than for the reasoning beneath them. She hurried forward, glancing anxiously about, determined not to miss a single copy.

For much of the way, she saw only handbills already clutched by eager readers or nailed to walls—only to be torn down by sbirri, the city’s enforcers, who seemed to take pleasure in erasing dissent. But then, near San Giovanni, she spotted Bratti, a familiar figure from the lower ranks of Florentine life. He stood hunched over a bundle of wet sheets, exchanging them for small coins.

“Bratti,” she called, stepping closer. “Do you have two kinds? Give me one of each.”

“Two kinds?” he replied, slowly separating the papers, his movements deliberate enough to make Romola impatient. “One says ‘Law.’ One says ‘Justice.’”

“And which sells better?”

“‘Justice’—it flies off fastest. So I raised the price to two danari. Then I thought—why should ‘Law’ not be worth as much? People don’t value things if they’re cheap. If I sold it for one danaro, I’d be doing it a disservice. I’m fair, I am. ‘Law’ or ‘Justice,’ they’re both good wares. I got them free, and I sell them fairly. You want more than one?”

“No,” said Romola, slipping a white quattrino into his hand. “Just one of each.” She folded the papers quickly and moved on.

She soon reached the outer cloisters of San Marco. Fra Salvestro waited beneath the arches, chatting with several lay visitors. Under the new government’s favor, the fear that had once kept people away after Savonarola’s excommunication had faded. Among the group, Romola recognized Andrea Cambini—a known follower of Francesco Valori—gesturing emphatically as he spoke. Fra Salvestro listened with mild interest, the kind of attention given to news without regard for its substance.

Romola felt a surge of irritation. She knew from the fragments of speech drifting toward her that Cambini was discussing the fate of the conspirators. She did not approach. Instead, she walked directly to the door of the chapter-house, and as Fra Salvestro noticed her, he gave a nod and stepped aside into the inner cloister.

A lay brother opened the door for her and closed it behind her. Inside, the air was still, the walls lined with frescoes of mournful saints and prophets. Romola glanced up—those painted figures seemed to watch her with quiet sorrow, just as they had when Dino died. For a moment, memories stirred—the grief, the silence, the desperate hope. But now, her mind was too full of the present to dwell on the past. It was not remembrance so much as a haunting echo, mingling with her fears like a ghostly prelude to what might come.

She pulled out the handbills and began to read. By the time she finished the one advocating the rule of law, the door creaked open. She folded the papers and stood waiting.

Fra Girolamo entered. Romola knelt, as custom dictated, but rose immediately after his blessing. It had been many months since she had seen him—since he had been cut off from public life—and the change in him was striking. His face bore deeper lines, etched by long hours of study, fasting, and sleepless nights. Yet it was not just age or exhaustion that disturbed her. There was something else—something harder, more distant. Was it the absence of the serene compassion she once saw in his eyes? Or was it the growing chasm between her own feelings and his? Perhaps both.

He looked at her with a glance that held a hint of self-assurance, almost defensiveness. He knew why she had come. He knew she would speak of the condemned men—especially Bernardo del Nero, her godfather. And he knew that such a plea would challenge not only his judgment, but his authority.

“It is a matter of great weight,” he began, his voice calm but controlled. “I know you do not come lightly.”

“Father,” Romola said, her voice rising with emotion, “you know what I need. It is my godfather—Bernardo del Nero. He has lived in truth and honor all his life. Since I can remember, he has been as a father to me. That is why I come—not because I expect you to save him, but because I must try. Perhaps you have already decided to use your influence to uphold the law. Perhaps you have already chosen to let justice be denied to those who most deserve it.”

“I do not meddle in state affairs,” Savonarola replied firmly. “I preached for good governance, yes—for a government that serves the Christian life. But I do not interfere in matters best left to experienced citizens.”

“Then how can you say that?” Romola interrupted, her voice trembling. “This is not a minor issue. This is about the right to appeal—about mercy. You helped win that right for Florentines. Does it not follow that you must defend it now, especially when it concerns men like Bernardo?”

She paused, then added, “If you believe in the power of mercy, then why deny it to those who are innocent of treason, yet accused because of their name? Why allow hatred to triumph over law?”

There was a flicker in Savonarola’s lips—a brief contraction, like pain. But he did not yield.

“My daughter,” he said, “I speak only as I am guided. I cannot claim divine insight in every matter. In this case, I have no vision beyond human wisdom. The law of appeal was passed to protect Florentines from tyranny. But these five men conspired to destroy the Republic. They refused to bring their case before the Great Council. They have forfeited their right to appeal.”

“How can they have forfeited it?” Romola demanded. “They have not been condemned yet. And if it is private hatred that denies them the appeal—if it is fear that drives the council apart—then why not stand against it? You yourself taught that hatred must not find its way into public acts. Do you not see that this is exactly what is happening? That very hatred you warned against is being used to justify murder.”

Savonarola’s voice grew heavier. “There is mercy, and there is weakness. At times, mercy becomes treason. Florence needs strength now. Not only to punish past crimes, but to prevent future ones. The tyrant still gathers forces in Romagna. The enemies of Florence wait in the courts of Italy. To spare these men would be to endanger the whole city.”

“What plot?” Romola asked, her voice sharp with alarm. “You speak of a second conspiracy. What proof do you have?”

“You carry papers,” Savonarola said, pointing to the handbills. “One may tell you.”

Romola unfolded the unread sheet. It confirmed her worst fears: the government had uncovered a new plot, set to unfold in August. Her breath caught. Tito had lied. He had claimed to want Savonarola’s help—when in truth, he had only wanted to escape. The betrayal was complete.

She crumpled the paper and turned back to Savonarola.

“Father,” she said, her voice breaking, “how can Florence be safe when the worst men always escape? And you—you encouraged this deception by favoring Lorenzo Tornabuoni, who wore two faces, while my godfather has always been honest. Ask Florence who among the five has the truest heart. I dare say Bernardo del Nero will be named by most. You once intervened for another prisoner. You were not neutral then. Why are you silent now?”

“I do not wish Bernardo dead,” Savonarola said, his face darkening. “Only banished.”

“Then why not speak for him?” Romola cried. “Why let an old man of seventy-five die in shame, when you could give him the chance the law allows? Is it because he does not flatter you? Because he is not loyal to San Marco? Is it because he is not one of yours?”

She paused, her cheeks flushed, her lips trembling. Silence stretched between them.

Then, suddenly, she stepped forward. “Forgive me, father. I know I have spoken harshly. But I cannot stop. I was weak, I was blind—but I believed in you. I trusted your light. Now… I cannot see it. You say the soul must listen to its own voice. Then I must speak.”

Savonarola stood motionless. He was not angry—though he could have been. But he was burdened. He saw the depth of her pain, the sincerity of her love. And he saw, too, the difficulty of his own position. He was not free to act as conscience alone commanded. He was bound by duty, by politics, by the weight of expectation.

“You have my pardon,” he said quietly. “You speak from love. But love must yield to the greater good. The leaders of Florence believe the safety of the Republic demands this punishment. I cannot override their judgment.”

“Then you desire their death,” Romola said, her voice low but fierce. “You do not protest. You do not urge the law. You let them die.”

“I do not desire their death,” he repeated.

“Then why do nothing?” she shouted. “You could have spoken. You could have reminded them of the law you helped pass. You could have said—this is not justice. But you are silent. You are afraid. Or worse—you are glad.”

Her words hung in the air. Savonarola’s face hardened.

“Be thankful,” he said, “that your soul has not faced such trials. You see only one side. I see many. I must choose the path that furthers the work God has given me. The death of five men—were they less guilty—is a small price to pay for stopping tyranny, for advancing God’s kingdom upon earth. That is my purpose. That is why I live. That is why I am ready to die.”

Romola stared at him. Once, she would have bowed her head in reverence. Now, she saw only contradiction. His words rang hollow. He spoke of God’s kingdom, yet defended the bloodshed of innocents. He claimed purity, yet stood by while hatred ruled.

“You speak of God’s kingdom,” she said, her voice shaking. “But what if it is not God’s kingdom you seek? What if it is only your own power? The French king brought reform? No. He brought conquest. Take care, father—lest your enemies be right. Lest your visions be only dreams of dominance.”

Savonarola’s eyes flashed. “The cause of my party *is* the cause of God’s kingdom.”

“I do not believe it,” Romola whispered, her body trembling. “God’s kingdom is wider. Wider than hatred. Wider than vengeance. If it is not, then let me stand outside it—with the ones I love.”

They stood facing each other—two souls, two convictions, two truths. No words remained.

Romola turned, covered her head, and walked out in silence.

Three days had passed since the city’s turmoil began, and now, just as midnight crept over Florence, the moon rose above the rooftops of the piazza before the Old Palace—yet it cast no light upon the stone pavement. Not a single inch of ground was visible; instead, the entire space was choked with a restless, surging crowd. Where silence once held sway, allowing the night wanderers’ footsteps, lute strings, and hurried whispers to echo sharply in the stillness, there was now only a deafening roar—a chorus of shouts, curses, trampling feet, clashing steel, and the sudden, jarring toll of a bell that cut through the din like a blade. Almost every citizen who could be called part of Florence’s public life was awake, drawn to this place or struggling to enter it.

Inside the palace, the council chamber remained filled with the chief magistrates—the eighty senators and other distinguished men—who had debated long into the night, their voices rising in heated contention over whether to grant an appeal or carry out the death sentence on the five imprisoned conspirators without delay. Their arguments had grown so fierce that the sound of their quarrel reached the throng outside. Only moments ago had the decision been made. The Signoria had been split—four members stood firm for the appeal, defying threats that their homes would be razed if they refused. Then Francesco Valori stepped forward, his voice sharp and unyielding: if the government failed to defend the people’s liberties by executing these five traitors, he warned, others would rise to do it—and not without consequence for those who opposed them. His words struck like thunder. When the votes were recounted, the four dissenting voices were gone. All nine now stood united in black beans—the verdict of death, immediate and final. And though not spoken aloud, another fate was sealed: that of Valori himself, whose bold stance would soon mark him as a target.

As the judicial Eight departed for the Bargello to prepare the execution, the five condemned men were led barefoot and bound through the very midst of the council chamber. It was their friends who had arranged this—knowing that Florentines would not remain unmoved by seeing such dishonor inflicted upon men of honor: Bernardo del Nero and Niccolò Ridolfi, both revered elders who had upheld their convictions long before the Medici order turned retrograde; Tornabuoni and Pucci, young luminaries whose absence from the halls of power would leave a hollow silence. But pity, when it came, was not of the kind that could save them—it was the bitter, helpless sorrow that only deepens resolve for vengeance.

Below, in the palace courtyard, Romola leaned against one of the massive pillars, waiting. She had won permission—through urgent entreaty—to visit her godfather that evening, to stay with him until the council’s decision was known. Now she waited beside his confessor, ready to follow the guard that would lead him to the Bargello. Her heart was fixed on being near him until the end, as her father might have done. She had silenced all protests. Giovan Battista Ridolfi, brother of Niccolò and a follower of Savonarola, stood at her side, promising protection. He was going to witness his own brother’s death, consumed by grief but resolute. Tito was also within the palace—but Romola had not seen him. Since the night of the seventeenth, they had avoided each other. Tito knew only by inference, from reports of the Frate’s neutrality, that her efforts had failed. He was surrounded by officials and foreign envoys, all awaiting the outcome of the council. He maintained a quiet demeanor, speaking little, even when addressed. If someone mentioned Romola in connection with the events, he replied that her mind was too agitated—her hostility toward him, he said, stemmed from the fact that he continued in office under a regime that condemned her godfather. For her sake, he felt it best not to approach her.

“Ah, the old Bardi blood!” Cennini remarked, shrugging. “I wouldn’t be surprised if this shakes _her_ loose from the Frate, as well as some others.”

“It is understandable in a woman,” said a young French envoy, bowing slightly to Tito, “especially one so beautiful—she is your wife, after all—to believe her affections should rule over the good of the State. Who among us wants a cousin executed? But such sentimentality must not be encouraged in men. Your Florentine polity suffers from it.”

“That is true,” Macchiavelli observed calmly. “But where personal bonds are strong, the conflicts they spark must be reckoned with. Many half-measures are merely rash blunders. The only safe strikes are those so severe they cannot be avenged.”

“Niccolò,” Cennini said, “there’s a clever wickedness in your speech sometimes—I begin to suspect your pleasant face is a mask for Satan.”

“Not at all, my dear Domenico,” Macchiavelli replied, smiling and placing a hand on the older man’s shoulder. “Satan was a blunderer—an innovator who failed spectacularly. Had he succeeded, we’d all be worshipping him, and his portrait would be more flattered than any saint’s.”

“Well then,” Cennini muttered, “I don’t say your doctrine isn’t clever enough for Satan. I only say it’s wicked enough.”

“I tell you,” Macchiavelli continued, “my doctrine is shared by all who seek goals beyond their own noses. Ask our Frate how he plans to bring about his universal renewal. He’ll say first, a free and pure government. But since love between Florentines can’t be forced, he concludes: cut off every head that stands stubbornly in the way. Yet if a man earns scorn by enforcing a severity that isn’t thorough enough, he makes a mistake. And I suspect the Frate has made just such a mistake. This was a moment when he might have gained strength by championing the appeal. Instead, he lost luster and gained nothing.”

Before anyone could respond, a servant announced that the prisoners were leaving the council chamber. Most present rushed toward the door, eager to secure a clear path behind the guards—because the execution site drew both the deeply passionate and the coldly curious alike.

Tito remained behind. He had always recoiled from scenes of violence and suffering. Five days earlier, he had hoped the sentence would not be carried out—exile would have sufficed. His safety demanded no more. But now, he felt a strange relief knowing Bernardo del Nero’s head would soon be severed. The new understanding Romola had shown him upon his return—her unwavering belief in Baldassarre, her growing defiance—had awakened a fresh fear in him. If she acted in ways that made him appear suspect, he foresaw not only failure, but humiliation. Her faith in Baldassarre had hardened her resistance. If Bernardo lived, she would easily win him over to her cause. Romola seemed more than ever an uncontrollable force in his life. But if Bernardo died, the barriers she would face in opposing him—her pride, her sense of duty—might prove insurmountable. So, Tito found comfort in the knowledge that the scaffold was being erected at once.

Four others—his allies—were to die alongside Bernardo. But a man’s own safety becomes a deity that demands terrible sacrifices. Even in pursuit of what he desired, Tito found himself seeking what he loathed. Yet he had faced such paradoxes before. As he heard the shuffle of boots and the clang of chains echoing up the stairs through the open doorway, he answered the French envoy’s questions with calm resignation, showing no trace of inner turmoil.

To Romola, those sounds fell like thunder in a heightened world. Her senses were sharpened beyond ordinary limits. She needed no support, shed no tears. She felt a presence so intense it transcended grief or joy—a communion with forces older than pain or pleasure. Since Bernardo’s fate was sealed, the conflict within her had dissolved. She was no longer separate from him—she was with him in spirit, defending his name. If he was punished for treason, he was not a traitor. He was caught between two duties, torn by loyalty to principles and to people. He did not choose the noblest cause, yet he died because of his nobleness. A lesser man might have avoided this guilt. But Bernardo could not.

Romola felt the full weight of sympathy for the individual soul—something that constantly rebels against rigid political judgments. She walked with her second father toward the scaffold, steeling herself to endure shame, knowing it was unjust. The path was guarded by three hundred armed men—ordered by Francesco Valori, in a strange contradiction: afraid both of popular fury and of a rescue attempt. Among the contradictions of the night, this was perhaps the most striking.

When they reached the court of the Bargello, Romola was allowed a brief farewell with Bernardo and his confessor. Though the crowd surged around them, many eyes turned to watch. The old man, forgetting his hands were bound, raised them toward Romola’s golden hair—then stopped himself, leaning forward to kiss her forehead. She seized his fettered hands and kissed them as if they were relics of sanctity.

“My poor Romola,” he whispered, “I have only to die. But you must live—and I shall not be there to help you.”

“Yes,” she said quickly, “you _will_ help me—always—because I will remember you.”

She was pulled away and led up the steps to the loggia overlooking the courtyard. There, she took her place, determined to see the last look of the man who had loved her as a daughter. Beside her, Battista Ridolfi promised to guide her through a rear door once the moment had passed. Still, in the depths of her mind, a fragile hope flickered: perhaps, somehow, a rescue would come—perhaps the scaffold would never taste blood.

For a long time, movement filled the court—flickering torchlight, swaying heads, muffled voices, waves of sound rolling in from outside. To Romola, it felt like standing in the eye of a storm, untouched by its fury, focused only on holding up a signal until the eyes searching for it could no longer see.

Then—silence.

Even the torches seemed to still. The executioner stood ready. Bernardo del Nero appeared on the scaffold, climbing the steps slowly, steadily. Romola did not move. She made no sound. She stood firmer, drawing strength from his composure.

She saw him pause. She saw his white head held high. In a voice clear and calm, he spoke:

“It is but a short space of life that my fellow-citizens have taken from me.”

He looked slowly around. Romola felt his gaze rest upon her. She felt her arms reach out across the distance.

And then—nothing.

Only when a long while later, it seemed, a voice said, “My daughter, all is peace now. I can conduct you to your house.”

She uncovered her head. The confessor stood beside her in a dim room, where other solemn men murmured quietly.

“I am ready,” she said, rising. “Let us lose no time.”

She believed all clinging was over. From now on, her strength would be used only to escape—away from a grasp that made her shudder.

On the eighth day after that fateful night, Romola stood at the edge of the Mediterranean, gazing out over the gentle swell of the sea just beyond what was then a quiet fishing village—Viareggio. Once more she had fled from Florence, and this time no voice called her back. She wore the grey robes of a religious woman, not as a disguise now, but as a mantle of surrender. In her sorrow, the distinction between truth and pretense had blurred; she cared little whether she was seen as who she was or something else. The old ties that once bound her—love, duty, identity—had snapped under the weight of disillusionment.

She had believed in marriage as a sacred bond, a union so profound it transcended mere choice. But now even that conviction had crumbled. What good was a vow if its foundation was illusion? Had she not tried to live by those promises only to find them impossible? And so, once again, the urge to escape rose within her—not as rebellion, but as exhaustion. Yet freedom offered no relief, only another kind of suffering. For a woman who feels that her deepest relationship was nothing more than a mistake, there is no redemption. She has lost not just love, but purpose—the crown of her life.

And now, even the vision that had sustained her—some grand design, some noble end that gave meaning to endurance—had vanished. The world seemed chaotic, effort futile. Every act felt like pulling at tangled threads. Fellowship, once a source of strength, now appeared selfish and exclusive. Where once she saw nobility, she now saw contradiction.

Her faith in Savonarola, the man she had once regarded as a living saint, had turned to ashes. He had been her hero—not for stoic silence, but for his fierce, self-giving love. But now, stripped of reverence, she saw only flaws: the rigidity, the hypocrisy, the way his reform became less about God’s kingdom and more about power in Florence. His words, once inspiring, now rang hollow. “The cause of my party is the cause of God’s kingdom”—she heard only pride beneath that phrase. Perhaps such declarations always carry a tinge of egoism. Yet they are also the breath of conviction, the fire that drives great deeds forward. Without them, life sinks into timidity, into care for only what is near, while ignoring the larger truths that elevate us.

But Romola could not see beyond her pain. Her grief had clouded her judgment. Anyone who has ever loved deeply and then lost that love will understand how such a blow can shake not just trust in another person, but belief in goodness itself. When the highest human ideals fall, we begin to doubt our own worth. We stop believing in the better part of ourselves, because it too seems tainted by the same brokenness.

Romola felt pity wither inside her. She had given so much—cared for others when few cared for her. Was it not enough? She had striven toward the unattainable, and now she was spent. All she wanted was peace—simple rest, free from thought, free from decision. She remembered the dreams of her youth: floating in water like a nymph, drifting without care. Now, the sea before her seemed to offer that dream. The waves shimmered golden under the setting sun, calling softly. Could she lie down upon them, sleep, and let death come gently?

She could not kill herself—not yet. Life still pulsed strong within her. But she longed for it to end.

Near where she stood, the shore curved inward, sheltering a small boat moored in the cove. A memory stirred—one from Boccaccio’s *Decameron*, read in secret when her father slept. It told of Gostanza, a young woman heartbroken by love, who did not have the courage to take her life, but instead rowed out to sea, wrapped her head in cloth, and waited for disaster. She wished to be destroyed, not by her own hand, but by fate.

Romola had never thought much of that story—until now. As she walked along the beach, she saw another boat approaching, carrying one man. He landed, pulled his vessel ashore, and began unpacking his catch. He was likely the owner of the smaller boat as well. Soon he would leave, and the chance would vanish.

“Is that little boat yours?” she asked, stepping forward.

He looked up, startled by the tall figure in grey, and bowed slightly. “Yes, Sister,” he said. “It’s old, barely seaworthy, but someone might mend it.” He smiled. “By the blessing of San Antonio, my fishing has prospered. I’ve a newer boat now—once belonged to Gianni, who passed on. But I haven’t sold this one yet.”

Romola asked the price. While he bent over his gear, she slipped the money into his satchel, which lay beside his half-eaten meal. Then she watched him fold his sail, asking how he’d set it to go out to sea. She paced, waiting, until finally he gathered his things and walked away.

The light faded. Gold bled into dusk. The sea grew dark. No sound but the soft lap of waves.

There was no tide here to help carry her off, but a faint breeze stirred from the land. She stepped into the boat, unfurled the sail as she had learned in a fleeting lesson, and secured it. It caught the wind. That was all she needed.

She cast off the moorings and rowed slowly outward, until the land was gone, the sky deepened, and stars began to appear—like distant hearts beating across the vastness.

At last, she rested. She pulled back her cowl, removed the kerchief binding her hair, and folded both beneath her head as a pillow. The boat rocked gently. The night air brushed her face. She was alone—truly alone. Free from duty, from expectation, from the crushing burden of choice.

Had she found the dream of her childhood? No. Memories clung to her like wings broken in flight—memories of love, of connection, of moments when she felt known. Even pain had carried warmth. But now, in this solitude, there was no comfort. The heavens offered no message. The stars shone coldly, like eyes that saw her but did not recognize her.

With a great sob, she wished she were slipping into death.

She drew the cowl over her face, choosing darkness. Then, in the stillness, she felt herself in a grave—but not at rest. She reached out, touched hands—familiar, beloved—and whispered, “Wake up.”

On the morning of February 27th, just past ten o’clock, the streets of Florence swelled with movement, all currents flowing toward San Marco. It was the final day of Carnival, and though the city knew that another Bonfire of Vanities awaited at the Old Palace, the people’s attention had shifted elsewhere. The Piazza di San Marco thronged with a dense crowd, pressed tightly together like waves against a shore—newcomers pushing forward from every entrance, yet unable to advance beyond the front ranks, which stood firm and unyielding. Around a semicircular barrier before the church, the multitude formed a silent ring. Inside it, the Dominican monks of San Marco began to gather. But the wooden pulpit raised above the church door remained empty.

It would soon be occupied by the man whom the Pope had exiled from the Duomo’s pulpit, forbidden by other bishops to be heard, and warned by the city’s rulers not to be listened to under penalty of excommunication. This man had spoken plainly: “A corrupt, faithless Pope who seized the papal throne through bribery is not Christ’s true Vicar. His curses are broken swords—mere hilts without blades. His commands contradict Christian life; therefore, obedience to them is not only unlawful—it is forbidden.” And still, the people came to hear him preach in his own church. Still, on this very morning, they knelt to receive communion from hands declared accursed by Rome.

Why? Because the question burned in their hearts: Was this friar truly chosen by God to rebuke the High Priest who wore sacred robes unworthily? For the common folk, no doctrinal argument could settle such a matter. Only a sign—a clear, undeniable miracle—would prove divine favor. As long as belief in the friar brought hope rather than fear, no sign was needed. His words were music, guiding them along a path they already desired. But now, when faith meant economic ruin, political isolation, and the threat of an interdict upon the city, doubt rose like a tide: *What wonder do you show us?*

That demand had grown louder in Savonarola’s ear—not as accusation, but as a cry from the people’s soul. He answered outwardly with calm assurance: “The time will come.” Within, his faith flickered—but never failed. No prophet speaks for the masses without drawing strength from yesterday’s conviction, trusting tomorrow may renew it. This moment was his response to that demand—the pulpit erected not merely for preaching, but for revelation.

As the monks in black cloaks filed into place, the crowd’s eyes were not yet fixed upon the pulpit. Savonarola had not yet appeared. Instead, there was curiosity—recognizing familiar faces among the brethren, some born to noble houses, others linked to the artisans and shopkeepers who made up the bulk of the assembly. Not until the last monk took his position, until the books were opened and the chant began, did whispers ripple through the crowd: “Fra Girolamo must be coming now.”

And then—silence. A hush spread across the square, not from command, but from expectation. The psalmody ceased, and in its place, a stillness so deep it seemed to hold breath. Then, abruptly, the pulpit was filled.

A figure wrapped entirely in black cowl and mantle stepped forward, knelt, and bowed his head, face turned away. The crowd waited. Time stretched. The monks chanted on, but the silence around them was electric. Even those who hated him felt their arms grow heavy—unable to raise a hand. There was awe in the air, even among enemies.

Then—a tremor. Like a shared shiver, the crowd stirred. The figure rose. He turned. Slowly, he pulled back his hood.

His face emerged—drawn, lined deeper than forty-four years should allow, marked by fire and burden. His hands, delicate, almost fragile, reached out—not in command, but in appeal. They seemed less like flesh and bone than vessels of pure feeling, extensions of a spirit too intense for mere touch. At the sight, some in the front ranks dropped to their knees—devout followers, perhaps, or those moved beyond resistance. Others stood firm, torn between reverence and fear. Some refused to kneel—afraid that blessing from an excommunicated man might invite divine wrath. Others sneered, seeing only a cunning actor staging a new spectacle, one they could not resist, despite themselves.

But then—his voice.

Low at first, clear and steady: _“Misereatur vestri…”_
More fell to their knees.
Higher it rose: _“Benedicat vos omnipotens Deus…”_
Now it thundered—like a trumpet blast echoing across the piazza. And beneath it, every head bowed.

After the blessing, Savonarola sank to his knees, face hidden, spent. These surges of emotion were part of his life. Long ago, he had told the people: “Without preaching, I cannot live.” Yet it was a life that consumed him. In minutes, some rose, but many stayed down. All eyes watched him.

He lifted a crystal vessel holding the consecrated Host.

“You remember,” he said, “three days ago I asked you—if I held this Sacrament before you, if my work is not from God—that you pray fervently to the Most High: if this is not His truth, let fire consume me, that I may vanish into eternal darkness, hidden from His light by my falsehood.”

He paused. Then: “I ask again. Pray now.”

A breathless hush. Perhaps none truly prayed. But all felt the weight of his prayer—soft, yet unmistakable in the stillness:

“Lord, if I have not spoken in sincerity, if my word does not come from You—strike me now with Your thunder. Let the flames of Your wrath surround me.”

He stopped. Stood motionless. Eyes raised. The Host in his hands. His body trembling with spiritual tension. The sun, after hours of gray clouds, broke through—first in scattered streaks across the convent wall, making some flinch in awe. Then, suddenly, a wider opening. Light poured forth—not sharply, but gently, like a smile. It touched the crystal vase, then washed over Savonarola’s face, gilding him in a soft, radiant glow.

At once, the cry burst forth: “Behold the answer!”

The warmth flooded his frame. The shout surged through him. It was his final moment of pure triumph—a vision bloomed within him: a greater scene ahead, before all Christendom, where he would stand again, sealed by heaven, charged with divine power, vindicated.

But it lasted only a heartbeat. While the echo of the cry still vibrated in the air, he turned and stepped inside the church, unable to bear the weight any longer.

When he was gone, the light returned to ordinary brightness—falling evenly on stone and sinner alike. The crowd stirred. Laughter broke through. Mockery rose in voices sharp and varied. The spell had shattered. The silence, the kneeling, the awe—they had been endured, not embraced.

From a loggia across the square, Tito watched, thoughtful. “It seems,” he said, “the cheers are turning to criticism.”

“Still,” replied Pietro Cennini, “it was a powerful moment, was it not? Fra Girolamo reminds us that once, the monk’s robe meant something more than mere piety—it meant authority over men’s souls.”

“Yes,” said Pietro. “And until I see proof that he trusts God’s judgment less than most men—rather than more—I shall not believe he would dare such a test unless his soul were free of conscious deceit.”

A month after the Carnival, on a morning near the end of March, Tito descended the marble steps of the Old Palace, heading toward San Marco with a purpose that carried more weight than it seemed. For some reason, he avoided the direct path—a straight line from the palace—and instead chose a longer route through the Piazza di Santa Croce, where the crowds were emerging from church after the early Lenten sermon.

It was in the grand cathedral of Santa Croce that the Lenten sermons had lately drawn the largest audiences. Savonarola’s voice was no longer heard even in his own church, San Marco—silenced by a hostile Signoria acting under orders from the Pope. The pontiff had threatened an immediate interdict upon Florence if the “wretched worm” and “monstrous idol” were not forbidden to preach and sent to Rome to beg forgiveness. In response, the city had taken up the challenge: not with silence, but with mockery. The most thrilling part of Lent now was not hearing Savonarola himself, but listening to those who attacked him, ridiculed him, and called for proof of his claims.

And so, at Santa Croce, the Franciscan assigned to deliver the Quaresimal sermons had declared a bold wager: he would walk through fire alongside Fra Girolamo, to test whether the Dominican’s prophetic words were truly divine. After all, Savonarola had claimed that miracles would accompany his message at the proper moment. Now was that moment. If Savonarola walked through the flames unharmed, his doctrine would be proven true. If he perished, his falsehood would be laid bare. And to show no fear of death, the Franciscan offered to go into the fire himself—willing to burn for the sake of truth.

Savonarola, as always, ignored the provocation. But fate had a twist: the fiery preacher was none other than Fra Francesco di Puglia, who had once faced off against Savonarola’s follower, Fra Domenico, in Prato. Then, the conflict had been purely rhetorical—until Fra Francesco was recalled by his superiors. Now, when news reached Fra Domenico, preaching to women along the Via del Cocomero, he stepped forward to defend his master. He accepted the challenge—declaring himself ready to walk through the fire with Fra Francesco.

The people, already stirred by the spectacle of faith tested by flame, began to take sides. But Savonarola, sensing the danger of such public debate, ordered Fra Domenico to withdraw. The Franciscan, however, insisted: his challenge was not to a disciple, but to Fra Girolamo himself.

After that, interest in the sermons cooled slightly. Yet this morning, when Tito arrived at the Piazza di64 - Part of that ground has been already seen by us as the hill of Bogoli, - CHAPTER LXIII
" />

But this morning, when Tito entered the Piazza di Santa Croce, he found, as expected, that the crowd was thick and restless, pouring out of the church in great numbers. Instead of scattering, many gathered near the entrance of the Franciscan monastery. Tito followed their gaze, moving through the throng with casual ease, yet watching closely for any sign of movement from within the cloistered gates.

It was not a person they awaited. Their attention was fixed on a large placard nailed to the wall by order of the Signoria, its surface covered in clear, official handwriting. But curiosity was frustrated by the fact that most of the text was in Latin. Though nearly every man could guess what it said, they craved precision, and the inability to read the script bred irritation—especially among those who knew just enough Latin to be annoyed by their ignorance. For while pulpit quotes were often explained by preachers, written Latin remained alien to the common eye. Spelling itself was chaotic, and few could decipher it with confidence.

“It’s the Frate’s doctrines,” said Goro, a familiar figure in the crowd, “that he’s to prove by being burned. The Signoria’s put it up so we know. That’s what the Padre’s been saying in his sermons.”

“Nay, Goro,” replied a sleek shopkeeper, “you’ve got your legs twisted. The Frate must prove his teachings by _not_ being burned—he walks through the fire and comes out whole.”

“Yes, yes,” chimed in a young sculptor, cap jauntily tilted, “but Fra Girolamo refuses to walk through the fire. He’s already sound and whole. Why should he risk it just to come out unchanged? He leaves such tasks to Fra Domenico.”

“Then he’s a coward!” cried Goro, voice rising. “Suffocation! That’s what he did at Carnival—had us all gather in the Piazza to see lightning strike him. Nothing happened.”

“Hush,” said a tall shoemaker, slippers dangling over his shoulder. “You’re as wise as a calf with water on its brain. The Frate won’t flinch. He may say nothing now, but when the time comes, he’ll step into the fire alone. But I’d give a shoestring to know what this Latin means.”

“There’s too much of it,” sighed the shopkeeper. “Is there no scholar here?”

A general turn of heads revealed Tito approaching from behind.

“There he is,” said the sculptor, smiling and lifting his cap.

“The secretary of the Ten,” said the shopkeeper, doffing his own hat. “He’s going to the convent, no doubt. Make way for him.” Though Florentines rarely showed such deference, Tito’s presence commanded it. His black mantle, fastened with a gold fibula, seemed regal; his velvet cap, ordinary in form, looked like something rare and noble. His face, hardened since arriving in Florence, only deepened his air of quiet authority. He raised his cap and said, “Thanks, my friend. I merely wished to see what the placard says—ah, as I expected.”

He had been informed that the government allowed anyone willing to volunteer for the trial by fire—provided they signed at the Palazzo. A generous gesture, worthy of the Signoria. Naturally, many believers might step forward. What was fire to one whose faith was firm? A man feared fire because he believed it would burn him. But if he believed otherwise?

Tito lifted his shoulders and paused, letting the thought settle. “For which reason,” he continued, “I have never doubted the Frate when he said he would enter the fire to prove his doctrine. In his place—if you believed the fire wouldn’t burn you—would any of you hesitate to walk through it, as easily as across the dry bed of the Mugnone?”

As he spoke, the crowd shifted. Some faces brightened with sudden understanding. Others tightened with hesitation. Faith was one thing—practice another. The prospect of real fire made them cautious.

“Then, Messer Segretario,” said the sculptor, “Fra Francesco is the greater hero. He offers to enter the fire for truth, knowing it will burn him.”

“I don’t deny it,” Tito replied smoothly. “But if he is mistaken, he’ll be burned for the wrong side. The Church does not count such deaths as martyrdom. We must wait until the trial happens.”

“True,” said the shopkeeper, impatiently. “But will you interpret the Latin for us?”

“Of course,” said Tito. “It lists the doctrines the Frate teaches—the ones the fire will prove or disprove. You’ve likely heard them before. First: *Ecclesia Dei indiget renovatione*—the Church of God needs renewal.”

“That’s true,” several voices agreed.

“It means the priests should live better lives,” said the shoemaker. “No miracle needed. That’s what the Frate has always said.”

“*Flagellabitur*, then,” Tito continued. “It will be scourged. *Renovabitur*: purified. *Florentia quoque post flagellam renovabitur et prosperabitur*: Florence, too, after the scourging, shall be renewed and prosper.”

“That means we’ll get Pisa back,” said the shopkeeper.

“And trade with England again,” added an old man in a berretta. “There’s been enough scourging already—our trade is sinking.”

At that moment, a tall figure wearing a red feather emerged from the monastery door. He exchanged a glance with Tito, who casually tossed his becchetto over his shoulder and turned back to the placard. The bystanders stepped aside respectfully for Messer Dolfo Spini.

“*Infideles convertentur ad Christum*,” Tito read on. “The infidels shall be converted to Christ.”

“Turks and Moors,” said the shopkeeper. “I’ve no quarrel with that.”

“*Haec autem omnia erunt temporibus nostris*,” Tito went on. “All these things shall happen in our times.”

“Why else would they?” asked Goro.

“*Excommunicato nuper lata contra Reverendum Patrem nostrum Fratrem Hieronymum nulla est*: the excommunication against our reverend father, Fra Girolamo, is null. *Non observantes eam non peccant*: those who ignore it do not sin.”

“I’ll judge that after the trial,” said the shopkeeper.

“Which will settle everything,” said Tito. “That’s all the Latin—just the doctrines to be tested. The rest is a Tuscan proclamation calling for volunteers to sign up. Can I help you further?”

He bowed slightly, cap raised, and moved away with effortless grace.

Once clear of the piazza, Tito quickened his pace. After a few turns, he stopped before a quiet door and gave a light, distinctive knock. It opened to reveal a young woman, whom he playfully tapped under the chin as he asked if the Padrone was in. Without ceremony, he passed through a half-open door into a spacious but cluttered room, where Dolfo Spini sat playing with a fine stag-hound—one moment sniffing at a basket of puppies, the next licking Spini’s hands with affectionate obliviousness to her master’s character.

Spini barely glanced up. Tito stood quietly, thumbs in belt, waiting.

“A fine *bracca*,” he remarked calmly.

Then, in a tone cool and clear, yet commanding attention, he added: “When you finish such distractions that can’t possibly wait, my Dolfo, let us speak of business. My time, though I wish it were eternity at your service, is not entirely mine this morning.”

“Down, Mischief, down!” Spini snapped, pushing the dog aside. “Malediction!” He rose abruptly, placing a hand on Tito’s shoulder. “I hope your sharp mind sees all the angles of this scheme. To me, it’s as dark as the bottom of a sack.”

“What troubles you, my cavalier?”

“These accursed Frati Minori at Santa Croce. They’re backing down. Fra Francesco himself seems afraid—worried the Prophet might use magic to fake a miracle. He fears he’ll be dragged in and burned, while the Prophet emerges unscathed by sorcery. And still, not a single lay brother offers to stand beside poor Fra Domenico.”

“It’s the stupidity of the tonsured skull,” said Tito. “They fail to see that whether they burn or not matters little. Have you sworn to them they’ll not touch the fire?”

“No,” Spini admitted, puzzled. “Because one must go in with Fra Domenico, who thinks it’ll take a thousand years to gather the fagots.”

“Not at all,” Tito said. “Fra Domenico won’t go in. I’ve told you before—only your brilliant mind needs repetition. Now that the Signoria has taken charge, all we need is a day, fuel prepared in the Piazza, and a crowd expecting a miracle. If the Prophet leaves without a sign, he’s ruined. The people will drive him out. The Signoria can banish him. The Pope can do as he pleases. So, my Alcibiades, swear to the Franciscans their grey robes will never come near the flames.”

Spini rubbed his head, tapped his sword against his leg—trying to grasp the invisible web of strategy.

“But,” he said, looking up, “unless we seize him in the Piazza when the people are enraged, Valori and the Salviati will rise for him. I know they talked of it after Ascension Sunday. And the people might turn. Rumors of the French king returning—some chance in his favor—could save him. The city won’t be safe until he’s gone.”

“He *will* be gone soon,” Tito assured him. “Without any effort beyond this little farce of the Trial by Fire. Wine turns to vinegar without shouting—your Florentine sages would say so. You’ll rid your city of an incubus with cleverness, not clumsy swords.”

“But suppose he *does* walk through the fire, with magic or the devil’s help?” Spini asked, grimacing. “How do you know there’s nothing in such things? Scholars believe in them. This Frate is capable of anything.”

“Oh, of course such things exist,” Tito shrugged. “But I know the Frate isn’t in league with the devil. The only magic he relies on is his own skill.”

“Skill?” Spini scoffed. “Calling it skill to make Florence clash with the Pope and all Italy—beckoning the French king who never comes? You may call him able. I call him a hypocrite who wants to rule everyone and become Pope.”

“You judge with your usual insight, my captain,” Tito replied. “But our views don’t clash. The Frate wants power. To oppose the Pope, he needs a foreign lever—and Florence as a fulcrum. Once I saw him as a narrow bigot. Now I see a shrewd, ambitious man who knows his goal and aims with the same precision you aim a ball in *maglio*.”

“Yes, yes,” Spini grinned. “I can aim well.”

“True,” Tito said gravely. “And I mention this only so you may see how this victory will enhance your reputation in Rome and Milan—useful when the city changes its policy.”

“Well, thou art a good little demon,” Spini said, patting Tito’s shoulder. “Thou shalt have good pay.”

“Of course,” Tito smiled, “any advantage to me depends entirely on your—”

“We’ll dine at my palace tonight,” Spini interrupted, nodding significantly. “I’ll explain the new plan to them all.”

“Pardon, my magnificent patron,” Tito said, “the plan has been the same from the start—unchanged except in your memory. Are you certain you hold it now?”

Spini repeated it.

“One thing more,” he said as Tito turned to leave. “There’s Ser Ceccone, the sharp-nosed notary. He’s been useful lately. Tell me—you who can see a man wink from behind, can I keep using him?”

Tito hesitated. He knew Spini too well to offer honest advice when vanity was involved. “Doubtless,” he said quickly. “I have nothing against Ceccone.”

That mention stung Tito. He had once feared Ser Ceccone. His nature made him alert to dangers—small things that could grow. He remembered vividly the October morning when Romola appeared at Nello’s shop and forced him to admit he knew Fra Girolamo wouldn’t leave the city. Ceccone had witnessed it. Worse, Tito sensed the notary disliked him. Now, after fleeing Medicean plots and returning to the city, Ceccone had allied himself with the Arrabbiati and Spini. And Spini, fond of boasting to intimates, might reveal secrets—especially if Ceccone’s jealousy sharpened his mind.

To be undone by a petty notary—by the sting of an insect offended by accident—was pitiful. “But,” Tito thought, “his dislike is no deeper than a hungry dog’s grumble. I’ll win him over with prosperity.” He had recently secured Ceccone a temporary post as extra *cancelliere* under the Ten—believing that bribe would silence the cur.

But perfect schemes require perfect foresight. Tito didn’t know that Ceccone’s envy had turned to hatred for reasons unknown to him. That evening, after Tito left the Special Council, he brushed past Ceccone on the stairs. The notary, fresh from Pistoja, had learned of the arrests. Like Tito, he had been involved in the Medicean plot—privy to plans, eager to betray. But he had been too late. His offer was coldly rejected. The council already had the information. And since he had meddled in sedition, he was advised to stay out of harm’s way.

Ceccone blamed Tito. He held his tongue, but his bitterness grew. And worse, he had begun receiving small payments from the Milanese court—spying on Florence. But his reports were dismissed as outdated. He suspected Tito of secret ties to Milan—though he had no proof. Still, he chewed the suspicion like a bitter root.

And so, as Tito walked away, the seeds of future trouble had already been planted—not in the fire, but in the shadows of ambition and resentment.

Tito’s visit to San Marco had been announced in advance, and he was promptly conducted by Fra Niccolò, Savonarola’s secretary, up the winding staircase into the long, quiet corridors lined with cells. These halls, though austere in their simplicity, bore traces of divine artistry—Fra Angelico’s frescoes flickered here and there like fleeting glimpses of heaven, delicate as rainbows on dissolving clouds. In them, the Madonna sat crowned in radiant glory, and the Divine Child gazed forth with an eternal promise. Yet the beauty was rare, broken by stretches of bare walls and narrow windows that let in only a sliver of light, illuminating nothing but the hard pallets and crucifixes within each cell.

It was a time of rest in the monastery. Most of the monks were absent, and silence reigned. At the end of one corridor, behind a door leading to a small antechamber where the Prior often sat at his desk or received visitors, the light fell upon a single figure kneeling before a crucifix. The back of the man was turned, clad in the long white Dominican tunic and scapular. He could have been any ordinary friar—someone whose sins were no worse than idle thoughts during choir, or petty satisfaction when another monk spilled ink on his illuminated manuscripts. His aspirations might have been no higher than to climb safely into Paradise through prayer, fasting, and obedience.

But beneath this plain robe beat a heart far more complex, burdened by a consciousness beyond the reach of most men. Savonarola was not merely praying—he was wrestling. Though his lips moved in Latin words—“_Cor mundum crea in me_”—his mind was elsewhere, consumed by visions of the trap his enemies had laid for him. He had entered his cell, fallen to his knees, and begun to pray, seeking peace, seeking assurance that the decisions pressing upon him were not betraying God’s will. But the turmoil within would not be stilled.

Images flooded his mind: the fire in the Piazza, the crowd, the challenge to walk through flames unharmed. He had spoken of miracles before, declaring that God would reveal Himself in due time. To the Florentines, eager for resolution, now seemed that time. They saw it as proof of faith—not just in Fra Domenico, who was ready to enter the fire, but in Savonarola himself. Why did he hesitate?

The answer was not simple. Savonarola believed in miracles—yes, in the sense of divine interventions, of inward revelations, of prophetic inspiration. But the idea of walking through fire, tangible and immediate, pressed too heavily on his imagination. It demanded not only belief but action. And his nature, shaped by both intense emotion and sharp reason, could not reconcile the two. He felt the heat of the flames even in thought, the agony of flesh meeting flame—so vividly that he recoiled from the very notion. Even if he resolved to go, he knew he might falter at the last moment.

Yet he could not dismiss the expectations of his followers. They too desired the trial, not out of malice, but out of hope—a hope that the miracle would confirm their faith and silence their doubters. And he could not satisfy them. Not without betraying something deeper.

He had told himself that the trial must not be a personal test, but a divine one. Only if the Pope’s legate came, if ambassadors from every great power stood witness, and if the fate of Church reform hung on the outcome—then, perhaps, would he step forward. Until then, he reserved himself for greater duties. He could not abandon the chariot of his mission to wrestle with every challenger.

And yet—what if Fra Domenico went? What if the Franciscan, driven by fervor, stepped into the fire? Then the miracle might still happen. Or perhaps not. But the mere act of offering himself could become a sign, a token of divine favor. Still, Savonarola could not allow it to proceed unchecked. He would do all in his power to prevent it. The fuel could be gathered, the people assembled, the rituals performed—but the actual entry could be stopped. If necessary, he would ensure that Fra Domenico carried the consecrated Host into the flames. That, at least, might offer a sacred warrant for protection. Or better yet, the demand itself might provoke resistance, and thus end the trial before it began.

But none of this could be said aloud. He must appear willing, trusting in God’s will. This gap between inner truth and outward appearance troubled him deeply. It was not the simplicity he had preached, nor the purity he once sought. In the heat of the day, when voices roared and passions flared, how could one remember the quiet yearnings of dawn? How could one know whether he had betrayed them?

“O God,” he whispered, “it is for the sake of the people—for they are blind, and their faith depends on me. If I put on sackcloth and cast myself among ashes, who will take up the banner? Have I not been led by a path I did not know, to this work?”

His prayers faded. He remained on his knees, but his mind drifted to visions of Europe transformed—of councils summoned, of corrupt popes deposed, of Christendom renewed. The immediate pain of doubt dissolved in the glow of prophecy.

Then came a knock at the door.

Savonarola rose slowly, drew on his mantle as custom dictated, and met Tito with a calmness that masked the storm within. Tito bowed deeply, not kneeling, but offering a gesture of profound respect. Savonarola nodded quietly, gestured him to sit, and asked at once:

“Your business is weighty, my son—one that could not be entrusted to others?”

“Assuredly, father,” replied Tito. “I shall not waste your time with preliminaries. From Messer Domenico Mazzinghi I learned you might wish to use the next courier sent to France with despatches from the Ten. I beg your pardon if I overstep my bounds—but since Messer Domenico is away at his villa, I wished to inform you that a courier departs for Lyons at daybreak tomorrow.”

Savonarola’s face remained composed, but his eyes—always keen, always alert—dilated slightly, catching the light. Tito had seen this before, and he expected it. He busied himself adjusting his gold fibula, seeming patient, yet watchful.

In truth, Savonarola had anticipated this news. He had already written a letter to the Florentine ambassador in France, urging King Charles to summon a General Council to reform the Church and depose Pope Alexander VI, whom he deemed unworthy, elected through corruption and ruled by simony. This letter lay sealed in his desk. But Tito did not know its contents—only that something important was at stake.

“It is true,” Savonardo said calmly, “that I have letters I would send under safe cover. Our community has affairs in France—among them, a debt owed to Signor Philippe de Comines, for the Medici library we purchased. But I expect Messer Mazzinghi to return before evening. I may gain time by waiting.”

Tito inclined his head. “Indeed, reverend father, that would be wise—except that if Messer Domenico fails to return, I would need to come again late, or you would send the letters through your secretary. And I know you wish to avoid any suspicion of undue closeness between yourself and government officers.”

He spoke carefully, testing the waters. The more hesitant Savonarola appeared, the more certain Tito became of his guess.

Silence followed. Then, a faint flush crept into the Frate’s cheeks. He was holding back—excitement, anticipation, the weight of what was to come.

“It is likely Messer Domenico will return in time,” Tito said, rising as if to leave. “With your permission, I’ll take my leave. But since I may not speak with you again soon, I venture to confide—what is not yet known beyond the Ten—that I intend to resign my secretaryship and depart from Florence.”

Savonarola looked up, surprised.

“I find,” continued Tito, “that I have mistaken my calling. I left the life of letters for politics, but in Florence, one becomes only an instrument—unless one can shape policy. And I cannot. Also, my wife has been alienated from Florence since the events of August. I wish to join her.”

Savonarola nodded. “I understand. You plan to travel, then—to the courts of Europe, to universities? First to Hungary, where scholars are welcomed?”

“Yes, father. I shall leave within ten days. I have not hidden from you that I am not a religious enthusiast. My wife’s faith exceeds mine. But I recognize the justice and grandeur of your vision—of nations and the Church. If you would entrust me with a commission, I would honor it.”

“Stay,” said Savonarola. “When you leave, I will write to your wife—I care for her spiritual welfare, though she departed in anger. As for the letters to France…”

He rose and walked to his desk. He took out a folded letter, its address in his own precise hand, visible but unreadable to Tito. He placed it in a larger envelope, sealed it, and handed it to him.

“Pardon me, father,” Tito said quickly, “but unless you insist, I would rather not carry it. Messer Mazzinghi may return. Or Fra Niccolò can bring it to me later, when I deliver the other despatches.”

“No,” Savonarola said firmly. “I wish you to address this packet yourself. Your handwriting is better suited than my secretary’s.”

Tito sat down to write. Savonarola stood beside him, arms crossed, his face flushed, his lip trembling slightly.

When Tito finished, he rose to leave.

“Take it,” Savonarola said abruptly. “There is no point in delay. I do not wish Fra Niccolò to make unnecessary trips to the Palazzo.”

Tito accepted the letter. Savonarola stood motionless, breath held, tension radiating from him. There is a subtle force, a current, that flows from such passionate souls—when they are inwardly agitated, the air seems to grow still, heavy with unspoken meaning.

Tito bowed low and departed, the letter hidden beneath his cloak.

It reached the courier and left Florence. But before that, another message—encoded, secret—had already been dispatched by Tito’s own agents. It traveled through relays, carried by armed men loyal to Ludovico Sforza, Duke of Milan, who waited along the borders to intercept any correspondence that threatened his interests.

On the seventh of April, barely a week had passed since the last tremor of civic unrest, and yet Florence stood transformed. The Piazza della Signoria, that proud heart of the Republic, now bore a spectacle more extraordinary than even the infamous Bonfire of Vanities—a gathering not merely of people, but of expectation, dread, and fervent hope. Never before had so many souls crowded into its vast expanse; even on the festival of San Giovanni, when joy ran high and the city burst with color, no such throng had gathered. This was no celebration—it was an oracle in the making.

It was nearing midday, and since dawn the city’s people had been climbing, creeping, clinging to every available perch: rooftops, ledges, iron rods jutting from walls, stone pillars, the backs of weathered statues—anywhere that offered a view. Men sat balanced precariously on architraves, their feet braced against rough stonework, hands gripping iron rings embedded in the ancient façades. They endured cramped limbs, chafed skin, hunger, and cold—not for mere spectacle, but for something greater: a miracle.

And they were not disappointed in their longing. Not entirely. For what they came to see was not just fire, nor just men, but divine judgment made manifest. If the monk did not emerge unharmed from the flames, then at least they would witness his entry—proof enough, they believed, that he was consumed. And if he did walk out whole? Then God Himself had spoken. There could be no doubt: the fire would burn, the monks would step forward. Before them lay the platform—eight feet wide, twenty yards long—piled high with fuel like a forest set ablaze: great oak trunks laid as foundation, thorns piled above, and beneath it all, oiled rags and tow, ready to leap into flame. It stretched from the corner of the marble terrace before the Old Palace, near Marzocco, the stone lion whose stern face seemed to watch over the pyre with ancient disapproval.

Three bodies of armed men stood guard: five hundred soldiers of the Signoria lined the palace steps; five hundred Compagnacci under Dolfo Spini watched from across the square; and three hundred citizens, loyal to Savonarola, stood beneath Orgagna’s Loggia, where the Franciscans and Dominicans would take their places. Money had been spent freely, dignity invoked, and faith stirred. Something immense was about to happen—and if the trial failed, the consequences would be no less profound.

Half the monks were already seated in the Loggia, their grey robes filling one side. The other half remained empty, save for a small altar. The Franciscans had arrived silently, their presence a quiet counterpoint to the coming storm. But then, from across the piazza, a chorus rose—two hundred voices chanting in unison: “Let God arise, and let His enemies be scattered.” The sound swelled, echoing off stone and sky, drawing murmurs from the crowd. Some found it stirring; others, unsettling—an echo of arrogance, perhaps, or a cry meant not to pray, but to intimidate.

At the head of this procession came Fra Domenico, draped in a cope of flame-red velvet, cross in hand, his face uplifted with genuine conviction. He truly believed he would enter the fire for God’s glory and for Savonarola’s truth. Behind him walked Savonarola himself, robed in white, carrying the consecrated Host.
