He too sang aloud, his voice strong, his gaze fixed ahead. As he stepped onto the Loggia and placed the Sacrament upon the altar, a strange energy seemed to pass through him. The weight of eyes—the scrutiny of thousands—had sharpened his features, enflamed his spirit. And yet, beneath that outward confidence, a deeper current flowed: fear, not of death, but of failure. For Savonarola knew the true cost of this moment. Not martyrdom, but ruin. The trial was not only about divine favor—it was about survival.

And in a window high above, overlooking the chaos, Tito Melema watched with quiet satisfaction. Two evenings prior, the Florentine courier of the Ten had been intercepted—his dispatches seized. Savonarola’s letter, now in the hands of the Duke of Milan, would soon reach the Pope. It was not mere betrayal; it was a catalyst. The tide was turning. And Tito, ever the strategist, felt no malice, only triumph. He had played his part well. Savonarola had been useful—this clever, fanatical preacher, mixing superstition with political insight, a man who could keep secrets and inspire masses. But now, Tito was ready to move on. Let the game end. He would leave Florence soon, stepping from one world into another, cleaner, safer. Today’s spectacle was but a final amusement—something he had helped engineer, though Dolfo Spini, thick-headed and impulsive, could never have managed it alone.

The chanting continued, rising louder, carried by the Piagnoni, those who wore little red crosses as badges of loyalty. They sang not for themselves, but for the prophet, expecting a sign from heaven. Meanwhile, on the opposite side, silence reigned. The Franciscans stood still, cloaked in grey, no music, no color—only resolve. But they had two champions: Fra Giuliano would oppose Fra Domenico, while Fra Francesco challenged Savonarola directly.

“Surely,” muttered the men perched atop rods and pillars, “it must begin now. Why does the chanting not cease? We should see them move toward the platform.”

But they did not move. The Franciscans watched uneasily, their pale faces peering past the dividing boards at the red-robbed Dominican. That color, they feared, might be enchanted—might summon false miracles by dark arts. A man could emerge from fire unscathed, and yet be deceived by the devil.

Then movement began again—between the Loggia and the Palazzo. The debate intensified. The dignitaries appointed to moderate the trial moved back and forth, consulting, arguing. Soon it became clear: Fra Domenico was being led toward the palace. Perhaps the fire had already been lit. Perhaps the miracle was about to unfold.

But no. The red cope vanished into the building. Another Dominican was summoned. The chanting persisted, tiresome, endless. Savonarola stood motionless, the Host still in his hands.

At last, Fra Domenico emerged again—now clothed in plain robes, flanked by two Franciscans, guarding him lest he be enchanted near Savonarola. The crowd understood: he would not enter the fire. It was Savonarola who would go.

But Savonarola did not stir. Instead, he spoke—firmly, boldly, almost with irony. Like Elijah confronting the prophets of Baal, he demanded an end to delays. Yet words were powerless to those hungry, cold, and weary. What need for speech? If the miracle was not to happen, it was Savonarola’s fault. He could end it now—step forward, offer himself.

More movement. More debate. The afternoon slipped away. Clouds gathered, darkening the sky, chilling the air. The chanting faded, replaced by hushed whispers, then shouts—rising in scorn.

“Light the fire! Drive them in!”
“Let us smell roast—we want our dinner!”
“Come, Prophet! Will anything happen before the twenty-four hours are up?”
“What’s your last vision?”
“Oh, he’s got a dozen in his belly—small change for a miracle!”
“Ola, Frate! Where are you? Don’t waste the fuel!”

Still, the monks and officials moved between the Loggia and the Palace, their arguments unheard, their motions meaningless to the crowd. Only Savonarola’s voice broke through—his voice, always the center of attention. It was he who delayed. He was the obstacle.

Then the shouting turned to uproar. Metal clashed. Feet pounded. Dolfo Spini, provoked by the people’s fury, saw his chance. This was no longer a trial—it was a rebellion. The Compagnacci surged forward, pushing through the crowd, driving toward the Loggia. The people recoiled, shoved aside, some falling into the ranks of the Signoria’s soldiers.

For a moment, all breath stopped. The monks froze. The magistrates hesitated. The Loggia, however, was guarded—by Marco Salviati’s men, brave and loyal. The soldiers of the Signoria joined them. The rush was repelled. The Compagnacci retreated, retreating toward the Tetto de’ Pisani.

And then—rain.

It began in drops, then poured. Dark clouds split open, drenching the platform, soaking the fuel, streaming down the edges of the Loggia. The rain fell on the weary, hungry people, soaking their clothes, their spirits. It ran over the altar, splashed the Host, soaked the robes of the monks. And in that instant, everyone knew: the trial was over.

No fire. No miracle. No judgment from heaven.

The Signoria, no doubt, welcomed the rain—as a natural excuse, better than any lie, to declare the event canceled. It was the outcome Savonarola had expected. Yet he was not glad. As the rain fell, he felt not relief, but a deep, cold certainty: his enemies had won. The trial had ended not in fire, but in disgrace. His honor was broken.

He would have to return to San Marco through the angry mob. Friends who once would have died for him now might turn away. And he knew it.

When the rain ceased, he asked for a guard. It was granted. Had he said he was willing to die for his cause? Yes. And he meant it. But to die in shame—mocked as a fraud, a false prophet? That was not martyrdom. That was the erasure of a life devoted to truth.

“O God,” he whispered, “let me die because of what I am, not because of my weakness.”

The light returned, breaking through the clouds. It fell upon Savonarola as he descended the steps of the Loggia, still holding the Host. But there was no glory in it. No divine smile. Only the indifferent sun, illuminating all things—good and bad, noble and base—in the slow unfolding of time.

He heard no blessing. Only taunts. Threats. He knew this was only the beginning of suffering. Yet his courage did not falter. He walked on, steady, silent.

“Well parried, Frate,” said Tito Melema, watching from above. “But I fear your career here is finished. What say you, Niccolò?”

“It is a pity his falsehoods were not all wise,” replied Machiavelli, shrugging. “With the times so much on his side, especially regarding Church matters, he might have done something great.”

The morning of Palm Sunday dawned clear and bright over the Valdarno, where the olive groves still bore the scent of rain from the night before. In San Marco, Savonarola stood once more at the pulpit, facing a congregation whose faith in him remained unshaken. With quiet dignity and deep sincerity, he declared himself ready to die—his vision of the future already shaped by the shadow of his own fate. He blessed the crowd, and their faces turned upward, filled with reverence and love. Then, without a word, he stepped down from the pulpit, turning away forever from that sacred place.

But by the time the sun dipped below the hills, Florence was aflame with fury. The tensions stirred the day before had smoldered through the quiet hours, now erupting into violence, guided not only by passion but by deliberate design—and with official silence, if not encouragement. It began at the Duomo, where members of the Compagnacci tried to disrupt the evening sermon, drawing the Piagnoni into open conflict. Once tempers flared, the cry rose: “To San Marco! Set fire to San Marco!” Before darkness fell, the church and its convent were surrounded by an ever-swelling mob.

Resistance came swiftly. The monks, long aware of mounting hostility, had arms hidden within their walls. Some fought fiercely in their white robes, like knights defending a holy fortress. Even the Dominican serge could not hide the strength beneath. Laymen who had refused to flee joined the defense—shots rang out near the great crucifix, stones and burning embers rained from the roof, swords clashed in the cloisters. Despite the numbers against them, the defenders held on until deep night.

The government’s actions had been no help. Guards were sent early—not to break up the attack—but to demand surrender: all laymen must leave, arms must be dropped, and Savonarola himself must depart Florentine soil within twelve hours. Had he fled then, he might have been torn apart by the mob. He was willing to go—but his followers would not let him. To leave would mean certain death; to stay meant enduring the storm. Still, some men of high standing slipped out through the garden wall, among them Francesco Valori, who hoped to rally aid from outside.

By midnight, the struggle was nearing its end. The Compagnacci were close to breaching the library, where Savonarola knelt in prayer with those Brethren who had laid down their arms at his command. Then came a second guard force, sent by the Signoria, demanding the arrest of Fra Girolamo and his two coadjutors, Fra Domenico and Fra Salvestro.

A roar of triumph surged through the crowd as lanterns lit the doorway and Savonarola emerged, escorted by guards offering no protection but prison. The mob surged forward—no longer just a mob, but a hunting pack—eager to be first to hurl insults, to strike, to spit upon the man they once called Prophet. As he was dragged through the narrow streets, lanterns lifted to reveal sneering faces, hands reached out to kick and curse. For Savonarola, this was the bitterest moment of life—not because of death, which he accepted, but because of the humiliation of being mocked by those he had sought to save.

Yet even martyrdom does not bring the deepest resignation. True humility comes when one bows in silence and thinks: *I am not worthy to be a martyr. The Truth shall prevail—but not through me.*

That brief, savage victory—seeing the Frate led away in shame—was only the first taste of blood for the beast. There was still work to do. The homes of his allies must be sacked. Half the mob, too late for the siege, had already turned to looting—selectively, with purpose. They targeted the houses of known Piagnoni, proving the riot was not chaos, but orchestrated. And among the targets was Francesco Valori, accused of conspiring with Savonarola to seize power—perhaps to become Doge or Gonfaloniere for life.

Valori, the man who eight months earlier had raised his voice in fierce demand for justice against five fellow citizens, now fled from San Marco only to meet the very justice he had demanded. His house was surrounded. His wife was shot dead by an arrow. And as he hurried to answer a summons to the Palazzo, he was killed by men named Ridolfi and Tornabuoni.

Thus, through the long night and into the grey light of dawn, Florence played out its masque—the Masque of the Furies. But the mastermind remained unseen. Too powerful, too careful, to appear in the fray. He issued orders from a private retreat—a quiet house between Santa Croce and San Marco, the same room where Tito had once visited Dolfo Spini.

There, Spini sat through the night, receiving visitors—some masked as Compagnacci, others disguised in plain clothes, weapons concealed. Wine flowed freely, though Spini watched carefully, drinking enough to sharpen his nerves, fueled by constant reports of the chaos unfolding. Among the most frequent visitors was Ser Ceccone, and later, Francesco Cei, who lingered in hiding, waiting for his exile to be lifted once Savonarola fell.

As the tapers burned low and the shutters showed the dimming sky, Spini rose abruptly, face flushed with anger after a heated exchange with his companions. “The devil spit him!” he thundered. “He shall pay for it, though. Ha, ha! The claws will come down on him when he least expects it. So *he* was to be the great man after all? He pretended to throw everything my way—as if I were blind and beggarly—while he winked and filled his own purse. I’d like to hang skins about him and set my hounds on him! And he has that fine ruby of mine—I was a fool to give it to him yesterday. Malediction! Two years ago he was laughing at me behind his sleeve, spoiling the best plan ever laid. I was a fool to trust such a schemer.”

“A Greek,” said Cei, smiling faintly, “who arrived in Florence with gems sewn into his clothes.”

“You didn’t choose your confidant wisely, Dolfo,” Cei added.

“He’s cleverer than you, Francesco,” Spini snapped, turning on him. “And handsomer too.”

“I humbly believe,” said Ser Ceccone, “that Messer Francesco’s poetic talent will outweigh—”

“Enough!” Spini interrupted. “I hate that notary’s trick of yours.” Then, spotting two Compagnacci entering with urgent looks, he leapt up. “Taddeo—or someone—what news?”

“Bad,” replied one. “The people decided they’d sack Soderini’s house. Now they’ve been stopped. If we don’t act fast, they’ll turn on us. I suspect Mediceans are stirring among them. We may see them attacking your palace across the bridge unless we find another distraction.”

“I have it!” Spini exclaimed, grabbing Taddeo by the belt and pulling him aside. After a swift exchange, he pushed him toward the door. “Go—make quick work.”

As the Compagnacci set out on their grim errand, another man moved through the chill, grey twilight on the opposite bank of the Arno. His purpose bore no connection to theirs; yet fate had drawn him to this desolate stretch of riverbank, where the city’s walls enclosed a forgotten corner. No houses overlooked this place—only warehouses and granaries stood in the distance, their backs turned as if ashamed of the solitude they guarded. The ground sloped gently toward the water, thick with long grass and rushes, dampened by open gutters that emptied into the river. It was here, amid the silence and the sluggish flow, that he came each dawn, drawn not by comfort but by memory.

Once, this very stream had brought him bread—once, a raw carrot, an apple peel—gifts from unseen hands. Such small things mattered when hunger gnawed and shame clung like frost. He would wait here, unseen, hoping for one more chance, one more scrap, anything that might spare him the silent begging he loathed. For Baldassarre hated begging—not merely the act, but the surrender it implied. He would have chosen death over it, had not one thought kept him alive: a hope so fragile it was no longer hope at all, but a persistent shadow clinging to his mind like breath in a dream.

After leaving the convent hospital of San Miniato, where monks had tended him during his helplessness, he had waited for her—the Wife. But she never came. The pestilence had swept through Florence, and he could only imagine her lost among the dead. With her gone, vengeance had seemed impossible. His knife was gone, his strength broken, his will scattered. Yet he lived. Not because he wished to, but because something within him refused to let go—something dim and shapeless, like a distant sound heard just before waking. He waited for it, though he did not know what it was.

Meanwhile, the Compagnacci advanced. Divided into two forces, they moved swiftly—one toward Ponte Rubaconte, the other toward Ponte Vecchio—both converging on the Via de’ Bardi. A single word passed between them, a signal whispered through the ranks: *Sack the house.* They knew their target, though few understood why. The leaders alone carried the full weight of the command: they were to meet at a certain dwelling near the eastern end of the street, where the master—Tito—was expected to be asleep.

But Tito was not sleeping. The events of the previous day had sharpened his desire to flee Florence. Investigations would follow, questions asked, delays imposed. And delay meant danger. Though he no longer wore armor or feared Baldassarre, a deeper fear had taken root—fear not of violence, but of exposure, of being caught in the web of lies he had spun. He had cast off his old life, severed ties, and now prepared to vanish. Maso would hand over the house; horses and mules awaited in San Gallo; Tessa and the children were already lodged beyond the gate, ready to join him.

He descended the stone steps into the courtyard, stepped through the great doorway—no longer the same man, yet still bearing the brilliance of his former self. He turned toward the Piazza dei Mozzi, intending to cross the Ponte Rubaconte. But as he walked, sounds reached his ears—muffled shouts, trampling feet. Was the mob coming into Oltrarno? It was a nuisance. He preferred the quieter route. Now he must take the Ponte Vecchio.

He pulled his mantle close, quickening his pace. No one saw him in the fading light. But before he reached the end of the Via de’ Bardi, the noise returned—louder, closer. The mob was crossing the bridge. Panic seized him. He turned back instinctively, then stopped. There was no time to retreat. He pressed forward, toward the bridge, the roar growing louder behind him.

Then he saw them—the crowd pouring from the Ponte Vecchio, filling the street. He was trapped. Before he could react, he was surrounded—not by common rabble, but by armed men. His cap flew off. He was shoved violently forward, thrust into the narrow passage of the bridge.

“Piagnone! Medicean! Piagnone!” the voices roared. “Throw him over!”

His mantle was torn away, his scarsella snatched at. But it slipped from their grasp. Still, he was dragged, pushed, half-drowned in noise. He felt himself nearing the central arches—his last hope: to be thrown over before they killed him.

And then—he acted.

With a desperate lunge, he broke the clasp of his belt and flung both belt and scarsella toward the parapet, shouting, “There are diamonds! There is gold!”

The crowd hesitated. A rush followed. In that instant, he leapt onto the parapet and plunged into the dark river below.

It was his only chance—and it worked.

He rose to the surface, gasping, swimming with all the strength left in him. The current carried him downstream. If he could reach beyond Ponte alla Carrara, he might escape. The mob above would think him drowned. They did. Peering over the edge, they saw nothing but the black water. Only later, from the opposite bank, would someone glimpse a pale face, half-submerged, eyes wide, nostrils flaring, veins taut on the temples.

Onward he floated—past Santa Trinita, past the last bridge. Exhaustion threatened to claim him. He was not fresh from sleep. But terror drove him onward. He heard cries—perhaps real, perhaps imagined—chasing him down the river.

And then—eyes.

Old eyes, sharp despite age, fixed on the dark stream. Baldassarre sat among the rushes, staring blankly at the runlet that brought him nothing. Then he saw it—a white shape drifting downstream. Could it be fortune? He watched. It grew clearer. A man. A body.

He leaned forward, heart pounding. The body crashed onto the grass beside him. He sprang up like a beast, clutching the velvet tunic, falling onto the form, staring into the face.

Dead?

The eyes were still. But not quite. Baldassarre knelt, fingers gripping the neck of the tunic, knees pressing against the chest. He waited. Fiercely. Trembling. Justice had brought him here. This was the moment.

The eyes flickered. Opened. Wide.

“Ah, yes! You see me—you know me!”

Tito recognized him. But did he recognize the moment? Was this life—or death? Was this redemption—or damnation?

Baldassarre cared not. He pressed his knuckles hard against the throat, kneeling upon the chest, holding fast. Let death come. Let it come now. He would not release the grip until justice was witnessed.

He watched the face. Waited. When the eyes closed again, he dared not trust them. He would not let go. Not until someone found them. Then he would speak—declare that he had slain the traitor who once called him father. They might believe him now. And then he would die content, knowing justice had been done—even if it was only in this world.

So he knelt. So he held. Until the light grew strong and his body could no longer bear the strain. Then he sat, still gripping the tunic, still watching.

Hours passed. No one came. No one saw. Florence was elsewhere—caught in its own storm.

Not long after, Savonarola was being tortured, screaming, “I will confess!”

And still, no one came.

It was not until the sun began to sink that a wagon, drawn by a gentle grey ox, approached the riverbank. The driver bent to gather stones, and saw something strange in the grass. An old man, fallen forward, his hands locked around the garment of a younger man.

They could not separate them. So they loaded both into the wagon and drove toward the Piazza, where the Eight would be told.

As the wagon entered the streets, a crowd gathered. No one knew the bodies at first. The old man’s face was hidden, half-buried in the younger’s shoulder.

But soon, recognition came.

“I know that old man,” said Piero di Cosimo. “I painted his likeness once. He is the prisoner who clutched Melema on the steps of the Duomo.”

“And I,” said Bernardo Rucellai, one of the Eight, “remember him from my gardens. I thought he had died in prison. But now… we cannot know the truth.”

Who can point to justice and say, “This is it”? Justice is not a deed, nor a law, nor a verdict. It is a longing—deep, quiet, enduring. Like the Kingdom of God, it is not outside us, but within. And sometimes, in the silence of a riverbank, it comes—slow, inevitable, and final.

CHAPTER LXVIII. Romola’s Awakening

Romola stirred in her boat, drifting through dreams that melted into deep sleep, then back again into vivid visions—until at last she awoke with arms outstretched, as if reaching for something lost. She found herself not in the dim court of the Bargello, where flickering tapers had once seemed to burn brighter and brighter until darkness vanished beneath their glow, but under a sky washed clean by dawn. Her eyes opened slowly to the soft light of morning.

The boat rested quietly in a sheltered cove. To her right, the Mediterranean stretched like a flawless sapphire, calm and unbroken. To her left, nestled in the embrace of mountains, lay a scene repeated countless times along the shores of this most beautiful sea—a valley shaped like a crescent, cradled between gentle slopes clothed in trees and shadow, rising toward rocky peaks. Here and there, paths wound upward through the greenery, leading to a cluster of buildings perched precariously on the hillside, as though they had scrambled up in haste to claim a place where a tall bell tower might rise above the village below, visible from afar as a mark of beauty and faith.

The morning sun, newly risen, touched only the western edge of the valley, casting golden rays across its curve while the rest remained wrapped in cool, dewy silence. The water itself seemed still, as if it too had come to rest. A warmth crept over Romola’s body, tender and gentle, like a breath of kindness. She lay motionless, not watching the landscape, but simply feeling it—the quiet, the peace, the softness of existence. In youth, there are moments when waking brings no thought, no memory, only a blissful suspension of desire. For a few minutes, the past slipped away like smoke; her childhood dreams returned, and she felt she could stay here forever, not because she wished to, but because she simply *was*.

Then came the awareness: she was in the boat, the one that had carried her through the night. It had not brought her to death, but to life—cradling her in its quiet sway like a lullaby. Though she had faced despair at dusk, now she welcomed the dawn. She was alive. She was in sunlight, not in the unknown dark beyond. Could she stay? No sound from Florence would reach her. But already, behind the veil of golden haze, the city’s towers and domes loomed faintly, separated by a river and ringed by hills.

She sat up, trying to resist the flood of thoughts that surged with the question: how far had she drifted? Why should it matter? This was a hidden place, safe from the world. Simple villagers lived here—they would not harm her. For now, she could rest. Later, she would find bread and milk. Then she would settle into the green hush and let time pause.

She turned to gaze once more at the valley, hoping to reclaim that first sense of peace. But scarcely had she settled into contemplation when a cry tore through the stillness—not a single cry, but long and piercing, growing louder and more desperate with each passing second.

Romola knew instantly: a child in distress, abandoned, pleading for help.

She sprang to her feet, one foot already on the gunwale, ready to leap onto the shore. But she paused, listening. Perhaps the mother was near. Perhaps the cry would stop. But it did not. It grew more unbearable, echoing against the silence, and drew her forward with an invisible force. She stepped ashore and walked without knowing which way to go, drawn by the sound.

It came from a rough garden plot to her right, where a half-ruined hut stood among weeds and overgrown crops. She climbed over a broken stone fence and made her way across patches of neglected corn and wild greens. The air changed as she moved away from the sea—thicker, heavier, as if some unseen poison lingered in the greenness that had seemed so inviting.

Now she could see the doorway of the hut. The cry stabbed through her like pain. She stepped inside.

The dim interior held three bodies lying on straw. A man, tall and gaunt. A girl, perhaps eight years old. And a young woman, her long black hair tangled and grasped by a living child—the child whose cry had led Romola here.

Her experience with death and disease made her act swiftly. She lifted the child, pressing it close to her chest, trying to soothe it. Yet her eyes never left the dead. Their features bore the sharp lines of a distant race, their clothing unfamiliar—Spanish or Portuguese Jews, perhaps, cast ashore by pirates who stole their goods and left them to die. Such things happened often. The Inquisition drove them from their homes; greed cast them into the sea; superstition refused them mercy even when they were drowned.

“But surely,” Romola thought, “there must be someone in the village—some woman whose heart will not deny care to this helpless child—if the mother truly is dead.”

She knelt beside the woman. The body looked stronger than the others, less wasted. Romola reached to touch her chest, lifting the yellow woolen cloth that covered her breast—and froze. Purple spots marred the skin. The plague.

A sudden dread gripped her. If the villagers knew, they might recoil from her, from the child, from anything associated with death. But she had money. She could offer it for goat’s milk. She rose, holding the child tightly, and set off toward the village, her mind focused on comforting the infant and finding someone kind.

As she crossed a field, she noticed something strange: patches of corn left untouched, overripe and golden. Apples and figs lay rotting on the ground. There were grasslands nearby, yet no cows, no sheep, no goats. The silence deepened, becoming ominous.

She hurried toward the cluster of houses, seeking life. She picked two figs and fed small pieces to the child, who began to quiet. Still, the fear grew. The air felt thick, tainted.

She entered between two rows of dwellings. It was long past the hour when people should be stirring. Yet not a soul moved. Every door stood open, revealing empty rooms. One house showed a man lying dead, his clothes still on, his head resting on a spade handle, a clay jug in his hand—as if he had dropped everything in sudden collapse.

Romola shuddered. Was this a village of the unburied?

She wanted to listen—but the child cried again, demanding attention. Then she saw a figure crawling from a house, sinking slowly against the wall. She rushed forward.

It was a young woman, fever-ridden, clutching a pitcher. She did not flinch at Romola’s approach. Only one thought filled her: “Water! Get me water!”

Romola took the pitcher gently. “You shall have water. Can you point to the well?”

The woman raised a trembling hand toward the far end of the street.

Romola ran, carrying both pitcher and child. The baby grew calmer with each piece of sweet fruit. As she neared the well, she glanced up at the slope below the church—where she glimpsed a cow and two goats. She hoped to return there after fetching water.

But she was not aware that someone else approached the well from the opposite side.

To the youth of fifteen who saw her, Romola was a vision. Her grey robe flowed like water, her hair catching the sun, the child on her arm with jet-black eyes and olive skin. She carried a pitcher—like the Virgin bringing water to the sick.

“She is the Holy Mother,” he whispered. “Come to care for us.”

He dropped his own vessel and fled.

Romola, now aware of the fleeing figure, continued. She filled her pitcher halfway and hurried back. Inside the house, she found salted meat and flour—no signs of poverty. She placed the child on the floor and gave the woman water. The woman drank eagerly, then closed her eyes, leaning back as if surrendering to relief.

When she opened them again, she murmured, “Who are you?”

“I came over the sea,” Romola said. “This morning. Are all the people dead?”

“I think all who are not dead are ill. My father and sister lie upstairs. No one to bury them. Soon I shall die.”

“Not so,” Romola replied. “I am here to help. I know the pestilence. I am not afraid. But there must be others still healthy. I saw a boy running toward the mountain.”

“I do not know. When the plague came, many fled. They took the cattle. Give me more water.”

Romola decided to follow the boy’s path. She needed to find those who were well, to win their help. She promised to return, lifted the child again, and began climbing the slope.

It was hard going. The heat pressed on her. Hunger and thirst gnawed. At a fork in the path, she paused, considering whether to wait near the cow, which someone might soon milk.

Then she saw him—peering through the branches, five yards away. A broad face, a black priest’s robe, a bucket in hand. He watched her silently.

Romola spoke gently. “I came over the sea. I am hungry. So is the child. Will you give us milk?”

The priest had just heard from the acolyte that the Madonna had appeared—tall as a cypress, with a halo around her head, coming to fetch water. He had doubted. But now, seeing her, hearing her voice—soft, refined, otherworldly—he hesitated. Was this a miracle? Or a woman sent by God?

He dared not run. He dared not advance.

“Come down,” Romola said after a moment. “Do not fear. Fear rather to deny food to the hungry.”

Then the boughs parted. The priest stepped forward, bucket in hand, eyes wide with awe. He sat beside the cow, nervously drew milk, and offered it in a brass cup.

Romola gave it to the child, then drank some herself. The priest watched, his fear shifting into something deeper—an instinctive obedience.

When she smiled at the child, sucking its milky lips, and said, “Give us more, good father,” he obeyed.

Then she looked at him, her voice firm yet kind: “Now tell me, father, how this pestilence came. Why did your people die without sacraments? Why were they left unburied? I came over the sea to help the living—and you will help them now.”

He told her the story. Three families of Jews, twenty souls, had been cast ashore weeks before, some already sick. The villagers refused them shelter except in a distant hovel. When they died, some threw the bodies into the sea. But a storm brought them back. Panic seized the villagers. They dug a grave, buried the dead—but then the plague struck the Christians. Most fled, taking their animals and supplies. The priest stayed, praying. He kept the boy Jacopo with him—but he feared the disease and dared not descend.

“You will fear no longer,” Romola said. “Come down with me. We will find the living, bury the dead. I have walked through plague-stricken lands for months. See—I am strong.”

She turned to the boy hiding behind a bush. “Jacopo,” she called, smiling. “Come. You will carry the child for me. Your arms are strong. I am tired.”

The boy stepped forward, as if pulled by invisible threads. The priest followed.

“Now we will take the milk,” Romola said, “and see who needs it.”

Together they descended. That morning, hope returned to the valley.

Fewer than twenty lived. But all were comforted. Most survived. The dead were buried.

Days passed. Weeks. Months. The men began to dig and sow again. Women smiled at Romola as they carried water jars to the well. The Hebrew child, now named Benedetto, toddled about, baptized in the mountain church.

Romola took a cottage abandoned by its owner, a little apart from the village. On a bed of clean straw—so soft and pure—she rested through the daylight hours, cared for by a widow who had lost her husband to the plague.

Each day, the priest, Jacopo, and the survivors visited her cottage. They brought honey, cakes, eggs, polenta—offerings of gratitude.

They never forgot what they saw: the fair-haired lady with golden hair and brown eyes full of kindness, lying weary after her labors, while the little black-haired Benedetto crawled beside her, claiming every morsel brought to her. And she always shared, saying, “If you love me, love Benedetto.”

Legends grew in that valley. Of the blessed lady who came over the sea, who healed, who buried, who saved. Not a miracle, perhaps—but a truth remembered: that once, in a time of sorrow, a woman came and loved the dying, and the forgotten, and the forsaken.

In the quiet stillness of winter’s early hours, Romola lay at rest, her body freed from exhaustion, her mind wandering through memory and into the uncertain reaches of what was to come. From this new vantage point, the world appeared transformed—its shapes altered, its meanings deepened. The journey she had just completed, since awakening in that small boat drifting on unknown waters, had left an imprint as sharp and enduring as a seal pressed into wax. Once, she had felt unmoored—no purpose, no tether, only a hollow resentment toward life itself. She had whispered to herself, “I am weary of living; I wish only to die.” That plea had echoed softly in her sleep. But the moment she awoke and heard the cry that drew her, something shifted within her. She did not pause to reflect, as she once had in Florence, that life was worth keeping because it allowed her to ease another’s pain. No—she simply *lived*, driven by an urgent need to respond, to act, to reach out where help was called for. The reasons for enduring, for laboring, for caring, no longer needed justification—they were instinct, necessity, truth.

It was as though she had been reborn.

In Florence, human bonds had always been entangled with complex obligations—marriage, civic duty, religious devotion. When those ties failed, when trust dissolved, the blow had seemed so great that it severed her from the world, numbing her compassion. But now she saw clearly: “How could I have ever wished death? If all else is uncertain, suffering is real—and I can ease it. If the cross holds no divine glory, then sorrow is all the more genuine. While my arm has strength, I will stretch it to the fainting. While my eyes can see, they will seek the forgotten.”

And with that realization, the past surged forward—not as a burden, but as a summons. Her work in this green valley was finished. The love and gratitude she had given here, once scattered among the people, now flowed back into the deeper currents of her own history—into the memories of Florence, of home, of those who had shaped her soul. For the first time, she stood apart from her old life, seeing it whole and unfiltered. In that rare clarity, self-examination came like a flood. A conscience attuned to others’ pain began to question her own actions. Had she been hasty? Proud? Always demanding virtue from others while failing to uphold the ideals she once believed in?

She reproached herself for fleeing—her second departure from Florence. Was it not cowardice, disguised as principle? Savonarola had once welcomed her back, not out of sentiment, but because he saw in her a deeper truth than she herself had recognized. His grounds were stronger, rooted in a faith that endured beyond personal comfort. How could she claim to feel the needs of strangers and yet ignore the deepest need—the one closest to her heart?

Yet even as guilt stirred, it was met with resistance. The memory of Tito returned—of their marriage, of the impossibility of true union beneath the façade of shared life. The duties imposed upon her were not love, but lies. She had been trapped between loyalty and integrity, between affection and moral revolt. And flight had been the only way out. All minds, unless dulled by indifference, must wrestle with such contradictions. Life does not allow easy substitutions. The presence of new bonds does not erase the brokenness of old ones. The wound remains—until healed, it calls again and again. Conscience never forgets.

Romola dreaded returning near Tito, yet she also feared being too far away to know his fate. What if he suffered? What if he needed her? A quiet ache remained—a remnant of Fra Girolamo’s words: “You cannot cease to be a wife.” Could anything truly be erased from the blood, from the heart? Once woven into the fabric of her being, could love or obligation ever be wholly undone?

Florence rose in her thoughts like hunger. It was not the future she craved, nor the unknown—it was the familiar. Her emotions fed on memory, on place, on people known and loved. The idea that she might never return grew heavier with each silent hour. What if Fra Girolamo had erred? What if Florence, with all its flaws and contradictions, was not so corrupt after all? Was she, then, elevated above it, entitled to turn her back and declare it unworthy? But if she were truly higher, would she not have held fast to her faith, instead of letting it falter?

Her grief for her godfather softened, replaced by a renewed sense of debt. Nothing could undo the fact that Savonarola had inspired her, awakened a new life within her. Who else had touched her soul so deeply? His mistakes might bring disaster—but could she abandon him because of them? She could not rest.

She scarcely knew whether it was spring’s return stirring her energy, or the burning desire to draw nearer to Florence. She did not imagine entering the city—yet the longing to be close enough to learn its fate consumed her. It drove her forward, eclipsing every other thought.

Then, on a March morning, the villagers gathered to bid farewell to the blessed Lady. Jacopo had brought a mule for her journey, and he would accompany her over the mountains. The Padre, too, would walk with her to the nearest town, guiding her toward the safest path to Pistoja. Her trinkets and money, untouched during her stay, were more than sufficient for her needs.

Had her heart not been pulled so strongly toward the distant city, this moment would have been unbearable. As she walked down the village street for the last time, the Padre and Jacopo waited near the well with the mule. People knelt before her, weeping, kissing her hands, clinging to her robe, begging her not to go.

“Ah, why leave us now, when the season turns fair and the fields will bloom?” they cried.
“Do not grieve,” Romola said gently. “You are well now. I shall remember you always. I must go—to see if my own people need me.”
“But if they suffer pestilence?”
“Look at us again, Madonna!”
“Yes, yes—we will care for little Benedetto!”

At last, she mounted the mule. But as she turned away, Benedetto let out a piercing cry—his small voice breaking the silence. It became the signal for everyone to follow, insisting he ride on the mule’s neck until the foot of the slope. The parting could not be delayed. Yet as Romola rode onward, she turned again and again, watching the little group linger, their faces raised, their hands waving—each gesture a final thread of connection, slowly unraveling into the distance.

On the fourteenth of April, Romola stood once more within the walls of Florence. She had not been able to stay in Pistoja, where conflicting rumors about the Trial by Fire unsettled her mind. Instead, she journeyed on to Prato, uncertain whether she could bear to return to the city that had known so much sorrow. Yet fate drew her forward. There, she met the Augustinian monk from San Spirito—once her father’s confessor—who carried with him the grim truth: Savonarola had been arrested, and Tito was dead.

The monk, speaking with the usual bias of the Black Brethren toward their more fiery brother, told her that Savonarola had confessed under torture, admitting he had deceived the people. Romola listened without flinching. She already knew the weight of what had happened; it was no mere tale to her, but a personal catastrophe. The news of Tito’s death was even more piercing. According to the monk, Tito had escaped a mob by leaping into the Arno River, only to be killed on its banks by an old man who bore him a long-standing grudge. But Romola did not believe that story entirely. She saw beyond the surface—a deeper tragedy unfolding in silence.

That evening, she arrived in Florence. She sat in quiet, her heart heavy beneath the noise of the city: joyous exclamations mingling with mournful cries, stories flying like sparks from a fire. Monna Brigida, her cousin, greeted her with wild gestures, having returned to wearing false hair during Romola’s absence—only now, upon seeing her, she tore it off dramatically. “I’ll be grey,” she declared, “if my dear child will only stay with me.”

But Romola was beyond such distractions. Her thoughts were fixed on two urgent matters: to see Savonarola if possible, and to find Tessa and the children. When Brigida began to chatter about plans for Romola to live with her, she cut in sharply. “Tell me, cousin—has anything been heard since Tito’s death of a young woman with two little children?”

Brigida froze. Her eyes widened. “Cristo! No. What? Was he so bad as that?” she gasped. “Ah, then that explains why you left—without saying much. I thought you were just flighty, leaving your husband when Bernardo was beheaded. But now I see—there was another woman, children… Why didn’t you tell me?”

Romola looked at her earnestly. “No, cousin—please do not speak so. I must find them. They are helpless. That is what I must do first.”

Brigida shook her head, puzzled. “If this is being a Piagnone, then I’ve been taking peas for paternosters. Fra Girolamo said widows shouldn't remarry—step through the door, it’s sin; come down the chimney, it’s welcome. Two children—Santiddio!”

“Cousin,” Romola said gently, “she has done nothing wrong. She knows nothing. I will explain—but not now.”

The next morning, Romola went to the house near San Ambrogio where she had once found Tessa. It was empty. The neighbors confirmed that old Lisa had left a week before, but no one knew where Tessa had gone. Romola suspected Tito had sent her away beforehand—surely he would not have abandoned the children willingly. And if she were outside Florence, finding her might be impossible. Romola imagined her waiting alone, confused and afraid, somewhere along a dusty road.

She had no leads. She could not publicly name Tessa’s connection to Tito—her pride and compassion alike forbade it. Days passed in silence, filled with longing. Every time she saw a pair of small brown legs or a woman in simple peasant dress, her heart fluttered. She did not think of herself as heroic—only that she needed someone to care for, someone to love. To hold those children, to make them feel safe—that would be a kind of peace after all her grief.

There was talk of Tito’s property, which she might claim. But she distrusted it. She decided to give it all to the State, keeping only enough to buy back her father’s library. That sum would support Tessa and the children modestly.

Yet Monna Brigida refused to let the matter rest. She insisted Romola live with her, declaring that if Romola wanted to raise others’ children, then she, too, would help—though they must first be found. Romola felt the sting of that remark. But hope, though fragile, still burned within her. _Somehow_, she believed, she would find them.

Then came a new clue. She learned that Tito had arranged horses and mules to wait for him in San Gallo—meaning he intended to leave Florence through that gate. Romola resolved to ask the gatekeepers if anyone matching Tessa’s description had passed through before the ninth of April.

Walking along Via San Gallo, her veil drawn low, she scanned the street carefully. Then she saw Bratti, the wandering peddler, haggling with a customer. He might know something, she thought. As she approached, lifting her veil, she noticed something dangling from his basket—a necklace. Her breath caught.

“Bratti,” she said, stepping forward, “where did you get that necklace?”

“Your servant, madonna,” he replied, turning slowly. “It’s worth money, but I’ll sell it cheap. My heart’s too soft for trade—I’ve promised to keep it in pledge.”

“Did you get it from a woman named Tessa?” Romola asked, her voice steady.

“Ah!” Bratti smiled. “If you know her, and want to redeem it at a small profit, you’d be doing a kindness. She wept when she gave it to me—seemed like she was drowning. I’ll let you have it for a florin. I don’t like to be hard-hearted.”

Romola paid him quickly, unclipping the necklace from the basket. “Where is she?”

“Outside the gate, at the other end of the Borgo, at old Sibilla Manetti’s house. Anyone can point it out.”

Her steps quickened. She reached the house, heart pounding. Inside, Tessa sat on a low bed, her tears dried into silent sobs. The children played nearby—Lillo hiding under his skirt, roaring at Ninna, then peering out to watch her reaction.

Romola stepped in, her veil cast aside. She held up the necklace. For a moment, she stood still. Then she spoke, her voice clear and gentle, like her father’s used to be:

“Tessa.”

Tessa jumped to her feet, staring.

“See,” Romola said, placing the beads around her neck, “God has sent me to you again.”

Tessa screamed softly, then clung to Romola, sobbing. The children came forward, gripping their mother’s dress, eyes wide as they looked up at the woman who had come to them from nowhere.

That day, they all went home—to Monna Brigida’s house in the Borgo degli Albizzi.

Romola gently told Tessa that Naldo would never return—not because he was cruel, but because he was dead. “But be comforted, my Tessa,” she said. “I am here now. I will take care of you always. And we have Lillo and Ninna.”

Monna Brigida watched, her lips twitching between awe and irritation. “Let it be for now,” she thought. “But I’ll tell this poor girl someday who Romola really is. She doesn’t even know how many fingers she has. But I’ll teach her humility—if she’s going to live under my roof.”

Still, when supper came, she couldn’t resist giving the children extra sweets. Before bed, she whispered to Romola, “You must let me help with their meals, Romola. You’ve never raised a baby. I had twins—only they died at birth.” And then, with a sigh, she added, “But it would be a shame not to care for such cherubs.”

When Romola returned home with Tessa and the children, April was drawing to its close, and another great anxiety had reached its peak—the publication of *Fra Girolamo’s Trial*, or rather, the confessions extracted from him by sixteen Florentine citizens appointed to interrogate him. Issued under the authority of the Signoria, this document stirred such widespread suspicion and unrest that it was swiftly ordered withdrawn. Yet copies had already slipped into circulation, and soon a second edition—this one unauthorized—found eager readers across the city.

Romola, who had begun to despair of ever speaking with Fra Girolamo again, read the confession over and over, seeking clarity amid the chaos of conflicting claims. She wanted not merely to believe, but to understand—to pierce through the noise of partisan outrage and cynical dismissal and find truth where it might lie.

Among Savonarola’s most devoted followers, his failure to hold firm under torture, and his retraction of prophetic claims, sparked a deep, almost unbearable grief. It seemed impossible that the man they had known as a prophet could have broken so completely. But then came the growing belief—soon confirmed—that many of the statements attributed to him were not his own words at all, but fabrications penned by Ser Ceccone, the notary of ill repute who compiled the record. If the Frate had denied divine inspiration, perhaps it was only because the agony of torture had forced the denial from him—not because he truly renounced it.

And indeed, there were troubling facts about the examination itself. Half the panel of sixteen examiners were known enemies of Savonarola. Among them was Dolfo Spini, a member of the newly elected Eight—a body chosen in haste, clearly designed to ensure a predetermined outcome. This was no trial; it was a political execution masked in legal form. The Old Palace had become a stage for slow, ceremonial murder.

The Signoria, eager to secure favorable terms with the Pope and the Duke of Milan, saw Savonarula as an obstacle—too dangerous to leave alive. He threatened corrupt clergy, greedy princes, and self-serving citizens alike. His influence undermined their power. So the decision was made: either hand him over to Rome, or execute him publicly in Florence, thereby asserting the Republic’s dignity. Which path would be chosen? That was the only question left.

Under such circumstances, how could anyone trust the confession? If he had denied prophecy, it was not by choice, but by pain—by the crushing weight of torment inflicted on a soul too sensitive, too finely tuned, to endure long without breaking. And yet, the official account claimed he had suffered only three sessions of the rope and pulley, all on a single day, and that his admissions were made freely, without coercion. Who could believe that?

Others knew better. They remembered the anguish of those who loved him—the way his confessions had caused such sorrow among his followers. They knew he had been tortured far more severely, repeatedly, until his spirit cracked under pressure. Still, some less fervent allies believed the core of the confession was genuine, even if shaped and distorted by the notary’s pen. But they insisted: no crime had been proven. No act of treason, no corruption, no betrayal of faith. Nothing that warranted death.

His fellow monks—men who had lived beside him, watched his conduct, shared his silence—spoke of a life unblemished. One, once his secretary for years and naturally inclined to critique, still affirmed his integrity. Even after the shock of the retraction, he could not deny the purity of Savonarola’s character, the consistency of his virtue. The Pope himself had failed to charge him with heresy beyond disobedience to papal mandates and defiance of excommunication. That breach of discipline was hard to justify, yet many men of conscience felt a moral resistance toward Rome’s authority. They began to see the Church not as a monolithic institution, but as a flawed human enterprise. Disobedience, in this case, did not seem so monstrous.

Men like Soderini and Piero Guicciardini, sharp observers of politics, may have smiled bitterly at the spectacle. Here was a man condemned not for lawlessness, but for being too honest, too zealous—too inconvenient. Had Savonarola been less pure, more cunning, his ambitions might have been welcomed as useful tools by powerful men. But because he spoke truth with fire, he was deemed a threat.

Still, these shrewd minds admitted: though the Republic's actions were clumsy and unjust, the Pope’s response was not entirely without reason. To allow a man who called for a General Council to depose him to remain free was to invite chaos. Savonarola was not just a rebel—he was a dangerous one. Florence had heard him say plainly: *I will not obey the devil.* There could be no peace between such a man and the Papacy.

Yet why must Florence be the instrument of his destruction? Why must the city become the executioner of a man whose vision had once lifted it?

Romola listened to these voices—the faithful, the skeptical, the worldly—and found herself torn. She could not accept either side fully. Her own heart had known both devotion and disillusionment. Over the years, she had observed things—small betrayals, subtle shifts, moments of pride beneath humility—that whispered a deeper truth: perhaps Savonarola’s retraction was not merely a reflex of torture, but something more complex, more tragic.

And yet, she refused to let go of the man she had once revered. She clung to the idea that, despite his flaws, his life had been driven by noble purpose. In the depths of her despair, a new kind of faith emerged—one born not of certainty, but of love. It was impossible, she now believed, that the light she had seen in him was false. That voice, which once stirred her soul, could not have been mere pretense. Any falsehood in him had been a fall, not a design—an entanglement, not a scheme.

She studied the printed confessions closely. Many passages struck her as crude fabrications—sentences marked by awkward repetition, heavy self-accusation, the kind used only by the most shallow hypocrites. But these very distortions made her more certain that the rest of the text reflected what had truly been spoken. For they contradicted not only Savonarola’s nature, but the overall tone of the confession. Only one aspect dishonored him: his claim to divine inspiration. Elsewhere, he remained steadfast in his intentions for Florence, for the Church, for the world.

He confessed nothing about using base means. He admitted no personal corruption. Even in this document, marred by the notary’s venom, Fra Girolamo appeared as a man who sought greatness—but not for himself alone. He desired fame, yes, but only as a means to achieve something greater: the moral renewal of humanity. He labored not with empty sermons, but with concrete efforts to transform belief into action.

One passage stood out:

> “Everything I have done, I have done with the intention of being famous in the present and future ages, of winning credit in Florence, and ensuring that nothing of great importance should be done without my sanction. Once established here, I intended to do great things in Italy and beyond, through the chief personages with whom I had formed friendships and discussed high matters—such as the calling of a General Council. As my efforts succeeded, I would have taken further steps. Above all, when the Council was convened, I meant to rouse the princes of Christendom, especially those outside Italy, to subdue the infidels. It was not much in my thoughts to become Cardinal or Pope. When I achieved my goal, I would, without being Pope, be the first man in the world in authority and reverence. If I had been made Pope, I would not have refused—but I believed that leading such a work was greater than being Pope, because a man without virtue can be Pope, but *a work like this demands a man of excellent virtues*.”

This mingling of ambition and moral idealism was familiar to Romola. She had heard it before in the Duomo, echoing through the pews. Savonarola believed in grand visions, and he believed he was the one to fulfill them. Evil must fall; justice, purity, love must prevail—and they would prevail through him, by his voice, by his blood.

In moments of ecstasy, his sense of self dissolved into the divine. Then, perhaps, he experienced true humility. But in the world, before others, preeminence was essential. It was not arrogance—it was necessity.

And perhaps the confession, even when it described a divided mind, revealed not deliberate deceit, but the natural wavering of a soul under immense pressure. How could one remain unchanged when the adoring crowd turned to scorn, when friends became judges, when torture tore at the nerves and the mind screamed, *“Yes, yes, I am guilty—let me go!”*?

Romola imagined the horror that followed—whether in solitary confinement, his conscience recoiled or reaffirmed the words he had spoken. That anguish pressed upon her own heart, forcing slow, bitter tears.

Every common man in Florence was quick to judge him, to condemn him. But *he* knew sorrow no one else could know—the sorrow of one who loved the highest good, who pursued it with every fiber, and who now saw himself fallen.

She had not yet seen—would not see until later—the evidence of his inner state after the confession. As days passed, reports of new examinations ceased. Savonarola was left alone in prison, granted pen and ink. He wrote—not to defend himself, not to protest—but to speak to God.

His writings were not pleas for vindication. They were a conversation with divine purity, a cry for spiritual rebirth. No trace of old defiance remained. No boastful assertion: *Look at my work!* Instead, a voice of profound sorrow:

> “God placed thee in the midst of the people as if thou hadst been one of the excellent. Thou hast taught others, but hast failed to learn thyself. Thou hast cured others, but thyself art still diseased. Thy heart was lifted up by the beauty of thy deeds, and through this thou hast lost thy wisdom and art become, and shalt be to all eternity, nothing... After so many benefits with which God has honored thee, thou art fallen into the depths of the sea; and after so many gifts bestowed on thee, thou, by thy pride and vainglory, hast scandalized all the world.”

And when Hope speaks, it does not promise glory. It says only:
> “Thou art not forsaken, else why is thy heart bowed in penitence? That too is a gift.”

There is no evidence that, from imprisonment to the final hour, Savonarola ever called himself a martyr. The idea of martyrdom had always been tied to triumph—to seeing his vision fulfilled. Now, that dream was gone. In its place was a quiet resignation, unnamed, unglorified.

Yet precisely because he endured not for honor, but for truth—because he faced power not for sin, but for greatness—because he sought not to deceive, but to elevate—*he may rightly be called a martyr by all who come after.*

For he suffered a double agony: not only the insults, the torture, the death—but the collapse of his own vision. From the heights of divine purpose, he fell into darkness. And in that darkness, he whispered:
> “I count as nothing. Darkness encompasses me. Yet the light I saw was the true light.”

Romola had heard, as if in a cry, the words repeated to her by many voices—the very words Savonarola had spoken when bidding farewell to his brethren of San Marco who came to witness his signing of the confession: “Pray for me, for God has withdrawn from me the spirit of prophecy.” Those words struck her with new unease, stirring doubts about how he truly saw his past during moments of clear self-awareness. And these doubts deepened when still more painful news reached her ears.

May 19th had come, and under its bright sun, two Papal Commissaries entered Florence—charged with completing Savonarola’s trial. They arrived amid the cheers of the people, who now demanded his death. The cry was loud and clear: “It is the Frate’s deceit that brought all our troubles upon us! Let him be burned, and peace will return, and our suffering will end.”

The next day, fresh torture was inflicted upon the already shattered man. At the sight of the instruments of pain, Savonarola fell to his knees, trembling and agitated. In brief, passionate words, he *retracted his confession*, declaring he had lied when denying his prophetic gift. “If I suffer,” he said, “I shall suffer for the truth. The things I have spoken—I received them from God.”

Yet the torture continued. When pressed under it, he was asked why he had spoken those words of retraction. Men were not demons in those days—but only confessions of guilt were seen as justification for release. His answer came, broken and weary: “I said it so they might think me good. Tear me no more. I will tell you the truth.”

Florentine assessors were present at this renewed trial, and soon the words of double recantation spread through the city. Romola was seized with dread and confusion. But then, an idea flashed through her mind: “There will come a moment when he may speak freely. When death stands before him, when falsehood is no longer feared, when he faces the people one last time—then he cannot be silenced. He will speak the final word. I must be there.”

Three days later, on May 23rd, 1498, a long narrow platform stretched across the great piazza, from the Palazzo Vecchio toward the Tetta de’ Pisani. This time, no grove of wood stood ready. Instead, a single great pile of fuel was heaped upon the circular space at the end of the platform. Above it rose a gibbet with three halters—its two arms shaped like a cross, though one had been cut short to avoid the resemblance. Still, some looked upon it with discomfort.

On the marble terrace of the Palazzo stood three tribunals: one near the door for the Bishop, who would perform the ceremony of degradation on Fra Girolamo and his two followers; another for the Papal Commissaries, who would pronounce them heretics and schismatics and hand them over to civil authority; and a third, close to Marzocco, where the Gonfaloniere and the Eight would deliver the sentence of death.

The piazza thronged with expectant faces. Again, a great fire would be lit. For most of the crowd, the expectation was not of justice, but of hatred or cruel curiosity. Yet among them were others—many still—whose hearts held a faint hope, even now, that God might intervene, that some sign might reveal Savonarola as His true servant. And there were still more who watched with trembling eagerness, just as Romola did, awaiting that final moment when he might say: “O people, I was innocent of deceit.”

Romola stood at a window on the north side of the square, far from the tribunal. Near her, watching with equal anguish, was a young Florentine named Jacopo Nardi, twenty-two years old. He would later earn respect as one of the few who, recognizing Savonarola’s greatness, wrote of him with honest intent. Seeing the conflict in Romola—her horror at the scene, yet her desperate desire to witness what might happen—he said gently, “Madonna, there is no need for you to see these cruel things. I will tell you when he comes out of the Palazzo. Trust me—I know what you wish to see.”

Romola covered her face, but the jeering shouts echoed through the air, making the horror impossible to ignore. Then a hand touched her arm. “He comes.”

She looked toward the Palace and saw Savonarola led forth in his Dominican habit. She watched as he stood before the Bishop, stripped of his black mantle, white scapulary, and long tunic—until he stood bare in a simple woolen undergarment, stripped of rank, office, and dignity. He was degraded, cast out from the Church Militant.

The lower part of the crowd reveled in such degradation, finding pleasure in humiliation. A fresh wave of mockery rose as the three men passed to the tribunal of the Papal Commissaries. Were they not now clearly schismatics and heretics? It was easy to believe a man damned when he stood stripped and broken.

Then they passed the third tribunal—the Florentine officials who would pronounce sentence. Even from afar, Romola recognized the loathsome figure of Dolfo Spini, dressed in the solemn black lucco, one of the Eight.

The three men, clad in plain white garments, walked along the platform amid yells and insults. “Cover your eyes, Madonna,” Jacopo whispered. “Fra Girolamo will be the last.”

But soon she had to uncover them again. Savonarola was near. He had mounted the steps. She saw him turn and look out over the multitude. In that instant, hope died. She saw what he saw—torches waving above the pyre beneath where his body would lie, faces illuminated by a harsher light than any flame. She heard what he heard—coarse jokes, curses, taunts.

The moment was gone. Her face sank back into her hands. She knew only that Savonarola’s voice had joined the eternal silence.

On the evening of May 22nd, 1509, five figures sat in a quiet upper room overlooking a loggia. From its right corner, the view stretched along the Borgo Pinti, past the city gate, toward Fiesole and the distant, solemn hills beyond.

At one end of the room, an archway opened into a small inner chamber—little more than a recess—where soft light fell from above onto a modest altar draped in white linen. Over it hung a portrait, barely visible from the distance: a full-length image of a Dominican friar. The painting was shaded by branches and flowers, and the candles below remained unlit.

But the altar seemed unfinished. The floor was scattered with blossoms and green boughs. Among them sat a delicate girl of thirteen, blue-eyed and fair-haired, tossing her hair aside as she wove wreaths. She glanced up at her mother, offering little instructions with a tone of gentle authority.

Her mother, Tessa, sat slowly, her fingers clumsy and thickened with age. She turned often, asking Ninna’s advice, still amazed by her children’s wisdom. She wore the same contadina gown, now broader, with the silver pin in her curly brown hair and the red cord necklace—its end hidden beneath her breast. Her face, round and peaceful, bore the contentment of someone who believed the world was kind. Monna Brigida, once sharp-tongued, slept soundly in a straight-backed chair nearby, her silver hair softly parted under her black hood, her chin resting on her chest.

At the doorway to the loggia sat two others. Lillo leaned against the post, legs stretched out, a large book open on his knee. He swatted a fly with idle focus, his gaze occasionally drifting to the finely printed Petrarch beside him—as if memorizing lines. Across from him, Romola sat quietly, hands folded in her lap, eyes fixed on the distant mountains. Her features had softened with time—the cheekbones less pronounced, the golden hair thinner—but her face carried a serenity unknown in youth. She had endured her deepest sorrows early, and now lived in a calm born of experience.

Lillo noticed she was not listening. He closed his book and waited, his expression growing slightly impatient. Finally, Romola turned, meeting his dark eyes with a warm smile. He shuffled closer, resting his chin on her knee.

“What is it, Lillo?” she asked, brushing his hair back.

“Mamma,” he said, pleased to talk, “what am I to be?”

“You could be a scholar,” Romola replied. “My father was a scholar. He taught me much, and so I can teach you.”

“Yes,” Lillo hesitated. “But he looks old and blind in the picture. Did he gain glory?”

“Not much,” Romola said gently. “The world was not always kind to him. Meaner men rose above him because they flattered and lied. His own son left him to become a monk. Then, blind and alone, he felt unable to use his learning fully—so his work did not live on after him.”

“I wouldn’t want that life,” Lillo said. “I’d like to be something great—something that brings happiness and pleasure too. Something that doesn’t stop me from enjoying myself.”

“That is hard,” Romola said. “True happiness does not come from chasing narrow pleasures. Only by thinking widely, feeling deeply for others, can we reach the highest joy. And that joy often carries pain—yet it is chosen, because we know it is good. The world is full of wrong and difficulty. No man can be great unless he gives up seeking comfort and learns to endure hardship. My father had greatness in integrity—he chose poverty and obscurity rather than falsehood. And Fra Girolamo—you know why I keep tomorrow sacred—*he* had greatness in fighting against powerful evil, striving to lift men to their highest deeds. So, Lillo, if you wish to act nobly and seek truth, fix your mind on that end, not on what may come to you. Remember: if you choose a lower path—seeking only pleasure, avoiding pain—calamity may still strike. And when it does, it falls on a base heart. That sorrow has no healing. A man may say, ‘It would have been better if I had never been born.’”

She paused. Her voice trembled. Lillo looked up, wide-eyed.

“There was a man I knew well—very close to me. He was young, clever, beautiful, and kind to all. I believe, when I first met him, he never thought of cruelty. But because he tried to avoid every unpleasant thing, caring only for his own safety, he ended in the basest acts—acts that bring infamy. He denied his father, leaving him to misery. He broke every trust, to save himself, grow rich, and prosper. Yet calamity found him.”

Romola paused again. Then, softly, “Another time, my Lillo, I’ll tell you another story. Look—there are Piero di Cosimo and Nello coming up the Borgo Pinti with flowers. Let us go to the edge of the loggia and wave to them.”

“How strange old Piero is!” Lillo said, watching them approach. “He scolds you for honoring Fra Girolamo, and yet he brings the flowers.”

“Never mind,” Romola said. “There are many good people who did not love him. Perhaps I would never have learned to love him—if he had not helped me when I was in great need.”
